# Sūrah Maryam (Chapter 19) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Named for a Woman**

Sūrah Maryam is the only chapter of the Qur'an named after a woman. That single fact is worth pausing over, because the Qur'an names its sūrahs after animals (the Cow, the Ant, the Spider, the Elephant), after objects (the Iron, the Fig, the Rope of fibre), after natural phenomena (the Sun, the Night, the Thunder), after events (the Victory, the Smoke, the Earthquake), and after an entire community of scripture (the Children of Israel) — but of all the human beings who walk through its pages, precisely one lends her name to a chapter, and she is Mary. The prophets around whom sūrahs are built — Yūnus, Hūd, Yūsuf, Ibrāhīm, Muḥammad, Nūḥ — are men; Mary alone stands outside that pattern, and she stands there without ever being called a prophet. Her rank is built entirely out of her truthfulness, her chastity, her solitude, and the miracle God chose to work through her. The lesson is not subtle: in the economy of the Qur'an, greatness is not a matter of office but of nearness to God, and nearness is open to anyone. Mary is also, notably, the only woman mentioned by name in the entire Qur'an. Every other female figure in it is identified by relationship — the wife of Adam, the wife of Noah, the wife of Lot, the mother of Moses, the wife of Pharaoh, the Queen of Sheba, the wife of ʿImrān — or by title. Yet no woman in the Book is described at greater length than Mary, and no woman is defended by God as directly: the Qur'an devotes dozens of verses across three chapters (3:35–62, 19:16–36, 21:91, 66:12) to clearing her name, establishing her purity, and placing her among the greatest human beings who have ever lived. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ named her in an authentic report as one of the women who "attained perfection":

> *"Many among men attained perfection, but none among women attained perfection save Maryam the daughter of ʿImrān and Āsiyah the wife of Pharaoh."* (al-Bukhārī 3411, 3433; Muslim 2431, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

And in another transmission, he named four women as the mistresses of the women of Paradise — Maryam, Āsiyah, Khadījah, and Fāṭimah (reported by Aḥmad and al-Ṭabarānī from Ibn ʿAbbās, with chains judged sound by several later scholars). Because nearly every named figure in the Qur'an is a prophet, and because Mary received the announcement of her miraculous conception from the Archangel Gabriel — the very angel of revelation — a small minority of Islamic authorities, including the Andalusian polymath Ibn Ḥazm (d. 456/1064) and the Persian Sufi Rūzbihān al-Baqlī (d. 606/1209), held that she was a female prophet. The majority did not, resting on the Qur'an's own description of those it sent as "men" (12:109; 16:43; 21:7). But the majority position does not lower her: it places her, in the words of the tradition, above every woman who has ever lived, ranked in spiritual station with the *ṣiddīqūn*, "the utterly truthful."

**A Makkan Sūrah with a Foot in Two Cities**

Maryam was revealed in Makkah, in the middle-to-late Makkan period. The Study Qur'an places it after Sūrah Fāṭir (chapter 35) in the chronological sequence, while others place it earlier, near Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ or al-Kahf in the "middle Makkan" group. Two verses are reported by some authorities to have been revealed in Madinah — verse 58 (the prostration verse) and verse 71 (the universal approach to the Fire) — but the overwhelming body of the sūrah belongs unmistakably to Makkah: it addresses idolaters, it argues against the ascription of a son to God, it answers the mockery of men who boast of wealth and lineage, and it comforts a small, persecuted community with stories of God's rescue of His servants. That Makkan setting is not incidental to the content. Maryam is, among other things, a sūrah about the *weak*: a barren old man, an old woman past childbearing, a girl alone in a prayer-room, a virgin with no husband and no defender, a newborn with no father. Every one of them is helpless by the standards of the market-place and the tribe — exactly the standard by which the Quraysh measured worth — and every one of them is chosen by God. The sūrah's opening movement is, in effect, God's answer to a community that had been told for years that it was small, poor, and forgettable: My mercy does not consult your résumé.

**The Sūrah That Converted a King**

Muslim history records an occasion on which this sūrah was recited to extraordinary effect. After the Battle of Badr (2/624), in which several of Makkah's leading men were killed, the Quraysh sent a deputation to the Negus (*al-Najāshī*), the Christian king of Abyssinia, who had granted refuge years earlier to a group of the Prophet's followers who had emigrated to his land. The Quraysh demanded that the Muslims be handed over. The Negus summoned the Muslims and asked them what this religion was that had caused them to leave their people. Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib — the Prophet's cousin, elder brother of ʿAlī, and the leader of that small community — stood and spoke. He described the ignorance (*jāhiliyyah*) they had been in, and then the mission of the Prophet. The king asked him to recite something of what had been revealed. Jaʿfar recited from Sūrah Maryam. The accounts in the sīrah literature (Ibn Isḥāq's *Sīrah* as transmitted by Ibn Hishām, and Aḥmad's *Musnad*) record that the Negus wept until his beard was wet, and the bishops around him wept until their scrolls were wet, and the king said of what he had heard:

> *"Verily this and what Jesus brought have come from the same niche (*miškāh*)."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad*, from Umm Salamah; also reported through other chains)

That moment is a miniature of this sūrah's purpose. Maryam is the Qur'an's bridge to the People of the Book, and it builds that bridge out of shared reverence rather than shared doctrine. It honors Mary more than most Christian sermons do; it insists on her virginity, her purity, her chosenness, the miraculous conception, the miracles of her son, and his status as a Word and a Spirit from God. And then, at the summit of that praise, it draws the line that separates Islam from Christianity with the utmost clarity and no hostility: *"It is not for God to take a child. Glory be to Him!"* (19:35). Theologically, this is the sūrah's spine. Its long, tender narratives of Zachariah, Mary, Jesus, Abraham, Moses, Ishmael, and Idrīs all converge on one claim — that God creates without means, without partner, and without offspring — and its closing polemic (vv. 88–95) is the fiercest sustained refutation of divine sonship anywhere in the Qur'an.

**The Name That Dominates the Chapter: al-Raḥmān**

The other structural feature a careful reader notices within a few verses is the Name. God is called *al-Raḥmān*, "the Compassionate," sixteen times in this sūrah — nearly a third of all occurrences of that Name in the Qur'an outside the *basmalah*. The sūrah opens with *raḥmah* ("mercy"), asks for mercy from God's "Presence" (v. 5), gives Aaron to Moses as a "mercy" (v. 53), pours "something of Our Mercy" upon Abraham's family (v. 50), makes Jesus "a mercy from Us" (v. 21), gathers the reverent "to the Compassionate" on the Last Day (v. 85), and promises that "the Compassionate will ordain love" for the believers (v. 96). The root *r-ḥ-m* is the same root as *raḥim*, the womb. That is not a coincidence of grammar; it is the sūrah's secret. This is a chapter about wombs: a barren womb opened (vv. 5–9), a virgin womb that has never been touched (v. 20), a womb that labours beside a dry palm-trunk in winter (vv. 22–25). Mercy and motherhood are the same word in Arabic because the mother's body is the first mercy any human being ever knows — and this is the root the Qur'an chooses when it wants to tell us what God is like. The God who made the womb to open for a child no one expected is the God who opens mercy for a servant no one expected.

**The Architecture of the Chapter**

The sūrah falls into three great movements with a short bridge between them. The first movement (vv. 1–40) narrates: Zachariah's secret prayer and the gift of John (vv. 2–15), Mary's withdrawal, the Annunciation, the virgin conception, the birth beneath the palm, and the infant's miraculous defense of his mother (vv. 16–36). It then draws the moral: the sects differed about Jesus, and woe to them for the witnessing of a tremendous Day (vv. 37–40). The second movement (vv. 41–65) narrates more briefly: Abraham's argument with his father, his migration, and the gift of Isaac and Jacob (vv. 41–50); Moses at the Mount and the gift of Aaron (vv. 51–53); Ishmael (vv. 54–55) and Idrīs (vv. 56–57). Then comes the great prostration verse — that these were the ones God blessed, and that when the signs of the Compassionate were recited to them they fell down weeping (v. 58) — followed by the tragic sequel: a later generation neglected the prayer and followed desire (v. 59), with the door of repentance still open (v. 60), and the Gardens of Eden promised to the reverent (vv. 61–63). Verse 64 is the bridge: the Prophet is reassured, after a painful gap in revelation, that the angel descends only by command and that his Lord is not forgetful. The third movement (vv. 66–98) turns to argument and warning: resurrection after death (vv. 66–72), the boasts of the wealthy idolaters (vv. 73–76), the man who claimed he would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter (vv. 77–80), the futility of false gods (vv. 81–82), the satans unleashed upon the disbelievers (vv. 83–84), the two processions on the Last Day (vv. 85–86), intercession (v. 87), the shattering refutation of the claim that God has taken a child (vv. 88–95), the promise of ordained love for the believers (v. 96), the ease of the Qur'an (v. 97), and the closing silence of the destroyed generations (v. 98).

**The Thread That Ties Every Story Together: Withdrawal**

A reader who works through the chapter from beginning to end will notice a repeated choreography. Zachariah goes into the sanctuary and calls in secret. Mary "withdrew from her family to an eastern place" (v. 16) and then "withdrew with him to a place far off" (v. 22). Abraham says, "I withdraw myself from you and that which you call upon apart from God" (v. 48), and immediately after his withdrawal he is given Isaac and Jacob (v. 49). Moses is called "from the right side of the Mount" (v. 52) — away from Egypt, away from Pharaoh's court, in the desert. In every case the pattern is identical: *separation first, then gift.* This is among the most practical things the sūrah teaches. The gifts of God in this chapter — a child, a revelation, a helper, a station — are never handed to people in the middle of the crowd. They are handed to people who have stepped out of it. The Arabic root for Mary's withdrawal, *ʿ-z-l*, and for Abraham's, *ʿ-t-z-l*, carries the sense of setting something apart for a purpose. It is not misanthropy; it is not the abandonment of duty. It is the recognition that a heart permanently saturated with noise cannot hear a Word. Every person who has ever had a serious thought in a quiet room, or a genuine prayer at four in the morning, has already verified the principle in miniature.

**Silence and Speech**

Woven through the same passages is a second pairing. Zachariah is struck mute for three nights (v. 10) and signals to his people (v. 11). Mary vows a fast of silence and refuses to answer her accusers, pointing instead to the infant (vv. 26, 29) — and then the infant *speaks* (vv. 30–33). The one who is usually the speaker is silenced; the one who cannot possibly speak is made to speak. The effect is deliberate: it strips the defense of Mary from the realm of human advocacy altogether. No argument she could have made would have cleared her name in that village. God answered for her with a miracle. And the commentators drew from it a rule of spiritual life: when the self falls silent, God speaks on its behalf.

**Prophetic Testimonies to Its Rank**

Every chapter of the Qur'an has attracted a body of reports describing a specific reward attached to its recitation, and Sūrah Maryam is no exception. It is worth stating plainly at the outset what the specialists have said about that genre, because such reports continue to circulate and a reader is entitled to know their standing before he acts on them. The narrations that specify a particular recompense for this sūrah — so many good deeds for each of its letters, or a reward proportionate to the number of those who denied Zachariah and believed in him — are weak, and the scholars of ḥadīth from the earliest period onward did not build upon them. What follows from that is not that the sūrah lacks rank but that its rank is not established by reports of this kind. The Qur'an does not require a narration to recommend it, and a reward promised in a defective chain is not a reward anyone is entitled to claim, which is why the responsible course is to say so rather than to repeat the report and let the reader suppose it is sound.

The sūrah's distinction is attested instead by event, and the event is the one already described above: the recitation of its opening verses before the Negus of Abyssinia, in the episode that turned a hearing convened to hand the Muslims over into the conversion of a king. That is not a promise about the Hereafter but something that happened in this world, before witnesses, and it remains the strongest single testimony the tradition possesses to what this chapter does to those who hear it with an open heart. A promise delayed can be doubted; an event cannot.

A second testimony is the sūrah's place in the community's worship rather than in its lists of rewards. Verse 58 is counted among the verses of prostration in the Qur'an, which means that the recitation of this chapter is interrupted at Maryam's weeping and the worshipper goes down — a small piece of enacted exegesis performed in every generation since, and one that teaches the sūrah's doctrine of tears through the body rather than through argument. The schools differ only over whether that prostration is binding or recommended; none of them disputes the verse's claim on the one who recites it.

Third, this sūrah belongs to the earliest stratum of the revelation that was recited aloud in Makkah, in defiance of a city that did not wish to hear it, and it has remained among the chapters the community carries most widely in memory. It is routinely among the sūrahs a child memorises early, its opening letters are among the most recognisable sounds in the entire Qur'an, and its recitation in the night prayer has been the practice of the devout in every century of Islam. A chapter that is known by heart by millions of people who have never studied it has a rank that no chain of transmission needs to establish.

The last testimony is the one the sūrah makes about itself, and it is the reason the foregoing reports — sound or otherwise — are unnecessary. It is the chapter that names the mercy of the Lord in its first verse and promises His love in its ninety-sixth; that carries within it the annunciation to Zachariah, the birth of John, the vindication of Mary, and the speech of Jesus in the cradle; and that closes by leaving its reader with the difference between the generations that are silent and the living who are still being spoken to. What more is required by way of recommendation than that?

**A Note on the Method of This Commentary**

The commentary that follows treats each of the ninety-eight verses as an independent composition, in strict order, exactly as the Study Qur'an's grouping is here resolved into individual verses. Passages that the source treats as units — 4–6, 14–15, 49–50, 54–55, 56–57, 62–63, 66–67, 69–70, 71–72, 77–78, 79–80, 81–82, 83–84, 85–86, 88–91, 93–95 — are separated and each given its own full treatment, since the workflow of this project requires that no verse be compressed on account of the length of the chapter. Quotations from the Qur'an, from authenticated ḥadīth, and from the classical commentators are given as blockquotes. Nothing has been shortened because the sūrah is long: the chapter whose final verse speaks of the silence of the vanished nations deserves to be read in full, out loud, all the way to *"or hear from them a murmur?"*

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:1

> **Kāf. Hā. Yā. ʿAyn. Ṣād.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Longest Sequence of Disconnected Letters in the Qur'an**

Five letters open this sūrah, pronounced one by one by their names: *Kāf. Hā. Yā. ʿAyn. Ṣād.* They belong to the family of *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*, "the separated (or disconnected) letters," which stand at the head of twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an — sometimes one letter (*Qāf*, *Nūn*, *Ṣād*), sometimes two (*Ṭā Hā*, *Yā Sīn*), sometimes three (*Alif Lām Mīm*), four (*Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād*, *Alif Lām Rā*), and five, as here and at the head of Sūrah 42 (*Ḥā Mīm. ʿAyn Sīn Qāf*). But of all twenty-nine, this particular combination appears nowhere else in the Book. Sūrah Maryam is the only chapter that opens with *Kāf-Hā-Yā-ʿAyn-Ṣād*, and it is the longest single set of such letters found in a single verse. That uniqueness is itself a statement. When a passage of scripture opens with something that occurs exactly once in the entire corpus, the reader is being told to attend. In the ordinary run of revelation, God opens with a command, a narrative, an oath, or a declaration. Here He opens with the alphabet — with the raw material of language, before language has begun — and then, immediately, with the word *raḥmah*, mercy. Sound before sense; then, out of sound, the first meaning that emerges is mercy.

**What the Early Generations Said**

The Companion and great exegete ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās (d. 68/687–88), from whom the largest body of early Qur'anic interpretation comes, is reported to have said of this opening that these five letters, strung together but pronounced individually, constitute one of the Names of God — or, in another report attributed to him and to others, a name of the Qur'an itself (al-Ṭabarī, *Jāmiʿ al-Bayān*). A related report is transmitted from ʿIkrimah and al-Ḍaḥḥāk: that these letters, when read as a single word, are among the Beautiful Names by which God may be invoked. Other early commentators took the letters individually, each as an allusion to one of God's Names or Attributes: *al-Kāf* for *al-Kāfī*, "the Sufficient"; *al-Hāʾ* for *al-Hādī*, "the Guide"; *al-ʿAyn* for *al-ʿAlīm*, "the Knowing"; *al-Ṣād* for *al-Ṣādiq*, "the True." Honest readers will notice that this scheme leaves the *yāʾ* without a natural match, and the classical mufassirūn were careful to present such mappings as pious possibilities (*iḥtimālāt*) rather than as established meanings. Al-Qurṭubī, in his *Jāmiʿ li-Aḥkām al-Qurʾān*, records the various proposals and then states the methodological principle that governs all of them:

> *"The soundest position concerning these letters is that they are among the things whose knowledge God has kept to Himself; we believe in them as they have come, we recite them as they were revealed, and we do not invent a meaning for them beyond what the Arabic language allows."*

**Why a Mystery at the Doorway Is Not a Defect**

For readers formed by modern habits of mind, an unexplained opening can feel like an evasion. It is worth saying plainly why the tradition regarded it as the opposite. First, the letters perform a function before any meaning is assigned to them: they interrupt. A listener expecting a story or a law receives, instead, five syllables of the alphabet. The effect is a doorway — a moment of pause that separates the everyday mind from the act of listening to revelation. Every Muslim who prays knows this physically: the recitation of the Qur'an begins after a break in ordinary speech, and the first sound of this sūrah in *tarāwīḥ* or in *tahajjud* is not a sentence but a set of letters. The worshipper's mind, arriving at that point expecting meaning, is briefly given none — and in that brief vacancy, attention sharpens. A mystery preserved at a threshold is not a locked door; it is a hand placed on the shoulder before entering. Second, the letters restrain a specific and durable spiritual disease: the assumption that the servant stands above the text as its examiner. Al-Rāzī, in his *Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb*, devotes a long discussion to the letters in which he argues that the human relationship to God necessarily includes both the rational and the supra-rational. Some rulings we can explain — the harm of intoxicants, the wisdom of charity, the social logic of the laws of inheritance. Other matters are obeyed out of loving compliance rather than full comprehension: the number of circuits around the Kaʿbah, the timing of the rites, the station of the pilgrimage. The disconnected letters, al-Rāzī says, keep that second space open *inside the Book itself* — a standing reminder, at the head of chapter after chapter, that the reader is a servant and not an inspector. In an age whose reflex is to demand that everything submit to immediate explanation, this small discipline recalibrates the soul. Human reason is a lantern, and a lantern is a precious thing; but it is not the sun.

**The Challenge Reading**

The most widely held explanation among the classical commentators, however, is not mystical at all but rhetorical. Reported from Ibn ʿAbbās and developed by al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, and many after them, it holds that the letters proclaim the Qur'an's inimitability (*iʿjāz*) by the most economical means possible. The reasoning runs: these are not foreign characters or secret ciphers. They are the Arabs' own letters — the very signs out of which every poet composed his odes, every orator built his speech, every soothsayer uttered his rhymed prose. By opening sūrah after sūrah with the bare inventory of the language, the revelation says, in effect: here is everything you have to work with. Do with it what you will; you will not produce the like of what follows. The challenge is made explicit elsewhere: *"Say: If mankind and the jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur'an, they would not bring the like of it, though they were helpers to one another"* (17:88); and, more simply, *"Then bring a sūrah like it"* (2:23; 10:38). The letters are the gauntlet thrown down in the medium of the challenged. It is as though a master calligrapher laid his brushes on the table before his rivals and said: with these, and none other, this was made. Two observations support this reading. The first is that in the great majority of sūrahs opened by disconnected letters, mention of the Book or of revelation follows almost immediately — here, within a single verse, the sūrah moves from letters to *dhikru raḥmati rabbika*, "a reminder of the mercy of thy Lord," which is a description of revelation itself. The second is that the letters are recited but never explained, which is exactly what one would expect of a sign whose purpose is to be *heard* rather than decoded.

**The Sound of the Opening as It Is Actually Recited**

Those who have heard this sūrah recited in the night prayer will have noticed something the page does not show: the five letters are elongated. In the transmission of Ḥafṣ from ʿĀṣim, which is the recitation in general use across the Muslim world, the letters whose names are made of three letters (*kāf*, *ṣād*, and — with a special leniency — *ʿayn*) are prolonged, while those whose names are made of two (*hā*, *yā*) are kept short. The result, heard rather than read, is a line of extraordinary gravity: a long, low, swaying invocation, five syllables with no semantic content, before the first sentence of the sūrah arrives. Generations of reciters have observed that the effect on a congregation is immediate and physical — a stillness that falls before meaning begins. Whatever else the letters are, they are a piece of *sound* placed at the entrance of the chapter, and sound does its work on a listener whether or not the intellect can paraphrase it.

**What Follows the Letters, and Why It Matters**

Finally, the placement is not arbitrary. Of all the chapters of the Qur'an, this is the one that most insistently defends God against the claim that He has a child — and it does so by telling the story of a child born without a father. The same chapter that opens with the alphabet opens its argument with the declaration *"It is not for God to take a child"* (19:35). Between those two poles, letters and doctrine, the sūrah makes one continuous point: God is not bound by the means through which human beings do things. He is not bound to build a sentence out of letters anyone can predict; He is not bound to bring a child into being through a father. The grandest theological claim in the chapter and the humblest literary feature of its opening verse are, in the end, the same claim.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:2

> **A reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord unto His servant, Zachariah,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Alphabet Straight into Mercy**

The very first sentence built out of the alphabet with which the sūrah opened is about mercy. *Dhikru raḥmati rabbika ʿabdahu Zakariyyā* — "A reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord unto His servant, Zachariah." A reader who comes from the five unexplained letters into this sentence experiences the transition almost as a relief: the Book has moved from pure sound into meaning, and the first meaning is *raḥmah*. Two roots in that opening phrase carry the entire chapter. The first is *dh-k-r*, from which *dhikr* comes — a word meaning remembrance, mention, and reminder all at once. It is the same word the Qur'an uses for itself (*"We have sent down the Reminder, and surely We are its Guardians,"* 15:9) and for the act of worship by which the heart is kept alive (*"Verily, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest,"* 13:28). So the sūrah announces its own genre at the outset: what follows is not merely information about a long-dead priest; it is *dhikr* — a remembrance addressed to the living, meant to be carried and repeated, in which God's mercy to one old man becomes a mirror for every reader who has ever wanted something that human causation had closed off.

The second root is *r-ḥ-m*, mercy — the sūrah's signature. As noted in the introduction, it is the root of *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm* and also of *raḥim*, the womb. The chapter that will open a barren womb and a virgin womb announces itself, in its first verse, by naming the mercy that does it. The Arabic lexicographers were struck by the same convergence, and it is worth pausing on why: *raḥmah* and *raḥim* are not two similar-sounding words that happen to share three consonants, but two branches of a single meaning, since the womb is the organ whose defining action is to enclose, protect, and sustain what cannot sustain itself. Al-Rāzī built an argument upon the connection, observing that if the mercy of God toward His servants is named after the womb, then the womb is among the created things through which that mercy becomes most legibly displayed — and the ḥadīth reinforces the point by describing mercy as a created reality distributed through creation rather than a bare abstraction. It is fitting, then, that the chapter whose two great miracles are both miracles of the womb should open by naming the attribute that explains them, and should go on to repeat that Name sixteen times before it closes.

**Who Zachariah Was**

Zachariah (*Zakariyyā*) is named in the Qur'an in four places: here (3:37–41, 6:85, 19:2–11, 21:89–90). He was a prophet of the Children of Israel, a descendant of Aaron and therefore of the priestly line, and — according to the well-known report of the commentators — the guardian of Mary, the one who took charge of her in the sanctuary: *"...and Zachariah took charge of her"* (3:37). He is described in the Qur'an as among the righteous (*"and Zachariah and John and Jesus and Elias — each was of the righteous,"* 6:85). He was also, by all accounts, a working man. Muslim reports an authentic ḥadīth in which the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Zakariyyā was a carpenter."* (Muslim 2379, from Abū Hurayrah)

That detail, small as it is, does real theological work. The man God chose to open the story of the sūrah, the man whose prayer is answered with a prophet, is not a king or a chieftain but a tradesman with sawdust on his hands. It is worth saying to every believer who has suspected that their obscurity disqualifies them from being answered: the door to this sūrah is a carpenter. The classical sources give his age at the time of this prayer variously — between sixty and eighty-five (al-Zamakhsharī) — and describe his wife as barren (*ʿāqir*). In the chronology of the sūrah he is old, his wife has never borne a child, and the religious leadership he has exercised for decades is about to pass to relatives he does not trust. Everything human in his situation points to an ending rather than a beginning.

**A Grammatical Ambiguity with Theological Weight**

The verse as it is written allows two parsings, and the commentators preserved both. The first, which yields the translation above, reads *ʿabdahu* as the object of the mercy: "a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord *unto His servant Zachariah*." The second reads *ʿabdahu* in apposition to *raḥmah*: "a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord, *[namely] His servant Zachariah*" — meaning that Zachariah himself *was* the mercy God bestowed (al-Rāzī). Both are true, and the tension between them is instructive. On the first reading, mercy comes to Zachariah. On the second, Zachariah *is* mercy — sent to his people as their guide, their priest, their teacher, the man who stands between them and corruption. Theologically, these are not alternatives but two moments in one life: a servant receives mercy, and having received it, becomes mercy for others. That is the shape of every genuine religious life in miniature — grace received at the top of the pipeline, grace delivered at the bottom. The Prophet ﷺ gave the community the measure of it when he said:

> *"The best of people are those most beneficial to people."* (al-Ṭabarānī, *al-Muʿjam al-Awsaṭ*; graded ḥasan)

**"His Servant" — The Title the Qur'an Gives Its Greatest Figures**

The word used here, *ʿabd*, is the same word the Qur'an uses for the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ at the most exalted moments of his life: *"Glory be to Him Who took His servant by night"* (17:1); *"And that when the servant of God stood up calling upon Him, they nearly swarmed upon him"* (72:19). Servanthood is not a降级 in the Qur'an's vocabulary; it is the highest designation a human being can carry, because it names the true relationship between the creature and the Creator without either collapsing the distance (as deification does) or exaggerating it (as despair does). Zachariah is called *ʿabdahu* — "His servant," with the pronoun attached to God. The possessive matters. He does not belong to the Temple, to his tribe, to his relatives, or to his own reputation. He belongs to God, and it is that belonging that makes the rest of the story possible.

**Why This Story Comes First**

It is worth asking why a sūrah named for Mary and centered on Jesus opens with Zachariah. The commentators give several answers, and together they explain the architecture of the chapter. First, the story of John's birth to an aged, barren mother is the *easier* miracle, and the Qur'an puts it first so that the harder one can be believed by the same logic. If God can bring a child from two people who are biologically finished, then the virgin birth is not a new category of impossibility — it is the same Power operating one degree further. Al-Ṭabarī makes this point explicitly: God mentions the birth of John to parents whom nature had excluded, so that the mind, having accepted the first, will not balk at the second. The sequence is pedagogical, and it is exactly how a skilled teacher works: from the known to the unknown, one step at a time. Second, Zachariah's story establishes the sūrah's emotional key: an old man praying in the dark for something the world says is over. That is the posture the whole chapter honors. Third, John is the bridge. He is the prophet who confirms "a Word from God" (3:39) — and that Word is Jesus. The two births are not two separate stories; they are one announcement delivered twice, in two registers, to make the point that God's power is not constrained by the ordinary boundaries of human possibility.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:3

> **when he cried out to his Lord with a secret cry.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What "a Secret Cry" Means**

The Arabic is *nidāʾan khafiyyan*. *Nidāʾ* is a call — specifically, a call made to someone at a distance, from one person to another. *Khafiyy* means hidden, concealed, secret. Put together, the phrase describes a call that is addressed across a distance but uttered so that no one else can hear it: Zachariah called out to God, and no one in the Temple heard him do it. Zachariah was, on the accounts of the commentators, in the *miḥrāb* — the sanctuary or the private prayer-chamber (see 3:39). He was alone. And then he did something worth examining closely: he did not compose a petition in his head and wait for a favorable mood. He *called out*. The verb indicates a raising of the voice in address, and yet the modifier tells us it was quiet. The two together give us the exact posture of the verse: intensity without volume. A whisper can be a shout if the one who utters it means it entirely.

**Why the Qur'an Praises Hidden Supplication**

The Qur'an commands this mode elsewhere and explains why, and the fact that it legislates the manner of address tells the reader something important about how seriously the Book takes the interior life: the remedy prescribed for the whisper is not argument but turning, and turning is an act of the heart that the tongue is commanded to express. Note too that the instruction is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ himself, which establishes that this discipline is not a concession to weak believers but the practice of the most perfect of them, and therefore the standard for everyone below him. The governing verse is in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf:

> *"Call upon your Lord humbly and in secret. Verily He loves not the aggressors."* (Qur'an 7:55)

The logic of the command runs in two directions, and both are visible in Zachariah's situation. The first direction concerns sincerity. A prayer performed where others can hear it is exposed to a corruption that is nearly impossible to detect from the inside: the corruption of performing for an audience. The Prophet ﷺ named this danger in the starkest possible terms when he said:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk."* They asked, "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Riyāʾ (showing off). God will say on the Day of Resurrection, when people are recompensed for their deeds: 'Go to those for whom you used to show off in the world, and see if you find any reward with them.'"* (Aḥmad, *Musnad* 23680; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The second direction concerns intimacy. A supplication made in secret is the verbal equivalent of a hand placed on a shoulder, a word spoken close to the ear. Volume is what you use when the one you are addressing might be far away or hard of hearing; quietness is what you use when you are certain of nearness. The Qur'an states the nearness plainly:

> *"And when My servants ask thee concerning Me — indeed I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me."* (Qur'an 2:186)

The commentators also offered circumstantial reasons, and they are worth noting because they show how concretely the early Muslims read this verse. Al-Zamakhsharī mentions that Zachariah's cry may have been quiet because of his age and physical frailty — an old man does not have the breath for a shout — or because he feared his relatives (see v. 5), who would not have welcomed the news that he was praying for an heir. Al-Ṭabarī adds the consideration that hidden supplication is, quite simply, more beloved to God. These readings are not rivals; a single prayer can be quiet for all of these reasons at once.

**The Prophet's Own Teaching on This Point**

There is a celebrated ḥadīth, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, that captures the etiquette of the secret cry with unusual vividness, and the scene is worth setting carefully because the detail is what carries the lesson. The Companions were on a journey with the Prophet ﷺ, and as they climbed a slope the exertion drew from them a loud *takbīr* — an understandable lapse, since the body's strain tends to raise the voice along with it, and there was nothing irreverent in what they did. The correction that follows is therefore all the more instructive, because it addresses a habit rather than a fault. The Prophet ﷺ said to them:

> *"O people, be gentle with yourselves. You are not calling upon one who is deaf or absent. You are calling upon One Who is All-Hearing, Ever-Near — and He is with you."* (al-Bukhārī 2992, 6409; Muslim 2704)

The rebuke is instructive precisely because it was addressed to people doing something *good*. They were remembering God out loud, and he told them to lower their voices — not because loudness is forbidden, but because volume can be a symptom of a wrong belief about distance. If you shout toward heaven, you may unconsciously be imagining that heaven is far off. The Prophet ﷺ also described the value of what is done quietly. In the authentic ḥadīth of the seven whom God will shade on the Day when there is no shade but His, one of them is:

> *"a man who gave charity and concealed it, such that his left hand did not know what his right hand spent."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

And of supplication specifically — the practice toward which everything in this verse has been leading — the Prophet ﷺ said, in a ḥadīth that gathers the entire theology of the secret cry into a single sentence and which the scholars have cited for centuries as the proof that nearness to God is measured by the heart's attention rather than the throat's volume:

> *"Supplicate God while you are certain of being answered, and know that God does not answer a supplication from a heedless, distracted heart."* (al-Tirmidhī 3479; graded ḥasan)

**The Psychology of the Hidden Prayer**

There is a reason this verse lands with unusual force on anyone who has lived long enough to be disappointed. Public religion is easy to perform and easy to fake. It is entirely possible to be known in a community as a devout person while the interior life has gone thin — and because the reputation is real and the applause is real, the emptiness is very hard to notice. Hidden prayer has no such cushion. Nobody is watching, so there is no reward for performance, and therefore the only possible motive for continuing is that one actually wants what one is asking for. This is why the hidden prayer is the diagnostic instrument of the spiritual life. A person who prays only in public is like a tree watered only on its leaves. Zachariah's cry was hidden, and — as the sūrah will go on to show — it was heard. That is the promise embedded in the verse: what is said in secret to God is not lost in secret. The One who hears does not need volume, and the fact that no human being witnessed the cry is irrelevant to the fact that it was answered. In a world in which so much of human effort is spent seeking an audience, this is the most liberating sentence in the chapter.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:4

> **He said, "My Lord! Verily my bones have grown feeble, and my head glistens with white hair. And in calling upon Thee, my Lord, I have never been wretched.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Body as the First Argument**

Zachariah's prayer does not begin with a request. It begins with a diagnosis. *"My Lord! Verily my bones have grown feeble, and my head glistens with white hair."* Before he asks God for anything, he tells God what has become of him — and, more precisely, he tells God something God already knew perfectly well. This is worth pausing on, because it is one of the most misunderstood features of supplication. A newcomer to prayer often assumes that its purpose is to inform God of a need, and then wonders why an all-Knowing God needs to be told. But the informing is not for God's benefit. It is for the supplicant's. Articulating one's own helplessness to the One who can remedy it is the act by which a human being stops pretending. Zachariah is not giving God information; he is giving up a pose. Every honest *duʿāʾ* in the Qur'an has this shape: Jacob says *"I only complain of my grief and my sorrow unto God"* (12:86); Job says *"Truly distress has seized me, and Thou art the Most Merciful of the merciful"* (21:83); Jonah says in the dark *"There is no god but Thou; glory be to Thee; I have indeed been of the wrongdoers"* (21:87). The confession precedes the request, and the confession is the request's foundation.

**Two Arabic Phrases That Became Classic**

The verse contains two expressions that Arabic literature has never stopped quoting. The first is *wahana al-ʿaẓmu minnī* — "my bones have grown feeble." The verb *wahana* (root *w-h-n*) denotes a weakening that is not a break but a giving-way: the structural integrity is still there, but the load-bearing capacity is not what it was. Note what Zachariah chooses as the emblem of his decline. He does not say "my skin has wrinkled" or "my sight has dimmed." He says *the bones* — the frame, the innermost support, the part of the body that is last to be seen and first to bear weight. The imagery is precise: the hidden structure has gone soft. He is describing the experience of being, at the deepest level, less able to carry what he once carried. The second is *ishtaʿala al-raʾsu shaybā* — "my head glistens with white hair." The verb *ishtaʿala*, from the root *sh-ʿ-l*, means "it blazed" or "it flared up," as a fire catches. So the literal sense is: *my head has blazed with whiteness.* This is one of the most famous metaphors in the language. The grammarians cited it for centuries as the textbook example of a particular construction (the *tamyīz*, or specification, *shaybā* clarifying in what respect the head has "blazed"), but the poetic force is what matters: the whiteness did not creep in gradually like a stain; it *caught fire* and spread. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the elegance — an old man's head does not merely go grey, it ignites, and the fire is cold and white.

Anyone who has watched a parent age has seen it: the change that seemed to take years, looked at from the outside, appears to have happened in a season. Zachariah is describing precisely that experience, and the word he chooses is deliberately physical rather than abstract. He does not say that he has grown old, or that his strength has faded, or that his years have multiplied; he says *wahana al-ʿaẓmu minnī* — "the bone within me has weakened" — locating the decay in the hardest and most structural substance of the body, the one material a person thinks of as fixed. When even that gives way, there is nothing left to lean on. The commentators noted the progression with care: he mentions the bones first, then the head's flame, then — in the verse that follows — his wife's barrenness, so that the prayer mounts from the innermost evidence of decay outward toward the impossibility that stands beyond him.

**The Qur'an's Own Theology of Old Age**

The Qur'an treats old age as one of the primary signs (*āyāt*) of human dependence, and it is worth asking why this should be so, since the Qur'an uses the same word elsewhere for the heavens, the earth, the ships, and the alternation of night and day. The answer is that a sign is something undeniable, universally available, and resistant to reinterpretation — and old age is the one sign that every human being is guaranteed to experience from the inside. No one can argue himself out of his own frailty once it has arrived, which is precisely what makes it useful as evidence. It describes the life-course in a single compressed line:

> *"God is He Who created you in weakness, then appointed strength after weakness, then after strength appointed weakness and grey hair."* (Qur'an 30:54)

And the same vocabulary of feebleness recurs elsewhere in the Qur'an, in a verse that names the condition outright rather than describing it — and the context there is instructive, since the failing of powers is spoken of as the natural falling-away of what was never owned in the first place rather than as a punishment imposed from outside:

> *"...that he may know, after having had knowledge, nothing at all."* (Qur'an 22:5)

The point of these verses is not to depress anyone. It is to establish a *logical* case for humility. If a human being is strong in his twenties and dependent in his eighties without having changed in essence, then strength was never his own possession in the first place — it was on loan, and the loan is being called in. The Qur'an makes the same argument from wealth, from knowledge, and from health; but old age is the version of the argument that everyone will eventually experience personally, which is why the Qur'an returns to it so often. Zachariah uses this argument on himself. He is, in effect, presenting God with the evidence of God's own claim on him: I am Yours, and here is the proof — I am falling apart.

**"And in Calling Upon Thee, My Lord, I Have Never Been Wretched"**

The verse's closing clause is the hinge of the entire prayer, and it is the most remarkable thing Zachariah says. *Wa-lam akun bi-duʿāʾika rabbī shaqiyyā* — "And in calling upon Thee, my Lord, I have never been wretched." The word *shaqiyy* comes from the root *sh-q-w*, the same root as *shaqāwah*, which denotes misery, wretchedness, and perdition. Its opposite in the Qur'an's vocabulary is *saʿīd*, "felicitous, blessed" (see 11:105–108, where humanity is divided on the Last Day into *ashqiyāʾ* and *suʿadāʾ*). So the plain sense is: I have never been made miserable by calling on You. But the commentators, including al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī, note that this is a litotes — an understatement that means its opposite: *every time I called upon You, You answered me, and I was enriched by it.* He is not merely saying that prayer has not hurt him. He is saying that prayer has an unbroken record of success in his life. This is the most important structural element in the prayer, and the most imitable. Zachariah is not asking God for a favour from a stranger. He is coming back to a well he has drunk from for sixty years and saying: it has never run dry. Theologically, this is the concept of *istidlāl bi'l-ḥāl* — arguing from one's own history of grace to the likelihood of future grace. It is the same logic a believer uses when he says, "God has never abandoned me before; He will not begin now."

And it is supported by the Sunnah, which takes the bare fact of ageing and turns it into instruction about what a believer should ask for as the years accumulate — not length of days, and not the restoration of youth, but soundness of faith and soundness of deed at the point where both are hardest to maintain. The Prophet ﷺ taught:

> *"Be mindful of God in times of ease and He will be mindful of you in times of hardship. Know that what has passed you by was not going to befall you, and what has befallen you was not going to pass you by."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And the Prophet ﷺ completed the picture by explaining what becomes of a supplication that is not answered in the form in which it was asked — a question that matters enormously for a man praying in old age, since Zachariah's own prayer would in fact be answered in a way he could not have anticipated, and every believer who asks for something long-delayed needs to know what God does with the asking in the meantime:

> *"No Muslim supplicates with a supplication in which there is no sin and no severing of the ties of kinship but that God gives him one of three: either He hastens his answer, or He stores it for him in the Hereafter, or He averts from him an equivalent evil."* They said: "Then we shall ask much." He said: *"God is more [generous still]."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad* 11133; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Twofold "My Lord"**

Note that the word *Rabb* appears twice in this single short verse: at the opening (*rabbi*) and again in the closing clause (*rabbi*). *Rabb* is not simply "Lord" in the sense of master; its root *r-b-b* carries the senses of nurturing, rearing, sustaining, and bringing to maturity. It is the word for the One who takes a thing from smallness to fullness. Zachariah bookends his description of his own decay with this Name. That placement is not incidental. It is the theological claim the verse is built on: the One who brought him from nothingness to maturity — the very point God will make to him in v. 9 — has not lost the capacity to bring something new out of him now. The God Who raised him is the God he is asking.

**What This Verse Teaches About Growing Old**

There is a practical dimension here that modern readers feel sharply. The contemporary attitude toward ageing is, in the main, one of two failures: either a frantic attempt to deny it, or a quiet despair about it. The Qur'anic posture is neither. It permits the servant to *state* his weakness plainly — Zachariah does not pretend to be vigorous, does not minimize, does not perform stoicism before God. And in the same breath it forbids him to conclude from that weakness that his usefulness is over. The very verse in which he announces his decrepitude is the verse in which he asks for the future. The man who says "my bones have grown feeble" in the next clause asks for a son. That is the sūrah's answer to despair about ageing: the right response to diminished capacity is not reduced expectation of God but increased reliance on Him. Zachariah has less strength, so he prays harder — and gets, at the end of his life, what vigorous young men do not get.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:5

> **Truly I fear my relatives after me, and my wife is barren. So grant me from Thy Presence an heir**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Fear That Is Not About Himself**

Having described his body, Zachariah now describes his community, and the two are connected. *"Truly I fear my relatives after me, and my wife is barren."* The word translated "relatives" is *al-mawālī*, whose singular forms (*mawlā*, *waliyy*) come from the root *w-l-y* — the root of nearness, allegiance, protection, and authority. It is one of the richest roots in Arabic: from it come *walī* (guardian, ally, friend), *wilāyah* (authority or guardianship), *mawlā* (master, patron, client, kinsman), and — in the phrase *walī Allāh*, "friend of God" — one of the central terms of Islamic spirituality. In this context *mawālī* means his kinsmen and, more broadly, those who would take over the religious leadership of his community when he was gone. What did he fear? The commentators are precise on this point, and their answer is surprising to modern readers who might expect a private, domestic worry. Al-Bayḍāwī and others explain that, since Zachariah was of the priestly Jewish family, his fear was that his relatives would not uphold the religion properly after his death — that the teaching, the worship, and the moral standard of the community would be corrupted once he was no longer there to guard them. Some, such as al-Zamakhsharī, add that the *mawālī* may mean his agnatic relatives who would inherit his position by default, or else unrelated scholars who would assume leadership of the Jewish community. This is the first thing to notice about the verse: Zachariah's anxiety is institutional, not personal. He is not asking for a child because he wants company in his old age, or because he wants someone to inherit his house, or because he feels the sting of being childless in a society that measures a man by his sons. He is asking because a trust is about to be left unguarded. His body is failing (v. 4); the people who will replace him cannot be relied upon (v. 5); and the thing he has spent his life protecting is more important to him than either fact.

**A Second Obstacle: Barrenness**

The second half of the sentence removes the ordinary solution. *"And my wife is barren"* — *kānat imraʾatī ʿāqiran*. The word *ʿāqir* denotes a woman who cannot bear children. The commentators note that she had been barren her whole life, not merely through age — she had never conceived, even in her youth. So Zachariah's situation is a closed box with two locks. He is old and physically spent; she is barren. Neither of them is a temporary condition that time or medicine could address. The Qur'an is meticulous about this, and it is worth asking why. The answer, which becomes explicit in vv. 8–9, is that the miracle is meant to be undeniable. If the wife were merely elderly, a skeptic could argue that a late pregnancy is rare but natural. If Zachariah were vigorous, a skeptic could argue the same. With both conditions stated, every natural explanation is closed off in advance, and what remains is the direct act of God. The verse is constructed the way a mathematician constructs a proof: eliminate the alternatives, and only one conclusion is left standing.

**"From Thy Presence" — The Phrase That Carries the Prayer**

Now comes the request: *fa-hab lī min ladunka waliyyan* — "So grant me from Thy Presence an heir." The phrase *min ladunka* — "from Thy Presence," or "from Thyself" — is one of the most theologically loaded expressions in the language of Qur'anic supplication. The word *ladun* indicates a giving that comes directly from the giver, without intermediary and without the ordinary chain of secondary causes. When the Qur'an uses it, it is always at moments of high spiritual intensity. The People of the Cave pray: *"Our Lord, grant us mercy from Thy Presence, and prepare for us right guidance in our affair"* (18:10). The steadfast pray: *"Our Lord, cause not our hearts to deviate after Thou hast guided us, and grant us mercy from Thy Presence; verily Thou art the Bestower"* (3:8). Moses and his servant say: *"...we have indeed encountered fatigue in this journey of ours"* after forgetting the fish, and then find a servant *"whom We had given mercy from Our Presence and taught him knowledge from Our Presence"* (18:65). In each case the force of *min ladunka* is the same: bypassing the normal channels. Zachariah has exhausted the normal channels. He is old; she is barren; the means are gone. So he addresses himself to the Source and asks for a gift that does not travel through the ordinary machinery of the world. He is not asking God to bless the means — there are no means. He is asking for what only God can hand over directly.

The word *waliyyan* — translated here as "heir" — deserves a note, because it is the same root as *mawālī* two words earlier. The word-play in the Arabic is deliberate and almost certainly intentional: he has *mawālī* (relatives who will inherit his position and mishandle it), and he asks God for a *walī* (a true heir of his own). The contrast is between inheritance by kinship, which is accidental and can be corrupt, and inheritance by divine appointment, which is neither. He is, in effect, saying: give me an heir who is an heir by Your choosing, not by biology's.

**The Three-Part Structure of a Complete Duʿāʾ**

This verse and the one before it together exhibit the full anatomy of supplication as the Qur'an models it, and it is worth extracting as a practical rule. First, *acknowledgment of need and helplessness* (v. 4): "my bones have grown feeble." Second, *acknowledgment that the ordinary means are closed* (v. 5a–b): "I fear my relatives, and my wife is barren." Third, *the request, addressed to God as the direct Giver, with the good of the community in view* (v. 5c–6): "grant me from Thy Presence an heir who will inherit from me and inherit from the House of Jacob." Most people's prayers contain only the third element. The Qur'anic pattern insists on the first two, and their function is not to flatter God but to cure the supplicant of the illusion that he has resources. A prayer that skips straight to the request is a prayer that may not yet know it needs anything.

**The Social Logic: Why Institutions Need Guardians**

There is a sober institutional insight buried in this verse, and it is one that any community — a mosque, a school, a charity, a family business, a nation — will recognize. Zachariah's fear is that the thing he built will be taken over by people who do not share its purpose. This is one of the most common failure modes in human affairs: founders are animated by conviction, successors by procedure, and successors' successors by nothing at all. Institutions rarely collapse from external attack; they collapse because the people who inherit them lack the reason the institution existed. Zachariah's answer to that danger is instructive. He does not scheme, lobby, maneuver, or attempt to secure the succession by politics. He prays — and, crucially, he prays for a *person*, not a plan. The Qur'anic remedy for institutional decay is not a better org-chart but a better human being. That is why he asks for a son rather than for legislation: because a righteous heir carries the trust in his character, which is the only place a trust can actually be carried.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:6

> **who will inherit from me and inherit from the House of Jacob. And make him, my Lord, well-pleasing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Kind of Inheritance Is in View**

Zachariah's request now specifies what he wants the child to inherit: *yarithunī wa-yarithu min āli Yaʿqūb* — "who will inherit from me and inherit from the House of Jacob." The verb *waratha* (root *w-r-th*) means to inherit, to receive what another leaves behind, and — in its Qur'anic usage — it applies to both material and non-material legacies. The Qur'an uses it for property (*"for men is a share of what parents and relatives leave"*, 4:7), for the earth itself (*"My righteous servants shall inherit the earth"*, 21:105), for the Garden (*"This is the Garden We shall bequeath unto those among Our servants who were reverent"*, 19:63), for knowledge (*"Then We gave the Book as inheritance to those We chose from among Our servants"*, 35:32), and even for God's own relationship to creation (*"Surely We shall inherit the earth and whatsoever is upon it"*, 19:40 — a phrase that appears in this very sūrah, thirteen verses later). So when Zachariah says "who will inherit from me," the meaning must be determined by context — and the context is decisive. He is a prophet presiding over a religious community, and the fear he has just expressed is that the religion will be corrupted. The inheritance in view is therefore the inheritance of *knowledge, prophethood, and religious authority*, not of silver and land. The commentators state this plainly, and their unanimity on the point is itself worth registering, since the verse's wording could on a superficial reading be taken to mean ordinary financial inheritance. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī note that what is meant is inheritance of religious knowledge, prophethood, and the religious leadership of the community — and al-Bayḍāwī grounds the point in an authentic Prophetic principle, drawing attention to the fact that the Lawgiver explicitly removed wealth from the category of what a prophet leaves behind, which settles the interpretation:

> *"We, the company of prophets, do not leave inheritance [of wealth]; whatever we leave is charity."* (reported by al-Bukhārī and Muslim from Abū Bakr and ʿĀʾishah)

That ḥadīth, which is among the most consequential in Islamic law, means that the Prophets' estates were not distributable among heirs in the ordinary way; what they left behind belonged to the community. It follows that when a prophet prays for an heir, he cannot mean an heir to an estate he does not have. He must mean an heir to the only thing a prophet actually possesses: the message. This distinction resolves a problem that readers sometimes raise when they compare this verse with the general principle that the Prophets leave no material inheritance. There is no contradiction: Zachariah was asking for a successor in prophethood and guidance, and that is precisely what he received — John, who is described elsewhere as one who would *"confirm a Word from God, and a master, and chaste, and a prophet from among the righteous"* (3:39).

**"And Inherit from the House of Jacob"**

The second half of the inheritance is the family line. *Āl Yaʿqūb* — "the House of Jacob," or "the family of Jacob" — refers to the progeny of Jacob (Israel), the father of the twelve tribes, from whom Zachariah, Mary, Moses, and Aaron all descend. Why add this? Because a religious trust is carried by two things at once: a *message* and a *lineage of people who carry it*. Zachariah prays for both. He wants a son who will inherit his own personal mission (his teaching, his station, his struggle) *and* who will stand within the long chain of Israelite prophecy going back to Jacob. Note that a prophet's authority in the Qur'anic framework is never a purely private illumination; it is always continuous with what came before. Jesus will say of himself, a few verses later: *"...confirming that which was before me of the Torah"* (3:50, in a parallel passage). The truth is one, revealed over generations, and each messenger inherits it from the one before and hands it on to the one after. There is a profound principle here about tradition itself. Zachariah does not ask God to send something entirely new. He asks God to continue something old through a new person. That is how revelation actually works in the Qur'an's account: not as a series of disconnected interruptions, but as one continuous stream with a single Source. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ expressed it in a famous image:

> *"My likeness and the likeness of the prophets before me is that of a man who built a house, made it beautiful and complete, except for the place of one brick in a corner. The people began to go around it and marvel at it, saying, 'Why has this brick not been placed?' I am that brick, and I am the seal of the Prophets."* (al-Bukhārī 3535; Muslim 2286, from Abū Hurayrah and Jābir)

**"And Make Him, My Lord, Well-Pleasing"**

The verse closes with the heart of the prayer: *wa-jʿalhu rabbi raḍiyyā* — "And make him, my Lord, well-pleasing." The word *raḍiyy* comes from the root *r-ḍ-y*, the root of *riḍā* — contentment, acceptance, being well-pleased. Morphologically it is an intensive form (*faʿīl*), so the sense is not simply "one who is accepted" but "one who is thoroughly pleasing, one in whom there is much to be pleased with." Two directions of meaning are possible in principle, and the commentators recorded both. According to one reading, it means "pleasing to God" — that is, a child whose life earns God's good pleasure. According to another, it means "one who is pleasing" in the more general sense, whose character is such that he is accepted and beloved — by God first and, as a consequence, by the people. Al-Ṭabarī and others favor the primary reading: Zachariah asks that his son be one with whom God is well-pleased. And note what this tells us about the man's priorities. He has just asked for a child after a lifetime of childlessness, in a culture in which a son was a man's social standing, his economic future, and his security in old age. What does he pray for? Not that the son be handsome, wealthy, long-lived, eloquent, powerful, or famous among men. He prays for one thing: that God be pleased with him. That is a fearsome and beautiful distillation of what parenthood ought to aim at. Every parent has ambitions for a child — success, health, status, comfort — and none of those is forbidden. But the prayer of a prophet cuts through the list to the single item that makes the rest either a blessing or a disaster: that God be pleased with the child. The Prophet ﷺ taught a related and much-loved supplication for children and for others:

> *"O God, make him a guide, guided [himself], one who guides others and is guided; and make him a means of guidance, and make him acceptable to You."* (al-Ṭabarānī, al-Bazzār; reported by Ibn ʿAbbās)

**Why the Order of the Prayer Matters**

Finally, notice the sequence. Zachariah asks: first, that the child *inherit* (v. 6a); second, that he be *well-pleasing* (v. 6b). The ability to carry the trust comes first in the asking; the character that makes the carrying acceptable comes second, as the seal on the whole request. The logic is worth stating because it is counterintuitive. Most people would pray for a good child and hope that good works follow. Zachariah prays for an effective child *and* a pleasing one, because he knows something that founders and teachers learn the hard way: competence without character corrupts everything it touches. A person who inherits authority without being *raḍiyy* becomes exactly the thing Zachariah feared in v. 5 — one of the *mawālī* who takes over the trust and mishandles it. So the prayer protects the gift: give me an heir, and make him an heir worth having.

**The Answer, Held Until the Next Verse**

The sūrah does not record the answer immediately in narrative terms; the response comes in v. 7, *"O Zachariah! Verily We give thee glad tidings of a boy whose name is John..."* The gap between the request and the response is filled only by the word *fa* in that verse — "and then," "so." The speed of the answer is itself part of the teaching: a lifetime of barrenness ended by a single sentence from God. For the believer reading this, the practical conclusion is direct. Zachariah asked for three things and got all three, and he asked with a body that was failing and means that were closed. The sūrah places his prayer at its very beginning, before Mary, before Jesus, before the great arguments of its closing movement, as if to establish the terms on which everything else in the chapter will happen: God is asked, and God gives — not according to the arithmetic of means, but according to the mercy whose Name this sūrah carries from its opening verse to its closing one.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:7

> **"O Zachariah! Verily We give thee glad tidings of a boy whose name is John. We have assigned no namesake to him heretofore."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Arrives Before the Prayer Ends**

The narrative moves with astonishing speed. Zachariah's request occupies three verses (vv. 4–6); the response begins in the seventh with *yā Zakariyyā innā nubashshiruka bi-ghulāmin ismuhu Yaḥyā* — "O Zachariah! Verily We give thee glad tidings of a boy whose name is John." There is no interval, no waiting period, no testing. The sūrah does not even say "so We answered him." It simply reports the answer. The Arabic construction used here — *innā nubashshiruka*, with the emphatic particle *inna* and the present-tense verb — is the language of an announcement already in progress: We *are* giving you glad tidings. The verb *bashshara* (root *b-sh-r*) means to bring good news, and it is the word the Qur'an consistently uses for the moment when God opens a future that human beings had closed. The angels bring *bushrā* to Abraham (11:71; 15:53; 51:28), to Mary (3:45), and here to Zachariah. The word is connected to *bashar*, "human being," and to *basharah*, "skin" — because news of this kind visibly changes a person's face. Theologically, the Qur'an treats glad tidings as a divine attribute of address: God does not merely decree good for His servants; He *tells* them, so that they may live the interval between promise and fulfilment in hope rather than despair. That interval is precisely what Zachariah is about to enter: he is told now, and the conception has not yet occurred. The whole point of the announcement is to carry him through the waiting.

Note also who speaks, because in a narrative this compressed every detail of the address is doing work. The call is direct — *yā Zakariyyā*, "O Zachariah" — and it comes, as 3:39 clarifies in the parallel account, from the angels as he stood praying in the sanctuary. The use of the vocative particle *yā* followed by the personal name is the ordinary grammar of being singled out, and the commentators read it as an honour: God did not send a message by implication or by circumstance but named him. That the call reaches him in the sanctuary, at the very place where the secret cry of the preceding verse was uttered, closes the circle — he prayed where he prayed, and he was answered there, in the same spot, by the God who had heard the prayer that no one else heard:

> *"And the angels called out to him as he stood praying in the sanctuary: 'Verily, God gives thee glad tidings of John, confirming a Word from God, and a master, and chaste, and a prophet from among the righteous.'"* (Qur'an 3:39)

**The Name, and What It Means That It Was Given by God**

The boy is named in the announcement itself. God does not say "a son" and leave the naming to the parents; He names the child: *ismuhu Yaḥyā*, "his name is John." Naming by God is a mark of election throughout scripture. Adam is taught the names (2:31); God announces the name of the Prophet's successor in the Qur'an's own way; and Jesus' name is likewise given by God — *"His name will be the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary"* (3:45). When God names a child, the name is not a label chosen by preference; it is a statement of what that life is for. What does *Yaḥyā* mean? The commentators split into two camps, and both are worth recording. The first holds that it derives from the Arabic root *ḥ-y-y*, the root of *ḥayāh* (life) and *ḥayy* (living) — so *Yaḥyā* means "he lives," a verb in the imperfect tense. On this reading, the name is a prophecy about the child's destiny: he lives, and through him the religious life of his community lives on after his father. It is also, more subtly, a statement about the God who gave him: the One who gives life to the barren womb is the *Muḥyī*, the Giver of Life, and the child's very name is a walking reminder of that act. The second camp, including several philologists, notes that *Yaḥyā* corresponds to the Hebrew *Yoḥanan* ("God has been gracious"), and treats it as a non-Arabic proper name, like *Isrāʾīl* or *Yaʿqūb*. Both readings are defensible, and al-Ṭabarī records them without forcing a choice. Muslim readers have always held both at once: the name is at once the child's own heritage and, in Arabic ears, a perpetual announcement of the miracle that produced him.

**"We Have Assigned No Namesake to Him Heretofore"**

The verse closes with a striking claim: *lam najʿal lahu min qablu samiyyā* — "We have assigned no namesake to him heretofore." The word *samiyy* comes from the same root as *ism* (name) and means a namesake, one who bears the same name, or one who is a peer or equal. Two interpretations were recorded, and they are not mutually exclusive. The first is literal: no one before this child had ever been called *Yaḥyā*. The name is unprecedented. That in itself is meaningful, because it matches the event — a child who comes into the world by an unprecedented route carries an unprecedented name. This is the reading al-Ṭabarī favours, and it harmonizes with the emphasis elsewhere in the story on the impossibility of what has happened: old bones, a barren wife, and now a name no one had borne. The second reading, transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, understands *samiyy* in its other sense — a peer, a match, an equal. On this reading the verse means: We have made no one like him before. This reading finds support in the descriptions that follow in this very sūrah: John is given "judgment as a child" (v. 12), tenderness, purity, and reverence (v. 13), filial duty without domination (v. 14), and peace on the three decisive days of his existence (v. 15). A human being described in those terms has indeed no *samiyy*.

The two readings converge on the same theological point, and it is a point worth stating as precisely as possible because the whole sūrah rests upon it: God does not repeat Himself. Every person He sends into the world is a fresh composition, and the fact that a thing has never happened before is not an argument against its happening. Zachariah's situation had no precedent either — which is precisely why God chose it. The underlying claim is about the nature of divine action rather than about biology: God creates by command rather than by process, and a Creator who brings a thing into being by saying "Be" is not constrained by what has or has not occurred previously in the world He made. This is why the Qur'an's answers to scepticism about the Resurrection return again and again to the *first* creation rather than to the second — the argument is never "resurrection is easy" but "the One who did it once is the One who will do it again." Zachariah, standing in the sanctuary with a body he has just described as failing, is being invited to draw the inference from a general truth to his own particular case:

**Why This Glad Tidings Is a Commentary on the Whole Sūrah**

Placed where it is, this verse performs a function that extends well beyond Zachariah's biography. The sūrah is about to narrate a second, harder birth — the birth of Jesus to a virgin, with no father at all (vv. 16–22). By putting John's birth first, with its milder but still impossible premise, the Qur'an is training the reader's mind. The logic is cumulative: if God can give a child to a man whose bones have failed and a woman who was barren, then the additional step of a child without a father introduces no new difficulty. Both are cases of God creating outside the ordinary chain of causes, and the chain of causes is His, not ours. That is why verse 9 will answer Zachariah's astonishment with the argument from his own creation — *"I had created thee before, when thou wast nothing"* — and why the same argument will be repeated, almost word for word, to Mary in verse 21. The sūrah is building a single case out of two instances, and the case is this: *the One who made the means does not need them.*

**A Practical Word on Waiting**

There is a human detail here that deserves to be drawn out. Between the announcement of this verse and the conception that follows, Zachariah must live. He has been told, and nothing visible has changed. He is still old; his wife is still barren; the neighbours still see an old couple with no children. This is the ordinary structure of divine promise in human experience, and it is why the announcement exists at all: not to remove the waiting but to make the waiting bearable. A person walking through a dark passage with a lamp does not find the passage shorter, but he does not stumble. The scholars of the inward life made much of this distinction. Knowledge that God has decreed something does not remove the trial; it changes the *quality* of the trial from despair to expectation. Zachariah's glad tidings were given before the fact precisely so that he would know, when the sign of silence came upon him (v. 10), that the silence was a confirmation and not a withdrawal.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:8

> **He said, "My Lord! How shall I have a boy, when my wife is barren, and I have grown decrepit with old age?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question, Not a Doubt**

Zachariah's response to the announcement is a question: *annā yakūnu lī ghulāmun wa-kānat imraʾatī ʿāqiran wa-qad balaghtu min al-kibari ʿitiyyan* — "My Lord! How shall I have a boy, when my wife is barren, and I have grown decrepit with old age?" The immediate question for any reader of this sūrah is whether Zachariah doubted. The answer of the commentators is very nearly unanimous, and it is a subtle and important one: he did not doubt God's power; he asked about the *mode*. Al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, and Ibn Kathīr all state that his question was of the form *"how?"* (*kayfiyyah*) rather than *"whether?"* — a request for information about the means, not an expression of scepticism about the outcome. Several supporting arguments are offered. First, Zachariah was a prophet, and prophets are protected from doubting a plain divine announcement. Second, he had just said, four verses earlier, that he had never been wretched in calling upon God (v. 4) — a man with that testimony does not immediately disbelieve when the answer arrives. Third, the response he receives in v. 9 does not rebuke him; it explains. Had the question been a denial, the answer would have been a correction. Instead God simply says, in effect: here is how.

Fourth, and most tellingly, there is a grammatical detail the commentators noted: the angels' announcement in v. 7 had used the word *ghulām*, "boy," rather than *walad*, "son/offspring." A *ghulām* is a male child, but the word does not specify how he comes to exist. Zachariah therefore asks, entirely reasonably: by what route is a *boy* to appear — through my wife's womb in the normal way, or otherwise (al-Rāzī)? The question is the question of a man trying to understand the shape of the miracle, not to escape it. This distinction matters enormously in practical religion, and it is worth making explicit because it is so often confused. There is a difference between *asking God how* and *asking God whether*, and the first is a form of faith, not a failure of it. Abraham asked: *"My Lord, show me how Thou givest life to the dead"* (2:260) — and God did not rebuke him; He performed the demonstration. Mary will ask in v. 20, *"How shall I have a boy when no man has touched me?"* — and the answer is the same as the one given here. Questions that seek understanding are honored throughout the Qur'an; it is questions that seek escape from obligation that the Qur'an condemns.

**The Language of Decrepitude: ʿItiyyan**

The word translated "decrepit with old age" is *ʿitiyyan*, from the root *ʿ-t-w*. It is an unusual and forceful choice, and its resonance is worth unpacking. The root *ʿ-t-w* carries two related senses in Arabic. The primary sense is *to become old, dried out, withered, hardened* — used of bones, of a plant past its season, of a body that has lost its sap. But the same root also means *to be insolent, arrogant, to exceed bounds*: it is the root used of Pharaoh when the Qur'an says *"Go unto Pharaoh; verily he has transgressed"* (*innahu ṭaghā*, 79:17) and of those who *"behaved arrogantly in the land"* (*ʿataw ʿutuwwan kabīran*, 25:21). The two senses are not as distant as they look. Both describe a thing that has gone beyond a limit and become unyielding: the old body has become rigid and unresponsive, just as the tyrant has become rigid and unresponsive. A commentator with an ear for the language would hear in *ʿitiyyan* an image of a man whose physical pliability is entirely gone — the opposite of the soft, growing, responsive thing a child is. It is a masterly word choice: the man describing himself as *ʿitiyy* is describing, in one word, the exact distance between himself and the baby he has just been promised. Note also the progression across the sūrah's opening verses. In v. 4, Zachariah said *"my bones have grown feeble"* — a statement about the internal frame. Here, four verses later, he says he has *reached, from old age, ʿitiyyan* — *qad balaghtu min al-kibari ʿitiyyan*. The verb *balaghtu* means "I have reached, I have attained," and it has a grim ring: old age is described as a destination he has arrived at, the far end of a road, and the thing he has arrived at is dryness. He is not approaching decrepitude; he has attained it.

**The Honesty of Stating the Impossible**
