What makes this verse morally instructive is that Zachariah states the obstacle plainly, in full, to the One who has just promised to remove it. He does not perform pious vagueness — "Lord, if it be easy for You" — or pretend that the biology is not what it is. He says: my wife is barren, and I have reached the extreme of age. Both facts are true, both are total, and he lays them both before God. This is the opposite of the posture that the Qur'an criticizes elsewhere. The Qur'an's condemnations of faithlessness are never directed at people who stated their difficulties honestly; they are directed at people who used difficulty as a pretext for refusal. The Children of Israel said to Moses, *"We shall never enter it, ever, as long as they remain therein; so go, thou and thy Lord, and fight; we shall be sitting right here"* (5:24) — that is refusal dressed as realism. Zachariah's statement is realism in the service of faith: here is the obstacle; it is absolute; and I am asking You anyway. The psychological wisdom here is considerable, and modern readers will recognize it. The person who pretends that his situation is not as bad as it is has not dealt with it; he has only hidden from it, and the hidden fear will resurface the moment the promise is delayed. The person who states the worst case out loud — *to God* — has already made the obstacle an object of thought rather than a source of dread. This is, in essence, what any honest person does in the two or three in the morning: names the thing exactly, and then says "and yet."

**Why God Sometimes Announces the Impossible in Advance**

There is a divine pedagogy visible in this exchange, and the sūrah repeats it in the case of Mary (vv. 19–21) so that no one can mistake it for a one-off. God announces the miracle *before* performing it, and He announces it to the very person whose condition makes it impossible. Why not simply perform it and let the result speak? Because the announcement is what makes the result readable. If a child had simply been born to Zachariah's wife without warning, onlookers could have constructed any number of natural explanations. The prior announcement, and the recorded impossibility, close off every explanation but one. The Qur'an consistently establishes the impossibility *first* so that the event, when it comes, is unmistakably a sign (*āyah*). The sūrah's whole architecture depends on this: John is meant to be a proof, and a proof requires a known starting-point. The same principle applies at the level of the individual life. Most believers, looking back, can identify moments when a door opened that had been structurally closed — and the recognition that it was God depends entirely on having known how closed it was. If you never register the impossibility, you will credit luck, or your own cleverness, or the kindness of a person who happened to be nearby. Zachariah's question, far from being a lapse, is part of the evidence. It puts the impossibility on the record.

**The Answer That Follows**

Verse 9 will respond not with a mechanism but with a precedent: *"I had created thee before, when thou wast nothing."* The move is worth anticipating here, because it reveals the logic of the entire exchange. God does not say "your wife's barrenness will be cured." He says, in effect: the problem is not the size of the obstacle; the problem is that you are measuring Me against the obstacle. The only relevant datum is not how impossible the thing is, but Who is doing it. The Prophet ﷺ taught his community this same reorientation in a compact and frequently cited supplication:

> *"O God, there is no ease except that which You make easy, and You make the difficult easy if You will."* (Ibn Ḥibbān 974; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:9

> **He said, "Thus shall it be. Thy Lord says, 'It is easy for Me! I had created thee before, when thou wast nothing!'"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer That Changes the Category of the Question**

God's reply to Zachariah is one of the most concentrated theological statements in the Qur'an, and it occupies a single verse. It has three movements, and each does a distinct job. The first movement is *kadhālika qāla rabbuka huwa ʿalayya hayyin* — "Thus shall it be. Thy Lord says, 'It is easy for Me.'" The word *kadhālika* ("thus," "like that") declares the matter settled: the thing will happen in the way that has been announced, without further negotiation. And the reason given is not a mechanism but a statement about the Agent: *huwa ʿalayya hayyin*, "it is easy *for Me*." The prepositional phrase is doing all the work. The difficulty was never in the object; it was always relative to the subject. For an aged, barren couple the conception of a child is impossible; for the One who makes wombs and makes sperm and makes time, it is not difficult at all. The second movement is the precedent: *wa-qad khalaqtuka min qablu wa-lam taku shayʾan* — "I had created thee before, when thou wast nothing." This is the argument from the first creation, and it is the Qur'an's single most repeated response to those who deny the Resurrection. It appears in the very structure of this sūrah: v. 67 will say to the denier of resurrection, *"Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was naught?"* — using the identical phrase, *lam taku shayʾan*. The same argument is used at 36:78–79, 17:49–51, 22:5, and 75:36–40. The logic is airtight and worth stating carefully:

The argument runs in three steps, and it is worth laying them out plainly because the logic is airtight and the Qur'an repeats it whenever the Resurrection or any other impossibility is denied. First, to bring a thing into existence from nothing is, by any human reckoning, harder than to reconstitute or to alter something that already exists. Second, God has already done the harder thing — to Zachariah himself, and to every human being who reads the verse. Third, therefore God's doing the easier thing requires no new power and raises no new difficulty; the only remaining question is whether He wills it, and He has just said that He does. That is why the Qur'an uses this argument specifically against the denial of resurrection, and why it uses it here to answer a question about a barren womb. Both cases are instances of the same underlying error: measuring God by the limitations of the means through which He normally operates. The third movement, implicit but powerful, is the personal address: *I created **thee**.* God does not cite a general principle; He cites Zachariah's own biography. The proof offered to this man is not an argument from cosmology but an argument from his own body. He is living evidence of the very power he is querying. There is a rhetorical tenderness in that: God answers him out of his own life, not out of a textbook.

**"When Thou Wast Nothing" — The Phrase the Qur'an Uses Twice in This Sūrah**

*Wa-lam taku shayʾan* — "when thou wast nothing." The phrase appears here and again in v. 67, spoken this time to the mankind who doubts his own resurrection. It is one of the Qur'an's most disarming assertions about human origins. The Qur'an states the same truth in a variety of registers. In Sūrah al-Insān it is framed as a question about time itself:

> *"Has there not come upon man a span of time when he was not a thing remembered?"* (Qur'an 76:1)

And in Sūrah al-Ḥajj, the argument from gestation is made in explicit stages, and the stages matter because they force the reader to acknowledge a process he did not supervise and could not interrupt. That is the whole point of the enumeration: the One conducting the development is not the mother, and not the father, and not the child, and every stage named is a stage that someone else is managing:

> *"O mankind! If you are in doubt concerning the Resurrection, then [consider that] We created you from dust, then from a drop, then from a clinging clot, then from a lump of flesh, formed and unformed, that We may make clear to you..."* (Qur'an 22:5)

The theological point is not incidental to the sūrah's argument. If a human being was "nothing" before God made him something, then human existence is not a possession but a gift held at every instant. Nothing in a person's condition is *his own* in the sense of being independent of the Giver: not his strength, not his fertility, not the span of his years. That is precisely the conclusion Zachariah is being led to. He has described himself, in the previous two verses, as feeble, grey, and decrepit; and God's answer is, in effect: *You were not even a thing. Who do you think brought you from that to this?*

**"Be!" and It Is — The Creative Fiat**

The underlying mechanism is named elsewhere in the Qur'an with a formula so famous that it has entered the devotional vocabulary of Muslims in every language: *kun fa-yakūn* — "Be! and it is." The formula is doing more than describing speed. It is denying that there are gradations of difficulty for God at all. With a human agent, creating and repairing differ enormously, and the difference is measured in effort, time, cost, and risk. With a Creator whose act is a command, there is no such scale: the word that produced a universe from nothing is the same word that opens a womb, and neither costs Him more than the other. This is why the Qur'an can put the creation of the heavens and the earth and the resurrection of a single dead man into the same sentence without any sense of disproportion:

> *"The Originator of the heavens and the earth! When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, 'Be!' and it is."* (Qur'an 2:117)

The same formula recurs at 3:47 and 3:59 (in the story of Jesus), 6:73, 16:40, 19:35 (in this very sūrah, applied to the refutation of divine sonship), 36:82, and 40:68. Its significance for this passage is that it removes the category of *difficulty* from the act of creation altogether. Creation is not, for God, a process of overcoming resistance — as though there were some material that had to be persuaded. It is a decree. The Qur'an's point, made repeatedly, is that God's act of bringing things into existence is not an exertion, and so the number of obstacles in the way is irrelevant to it. A man with no son and a woman who cannot conceive are, in this respect, in exactly the same position as a man and woman in their twenties: both need a decree, and a decree costs the One who makes it nothing. The scholars were careful, however, about what this does and does not mean. *Kun fa-yakūn* does not mean that God literally utters a sound with letters; the "saying" is a metaphor for the immediacy and unresisted efficacy of His Will. Nor does it mean that God bypasses means out of indifference to them — He ordinarily creates *through* means, and He taught human beings to use means. It means that the means are not the source. Zachariah will, in fact, conceive a child in the ordinary way through his wife; the miracle is not that the means were bypassed but that dead means were made to live.

**Theological Boundaries: What This Verse Does Not Say**

Because this verse speaks of God's power in absolute terms, it is worth stating what classical theology drew from it and what it refused to draw. What it yields: God's power is unlimited, and nothing that is *possible in itself* is difficult for Him. What it does not yield: that God does what is self-contradictory or what His own Wisdom precludes. The mutakallimūn were careful on this point. God does not "create a second god," not because His power is limited but because the object is incoherent; nor does He wrong anyone, not because He cannot, but because His Perfection and His Justice preclude it and He has forbidden it to Himself. Ibn Taymiyyah made exactly this distinction in his discussions of the divine attributes, insisting that omnipotence is not the power to do nonsense, and that affirming God's absolute ability is not the same as denying His Wisdom, His Justice, or His Truthfulness. This matters for the sūrah's closing argument. When the Qur'an says, in v. 35, *"It is not for God to take a child,"* it is not saying that God lacks the power to do so in the way a weak man lacks power. It is saying that such a thing is *incompatible with what God is* — with His self-sufficiency, His eternity, His having no peer and therefore no like from whom a child could be generated. Omnipotence here is expressed as the impossibility of a defect, not as a limitation of ability.

**The Ease of God in the Life of the Believer**

For readers who are not prophets and will not be given miraculous children, the verse has a plain practical corollary, and it is the one that makes this passage devotional rather than merely historical. The God who says *"it is easy for Me"* is the God to whom the believer brings the impossible — and the Qur'an's insistence that He be asked is not rhetorical. Asking is itself the worship being requested; the one who stops asking because the thing looks impossible has already decided the question on evidence he was never meant to weigh. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Your Lord is Generous and Shy; when His servant raises his hands to Him, He is too shy to return them empty."* (Abū Dāwūd 1488; al-Tirmidhī 3556; graded ḥasan)

And he said, in words that echo this verse directly, and which the scholars have paired with it because they translate the divine attribute of ease into the etiquette of the servant's asking — the point being that if ease belongs to God, then the appropriate response is confident asking rather than resigned silence:

> *"Ask God for His bounty, for God loves to be asked."* (al-Tirmidhī 3571; graded ḥasan)

The sūrah will return to this theme at its very end, in v. 97, where the Qur'an itself is described as having been *made easy* — *"We have only made this easy upon thy tongue."* Ease belongs to the Giver; difficulty belongs to the creature. That is the axis on which the whole chapter turns, and it is worth fixing in mind now, at the ninth verse, because it will recur at every crisis of the narrative: whenever a human being in this sūrah faces something that cannot be done, the sūrah's answer is not an explanation but a reminder of Who is doing it. Zachariah's bones have weakened, his wife is barren, Mary has no husband, and the grave will have to give up its dead — and to each impossibility the chapter returns the same reply, that the One who made the first thing from nothing is not inconvenienced by the second.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:10

> **He said, "My Lord! Appoint for me a sign." He said, "Thy sign shall be that thou shalt not speak with men for three nights, [while thou art] sound."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Asking for a Sign Is Not a Deficit of Faith**

Zachariah, having received the announcement and the explanation, now asks for something more: *"My Lord! Appoint for me a sign."* The word *āyah* — sign — is the same word used throughout the Qur'an both for the verses of revelation and for the signs of God in creation, in history, and in the self (*"And of His signs is that He created for you spouses from among yourselves,"* 30:21; *"And on the earth are signs for those possessed of certainty, and in yourselves; do you not see?"* 51:20–21). The commentators make the same point here that they made at v. 8: the request for a sign is not a symptom of doubt. Zachariah had already been told that it would happen; his question now is *when* and *by what marker shall I know*. He wants, in the idiom of the tradition, to know the time of the pregnancy's onset, so that he may prepare himself in thanksgiving, and so that he may recognize the moment rather than be overtaken by it. This is important, because there is a widespread modern confusion on the subject. Asking God for clarity is not the same as testing God for proof. The Qur'an condemns the latter — those who say *"We will not believe until we see God plainly"* (2:55), or who demand a sign as a condition for believing something they have already decided to reject. Zachariah has already believed; the sign is requested as a *confirmation for a servant who wants to be sure he is not misunderstanding his Lord*. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the request here is for a mark by which he would know that the promise had begun to be fulfilled, and that this is a legitimate and praiseworthy desire in a servant who wishes to meet grace with gratitude.

There is a useful analogy. A man told by a doctor that he is recovering does not insult the doctor by asking how he will be able to tell. He is asking so that he may know what to watch for. Zachariah's request has the same texture: he wants to be awake when it happens. The classical commentators were careful here, because a surface reading could suggest that Zachariah doubted the promise — and doubt of that kind is, for a prophet, theologically impossible. Al-Rāzī's resolution, followed by most later exegetes, is that a request for a sign may seek an increase of certainty and tranquillity (*ṭumaʾnīnah*) rather than the removal of doubt, and the difference is real and experientially recognisable. A believer who has been told that God will provide does not disbelieve when he asks how the provision will arrive; he wants to meet it rather than be surprised by it. The Qur'an itself uses the language of increased certainty in just this sense when Abraham asks to be shown how the dead are given life — *"but that my heart may be at rest"* (2:260) — and Abraham's question, like Zachariah's, is answered with a demonstration rather than a rebuke. Zachariah wants to be awake when it happens:

**The Sign: Silence, Not Speech**

The sign God gives is extraordinary and, at first reading, strange: *"Thy sign shall be that thou shalt not speak with men for three nights, [while thou art] sound."* Why silence? Several layers suggest themselves, and the commentators drew out most of them. The first is *correspondence*. Zachariah had asked in secret (v. 3), and now the answer is sealed in silence. His entire relationship with this promise has been interior — a hidden cry, a private sanctuary, an unheard petition — and it is fitting that the confirmation should arrive in the same register. The sign is not written in the sky where anyone could read it; it is written in his own body, in the loss of a faculty, and it is legible to him alone. The second is *the pedagogy of interruption*. Zachariah was a priest, a teacher, a public speaker, a man whose whole vocation was words — words of prayer, words of instruction, words of Scripture. To remove that faculty for three nights is to place him in a silence he cannot fill with his customary competence. He cannot teach, argue, recite to others, or explain. He can only *be*, and remember. The commentators note that in this state he was still able to remember God and to pray (al-Ṭabrisī); what was removed was specifically *speech with people*. That distinction carries the whole meaning: the tongue is not struck dumb toward God; it is struck dumb toward creation. God took away the horizontal use of the faculty and left the vertical use intact.

The third is that the silence *is itself a sign to others*. Al-Ṭabrisī records that his sudden inability to speak was a sign for his people, who thereby knew his request had been granted. In 3:41 the same event is described as *"thou shalt not speak to men for three days save by signs"* — and v. 11 of this sūrah shows him coming out to his people and signalling to them. So the silence that sealed the private confirmation also served as the public announcement. His people saw a healthy man unable to speak and understood that something had happened.

**"Three Nights" and "Three Days" — Reconciling the Two Accounts**

The parallel account in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān says *"three days"* (3:41), while this verse says *"three nights."* The commentators reconciled the two in a way that is typical of their precision. The primary reconciliation is linguistic: in Arabic idiom, a period expressed by nights necessarily includes the days between them, just as a period expressed by days includes the nights. *Thalātha layālin* and *thalāthat ayyām* both denote a three-day period. The difference is one of emphasis, not of duration. A second, complementary reading is that the fast and the silence were continuous by day and by night, so that the same three-day period is described once by its days and once by its nights. The number three itself recurs in the Qur'an in contexts of waiting and purification: three days of expiation for a broken oath (5:89), three days and three nights in the belly of the fish for Jonah (37:142, in some readings), three days of respite in the story of Thamūd (11:65). In each case, three marks a bounded interval — long enough to be real, short enough to be bearable. Zachariah's silence was not permanent and not punitive; it was a corridor.

**"While Thou Art Sound" — The Detail That Proves the Miracle**

The final word of the verse, *sawiyyan*, is the hinge on which the whole sign turns. It means sound, healthy, whole, unimpaired. The commentators are emphatic about its function: Zachariah will be unable to speak *while being perfectly well*. Without that word, the sign would be ambiguous. A man who has lost his voice might simply have fallen ill; his family would send for a physician, not for God. With it, the event is unmistakable: here is a man in full health, whose tongue works and whose mind is clear, who cannot form words to address his fellows. The silence is therefore not a symptom of anything. It is a divine restraint laid on a functioning faculty — a private, temporary suspension of the normal order, observable in the body of one man. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī both stress this: the point of *sawiyyan* is to exclude the interpretation that he was ill. The miracle is precisely that the healthy faculty is held.

**The Spirituality of Silence**

The Islamic tradition — and especially its Sufi strand — drew a great deal from this verse, and with reason. Silence in this passage is not emptiness; it is the condition under which something else can be said. Zachariah's silence and Mary's silence (v. 26) frame the two birth-narratives of this sūrah, and in both cases the silence precedes a miracle of speech: Zachariah is silenced so that John may be born, and Mary is silenced so that Jesus may speak (vv. 30–33). The sūrah is constructed around this exchange: the adult loses his tongue, and the infant is given one. The underlying principle is stated by the Prophet ﷺ in a ḥadīth every Muslim knows:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47, from Abū Hurayrah)

The commentators' spiritual reading goes further. Many of them held that silence is the only adequate response to a direct encounter with the Divine, since every word one might say is smaller than the thing one has met. Outward silence, they said, can be the visible form of an inward emptying — a self that has stopped asserting itself long enough to receive. Al-Qushayrī, in his commentary, treats this verse as a type of the spiritual state in which the servant's own agency is withdrawn so that only God's action remains visible. There is also a sheerly practical dimension that any person can test. A human being who cannot speak for three days cannot argue, cannot defend himself, cannot flatter, cannot lie, cannot interrupt, and cannot perform. All of those are things the tongue does constantly and largely without supervision. Three days of enforced silence reveals how much of ordinary speech is noise. The experiment is available to anyone who wants to try it, and the tradition of *iʿtikāf* (seclusion in the mosque) has always recommended a version of it.

**The Sign as a Foreshadowing**

One further connection deserves to be drawn, because the sūrah makes it. Zachariah's silence, imposed at the moment of John's conception, is the sūrah's first statement of a theme it will develop at length: that God's greatest works are often announced by a *withholding* before they are announced by a *giving*. Zachariah loses his speech; Mary withdraws to the east (v. 16); Abraham withdraws from his people (v. 48); Moses is called away to a mountain (v. 52). In each case, the subtraction comes first, and it is not a punishment. It is preparation.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:11

> **So he came forth from the sanctuary unto his people, and signaled to them that they should glorify morning and evening.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sanctuary: Miḥrāb and Its Surprising Root**

Zachariah emerges from a place the Qur'an calls *al-miḥrāb*. The word is one of the most interesting in the sūrah, because its meaning is architectural, its root is military, and its later history is devotional. In the Qur'an, *miḥrāb* denotes a chamber or elevated place of private worship. It is used of Mary's place of seclusion — *"Whenever Zachariah entered the sanctuary (*al-miḥrāb*) unto her, he found provision with her"* (3:37); of Zachariah's own prayer-place, as here and at 3:39; and of David's private quarters, in the striking opening of Sūrah Ṣād: *"Has the story of the litigants come to thee, when they scaled the wall of the sanctuary (*al-miḥrāb*)?"* (38:21). In every Qur'anic instance the word denotes a space set apart, elevated, and private — not, in itself, the niche of a mosque. The root of the word is *ḥ-r-b*, whose primary meaning in Arabic is *war* and *fighting* — the same root as *ḥarb* (war) and *muḥārabah* (combat). Al-Ṭabrisī drew the devotional consequence that generations of Muslims have repeated: the prayer-niche (*miḥrāb*) of the mosque is called by a word that means battlefield, because when a person stands in it and turns toward the *qibla*, he is waging war against Satan. Al-Ṭabrisī's point is not poetic decoration. It is a statement about what worship is: a conflict, conducted in a posture of surrender, against an enemy that cannot be fought with anything else.

That etymology is singularly apt for the story unfolding here, and the sūrah's choice of the word is unlikely to be accidental. Zachariah has just been struck silent by God; he emerges from the place where he was alone with his Lord and walks out to a community that does not yet know what has happened. He is, in the most literal sense, coming out of the battle-line into the camp — and what he carries out with him is not a message he can speak but a sign he must enact. The commentators noted the fittingness of the detail: the *miḥrāb* is the place where a man fights his greatest battle and the place where he prays, and Zachariah has done both in the same spot. He now returns to his people unable to explain himself, which means that the first announcement of John's birth is delivered not in words but in gesture — a silence that is itself the message, and one his people will have to interpret:

**The Verb: "He Signaled" and the Qur'an's Use of Waḥy**

The Arabic says *fa-awḥā ilayhim* — "and he signaled (literally, 'revealed' or 'inspired') to them." The verb *awḥā* is the standard Qur'anic verb for revelation: *"Verily We have revealed (*awḥaynā*) unto thee as We revealed unto Noah and the prophets after him"* (4:163). Its use for a dumb show is therefore deliberate and carries meaning. The Qur'an uses *waḥy* for several kinds of non-verbal, non-prophetic communication. Of the bee:

> *"And thy Lord inspired (*awḥā*) the bee: 'Take unto thyself houses among the mountains and among the trees and in that which they build.'"* (Qur'an 16:68)

And the same verb is used of the earth on the Last Day, in verses that describe the Resurrection as an emergence — as though the dead were coming out to their people from a place of seclusion exactly as Zachariah did, and with the same suddenness:

> *"On that Day she will recount her tidings, for thy Lord has inspired (*awḥā*) her."* (Qur'an 99:4–5)

And of Zachariah's gesture here. The common thread is that *waḥy*, at its root, means a swift, hidden indication — a communication that goes directly from one to another without the medium of public speech. It is exactly the right word for what Zachariah is doing: he cannot speak, so he indicates by gesture, and the Qur'an dignifies that gesture with the verb of its own descent. The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabrisī, mention two forms the signalling took: gestures with the hands and head, and writing. Either way, the content is the same, and it is worth dwelling on, because it is not what anyone would have expected.

**What He Signaled: Not "I Am Mute" But "Glorify God"**

A man who has just lost his speech and been promised a son might reasonably be expected to communicate either of those two facts. He communicates neither. What Zachariah signals is: *"that they should glorify (*sabbiḥū*) morning and evening."* The verb is *sabbihū*, from the root *s-b-ḥ*, the root of *tasbīḥ* — the declaration *subḥān Allāh*, "Glory be to God." The word's root sense is *to swim* or *to move swiftly and freely through something*; in its theological usage it means to declare God free from every imperfection, every defect, every limitation, every association with the created. *Tasbīḥ* is thus the verbal form of *tanzīh* — the doctrine that God is not like anything. It is, appropriately, the sūrah's central confession, for this is the chapter that will say in v. 35, *"Glory be to Him (*subḥānahu*)!"* in response to the claim that God has a child. So the first words Zachariah communicates after the promise are not about himself at all. They are a command to worship. The man who has just received the greatest private blessing of his life comes out of his prayer-chamber and tells his people to glorify God. That is the sūrah's model of how to receive a gift: not by announcing the gift, but by turning immediately to the Giver. The Prophet ﷺ taught the same reflex in a ḥadīth:

> *"Whoever is pleased to have his provision expanded and his term of life prolonged, let him maintain the ties of kinship."* (al-Bukhārī 5986; Muslim 2557)

—and, more pointedly, the Qur'an itself records the pattern of every prophet upon receiving a blessing, which is to say that the sūrah is showing Zachariah behaving exactly as the righteous whom it praises elsewhere behave. The thanksgiving is not a formality appended to the miracle; it is the miracle's completion, since a gift received without gratitude is a gift that has not yet reached the heart: *"Praise be to God, Who has taken away from me sorrow"* (35:34); *"My Lord, enable me to be grateful for Thy blessing"* (27:19).

**"Morning and Evening" — The Rhythm of Remembrance**

The instruction specifies two times: *bukratan wa-ʿashiyyan* — morning (*bukrah*, the early part of the day) and evening (*ʿashiyy*, the late part, from late afternoon into night). The Qur'an pairs these two times repeatedly in connection with remembrance, and the recurrence is meaningful. *"And mention thy Lord within thyself, humbly and with awe, without loudness of speech, morning and evening"* (7:205). *"And glorify Him morning and evening"* (33:42). *"So be patient over what they say, and glorify the praise of thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting; and in the watches of the night glorify Him, and at the ends of the day"* (20:130). *"And remember the Name of thy Lord morning and evening"* (76:25). And, in the parallel account of this very event, *"and remember thy Lord much, and glorify in the evening and the morning"* (3:41). Two readings of this pairing were offered by the commentators, and both are operative. The first is literal: these are the two appointed times of the day at which remembrance is especially emphasised — corresponding in the Islamic dispensation to the *fajr*, *ʿaṣr*, and *maghrib* prayers, at which the believer's tongue and heart are turned deliberately to God. The second, favoured by al-Ṭabrisī and others, is that "morning and evening" is an idiom for *continuity*: the two ends of the day stand for the whole of it, so that the instruction means "glorify God always, at the opening and the closing and everything between." The two readings are complementary, and al-Ṭabarī records them side by side.

The symbolism is also worth noting. Morning and evening are the two moments of transition — the boundaries at which light enters and leaves the world. To place remembrance at the thresholds is to acknowledge that the alternation of day and night is not a mechanism running on its own but a repeated act of the One who *"causes the dawn to break, and has made the night for rest and the sun and the moon for reckoning"* (6:96). The Qur'an explicitly connects the cycle of day and night with the cycle of death and resurrection: *"It is He Who gives life and causes death, and to Him belongs the alternation of night and day. Do you not understand?"* (23:80). A servant who remembers God at the two transitions is rehearsing, twice daily, the transition that awaits him.

**The Scene as a Whole**

The verse is one small sentence, but it contains a complete picture of what a religious community is for. Zachariah comes out of private worship (the *miḥrāb*), goes to his people (the community), and delivers to them not a personal announcement but a liturgy. He has been silenced toward them in speech and yet continues to teach them. His disability has not removed his function as their guide; it has simply changed the medium through which he fulfills it. There is a lesson here for anyone who has ever felt that a limitation — illness, age, poverty, loss of position, loss of a platform — has ended their usefulness. Zachariah, at the moment of his greatest impairment, is still doing the thing he was made to do. The tongue that could not say a word to a human being was still, by gesture and by writing, turning a whole community toward God. And behind that, the sūrah is quietly making its larger point: the miracle is not the silence. The miracle is what the silence was about. Zachariah's three nights of muteness were the sealed envelope around the announcement of John — and had his people been attentive, the first thing they would have learned from his closed mouth was not that a child was coming, but that God is great.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:12

> **"O John! Take the Book with strength!" And We gave him judgment as a child,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Child Is Addressed Before He Can Answer**

The narrative leaps. In v. 10 Zachariah was struck silent; in v. 11 he was signalling to his people. Now, without transition, the sūrah addresses the child directly: *yā Yaḥyā khudh al-kitāba bi-quwwatin* — "O John! Take the Book with strength!" The commentators note that there are two possible moments for this address. It may have been spoken to John immediately after his birth, as a divine command issued to an infant whom God had enabled to comprehend it (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī); or it may be a summary, compressed by the Qur'an's narrative style, of the command that came to John when he reached maturity and prophethood. The majority inclined to the first reading, on the grounds that the verse goes on to say *"And We gave him judgment as a child"* — the address and the gift belong together in childhood. Either way, the theological point is the same and it is one this sūrah cares about deeply: John's prophethood and his grasp of scripture were not the product of age, study, or experience. They were given. The chapter that opened with an old man's failing body now presents a child's perfected understanding, and the two belong to one argument — that God distributes capacity as He wills, and that neither youth nor age is a barrier or a qualification. The sūrah is consistent about this throughout: Zachariah is old and gets a son (vv. 4–9); John is young and gets wisdom (v. 12); Jesus is a newborn and speaks (vv. 30–33); Mary is a woman and is chosen above the women of the worlds (v. 16 ff.). Age, gender, and social weight are systematically neutralised in this chapter, because the only relevant variable is God's choice.

**"Take the Book with Strength"**

The command has two parts, and both are dense. The first is *khudh al-kitāb* — "take the Book." The definite article matters: *the Book* is the scripture of his people, the Torah. The verb *akhudh* means to take, to seize, to take hold of. It is not the vocabulary of passive reception. John is not told to *listen* to the Book, or to *receive* it, or to *study* it; he is told to *take* it — to reach out and grasp it, as one grasps something handed over that must then be carried. The second part is *bi-quwwatin* — "with strength." The word *quwwah* means power, force, resolve, firmness. The commentators gloss it as earnestness, diligence, and firm determination in following the Book's commands and avoiding its prohibitions (al-Ṭabarī). The phrase *take... with strength* is not unique to John; it belongs to a distinct Qur'anic idiom applied to those who receive revelation corporately. God says to the Children of Israel at Sinai:

> *"And [remember] when We took your covenant and raised the Mount above you: 'Hold fast to what We have given you with strength (*bi-quwwatin*), and remember what is therein, that you may become reverent.'"* (Qur'an 2:63, also 2:93)

And the same word is used of Moses' tablets, where the sense of a scripture that inscribes rather than merely informs becomes explicit — the recipient is not simply told what to do but is marked by what he has been given, which is the difference between hearing an instruction and being formed by it:

> *"And We wrote for him upon the tablets an admonition concerning all things, and an exposition of all things. 'So take them with strength (*fa-khudhhā bi-quwwatin*), and bid thy people to take the fairest of them.'"* (Qur'an 7:145)

The recurrence establishes the meaning precisely. Revelation is not delicate. It is a weight to be carried, and carrying it requires a spine. The phrase implies that a scripture received without resolve will be dropped — not rejected, necessarily, but simply set down, slowly, under the accumulating pressure of convenience. That is why the Qur'an pairs it so consistently with the warning that follows in the Israelite narrative: *"Then you turned away thereafter"* (2:64). Taking with strength is what prevents the turning away. This is a genuinely practical insight and worth applying to contemporary religious life. Most people who abandon religious practice do not do so through a dramatic intellectual repudiation. They do it gradually, by holding the commitments loosely: the prayer that slips when work is busy, the obligation that is deferred when it is inconvenient, the standard that is adjusted when nobody is looking. The Qur'an's remedy is prescribed at the moment of reception: take it *with strength*. Grip it. The metaphor is of a hand closed firmly rather than fingers barely touching.

**The Question of Effort After Gift**

There is a theological subtlety here that the verse forces into the open. If God gave John judgment as a child (v. 12b), and if God gave him the Book, what is left for John to do? Why command him to take something he has already been handed? The sūrah's answer, and the answer of the tradition generally, is that grace and effort are not competing explanations. The gift of the Book is God's; the *taking* of it with strength is John's. This is the same structure the Qur'an sets out for every believer: *"God tasks no soul beyond its capacity"* (2:286), and *"man shall have only what he strives for"* (53:39), and yet *"had it not been for the bounty of God upon you and His Mercy, not one of you would ever have been purified"* (24:21). The tradition's resolution, articulated most carefully by the Ashʿarī and Māturīdī theologians, is that human beings possess a *kasb* — an acquisition — by which the acts God creates are attributed to them as their own. Whatever the precise metaphysics, the practical upshot is identical to the one this verse enacts: receive everything as gift, and then handle it as though everything depended on your grip.

**"And We Gave Him Judgment as a Child"**

The second clause of the verse is the one that has most exercised the commentators: *wa-ātaynāhu al-ḥukma ṣabiyyan* — "And We gave him judgment as a child." The word *ṣabiyy* means a young boy, a child. What is *al-ḥukm*? The root *ḥ-k-m* yields *ḥukm* (judgment, ruling, wisdom), *ḥikmah* (wisdom), *ḥākim* (judge), and *al-Ḥakīm* (one of the Beautiful Names of God). The commentators offered several senses, and they accumulate rather than compete. According to al-Rāzī, it means the understanding of the Book that God gave him in youth. According to al-Ṭabarī, it means prophethood itself. Others gloss it as the capacity to judge between people correctly — that is, the judicial and moral discernment that qualifies a person to arbitrate. Still others add that it includes the *comprehension* of the law in all its detail, which is why John is said to have been given the Torah's knowledge while still young. The sources also record the social consequence of this gift. Al-Ṭabarī transmits reports that John's spiritual seriousness made him uninterested in the play of other children — that when the children of his town called him to play, he would reply, in words attributed to him in the tradition, "Were we created for play?" The reports are not authenticated to the level of Prophetic ḥadīth, and should be read as edifying tradition rather than as law; but they capture something the Qur'anic text itself supports, namely that John's interiority was unusually developed, unusually early.

The Qur'an describes the same phenomenon in the case of Jesus, who speaks from the cradle (vv. 30–33) and whose first words are a defence of his mother's honour rather than a child's babble, and in the case of Solomon, of whom it says — in a passage that is the closest parallel to John's, since it too concerns judgment granted before the age at which judgment is ordinarily acquired:

> *"And We made Solomon understand it [the judgment], and to each We gave judgment and knowledge."* (Qur'an 21:79)

The point being made across all three cases is that *ḥukm* is not a function of years lived. It is a light God places in a heart, and He places it where He wills. That is at once a comfort to the young and a warning to the old: neither the absence of grey hair nor its presence is evidence of anything.

**What the Verse Teaches About Childhood**

For anyone who works with children — as a parent, a teacher, or an imam — this verse is quietly revolutionary. It asserts that a child is capable of carrying revelation, of receiving judgment, and of being addressed directly by God. The sūrah does not treat John as a vessel to be filled slowly over decades; it treats him as a person whom God addresses with a command in the present tense. That has a direct practical implication, and it is one that Islamic educational practice has historically honored: children are not to be postponed. The tradition taught children the Qur'an, the *sīrah*, the creed, and the rudiments of law from the earliest age — not on the theory that they would fully understand it at the time, but on the theory that what is deposited early is what the adult will find already inside him when he needs it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Command your children to pray when they are seven years old, and discipline them for it when they are ten."* (Abū Dāwūd 495; al-Tirmidhī 407; graded ḥasan)

And Ibn ʿAbbās, who was a child when the Prophet ﷺ died and who became the greatest exegete of the community, is himself the proof of the principle in Islamic history: he was thirteen when the Prophet died, and by his twenties the senior Companions were coming to him for judgment. The Prophet ﷺ had prayed for him in exactly these terms — *"O God, give him understanding of the religion and teach him the interpretation"* — and the community's scholars have always read the fulfilment of that prayer as evidence that the gift described in this verse did not close with John. What is being established, then, is a standing divine practice rather than a single anomaly: God gives the understanding, and He gives it to whom He wills, at whatever age He wills.

**A Closing Note on Firmness as Mercy**

It is worth noticing, finally, that the command *take the Book with strength* is given to John in the same sūrah in which God is called *al-Raḥmān* sixteen times. Firmness and mercy are not opposites here; they belong to one another. God's mercy to John is not that the demand is lowered to suit him. It is that he is *given the strength* with which to meet the demand. That is the pattern throughout the Qur'an: God does not reduce the road because the traveller is weak; He strengthens the traveller for the road. And the evidence of it is the next verse, where the gifts multiply — tenderness, purity, and reverence, poured out on the one who was told to hold on tightly.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:13

> **and a tenderness from Our Presence, and purity; and he was reverent,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Gifts in Three Words**

Having commanded John to take the Book with strength (v. 12), the sūrah now enumerates what God placed inside him to make that command possible. The verse is a list of three items, and each is a study in itself: *wa-ḥanānan min ladunnā wa-zakātan wa-kāna taqiyyan* — "and a tenderness from Our Presence, and purity; and he was reverent." The sequence is deliberate. Strength without tenderness produces a tyrant; strength with tenderness produces a shepherd. The sūrah has just told John to grip the Book firmly, and the immediate next breath supplies the qualities that keep firmness from becoming harshness. That is the Qur'an's consistent moral psychology: it never issues a command without also supplying, or demanding, the interior character that alone makes the command safe to carry out.

**Ḥanān — A Word With No Single English Equivalent**

The first gift is *ḥanānan*, from the root *ḥ-n-n*. This is one of those Arabic roots whose range is best understood by walking around it. From *ḥ-n-n* come: *ḥanān*, tender affection and yearning mercy; *ḥanīn*, the longing that makes a thing yearn — famously the *ḥanīn* of the palm-trunk that longed for the Prophet ﷺ when he moved away from it in the mosque, a miracle transmitted in the authentic collections; *ḥannān*, one of the Beautiful Names of God in the tradition, the Tenderly Compassionate; and *ḥanīnah*, a woman whose heart is moved with pity for her child. The common thread is a mercy that *bends toward* its object — not a distant goodwill but an affection that leans down. The commentators, following al-Ṭabarī, note the double direction of this quality in John's case. On one reading, *a tenderness from Our Presence* means the tenderness God had *for* John — the divine pity and affection that enveloped him. On another, it means the tenderness God placed *in* John — so that he was himself a tender, compassionate person toward others. A third reading, also recorded, takes it to mean that John was a "tender mercy" to his aged father, the consolation of Zachariah's old age. All three are true at once, and the verse's phrasing is deliberately built to allow it: the mercy comes *from God*, is placed *in the servant*, and thereby becomes a mercy *to everyone near him*. That is the standard pipeline of grace in the Qur'anic account.

Note the phrase *min ladunnā* — "from Our Presence" — the same phrase Zachariah used in his prayer at v. 5 (*min ladunka*). The echo is intentional and rather beautiful: the servant asked for an heir "from Thy Presence," and God now describes the gifts given to that heir as coming "from Our Presence." The request has been answered in the same register in which it was made. What comes directly from God cannot be obtained by any other route, and tenderness of heart is precisely such a thing — it cannot be manufactured by effort, though it can be cultivated by those God has given a seed of it.

**Zakāh — Purity That Grows**

The second gift is *zakātan*, from the root *z-k-w*. This is the same word used throughout the Qur'an for the obligatory almsgiving, and that is not a coincidence of vocabulary: the root's core meaning is *to grow, to increase, to become pure, to flourish*. Almsgiving is called *zakāh* both because it purifies the remaining wealth and because it is the mechanism of its growth. The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī, take *zakāh* here in its primary sense: John was given purity from sins, and righteousness in deed. The two belong together. Purity in the Qur'anic vocabulary is never merely a negative state — the absence of stain. It is always also a positive one: the presence of sound growth. Consider the Qur'an's usage elsewhere: *"He has certainly succeeded who purifies it [the soul], and he has certainly failed who corrupts it"* (91:9–10); and the prayer attributed to Abraham, *"Our Lord, send among them a messenger from among themselves who will recite Thy signs to them and teach them the Book and wisdom and purify them (*wa-yuzakkīhim*)"* (2:129). The verb *zakkā* means both "he purified them" and "he caused them to grow." That double sense is exactly what the sūrah wants for John. He was not merely unstained; he was *growing*, from childhood on, in the direction of goodness. And this is why the commentators were able to read the word *zakī* as it is applied to Jesus in v. 19 in the same double sense: pure of sin, and growing in goodness from infancy to manhood (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī). The sūrah presents both John and Jesus as planted rather than merely preserved.

**Taqwā — The Reverence That Guards**

The third quality is stated in a full clause rather than a single noun, which is itself a signal of its importance: *wa-kāna taqiyyan* — "and he was reverent." The shift in construction is meaningful. The first two gifts are things *given*; the third is a state of *being*, expressed with the verb *kāna* (he was, he used to be). Reverence is described as the settled condition of the man, not a single endowment. *Taqwā* derives from the root *w-q-y*, which means to protect, to shield, to guard against. The noun's form implies "the state of one who has taken protection." A definition attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib captures the composite meaning:

> *Taqwā is fear of the Majestic, acting according to what has been revealed, contentment with little, and preparing for the Day of Departure.*

And Abū al-Dardāʾ, one of the sage-Companions and among those most given to reflection on the inward life, defined it in an image that has been repeated ever since because it captures what a bare definition cannot — the sense that this is a carried condition rather than a momentary act of resistance:

> *The perfection of taqwā is that the servant should fear God concerning even the weight of a mustard seed, until he abandons some of what he sees as lawful, fearing that it may be unlawful — that is a veil between him and the Fire.*

The classical image, which the tradition repeats and which this sūrah's material supports, is that of a traveller crossing a thorny path. A person walking barefoot through a field of thorns does not move slowly because the path is interesting; he moves carefully because each step carries a risk. He gathers his garment, watches where he places his foot, and avoids what he could have blundered through. *Taqwā* is exactly that: not anxiety, but *attentiveness born of the recognition that some things injure*. The careful driver on an icy road is not a frightened driver; he is a competent one who has registered the actual conditions. In the case of John, the sūrah has placed *taqwā* as the capstone of the list — the quality that keeps the tenderness from becoming softness and the purity from becoming naivety. A person may be warm and clean and still be ruined; the thing that preserves him is the constant awareness that he stands before Someone.

**The Three Together: The Portrait of a Servant**

Put the three items side by side and a complete human being appears. *Ḥanān* is the heart's orientation toward others — mercy that bends down. *Zakāh* is the self's condition — cleansed and growing. *Taqwā* is the soul's orientation toward God — guarded, awake, reverent. The three correspond to the three relationships a human life is made of: with creation, with oneself, and with the Creator. The Qur'an's moral system insists that all three be sound simultaneously, because a failure in any one of them destroys the others in time. Mercy without purity becomes indulgence — the person who forgives everything, including what God has forbidden, and calls it kindness. Purity without mercy becomes harshness — the person who is clean and cruel. Reverence without either becomes abstraction — a piety of rules with no love in it and no growth. John, the sūrah is saying, had all three, and he had them as a child. The point is not biographical curiosity; it is the establishment of a type. The one who is to carry scripture "with strength" must be tender, clean, and God-aware, and none of those three can be faked for very long.

**Why God Gives What He Commands**

There remains the implicit theology of the verse, and it is worth stating because it is the quiet engine of this whole passage. God commanded John to take the Book with strength, and then supplied the tenderness, the purity, and the reverence without which the command could not be carried. He did not say "take it firmly, and by the way, develop the character to manage firmness on your own." He gave the character. That is the Qur'anic pattern everywhere, and it is the source of the believer's confidence. The Prophet ﷺ expressed it in a ḥadīth that Muslims have repeated for fourteen centuries in every crisis:

> *"O Turner of the hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140; graded ḥasan)

And the same body of Prophetic teaching settles the question that this verse raises implicitly — whether a human heart can be relied upon to remain as it is. The command to hold fast presupposes that holding fast is possible, but the Sunnah is emphatic that it is not possible by the heart's own resources, and the Prophet ﷺ was the most insistent of people on the point. He described the heart in terms that leave no room for complacency:

> *"The heart of the son of Adam is between two of the Fingers of the Most Merciful; if He wills He makes it steadfast, and if He wills He turns it away."* (Muslim 2654)

The practical conclusion is a straightforward one, and it is the reason this verse sits where it does: the believer asks for the qualities needed to obey, rather than assuming that obedience will produce them. John's gifts were not rewards for his taking of the Book. They were the equipment for it. The order is important and easy to reverse: God commands, and the command is accompanied by the means of fulfilling it, but the means are requested rather than presumed. This is why the sūrah places John's endowment before any account of his deeds — the gifts are described in vv. 12–14, and the obedience that follows is their fruit rather than their cause. A believer who reverses the order, treating his obedience as the thing that earns him the capacity to obey, has inverted the relationship and will find the whole arrangement exhausting.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:14

> **and dutiful toward his parents. He was not domineering, rebellious.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Birr — The Word for Righteousness as a Whole, Applied to Parents**

The sūrah's portrait of John continues with his conduct at home: *wa-barran bi-wālidayhi* — "and dutiful toward his parents." The word is *barr*, from the root *b-r-r*. This root deserves attention, because its range in the Qur'an is exceptionally wide. From it come *birr* — righteousness in the most comprehensive sense; *al-Barr* — one of the Beautiful Names of God, the Beneficent; *al-barr* meaning the open land or the mainland; and *bararah*, the word used for the angel-scribes in 80:16 ("noble and virtuous"). The root's core image is *expansiveness* — a goodness that is broad, open, ungrudging. That breadth is the point. When the Qur'an wants a single word for the whole of practical righteousness, it uses *birr*:

> *"Righteousness (*al-birr*) is not that you turn your faces toward the east and the west; but righteous is the one who believes in God and the Last Day and the angels and the Book and the prophets, and gives wealth, for love of Him, to kinsfolk and orphans and the needy and the traveller and those who ask, and to free slaves; and who performs the prayer and gives the alms; and those who fulfil their covenant when they have covenanted; and the patient in hardship and adversity and in time of conflict. Those are they who are true, and those are the reverent."* (Qur'an 2:177)

So when this verse calls John *barran bi-wālidayhi*, it is not describing mere obedience. It is describing expansiveness of goodness directed specifically at the two people to whom he owes his existence. The commentators accordingly glossed it as obedient, loving, and constantly gentle (al-Ṭabarī). The philologists took the point further: the root *b-r-r* carries in it the sense of width and open space — *al-barr* is the land as opposed to the sea, and *birr* is goodness so called because it is expansive. What is being described, then, is not the bare discharge of an obligation but a spaciousness of character that happens to be directed first at the two people to whom he owes his existence. It is worth noting too that John's parents were, by the standard measures of the world, unimpressive: an old man past the age of strength and a woman who had been barren all her life. Filial goodness is easy to feel toward parents who are impressive, and the Qur'an's praise of John is praise for goodness shown to parents who needed it.

**The Qur'an's Insistence on This Duty**

The importance the Qur'an attaches to filial piety is difficult to overstate, and it is best shown by the fact that God joins it to His own rights in the same breath, over and over — a pairing so consistent across the sūrahs that the scholars treated it as a principle of the religion rather than the emphasis of a particular passage. The consistency is itself the argument, since a single verse could be explained away as context while a repeated structural pairing cannot:

> *"Worship God, and ascribe no partner unto Him, and be good to parents..."* (Qur'an 4:36)

> *"...and [We enjoined upon man] goodness to parents..."* (Qur'an 29:8; 46:15)

> *"And thy Lord has decreed that you worship none save Him, and that you be good to parents. If one of them or both of them reach old age with thee, say not 'uff' to them, nor repulse them, but speak to them a noble word. And lower to them the wing of humility out of mercy, and say: 'My Lord, have mercy upon them, as they raised me when I was small.'"* (Qur'an 17:23–24)

That last passage is worth reading slowly, because the sūrah we are in is saturated with its concerns. The command is not merely "obey them." It is: do not express even the smallest irritation — *uff*, the Arabic equivalent of a tut or a sigh; do not speak sharply; *lower the wing of humility*, the image being of a bird folding its wing down over its young; and then *pray for them in the very terms in which they cared for you* — "as they raised me when I was small." The Qur'an's instruction is to remember, deliberately, the period of one's own utter helplessness, and to let that memory generate the mercy one now owes. The Prophet ﷺ placed the duty among the highest of all. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd:

> *"I asked the Prophet ﷺ: 'Which deed is most beloved to God?' He said: 'The prayer at its appointed time.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Goodness to parents.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Striving in the path of God.'"* (al-Bukhārī 527; Muslim 85)

And he placed its violation among the gravest of sins, and not incidentally or in passing but by naming it when he had been asked to name the worst of them — which is the clearest way a teacher has of indicating where a matter stands:

> *"Shall I not inform you of the greatest of the major sins?"* — three times. They said: "Yes, O Messenger of God." He said: *"Ascribing partners to God, and disobedience to parents"* — and he had been reclining, and he sat up and said: *"And false speech, and false testimony."* (al-Bukhārī 2654; Muslim 87)

Note the severity of the pairing. Disobedience to parents is listed immediately after *shirk*. That is not rhetorical inflation; it is a statement about how badly the corruption of the family corrupts everything downstream. The reason is structural rather than sentimental: the family is the first school of authority, and a person who has learned to treat the authority he can see with contempt is not going to treat the authority he cannot see with reverence. The tradition understood this so well that it made filial piety the fastest route to God's pleasure and its opposite the fastest route to His displeasure — and it did so without ever suggesting that the parents' rights are unlimited, since obedience to them is bounded by obedience to God and does not extend into it.

**Why the Duty Lands Where It Does in This Sūrah**

The sūrah's placement of filial duty in John's portrait is not incidental. This is a chapter about parents and children from beginning to end: a father praying for a son (vv. 4–6), a son given to aged parents (v. 7), a mother alone with a newborn (vv. 22–26), a son defending his mother before he can walk (vv. 30–33), a son arguing with his father (vv. 42–48), and fathers given sons (v. 49). The family is the sūrah's primary theatre, and *birr* is the virtue that holds it together. And there is a specific poignancy to John's case. He was born to parents who had been denied children for a lifetime; he was, in the full sense, a wished-for child. The Qur'an describes him as dutiful, not as successful, wealthy, or famous. Of all the things God could have said about the answer to Zachariah's prayer, He says that the boy was good to his father and mother. That tells the reader what God is after in granting such a prayer: a child who becomes a source of mercy to those who longed for him.

**"He Was Not Domineering, Rebellious"**

The verse closes with a negation, and the negation is as important as the affirmation: *wa-lam yakun jabbāran ʿaṣiyyā* — "He was not domineering, rebellious." Two words, and each is heavy. *Jabbār* comes from the root *j-b-r*, which means to compel, to force, to mend by force — as a bone-setter sets a broken bone, hence *jabīrah*, a splint. The intensive form *jabbār* denotes one who compels others: a tyrant, an overpowerer. It is the word the Qur'an uses for the hard-hearted: *"Thus does God seal up every arrogant, tyrannical (*jabbār*) heart"* (40:35). *Al-Jabbār* is also one of the Beautiful Names of God, but in the divine case it means the One Whose greatness compels all things to submit and Who mends every broken thing — a meaning wholly unlike the creaturely one. *ʿAṣiyy* comes from the root *ʿ-ṣ-y*, to disobey, to resist. It is the intensive form: habitually, persistently disobedient. From the same root come *maʿṣiyah* (sin) and the Qur'an's description of Pharaoh: *"And Pharaoh disobeyed (*ʿaṣā*) the Messenger"* (73:16). So the verse denies of John both the horizontal vice and the vertical one: he did not overpower people, and he did not resist God. The commentators recognised the pairing and drew out its logic. Al-Rāzī notes that a person who is domineering toward people is, as a rule, ultimately disobedient toward God — because the underlying disease in both cases is the refusal to be commanded. Tyranny over others and rebellion against God are the same posture pointed in two directions. The person who cannot bear to have anyone above him will, whenever possible, arrange the world so that there is no one above him at all, and that includes God.

Conversely, the person who has genuinely accepted a Lord becomes, by that very fact, gentler with his inferiors. The connection is not psychological speculation; it was lived out in the Prophet's own conduct. ʿĀʾishah described him in words every Muslim knows, and the description is all the more striking for having been given by the person who saw him when no one else was watching — which is the only testimony that settles the question, since public gentleness can be performed and private gentleness cannot:

> *"He never struck anything with his hand — neither a woman nor a servant — except when striving in the path of God; and he never took revenge for himself, unless the sacred limits of God were violated, in which case he took revenge for God's sake."* (Muslim 2328)

**The Positive Virtue Hidden in the Double Negative**

The construction is worth a final note. The sūrah could have said "he was gentle and obedient." Instead it says "he was *not* domineering, *not* rebellious." The difference is more than stylistic. A positive statement names a virtue that can be performed. A negative statement removes a failure-mode that is easy to rationalise. Arrogance and disobedience are, after all, the two vices that religious people are most skilled at disguising — the first as firmness, the second as independence. By phrasing the praise as the absence of these, the Qur'an invites the reader to check himself specifically on those two points: *do I coerce? Do I resist being told?* For anyone in a position of authority — a parent, a teacher, a manager, an imam — this is the sharpest question in the verse. Authority is the standard solvent of character, and the two things it dissolves first are gentleness and obedience to God. John is described as having authority from childhood (v. 12: "We gave him judgment as a child") and as being entirely free of both corruptions. That combination is rare enough that the Qur'an records it as praise, and the tradition has always treated that rarity as a warning. The sūrah will say the same thing of Jesus in v. 32 — *"and [has made me] dutiful toward my mother. And He has not made me domineering, wretched"* — using nearly identical language. The repetition is the point: this is what a rightly-formed human being looks like, and both of the sūrah's miraculous children are described in precisely these terms.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:15

> **Peace be upon him the day he was born, and the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Days, One Blessing**

The sūrah closes the account of John with a benediction that has become one of the most recited lines in the Qur'an: *wa-salāmun ʿalayhi yawma wulida wa-yawma yamūtu wa-yawma yubʿathu ḥayyan* — "Peace be upon him the day he was born, and the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive." Three days are named, and they are the three thresholds every human being crosses: entry into the world, exit from it, and re-entry on the Day of Resurrection. The verse is a prayer, a prophecy, and a theology of the human life in a single line. Note that the three moments bracket existence entirely — nothing before birth and nothing after resurrection is mentioned, because these three are the moments at which a human being is most exposed. *Salām* (root *s-l-m*) means peace, safety, wholeness, freedom from defect and harm. It is also one of the Beautiful Names of God (*al-Salām*), and it is the greeting of the believers in this world and the greeting with which they will be received in the next: *"Their greeting on the Day they meet Him will be 'Peace'"* (33:44). So the word carries both a negative sense (absence of harm) and a positive one (presence of well-being), and the verse asks for both on all three days.

**Why These Three Days Are the Dangerous Ones**

The commentators were precise about what is being asked for at each threshold, and their analysis follows a logic that any reader can verify from the rest of the Qur'an and Sunnah. *The day he was born.* Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī explain that this is the day of Satan's assault. The authentic ḥadīth, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, is explicit — and the choice of the day of birth as the first of the three thresholds is itself telling, since on any ordinary reckoning birth is the day of safety, the day on which a child has survived the greater danger. The tradition reverses that reading: the day of entry is the day of assault, and the fact that virtually no one remembers it does not make it less real:

> *"No child is born except that Satan touches it when it is born, and it cries out from the touch of Satan — except Mary and her son."* (al-Bukhārī 3431; Muslim 2366, from Abū Hurayrah)

The tradition explains the mechanism: the newborn enters the world unprotected, and Satan's first effort is directed at that moment of maximum vulnerability. The Prophet ﷺ taught his community the practical response — the *adhān* in the newborn's ear, and the supplication for protection. The commentators note that John's blessing on the day of his birth is God's declaration that he was protected from that touch and from the whispering that follows it. *The day he dies.* This is the day of the trial of the grave. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"When one of you is buried, two angels come to him..."* — and he described the questioning of the grave, and said: *"This is the trial of the grave, and by Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you will be tried in your graves as you are tried here."* (al-Bukhārī 1338; Muslim 2870)

And he sought refuge from it constantly, teaching his Companions to do the same — and the regularity of the practice is the point, since a danger that must be guarded against daily is not a danger one outgrows. The blessing upon John at his death is therefore the gift of firmness in the questioning of the grave and safety from its punishment, and the tradition is explicit that this is the first of the three thresholds that every human being will actually cross. *The day he is raised alive.* This is the Day of Resurrection, of which the Qur'an says — in language that strips the event of every distraction and leaves only the naked fact of standing before God:

> *"The Day when a man shall flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his companion and his children — every man among them on that Day has a concern that suffices him."* (Qur'an 80:34–37)

The blessing here is freedom from the terror of that Day — what al-Ṭabarī calls the fear and dread that will overwhelm people — and from any punishment thereafter. The commentators noted that the blessing is not described as a reward earned but as a gift granted, which is consistent with the whole grammar of the sūrah: what is asked for at each of the three thresholds is safety, and safety is not something a person can secure for himself. The Prophet ﷺ described the condition of that Day in a ḥadīth:

> *"The sun will be brought near to the creation on the Day of Resurrection until it is a mile away from them... and they will be submerged in sweat according to their deeds."* (Muslim 2864)

Blessing John on these three days is thus a comprehensive prayer: protected at the entrance, secure in the interval, and safe at the return. The three points are not arbitrary; they are the three moments at which a human being has nothing whatever to rely on. At birth he cannot speak for himself, in the grave he cannot act for himself, and at the Resurrection no one will speak for him except by permission. A blessing that covers all three therefore covers the entire arc of a life at exactly the points where that life is most exposed, and the sūrah's economy in naming them — one clause each, no elaboration — is part of what has made the verse so often recited.

**The Word Used for Resurrection: Yubʿathu**

The verb *yubʿathu* (root *b-ʿ-th*) means "he is raised, sent forth, resurrected." It is the same verb the Qur'an uses for God's sending of messengers — *"He it is Who sent (*baʿatha*) among the unlettered a messenger from among themselves"* (62:2) — and the same verb used for being raised up from sleep or from death. The double usage is meaningful: resurrection is a *sending*, an act of dispatch by the One who owns the person. Human beings do not reassemble themselves on that Day; they are *sent forth* by command. The addition of *ḥayyan* — "alive" — closes the sentence with the point that matters most. He is not raised as a shade, a memory, or a reconstructed likeness. He is raised *alive*, in full possession of himself, to stand before his Lord. That is the plain Qur'anic answer to every attempt to spiritualise the Resurrection out of existence: bodies, alive, standing.

**Why the Same Blessing Is Spoken of Jesus, in His Own Voice**

The sūrah will repeat this triple blessing almost verbatim in v. 33, in the mouth of the infant Jesus, with one grammatical difference: *"Peace be upon **me** the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive."* The repetition is not redundancy, and the commentators drew out the theology of it. The two human beings who, in Islamic teaching, were born outside the ordinary human pattern — one to a barren mother and an aged father, one with no father at all — are the two singled out by name for this threefold peace. And in the case of Jesus, the blessing is pronounced *by himself*, as a sign to his people on the day of his birth that he is not what they will later say he is. A baby cannot make speeches; and this baby does, in order to say, before anyone else can say it for him, that he is a servant of God (v. 30). There is also a christological point. Muslim commentators read the "day I die" in Jesus' self-benediction in the light of the Qur'an's statements elsewhere: *"They did not kill him, nor did they crucify him, but it was made to appear so to them"* (4:157), and *"God raised him up unto Himself"* (4:158). Jesus has not yet died; he will die after his return to earth before the Hour, and it is that death to which v. 33 refers (al-Ṭabarī). So the blessing upon him covers a death still in the future at the time he speaks — which is itself part of the miracle.

**A Practical Reading: The Three Days of Every Life**

For a reader who is not a prophet, the verse functions as a template for what to pray for. The three thresholds named here are universal. Everyone is born, everyone dies, everyone is raised. The blessing encapsulates the whole of what any person could want: to be safe at the beginning, safe at the end, and safe at the reckoning. The Prophet ﷺ taught supplications corresponding to each, and Muslims recite them daily. For protection from the whisper, the two sūrahs of refuge:

> *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind, the King of mankind, the God of mankind, from the evil of the slinking whisperer..."* (Qur'an 114:1–4)

For the death and what follows it — the threshold that every listener knows he will have to cross and about which no one has any direct information — he frequently prayed, and the frequency is itself part of the teaching: a matter about which the Prophet ﷺ sought God's refuge repeatedly is not a matter the community is entitled to treat lightly:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from the punishment of the grave, and from the trial of the grave, and from the trial of the False Messiah."* (al-Bukhārī 1377; Muslim 588)

And for the Day — meaning the standing and everything that follows it — he sought refuge as well, and his practice here is worth dwelling on, because it corrects a common misreading of the religion's emphasis on hope. The Prophet ﷺ did not treat confidence in God's mercy as incompatible with fear of the standing; he treated the two as the two wings of the same bird, and he asked for both in the same breath:

> *"O God, shelter me from the punishment on the Day You resurrect Your servants."* (Muslim 2860)

The sūrah's benediction over John is the compressed form of all three. It is also, quietly, a statement about what constitutes a life well lived: not length, not achievement, not reputation — but safety at the three points where nothing else can help. Everything the world counts as the substance of a life — its duration, its accomplishments, the things said about it afterwards — is absent from this benediction, and the absence is the teaching. John's life occupies very little space on the page and his death is not narrated here at all; what is narrated is that he was blessed at the points of maximum exposure. For a reader deciding what to pray for, on his own behalf or on behalf of a child, the verse is a template: ask for what will matter when nothing else does, and ask for it in the three places where the asking will count.

**The Last Word on John**

With this verse, the account of Zachariah and John closes as it opened, in the register of mercy. The story began with a hidden cry (v. 3) and ends with a public blessing (v. 15). Between those two points, an old man was given a son, a son was given wisdom, and a community was given a prophet. Everything in between was impossible by ordinary reckoning, which is precisely the sūrah's point. And the closing benediction is the appropriate seal: the child who came from nowhere is commended to God at the three moments that define a human life, as though to say that the One who managed his beginning will not lose him at the end.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:16

> **And remember Mary in the Book, when she withdrew from her family to an eastern place.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Formula: "And Remember in the Book"**

The sūrah's second great narrative opens with a command to the Prophet ﷺ: *wa-dhkur fī'l-kitābi Maryama* — "And remember Mary in the Book." The imperative *udhkur* ("remember/mention") introduces several of the sūrah's accounts — Mary (v. 16), Abraham (v. 41), Moses (v. 51), Idrīs (v. 56) — and the recurrence is part of the chapter's structural skeleton. The formula does two things at once. It instructs the Prophet to *recite* these accounts as part of the revealed recitation, and it instructs the community to *hold them in memory* — for *dhikr*, as noted earlier at v. 2, is both mention and remembrance. The phrase *fī'l-kitāb* — "in the Book" — is significant. Mary's story is not being told as folklore or as private consolation; it is being inscribed in the scripture, and thereby made permanent, public, and normative. That is no small matter in a seventh-century Arabian setting in which women's reputations were community property and a woman's word was worth little. The Qur'an does not merely defend Mary; it *enshrines* her defense in the text that Muslims recite in every prayer. For fourteen centuries, every Muslim who has recited this sūrah has recited the vindication of a woman whom her neighbours had condemned. That is not incidental to the sūrah's design; it is the sūrah's design.

**Who Mary Was**

Maryam, the daughter of ʿImrān (66:12), belonged to a family the Islamic sources describe as pure, noble, and of the Davidic line (Ibn Kathīr). Her mother, the wife of ʿImrān, had vowed the child in her womb to the service of God — *"My Lord, I have vowed to You what is in my womb, consecrated [to Your service]"* (3:35) — and when the child turned out to be female, God accepted her nonetheless. Her mother named her Mary and sought refuge for her and her progeny from Satan:

> *"...and I have named her Mary, and I seek refuge for her and for her progeny with You from Satan, the accursed."* (Qur'an 3:36)

The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the acceptance of that prayer in the authentic ḥadīth already cited: *"No child is born except that Satan touches it... except Mary and her son"* (al-Bukhārī 3431; Muslim 2366).
