She was raised in the sanctuary under the guardianship of Zachariah, and the Qur'an records the wonder of that upbringing — the miraculous provision that came to her in her place of seclusion, which the commentators read as the visible sign that her mother's dedication had been accepted:

> *"So her Lord accepted her with a goodly acceptance, and made her grow in a goodly manner, and entrusted her to Zachariah. Whenever Zachariah entered the sanctuary unto her, he found provision with her. He said: 'O Mary, from where does this come to you?' She said: 'It is from God; truly God provides for whom He wills without reckoning.'"* (Qur'an 3:37)

Her purity is attested in several places: 3:36; 21:91 (*"And she who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her of Our Spirit"*); and 66:12 (*"...and Mary, the daughter of ʿImrān, who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her of Our Spirit, and she testified to the truth of the words of her Lord and His Books, and she was of the devoutly obedient"*).

**"She Withdrew" — The Verb and What It Implies**

The key word of this verse is *intabadhat* — "she withdrew." The root is *n-b-dh*, whose primary meaning is *to cast away, to throw aside, to put behind one*. The Form VIII verb *intabadha* means to withdraw oneself, to remove oneself to a distance, to separate off. The commentators, including al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī, explain the withdrawal as undertaken in order to devote herself to solitary worship. The verb's sense of *putting something away* is important: Mary did not drift eastward by accident. She put something behind her — her family's household, its occupations, its conversations, its claims. This is the second occurrence of the sūrah's recurring choreography, and readers should register the pattern now, because it repeats several more times. Zachariah withdrew to the sanctuary and called in secret (vv. 2–3). Mary withdraws east (v. 16) and then further still (v. 22). Abraham withdraws from his father and his people (v. 48) and is given Isaac and Jacob (v. 49). Moses is called away to the side of the Mount (v. 52). In every case the sequence is: *separation, then gift.* The sūrah is not shy about the causality — God bestows His greatest favours on people who have stepped out of the crowd.

**"An Eastern Place" — The Geographical and Symbolic Readings**

*Makānan sharqiyyā* — "an eastern place." What and where was it? The commentators offered several concrete readings. It may mean a place to the east of the Temple sanctuary in Jerusalem (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī); or a place east of her family's house (al-Zamakhsharī, al-Jalālayn); or, in some accounts, a hillside or elevated open area near the Temple where she could be alone. They also offered circumstantial reasons, which are worth recording because they show how concretely the tradition read the text. Some said she withdrew eastward after the onset of menstruation, in accordance with the practice of ritual separation; others said she withdrew in order to purify herself afterwards. Both readings fit a girl dedicated to the Temple, whose entire life was ordered around ritual purity — and both make the point that she did not leave the service of God in order to be alone. She left the building in order to continue the service without interruption. Alongside these, the Sufi commentators proposed a symbolic reading that the tradition has preserved with affection. Rūzbihān al-Baqlī read the *eastern place* as the realm of the Spirit — east being the direction of sunrise, of the dawning of light — and read Mary's *family* as symbolising the world of created things from which she turned in order to encounter the Divine. Ibn ʿAjībah wrote in similar terms: that one cannot encounter the spiritual dimension of reality until one withdraws from everything worldly that distracts from the remembrance of God.

These readings are not to be preferred over the literal ones, and the sober mufassirūn were careful to say so; but they are not arbitrary either. The Qur'an itself uses the symbolism of light's dawning throughout — *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth"* (24:35); the rising of the sun as a sign in Abraham's own story (6:78) — and the east is, in the plain physical sense, where light comes from. A reader may hold both: Mary went to a place east of the Temple, and what she found there was the Light.

**Solitude: Distinguishing the Qur'anic Ideal from Its Counterfeits**

Because the sūrah places such weight on withdrawal, it is worth stating clearly what this verse does and does not endorse. It does endorse *periodic, purposeful solitude* for the sake of worship and clarity — the practice the tradition calls *khalwah* or *ʿuzlah*, and which in its Prophetic form is *iʿtikāf*, seclusion in the mosque. The Prophet ﷺ practised it:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ used to seclude himself in the cave of Ḥirāʾ for a number of nights before the revelation came to him."* (al-Bukhārī 3, the opening of the Book of Revelation)

And after the Hijrah he would perform *iʿtikāf* in the mosque for the last ten nights of Ramaḍān every year (al-Bukhārī 2026; Muslim 1171). It emphatically does *not* endorse permanent withdrawal from human obligation. The classical Sufis were themselves the ones who said this most sharply. Ibn ʿAjībah, commenting on this very sūrah at v. 49, states plainly that withdrawal from creatures is a necessary condition *at the beginning* of the path, but that once the seeker has reached a certain station it becomes incumbent upon him to return and mingle with people — because at that point encounters with others increase his knowledge and complete his character. The Prophet ﷺ made the point in a ḥadīth:

> *"The believer who mixes with people and is patient with their harm is better than the one who does not mix with them and is not patient with their harm."* (al-Tirmidhī 2507; Ibn Mājah 4032; graded ḥasan)

The distinction is simple enough to be practical. Mary's withdrawal was temporary, purposeful, and directed toward God; it produced a gift that she carried back into the community. Any solitude that produces irritation with people rather than service to them is not Mary's solitude. The test is therefore a test of what a person brings back: Mary returned with a child who would speak to her people on her behalf, and a withdrawal that returns nothing to the community is indistinguishable from flight. The tradition's own vocabulary makes the distinction — the *khalwah* of the devotional life is for a purpose and for a season, and the scholars who wrote most about it were uniformly insistent that its value is measured by what it improves in a person's dealings with others rather than by how much of it he can accumulate.

**What Happens Next, and Why the Verse Is Framed This Way**

The verse ends on the word *sharqiyyā* — "eastern" — and the next verse opens with *fa-attakhadhat min dūnihim ḥijāban*, "and she veiled herself from them." The reader is thus told, in the space of two verses, that Mary withdrew from her family and then screened herself even from those from whom she had withdrawn. The intensification is deliberate and serves a clear narrative purpose: it establishes that she was utterly alone before the angel came. That isolation is the whole point. The sūrah is about to place before its readers a claim that will strain credulity for some of them — a virgin conception — and it is constructing the scene so that no intervening explanation is available. There was no man, no witness, no ambiguity. She had withdrawn; she had screened herself; and then God sent His Spirit. The next four verses will build the case, and this verse is its foundation stone.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:17

> **And she veiled herself from them. Then We sent unto her Our Spirit, and it assumed for her the likeness of a perfect man.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Screen, and the Logic of Complete Privacy**

The verse opens where the last one left off: *fa-attakhadhat min dūnihim ḥijāban* — "And she veiled herself from them." The phrase *min dūnihim* means "apart from them," "excluding them," "in front of them so as to be cut off from them." The noun *ḥijāb* (root *ḥ-j-b*) denotes any screen, curtain, partition, or barrier that conceals. The commentators explain this variously: that she hung a curtain; that she took shelter behind a wall; or that she withdrew behind a screen while she was purifying herself. The precise mechanism is undocumented in the revelation, and the classical exegetes were content to leave it at that. The *function* of the screen, however, is unambiguous, and it is worth stating plainly because the sūrah's argument depends on it: Mary was entirely concealed from human view at the moment the Spirit came to her. This is why the Qur'an mentions it. The chapter is about to narrate a conception with no human father, and it takes pains — two verses in a row — to establish that no human being was present, could have been present, or could be proposed as an explanation. The screen is not a decorative detail of the story; it is part of the sūrah's forensic structure. Muslim readers have always understood this: the virginity of Mary is not defended by assertion alone but by the *architecture of the scene*.

**"Our Spirit" — Who Came to Her**

*Fa-arsalnā ilayhā rūḥanā* — "Then We sent unto her Our Spirit." The overwhelming majority of commentators identify *Our Spirit* (*Rūḥanā*) here as the Archangel Gabriel (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). The grounds for this identification are several and mutually reinforcing. First, Gabriel is called *al-Rūḥ al-Qudus*, "the Holy Spirit," elsewhere in the Qur'an: *"We gave Jesus son of Mary the clear proofs, and supported him with the Holy Spirit"* (2:87, 253); and *"God said: 'O Jesus son of Mary, remember My blessing upon you... when I strengthened you with the Holy Spirit'"* (5:110). Second, he is called *al-Rūḥ al-Amīn*, "the Trustworthy Spirit": *"The Trustworthy Spirit has brought it down upon thy heart"* (26:193–94). Third, Gabriel is the angel specifically charged with conveying announcements of children, as the sūrah itself has already shown in the case of Zachariah, and as the angels do in the case of Abraham (11:69–73; 15:51–55; 51:24–30). Fourth, the Qur'an elsewhere names him as the agent of the breathing into Mary: *"And she who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her of Our Spirit"* (21:91; 66:12), and the exegetes understood the *breathing* to have been performed by Gabriel at God's command. Why is an angel called "spirit"? Al-Zamakhsharī and others note the connection between *rūḥ* (spirit) and the giving of life: religion is revived through revelation as a body is revived by breath, and the one who carries that reviving breath is therefore called spirit. The same logic lies behind the Qur'an's description of the Qur'an itself as *rūḥ* — *"And thus We have revealed to thee a spirit (*rūḥan*) from Our Command"* (42:52). A minority reading, transmitted from the Companion and famed reciter Ubayy ibn Kaʿb (d. 29/649–50), holds that *Our Spirit* and the *perfect man* both refer to the spirit of Jesus himself — that is, that God sent the spirit of Jesus, who then appeared to Mary in human form. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabrisī record a similar reading without attributing it to Ubayy. The majority did not adopt it, both because the angelic identification fits the Qur'an's usage better and because the reading raises the question of how Jesus' spirit could exist in human form before his conception. The report is preserved, as the mufassirūn preserve Companion-reports generally, but the weight of the tradition rests with Gabriel.

**The Possessive: "Our" Spirit**

The phrase is *Rūḥanā* — "Our Spirit," with the plural of majesty. This construction, like *Rūḥ Allāh* ("the Spirit of God"), has been the subject of careful theological discussion, and it is worth stating what mainstream Islam concluded. The possessive does not indicate that God has a part, an emanation, or a piece of Himself dwelling in creation. It indicates *ownership and honour*: the spirit is "Ours" in the sense that God created it and singled it out for a special dignity. The exegetes explained it with an analogy the Qur'an itself supplies — *"and He has subjected to you whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth, all of it from Him"* (45:13) — where "from Him" means from His creation and His bestowal, not from His essence. Similarly, the Qur'an speaks of *"the she-camel of God"* (*nāqat Allāh*, 7:73; 11:64; 91:13), *"the House of God"* (*Bayt Allāh*), and *"the servant of God"* (*ʿabd Allāh*) — all cases where the possessive denotes a created thing specially honoured by its relation to God, not a divine fragment. This distinction is not academic fussiness; it is precisely the doctrinal line this sūrah exists to draw. If "a spirit from God" meant a *part* of God, then the sūrah's closing refutation — *"It is not for God to take a child"* (v. 35) — would be undermined by its own narrative. The entire chapter would contradict itself. The tradition therefore read these possessives with great care: God honours what He creates, but He is not composed of His creation.

**"It Assumed for Her the Likeness of a Perfect Man"**

The last clause is one of the most discussed in the sūrah: *fa-tamaththala lahā basharan sawiyyan* — "and it assumed for her the likeness of a perfect man." The verb *tamaththala* (root *m-th-l*) means to take on a likeness, to present oneself in a form, to become formed as something. It is a strong claim: the angel did not appear *as though* he were a man, in some vague or dreamlike way; he *presented himself as* a man, in full, visible form. The commentators were insistent on the reality of this, because the alternative — that Mary merely imagined a man — would destroy the story's meaning entirely. Gabriel, whom God created with the power to take forms, took the form of a man. The word *bashar* means a human being, a mortal of flesh and blood. It is the word the Qur'an uses for human beings precisely as embodied creatures — *"Say: I am only a mortal (*bashar*) like you; it is revealed to me..."* (18:110). The choice of *bashar* rather than *insān* or *rajul* emphasises the physical, embodied character of the appearance. The final word, *sawiyyan*, is the one that repays the closest attention — and it is the same word the sūrah used in v. 10, where it was said that Zachariah would be unable to speak for three nights *sawiyyan*, "while sound." From the root *s-w-y*, meaning evenness, soundness, right proportion, the word here means: *perfectly formed, without defect, well-proportioned, whole.*

The commentators expand on what that perfection consisted of. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī transmit descriptions of the form: a young man, beardless, beautiful, radiant in appearance, perfectly proportioned, without blemish. Al-Zamakhsharī adds the reason: he appeared in this form so that she would not be overcome with fear and recoil from him. A being appearing in its true angelic magnitude would terrify any human; a form of perfect human beauty and composure would not. There is a second resonance of *sawiyyan* that the sūrah's own wording invites. In v. 10, *sawiyyan* described Zachariah: sound in body, yet deprived of speech. Here, *sawiyyan* describes the angel: sound in form, yet not human. In both cases the word marks a *departure from what the appearance would suggest*. Zachariah looks healthy and cannot speak; this figure looks perfectly human and is not. The sūrah is, in both places, teaching the reader that appearance is not a reliable guide to reality — which is the lesson Mary's neighbours will fail to learn when they judge her in v. 27.

**Angels Taking Human Form in the Sunnah**

The phenomenon of an angel appearing as a man is not unique to this passage; it is established in the authentic Sunnah, and the most famous instance is the ḥadīth of Gabriel, which Muslim places at the head of his collection's discussion of faith and which the tradition calls the mother of the Sunnah — both because of what it contains and because of the form in which it is reported. The angel comes as a traveller with intensely white clothes and very black hair, unmarked by the dust of the road, and sits before the Prophet ﷺ with his knees against his knees — a detail the narrators preserved because it fixes how utterly ordinary the appearance was. Everyone present saw a man. Only the Prophet ﷺ was told afterwards who had been sitting there, which is the point the commentators drew from it for this verse: the form a messenger takes is not evidence about the messenger, and Mary's perceiving a man is in no tension with his being an angel:

> *"While we were sitting with the Messenger of God ﷺ one day, there appeared before us a man whose clothes were exceedingly white and whose hair was exceedingly black; no trace of travel was visible upon him, and none of us knew him. He sat down before the Prophet ﷺ, resting his knees against his knees and placing his hands upon his thighs, and said: 'O Muhammad, tell me about Islam...'"* (Muslim 8; cf. al-Bukhārī 50)

The man in that narration was Gabriel, come to teach the community its religion — and only after he had gone did the Prophet ﷺ tell his Companions who he had been. The same pattern appeared in the case of Abraham and Lot, where the angels came to Abraham as guests (11:69–70; 15:51; 51:24–26) and were not recognised until they spoke. The theological principle is that angelic appearance in human form is entirely within God's power and is a mercy toward human beings, who cannot bear to see the created order's other inhabitants as they are. Gabriel's taking of a human form for Mary was therefore not a novelty but an instance of a well-established divine courtesy.

**The Moment Itself**

The verse compresses into one line an event that has occupied painters, poets, and theologians for two millennia. A young woman alone behind a screen; a being in the form of a man appearing before her; a message that will overturn her life and reorganise the religious history of the world. The sūrah's handling of it is notably restrained. There is no description of Mary's inner state at this moment, no narrative commentary, no editorialising. The Qur'an states the fact and moves on — and it is only in the next verse that we learn, from her own mouth, what she felt. That restraint is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of the sacred: it reports rather than embellishes, and it leaves the reader to stand before the event rather than to be told how to feel about it.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:18

> **She said, "I seek refuge from thee in the Compassionate, if you are reverent!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Words of Mary in the Qur'an**

This is the first thing Mary says in the Qur'an, and the commentators drew meaning from the choice. A young woman, alone, behind a screen, is confronted by what appears to be a strange man. And what she says is not a greeting, not a question, not a scream. It is: *"I seek refuge from thee in the Compassionate, if you are reverent!"* Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī note that she was afraid — with good reason, since an unfamiliar man had appeared before her in a place where she had every expectation of being alone, and she could not know his intentions. Her response was not panic but *istiʿādhah*: the formula of taking refuge. There is a great deal to unpack in that, because it reveals what was already established in her character. Her first instinct, at the moment of maximum danger, was to reach for God. That reflex is not produced at the moment of crisis; it is installed by a lifetime. She had been raised in the sanctuary, had been taught by Zachariah, had been provided for by God in ways she herself described (*"It is from God; truly God provides for whom He wills without reckoning"*, 3:37). When the test came, the installed reflex fired. That is one of the most practically useful things in the entire sūrah: *what you practise in safety is what appears in danger.*

**The Root of Istiʿādhah**

The verb is *aʿūdhu*, from the root *ʿ-w-dh*, which means to seek shelter, to take refuge, to cling to something for protection. The Form X verb *istaʿādha*, used for the act of taking refuge, carries the sense of *asking for and seeking out* that shelter. The image underlying the root is vivid: a person who sees a threat and runs behind something solid. The Qur'an commands this reflex:

> *"And if an incitement from Satan should incite thee, then seek refuge in God. Verily He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing."* (Qur'an 7:200; cf. 41:36)

And the two sūrahs that the Prophet ﷺ described as the greatest protection — the *Muʿawwidhatayn* (113 and 114) — are built entirely on it: *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of the daybreak..."* (113:1); *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind..."* (114:1). Al-Bukhārī records that the Prophet ﷺ would blow into his hands and recite these two sūrahs over himself every night before sleeping (al-Bukhārī 5017). The Prophet ﷺ also taught specific formulas for moments of fear:

> *"I seek refuge in the perfect words of God from the evil of what He has created."* (Muslim 2708)

And he instructed the community about what to do for a person who uses this formula — which is worth dwelling on, because the instruction converts a private act of self-defence into a public obligation of the community, and means that whoever hears the words has a duty to back them up:

> *"Whoever seeks refuge in God, then grant him refuge."* (Aḥmad; Abū Dāwūd; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

That last ḥadīth is particularly relevant here, and the commentators noted it: Mary sought refuge in God, and God honoured the refuge by having His angel reassure her immediately. The sequence is worth noting for what it teaches about the order of operations in a moment of fear. Mary did not argue, did not negotiate, and did not attempt to assess the figure in front of her — she turned to God first and asked the question afterwards. The reassurance came from the One in whom she sought refuge, and it came through the very messenger who had frightened her, which is the sūrah's way of showing that the thing which alarms a believer is frequently the delivery mechanism of what he asked for.

**Why the Name al-Raḥmān, and Why Here**

Mary says *innī aʿūdhu bi'l-Raḥmāni minka* — "I seek refuge *from thee in the Compassionate*." The Name she chooses is *al-Raḥmān* — the very Name that dominates this sūrah, appearing sixteen times. This is worth pausing on. A person in terror seeking protection does not instinctively reach for the attribute of mercy; one might expect *al-Qādir* (the Powerful), or *al-Qahhār* (the Subduer), or *al-Jabbār* (the Compeller). Mary reaches for *al-Raḥmān* — the One Whose mercy encompasses everything. The commentators saw in this a mark of her spiritual discernment, and the reasoning is coherent. She did not know whether the figure before her was a man with evil intentions or something else. What she knew was that whatever he was, he was a creature, and the One Whose mercy encompasses all things could protect her from him. Moreover, her choice of the mercy-Name reflects an understanding that protection is not merely the destruction of a threat but the surrounding of the threatened person with care. She was asking to be enveloped, not merely avenged. There is also a structural point. The sūrah opened with "a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord" (v. 2) and goes on to use the Name *al-Raḥmān* at v. 26 (Mary's fast of silence), vv. 44–45 (Abraham's warning), v. 61, v. 69, v. 75, v. 78, v. 85, v. 87, v. 88, vv. 91–93, and v. 96. A reader who notices the Name's recurrence will find that Mary's cry in this verse is among the first times a *human being* in the sūrah speaks it — and she speaks it at the moment of her greatest fear. The sūrah is demonstrating what the Name is for: it is what a servant calls upon when there is nowhere else to go.

**"If You Are Reverent" — A Remarkable Conditional**

The last clause is *in kunta taqiyyan* — "if you are reverent." The word *taqiyy* is the same word applied to John in v. 13, from *taqwā*. The conditional has generated discussion, and the readings are complementary rather than exclusive. On the most natural reading, Mary is saying: *if you have any fear of God in you, this appeal should stop you.* It is an appeal to the conscience of whoever stands before her — a way of saying, "I have nothing to defend myself with except this, and if there is anything of God left in you, it will work." There is a quiet courage in this. She does not beg; she invokes. She places God between herself and the threat, and she names the fact that a person with *taqwā* cannot violate such a shield. A second reading, recorded by the commentators, takes the conditional as a *tacit rebuke*: "if you are reverent, you will not approach me" — implying that the very act of coming to her in this manner shows that he is not. On this reading, the phrase is a form of moral confrontation. A third reading understands it as a genuine condition attached to the efficacy of the refuge: the protection of God is realised in the one who has *taqwā*. This is the reading with the most theological depth, and the Qur'an supports it directly. *Taqwā* is, throughout the Qur'an, the condition of divine protection and divine deliverance: *"And whosoever is reverent toward God, He will make for him a way out, and will provide for him from whence he does not reckon"* (65:2–3). Mary, who was of the devoutly obedient (*al-qānitīn*, 66:12), could say this from within her own certainty.

**What Mary's Response Teaches About Fear and Faith**

There is a practical lesson in this verse that deserves to be drawn out, because it directly addresses a modern confusion. The confusion is this: many people assume that faith means the absence of fear. Mary was afraid — the Qur'an records no rebuke of her for it, and the commentators affirm that she was frightened. Faith did not remove the fear. What faith did was determine what she *did* with the fear. She did not freeze, and she did not attempt to manage the situation by her own resources. She reached immediately for the one thing that was stronger than whatever was in front of her. That is the correct account of courage in the Islamic tradition, and the Prophet ﷺ stated it plainly:

> *"The strong man is not the one who wrestles; the strong man is the one who controls himself when he is angry."* (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609)

Strength is not the absence of the reaction; it is the governing of it. Mary's fear did not produce panic because it was immediately converted into *duʿāʾ* — and *duʿāʾ* is fear's proper destination. The Prophet ﷺ described the strong man in exactly these terms: not the one who does not feel the provocation but the one who masters himself when he feels it. The definition matters, because a great deal of unnecessary guilt is generated by believers who mistake the first movement of the heart for a sin. Fear, anger, and grief are not sins; they are movements, and they become sins or become worship according to where they are taken. Mary took hers to God, and the sūrah records the consequence: the angel's reply begins by naming her as one who has already been chosen.

**Chastity as the Sūrah's Theme, Already Present**

Finally, note what the verse establishes about Mary before the Annunciation is even spoken. A young woman alone with a strange man, in a culture where such an encounter could ruin her, does not flirt, does not negotiate, does not calculate. She invokes God. The sūrah is building a case for her innocence that will be tested in vv. 27–28, when her own people accuse her. The defence begins here, in the structure of the scene. The Qur'an shows the reader who she is *before* the accusation arrives, so that when it does arrive, the reader already knows it is false. That is one of the sūrah's most effective literary strategies, and it is entirely typical of the Qur'an's narrative method: it does not argue the character of the righteous by assertion, but by placing the reader inside the moment of testing.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:19

> **He said, "I am but a messenger of thy Lord, to bestow upon thee a pure boy."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"I Am But" — The Restriction That Reassures**

The angel's first words to Mary are a correction of the situation she believes herself to be in: *innamā anā rasūlu rabbiki* — "I am but a messenger of thy Lord." The particle *innamā* is a particle of restriction: "only," "nothing but." Its function here is to close off every other possibility at once. He is not a man; he is not a threat; he is not a suitor; he is not a robber; he is nothing but a *rasūl*, a messenger sent by *thy Lord*. Note the possessive: *rabbiki* — "thy Lord," not "the Lord." The angel describes God to Mary in personal terms, as *her* Lord. For a young woman alone and frightened, that is the most consoling possible framing: the One who has sent me is the One who owns you, and the One who owns you has sent me with good. The word *rasūl* is used in the Qur'an both for human messengers and for angelic ones. Of angels, the Qur'an says: *"God chooses messengers from among the angels and from among mankind"* (22:75); and *"[He sends] messengers having wings, two, three, and four"* (35:1). The use of *rasūl* here rather than *malak* ("angel") is itself significant: the angel identifies himself by his *function* — he has been sent with something — rather than by his nature. For Mary, in that moment, the function is what matters.

**The Purpose: "To Bestow Upon Thee a Pure Boy"**

The stated purpose is *li-ahaba laki ghulāman zakiyyā* — "to bestow upon thee a pure boy." The verb *ahaba* (root *w-h-b*) means to give freely, to bestow as a gift without expectation of return. It is the root of *al-Wahhāb*, "the Bestower," one of the Beautiful Names. The word choice is deliberate and theologically important: the child is not described as something earned, merited, or produced. He is a *gift*. This matters particularly in Mary's case, and the sūrah will make the point explicit in v. 21: *"And [it is thus] that We might make him a sign unto mankind, and a mercy from Us."* The birth of Jesus has no human cause to which it can be attributed, and the verb *ahaba* removes even the conceptual possibility of crediting it to anything other than God. What, precisely, is the angel's role in the bestowal? The commentators split into two readings, and both are recorded by al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, and al-Bayḍāwī. Some hold that the angel was the *means* of the conception — that he breathed into her sleeve or the opening of her garment, and that the breathing was the mechanism by which she conceived (cf. 21:91; 66:12). Others hold that he was only the *bearer of the announcement*, and that the conception occurred by God's direct creative act without any intermediary mechanism. The second reading is preferred by those who note that the text says merely that he was sent "to bestow" — and that a messenger's role is to deliver, not to manufacture. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabrisī both incline to the view that God created the child directly, with the angel's words as the announcement and the "breathing" as God's own act described in the language of the angel's agency. Either way, the causal agent is God; the question is only how the language distributes the description.

**Zakiyy — Purity and Growth Together**

The boy is described as *zakiyyā*, from the root *z-k-w* — the same root encountered at v. 13, where John was given *zakāh*. The double sense noted there is operative here as well, and the commentators drew it out explicitly (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī, Ibn ʿAjībah). The first sense is *purity from sin and defect*. Jesus would be free of moral fault — a claim Islam makes of all the prophets, and which the sūrah will press into service in the argument against the claim of Jesus' divinity, since moral purity is a quality of a created servant, not a definition of divinity. The second sense is *growth*. Al-Ṭabrisī and others note that the word implies that Jesus would grow in purity and goodness from infancy through manhood. That is a subtle and pastorally important point: the child announced here is not born complete in his human development. He will grow, learn, eat, sleep, and be tested — which is precisely why, at v. 33, the sūrah will say of him *"and the day I die."* The combination produces a striking picture. The child is to be *pure* — untouched by sin — and yet *growing*. He is not a static perfection dropped into the world but a human being developing in a sustained state of goodness. Muslims have always read this as part of the Islamic doctrine of *ʿiṣmah*, the protection of the prophets from major sin and from error in conveying the message: it is not a removal of human nature but a preservation within it.

**Why the Boy Is Called "Pure" Before He Exists**

There is a logical sequence here worth noting. The angel announces a child who does not yet exist and describes him, in advance, as *zakī*. This is a divine declaration about a person's future before he has acted. Theologically, this raises no difficulty for Islam — God's knowledge precedes existence, and the Qur'an makes similar declarations elsewhere (of John: *"a master, and chaste, and a prophet from among the righteous"*, 3:39). But it does establish something important about the Qur'anic view of vocation: a human being's life-purpose is not discovered by experiment but assigned by God. Jesus is announced as pure; John is announced as a confirmer of the Word; Ishmael is announced as true to his promise (v. 54). In each case, the identity precedes the biography. For readers, the practical implication runs both ways and is worth stating carefully. On the one hand, this verse instructs every parent to speak well of a child before birth — to name goodness over the child's life in advance, as Mary is told here. The Prophet ﷺ taught parents to have a good opinion of God and to make good supplication for their children, warning:

> *"Do not supplicate against yourselves, or against your children, or against your wealth, lest you coincide with an hour in which God is asked and He answers."* (Muslim 3009)

On the other hand, the declaration of purity is God's alone to make. No human being is entitled to declare himself *zakī*; the Qur'an explicitly forbids *tazkiyat al-nafs*, self-praise — and the prohibition is directed at the claim rather than at the quality, since a person may in fact be pure and still have no business announcing it. The scholars distinguished carefully here between the purification that God performs and the purification a person claims for himself: the first is a fact about the servant, the second is a statement about himself that he has no reliable way of verifying and every reason to suspect. The angel therefore says *"I am only a messenger of thy Lord"* before he says anything about what will be given, establishing the source of the announcement before delivering its content, so that Mary will know the purity attributed to her is attributed by God and not claimed by her:

> *"So do not claim purity for yourselves; He knows best who is reverent."* (Qur'an 53:32)

**The Announcement as the Sūrah's Central Claim**

Placed at the centre of the sūrah's first movement, this verse makes the claim that the rest of the chapter will defend. A "pure boy" is to be given, by God, to a virgin, without a father. The sūrah will narrate it (vv. 20–26), defend it through the mouth of the infant (vv. 30–33), and then draw from it the theological conclusion it is aiming at: that the God who creates without a father has no need of a son (vv. 34–36, 88–92). That is why the purity of the child is emphasised at the moment of announcement. If Jesus were a son of God in the sense claimed by later theology, his purity would be a metaphysical consequence of his nature and would require no announcement. The Qur'an announces it as a *gift* bestowed on a *servant* — which places him, along with John, Abraham, Moses, Ishmael, and Idrīs, in the company of those God blessed (v. 58), and in no other company.

**A Note on the Angel's Manner**

Finally, the manner of address deserves a word, because the sūrah records it with economy and the commentators noticed. The angel does not say "Fear not" before he says anything else; he identifies himself. That ordering is significant. Reassurance without identification is worthless — frightened people need to know *who* is speaking before they can take comfort in what is said. The angel therefore establishes his credentials first ("I am a messenger of thy Lord"), then states his errand ("to bestow upon thee a pure boy"). Only in vv. 21 and 24 will comfort be offered, once the identity of the speaker is established. That is a small thing, but the tradition of *daʿwah* has always taken note of it. The Prophet ﷺ, when he first spoke publicly to the Quraysh from Mount Ṣafā, began by establishing his credibility — asking them whether they would believe him if he told them an army was behind the mountain, and receiving the answer "we have never known you to lie" (al-Bukhārī 4770) — before delivering the message. Truth-telling begins with the establishment of trust.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:20

> **She said, "How shall I have a boy when no man has touched me, nor have I been unchaste?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Question, Asked from a Deeper Impossibility**

Mary's response to the angel mirrors Zachariah's almost exactly, and the sūrah intends the parallel to be seen. Zachariah asked, in v. 8: *"How shall I have a boy, when my wife is barren, and I have grown decrepit with old age?"* Mary asks here: *"How shall I have a boy when no man has touched me, nor have I been unchaste?"* The Arabic shares the opening: *annā yakūnu lī ghulāmun* — "How shall I have a boy?" The identical phrasing signals to the reader that these two stories are one argument, told twice. And the answers will also be nearly identical: *"Thus shall it be. Thy Lord says, 'It is easy for Me'"* (v. 21), which is word-for-word the response given to Zachariah in v. 9. The differences, however, are what make the second account the harder one. Zachariah's impossibility was *quantitative*: two people who could once have had a child can no longer do so. Mary's impossibility is *categorical*: there is no second party at all. And that difference matters enormously for what the sūrah is doing, because the sūrah is climbing. It begins with the milder impossibility and escalates, so that by the time the reader reaches the virgin birth, the logic has already been established.

**Was Mary Doubting? The Same Answer as Before**

The commentators apply to Mary exactly the analysis they applied to Zachariah, and for the same reasons: her question is a question about *how*, not about *whether*. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī state plainly that she was asking by what means the child would come — whether through marriage to someone, or in some other way entirely — rather than expressing scepticism about the angel's message. The supporting evidence is the same in both cases: she was of the *ṣiddīqīn* (5:75), she had been raised under divine provision, and the response she receives in v. 21 contains no rebuke. There is, however, an additional consideration specific to Mary that the commentators raised. Zachariah's question concerned a mechanism that, however unlikely, existed within nature: a womb to be opened. Mary's question refers to a mechanism that does not exist at all in her case. She therefore has a stronger reason to ask, and the question is not merely permissible but *necessary* — because she is being told that she will bear a child while having done nothing whatever to bring that about, and she needs to know whether the angel is telling her that she is to enter into marriage (in which case there is an instruction attached) or that God will act directly (in which case there is a miracle attached). The distinction matters practically. She is, in effect, asking: *what am I being commanded to do?* That is the question of a servant, and it is the opposite of the question of a doubter. A doubter asks whether the thing will happen; a servant asks what he is to do about it. The difference shows in the grammar of the two questions, and it shows in what each one does next: the doubter waits for evidence before moving, while the servant asks for the instruction and moves. Mary's question is therefore not a hesitation in the narrative but the narrative's turning point, and the commentators noted that she was given the sign she did not ask for precisely because she did not ask for one — she asked for the command, and the command came with the sign attached.

**The Two Negatives: "No Man Has Touched Me, Nor Have I Been Unchaste"**

Mary's statement has two clauses, and each closes a different door. The first is *wa-lam yamsasnī basharun* — "no man has touched me." The verb *yamsas* (root *m-s-s*) means to touch, and in the Qur'an it is used as a decorous term for sexual contact (*"...or you have touched women and you find no water, then perform tayammum..."*, 4:43; 5:6). The word *bashar* means a human being — here, a male human being. So the first clause states: I have had no sexual contact with any man. The commentators note that this covers the lawful route — she is not married and has never been — and thereby removes the ordinary explanation for pregnancy. The second clause is *wa-lam aku baghiyyā* — "nor have I been unchaste." The word *baghiyy* comes from the root *b-gh-y*, whose core meaning is *to seek, to desire excessively, to transgress, to act unjustly*. In its sexual usage it denotes a fornicatress or adulteress — one who seeks sexual gratification outside the lawful bounds. The same root-sense of transgressive seeking appears in the Qur'an's indictment of the people of Lot: *"Do you approach males among the worlds, and leave what your Lord has created for you of your spouses? Nay, you are a people who transgress"* (26:165–66). So the second clause closes the *unlawful* route. The two together are exhaustive. Every possible human route to pregnancy is either lawful contact (denied in clause one) or unlawful contact (denied in clause two). There is no third. The commentators made this point explicitly: Mary's statement is not a protestation of innocence offered for its own sake, but a precise elimination of alternatives, and it is the reason the sūrah's later vindication of her is so complete. Note also the tense and form of the second clause: *wa-lam aku baghiyyā* uses the verb *kāna* — "I have not *been* unchaste" — implying a settled state of character, not merely a particular act avoided. She is not saying "I did not do this thing." She is saying "this is not what I am."

**The Vindication of Mary as a Doctrinal Necessity**

Muslim readers should understand that Mary's innocence is not a devotional preference in Islam; it is a doctrinal requirement, defended by the Qur'an with unusual vigour. The reason is straightforward. If Jesus was conceived through any human agency, he was an ordinary human being and there is no miracle. If he was conceived through unlawful human agency, then one of the greatest figures in the Islamic catalogue of prophets — a messenger of God, the Messiah, a Word from Him, strengthened with the Holy Spirit — would have been born of sin, and his mother would stand condemned by the very scripture that calls her *"chosen... and purified... and chosen above the women of the worlds"* (3:42). The whole edifice collapses. So the Qur'an defends her in the strongest terms available, in separate chapters (3:35–62; 19:16–36; 21:91; 66:12), and it describes the slander against her in terms that make the gravity unmistakable:

> *"And [for] their disbelief, and their uttering against Mary a tremendous calumny."* (Qur'an 4:156)

The sūrah we are in will dramatise that calumny in vv. 27–28, and it will answer it not with argument but with a speaking infant. The miracle of the cradle exists, in the sūrah's own logic, precisely because no human testimony could have cleared her — and this is the reason the miracle takes the form it does rather than some other form. A defence offered by an adult, even a miracle-working adult, could have been answered, disputed, or dismissed; a defence offered by a day-old infant could not, because there is no possible account of it that leaves the accusers' premise intact. The sūrah's logic is therefore exact: the charge is unspeakable and the answer is unanswerable, and the only witness capable of settling the matter is one whom no one could have coached.

**Is Mary a Prophet? The Discussion This Verse Provokes**

Because Mary received a direct angelic announcement and was the recipient of an evidentiary miracle (*muʿjizah*), a minority of Islamic authorities — including Ibn Ḥazm and Rūzbihān al-Baqlī — held that she was a prophet. The majority did not, on the grounds already noted: the Qur'an describes those it sent as men, and it does not name her among the prophets. The majority position requires an explanation of the miracle, and al-Ṭabrisī supplies it: Mary's miraculous conception is proof that inimitable evidentiary miracles, normally given to prophets as proof of their prophethood, may also be given to those who are not prophets. The technical term for such a wonder is *karāmah* — a marvel granted to a saint (*walī*). This is not an ad hoc solution; it is a well-developed position in Sunni theology. The mainstream schools affirm the reality of the *karāmāt al-awliyāʾ* while distinguishing them from *muʿjizāt*: a *muʿjizah* is granted to a prophet in the context of a claim to prophethood and is accompanied by a challenge; a *karāmah* is granted to a righteous person as an honour and a help, carries no challenge, and establishes no law. Ibn Taymiyyah devoted a substantial discussion to the subject, affirming the *karāmāt* of the righteous while insisting that they neither abrogate the law nor establish a new one, and warning that a wonder worked by a person is not evidence of that person's rectitude unless it is measured against the Qur'an and Sunnah.

Some commentators, al-Ṭabrisī notes, offered an alternative resolution: that the miracle in this case was granted not to Mary but *for the sake of* Jesus or Zachariah — that is, the child was the prophet, and the wonder attended him from the womb. That reading keeps the miracle tied to prophethood without making Mary a prophet, and it fits the sūrah's presentation, in which the infant himself speaks and testifies (vv. 30–33).

**What Mary Teaches About Being Slandered**

There is a final, very practical dimension to this verse, and it is one of the reasons the sūrah's account of Mary has been so cherished. Mary is about to be accused of something she did not do, by people who know her, on the basis of evidence that will look overwhelming. She will have a newborn and no husband. Every circumstance will be against her, and the sentence the community will pass is the one that communities have always passed in such cases. What does the Qur'an record her doing? She does not argue, does not defend herself, does not name the angel, does not produce an alibi. She is instructed to remain silent (v. 26) and to point (v. 29) — and God answers for her. The lesson is not that a person should never defend himself. The lesson is that there are circumstances in which self-defence is structurally impossible, and in those circumstances the believer's recourse is God, who is not structurally limited. Every person who has been falsely accused and found the evidence against them unanswerable will recognise the situation. The sūrah's answer is not "explain better." It is: *be silent before them and speak to God, and let God speak for you* — and the promise attached to that posture is the promise the sūrah will fulfil in the cradle. The Prophet ﷺ gave the same counsel in a ḥadīth that speaks directly to this, and it is worth noting how comprehensively the tradition closes the circle around a person who is falsely accused: the slanderer is promised punishment, the one who defends the accused is promised reward, and the accused herself is told that the matter rests with God — so that no one in the situation is left to manage the outcome alone:

> *"Beware the supplication of the wronged, for there is no veil between it and God."* (al-Bukhārī 2448; Muslim 19)

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:21

> **He said, "Thus shall it be. Thy Lord says, 'It is easy for Me.'" And [it is thus] that We might make him a sign unto mankind, and a mercy from Us. And it is a matter decreed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Identical Answer, Given Twice, and Why**

The angel's reply to Mary is, in its first clause, word-for-word the reply given to Zachariah in v. 9: *"Thus shall it be. Thy Lord says, 'It is easy for Me.'"* That repetition is the sūrah's central instructional device. Two very different people — an old man whose body has failed and a young woman who has never been touched — raise the same objection, and they receive the same answer. The Qur'an is not economising on words; it is demonstrating that there is only one answer to this kind of question, regardless of the shape of the impossibility. The obstacle changes; the principle does not. *Huwa ʿalayya hayyin* — "it is easy for Me" — is not a statement about the size of the task but about the One performing it. The opening word, *kadhālika* ("thus"), is worth a note. It means "like that" — that is, *the matter will be as I have told you*, in the manner announced, without qualification and without negotiation. Grammatically it is an emphatic confirmation; rhetorically it closes the door on further inquiry about mechanism. Mary asked *how*; the answer is not a mechanism but a decree.

**The Stated Purpose: "That We Might Make Him a Sign"**

Where the reply to Zachariah stopped at the explanation of power, the reply to Mary continues into purpose, and the asymmetry between the two answers is one of the sūrah's quieter structural achievements. Zachariah's difficulty was *how* — how can this be, given who I am — and the answer addressed ability. Mary's difficulty was never stated; she asked what she was commanded, and the answer addresses meaning. The sūrah thereby teaches the reader something about the two kinds of question a servant can bring: one asks whether God can, and the other asks what God wants, and the second is the higher question. Three purposes are named, and each expands the meaning of the event:

> *"...that We might make him a sign (*āyah*) unto mankind, and a mercy (*raḥmah*) from Us. And it is a matter decreed."*

The first purpose is that Jesus be an *āyah* — a sign — *li'l-nās*, "unto mankind," not merely to his own people or to his own generation. The word *āyah* means a sign, a proof, a portent that points beyond itself to something greater. The commentators, led by Ibn Kathīr, identified the specific thing it points to: God's power as Creator. Jesus' birth demonstrates that God is capable of bringing a child into being without a father — just as He was capable of bringing Adam into being without either a mother or a father (Ibn Kathīr). That comparison is made explicitly by the Qur'an itself:

> *"Truly the likeness of Jesus in the sight of God is that of Adam; He created him from dust, then said to him, 'Be!' and he was."* (Qur'an 3:59)

The logic is worth following carefully, because it is the pivot of the entire sūrah. If Adam — who had neither father nor mother — is universally acknowledged as a creature and not a god, then Jesus — who had a mother but no father — is, *a fortiori*, a creature and not a god. The absence of a human father is not evidence of divinity; it is evidence of the power of the One who does not need fathers. The sūrah will deploy this argument at length in vv. 88–95, and it plants it here, in the announcement itself. Al-Ṭabrisī adds a second dimension to the *āyah*: through his miraculous conception, his birth, and his speaking as an infant, Jesus is also a sign or proof of his own prophethood and of his mother's innocence. That is precisely how the story will play out — the infant's speech in vv. 30–33 is the proof that vindicates Mary before her people. The Qur'an makes the same point elsewhere by pairing mother and son together as a single sign:

> *"And We made the son of Mary and his mother a sign, and We sheltered them upon a height, a resting-place with a spring."* (Qur'an 23:50)

**"And a Mercy from Us"**

The second purpose is that Jesus be *raḥmatan minnā* — "a mercy from Us." The word is the sūrah's signature term, and its appearance here ties the whole Mary-narrative back to the sūrah's opening phrase, "a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord" (v. 2). Whose mercy is he? The commentators answer: a mercy to Mary, and a mercy to those who believe in him and are guided by him (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī). For Mary, he was mercy in the most concrete sense — the child who spoke when she could not, and whose first sentence cleared her name. For mankind he is mercy as guidance: a messenger who called people back to the worship of the One God, as v. 36 will make explicit in his own words. There is a linguistic nicety the commentators relished. The word *raḥmah* shares its root with *raḥim*, the womb. Jesus is "a mercy from Us" — and the mercy in question entered the world through a womb that no human being had touched. The sūrah's vocabulary is, as so often, doing theology at the level of the root. The Prophet ﷺ spoke of his own nearness to Jesus in terms that complement this description:

> *"I am the closest of people to Jesus son of Mary in this life and the next."* They said: "How so, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"The prophets are brothers from different mothers; their mothers are different but their religion is one — and there is no prophet between me and him."* (al-Bukhārī 3443; Muslim 2365)

**"And It Is a Matter Decreed" — The Verse's Theological Weight**

The third and closing phrase is *wa-kāna amran maqḍiyyā* — "and it is a matter decreed." Four Arabic words that have generated centuries of theological discussion, and they deserve careful handling because they touch the question of divine decree (*qadar*). The root *q-ḍ-y* means to settle, to complete, to judge, to decree. An *amr maqḍiyy* is a matter already settled, finished, and beyond alteration. Applied here to the conception of Jesus, it means: this is not a possibility under discussion; it is a thing decided in God's knowledge and will, and it will occur. Now, what does the tradition say about decree generally, and what does it refuse to say? *What it affirms.* God's knowledge is eternal and encompasses everything; His Will is effective; nothing occurs in His creation without His knowledge and His permission. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The first thing God created was the Pen, and He said to it: 'Write.' It said: 'What shall I write?' He said: 'Write the measure (*qadar*) of everything until the Hour comes.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 4700; al-Tirmidhī 2155; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And in the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās, the young man riding behind him — a report whose setting is itself part of its content, since the Prophet ﷺ chose the moment of a shared ride, alone with a boy of perhaps ten or thirteen, to deliver the instruction that would define that boy's life, and began by addressing him directly rather than waiting to be asked:

> *"...and know that if the whole nation were to gather together to benefit you with something, they would not benefit you except with something God has already written for you; and if they were to gather together to harm you with something, they would not harm you except with something God has already written against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

*What it refuses.* It refuses the conclusion that decree removes human responsibility, and it refuses the opposite conclusion that human acts are outside God's knowledge. The most careful statement in the Sunnah on this point is the ḥadīth of ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn (also transmitted by ʿAlī and others), in which a man asked whether the people of Paradise and the people of the Fire were already distinguished, and whether they should therefore abandon works. The Prophet ﷺ replied:

> *"No. Work, for everyone is facilitated toward that for which he was created."* Then he recited: *"As for him who gives and is reverent, and believes in the fairest, We shall ease his way unto ease. But as for him who is niggardly and deems himself self-sufficient, and denies the fairest, We shall ease his way unto hardship."* (al-Bukhārī 4949; Muslim 2647 — Qur'an 92:5–10)

The formula "work, for everyone is facilitated" is the tradition's balanced answer, and it is the answer this verse supports. The conception of Jesus was decreed — *and* it occurred through a real process, in a real body, at a real time. Decree does not abolish means; it establishes them. Mary will conceive, carry, labour, and give birth (vv. 22–26); the decree does not exempt her from any of it. Ibn Taymiyyah devoted extensive writing to precisely this balance, arguing that the Qur'an affirms both the universal scope of divine will and the genuine agency of the servant, and that the two are reconciled by understanding that God creates the servant's act while the servant owns it as his own — a position he called the middle way between the *Jabriyyah* (who denied human agency) and the *Qadariyyah* (who denied that God creates human acts). For the practical reader, the upshot is this: *amran maqḍiyyā* is a reason for trust, never for passivity.

**The Three Purposes Held Together**

The verse thus moves in a deliberate arc: *it is easy for Me* (power), *a sign and a mercy* (purpose), *a matter decreed* (certainty). Power answers the objection; purpose answers the "why"; certainty answers the "when." Mary asked one question; she received three reassurances. That is a pattern worth noticing for its own sake, because it is the pattern of how God answers in the Qur'an generally. The servant asks about the mechanism, and God answers about the Agent, the meaning, and the outcome. Mechanism is almost never what God supplies — not because He is evasive, but because the mechanism is the least useful thing a servant could know.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:22

> **So she conceived him and withdrew with him to a place far off.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sentence, an Entire Pregnancy**

The sūrah compresses into nine Arabic words an event that occupies nine months of a human life: *fa-ḥamalathu fa-intabadhat bihi makānan qaṣiyyā* — "So she conceived him and withdrew with him to a place far off." The verb *ḥamalat* (root *ḥ-m-l*) means she conceived, and also she carried — both senses are present in the single word. The Qur'an elsewhere describes human gestation in stages of extraordinary precision:

> *"And verily We created man from an extract of clay. Then We made him a drop in a secure resting-place. Then We made the drop a clinging clot, then We made the clot a lump, then We made the lump bones, then We clothed the bones with flesh; then We brought him into being as another creation. So blessed is God, the Best of creators."* (Qur'an 23:12–14)

The commentators understood Mary's pregnancy to have followed the ordinary course after its miraculous beginning. The miracle was at the inception; the gestation was human. That distinction is theologically important and worth stating plainly, because it guards against a misunderstanding into which readers sometimes fall: Islam does not teach that Jesus was generated instantaneously as a fully-formed being. He was conceived by a divine act, and then carried, nourished, and born as every human child is. The Qur'an's insistence on the ordinary physiology of the pregnancy is part of its insistence on his full humanity.

**How the Conception Occurred**

The Qur'an describes the mechanism in two other places with the language of breathing, and the choice of metaphor repays attention. Breath is the most intimate, most continuous, and least visible sign of life: a person cannot see it in another except by the movement it produces, and cannot perceive it in himself except as a rhythm he is not maintaining. The verses use that register because the act being described is the imparting of life from a living source rather than the manufacture of a body out of material:

> *"And she who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her of Our Spirit, and We made her and her son a sign unto the worlds."* (Qur'an 21:91)

> *"...and Mary, the daughter of ʿImrān, who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her of Our Spirit..."* (Qur'an 66:12)

The classical commentators, citing reports from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, describe Gabriel as breathing into the opening of her garment or her sleeve, and the breath reaching her — a description recorded by Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Ṭabrisī. As noted at v. 19, other commentators held that the breathing is God's own creative act attributed to the angel as its agent. Either way, the *blowing* (*nafkh*) is the operative image, and it is the same image the Qur'an uses for the creation of Adam:

> *"And when I have proportioned him and breathed into him of My Spirit, then fall down before him in prostration."* (Qur'an 15:29; also 32:9; 38:72)

The parallel is exact and is the sūrah's point. Adam was formed from clay and then enlivened by a breathing; Jesus was formed in the womb by a breathing. In both cases the thing that makes the difference between matter and a living human being is not a second human parent but the act of God. The Qur'an states the comparison itself in 3:59, and it is worth memorising because it is the single most concise statement of the Islamic position on Jesus: *"Truly the likeness of Jesus in the sight of God is that of Adam; He created him from dust, then said to him, 'Be!' and he was."*

**A Detail the Commentators Preserved: The Meeting of the Two Mothers**

The commentators transmit an account whose parallel appears in the Gospel of Luke (1:39–45). When Zachariah's wife conceived, she came to Mary — the two were relatives — and found Mary pregnant as well; and the child in her womb is said to have prostrated, or moved, in recognition of the child in Mary's womb. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī record versions of this, and they read it as the fulfilment of the angels' announcement about John in 3:39, that he would *"confirm a Word from God"* — the Word being Jesus. Reports of this kind, transmitted through the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* literature rather than through authenticated Prophetic chains, are to be received as edifying narrative rather than as binding doctrine. Muslim scholars established the rule for them early: what agrees with the Qur'an and Sunnah is accepted, what contradicts them is rejected, and what is neither is neither affirmed nor denied. The account here agrees with the Qur'an's portrayal of John as the confirmer of Jesus, and it has therefore been received warmly — but with the caveat that its details rest on reports whose chains do not reach the level of *ṣaḥīḥ*.

**"A Place Far Off" — The Second Withdrawal**

The second half of the verse has Mary withdrawing again, this time *makānan qaṣiyyā* — "a place far off." The adjective *qaṣiyy* comes from the root *q-ṣ-w*, meaning distant, remote. It is worth registering that this is the sūrah's second withdrawal for Mary (the first was to "an eastern place," v. 16), and the second withdrawal overall in the chapter's pattern of separation-then-gift. The commentators proposed two kinds of explanation for the destination, and al-Rāzī's is the most psychologically acute. Some identified the place as another spot east of the Temple, following the same reasoning as at v. 16. Al-Rāzī argued instead — and convincingly — that what mattered was not the direction but the *distance*: she went to a place where she and her family were unknown. Why? Al-Rāzī gives reasons that any reader will recognise as true to the situation. She went far off in order to avoid embarrassing Zachariah, whose household she had been raised in and whose reputation would be damaged by her condition. She went out of fear for her child — a pregnant woman alone in a community that would assume the worst is in physical danger. And she went because she anticipated that her people, among whom she had a reputation for great piety and asceticism, would not understand. That last reason is the most painful and the most human. Mary's problem was not only that she was pregnant; it was that she was *unbelievably* pregnant. The more exalted a person's reputation for piety, the more catastrophic the fall appears — and the less inclined people are to accept an explanation that contradicts appearances. She knew that the women who had watched her grow up in the sanctuary would be the last people on earth to believe her. So she left. There is a lesson here about how communities treat their own saints. The people who should have known Mary best were precisely the ones who would judge her most harshly, and the sūrah does not soften this. It will dramatise it in vv. 27–28, where they accuse her by invoking the piety of her own parents.

**Joseph, and the Question of the Islamic Sources**

Wahb ibn Munabbih (d. 110/728), a scholar of the generation after the Companions who was deeply familiar with Jewish and Christian scripture, transmitted an account — recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī — that Mary's pregnancy was known to a relative named Joseph (*Yūsuf*) who also served in the Temple and who helped her during her pregnancy. Some accounts add that she later travelled with Joseph toward Egypt as her delivery approached (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī), a detail with an evident parallel in Matthew 2:13–14. Two points must be made about such material. First, the Qur'an itself names no Joseph and no flight to Egypt in this sūrah; the narrative of Mary in the Qur'an is entirely without a male companion. Second, material of this kind reaches the Islamic sources through *Isrāʾīliyyāt* channels, and the scholars of ḥadīth applied to it the rule mentioned above: it is neither affirmed as revelation nor rejected as false, and it carries no doctrinal weight. What the Qur'an asserts about Mary — her chastity, her solitude, the miracle, the birth beneath the palm — is what Muslims believe; the additional details belong to narrative tradition.

**The Withdrawal Pattern, Again**

It is worth pausing on the recurrence one more time, because the sūrah is insistent about it. Zachariah withdraws to pray. Mary withdraws to the east. Mary withdraws far off. Abraham will withdraw from his people (v. 48). Moses will be called to the side of a mountain (v. 52). In every case the separation precedes the gift, and in every case the separation is *voluntary on the human side and purposeful on the divine side*. The logic is not that God rewards isolation as such. It is that a human being who is saturated with the opinions of others cannot receive something that will contradict all of them. Mary needed to be away from the people who knew her, because what was happening to her could not be processed by consensus. Every person who has received a calling, an insight, or a conviction that their community would reject has experienced the necessity of that distance — not out of contempt for the community, but out of the need to let the thing be real before submitting it to the judgment of others. And the sūrah's account is careful to note that the withdrawal was *with him*: *intabadhat bihi* — "she withdrew *with him*." She did not go alone. From the moment of conception, the child she carried was the companion of her solitude, and — as the next verses will show — the one who would eventually speak for her.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:23

> **And the pangs of childbirth drove her to the trunk of a date palm. She said, "Would that I had died before this and was a thing forgotten, utterly forgotten!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb: She Was Driven**

The verse opens with a word whose force is easy to miss in translation: *fa-ajāʾahā al-makhāḍu* — "the pangs of childbirth *drove* her." The verb *ajāʾa* (root *j-y-ʾ*) is a causative form meaning *to compel, to drive someone to a place*. The labour did not merely happen to her; it drove her. She was moved by a force that would not be negotiated with. The noun *makhāḍ* (root *m-kh-ḍ*) denotes the pains of parturition — the contractions that precede delivery. It is the same root used, in a different form, for the churning of milk into butter, and the image is apt: a violent internal agitation that produces something. So the scene the Qur'an sets is stark. A young woman, alone, in a remote place, in advanced labour, with no midwife, no family, no husband, and — in the commentators' account — a dried-up palm trunk and winter weather. She is driven to the trunk of the tree, seeking both support for her body and concealment from any eye. The commentators add details that sharpen the picture: the palm was said to be a withered trunk, its top cut off, without green fronds or fruit; and the season was said to be winter (al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī). Both details are circumstantial reports rather than revealed facts, but they converge on the same point that the Qur'an makes explicitly two verses later: what is about to happen is not natural. A dead trunk in winter does not produce fresh dates.

**"Would That I Had Died Before This"**

Then comes one of the most quoted and most discussed sentences in the entire sūrah: *yā laytanī mittu qabla hādhā wa-kuntu nasyan mansiyyan* — "Would that I had died before this and was a thing forgotten, utterly forgotten!" Consider what Mary is wishing for, and what she is not. She is not wishing that the child not exist. She is not wishing that she had refused the angel. She is not cursing God, or questioning His wisdom, or renouncing her trust. She is wishing that she had not had to *be present* for what she is about to endure. The commentators were unanimous in their care here, and their care is instructive. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr explain that she wished she had died before the onset of the difficulties she now faced as a woman giving birth alone and unmarried: the physical pain of labour, and the anticipation of what people would say. Ibn ʿAjībah adds the important qualification that Mary's longing for death should not be understood as a lack of patience, contentment, or trust in God. And al-Rāzī offers the general observation that makes the verse intelligible: longing for death is often the response of the righteous to suffering, because they will not abandon their moral or spiritual duty, but they are nonetheless grieved and frightened by what that duty will cost them. Al-Rāzī instances ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib's statement before the Battle of the Camel (36/656), the opening battle of the First Civil War during his caliphate — a man who was about to fight fellow Muslims and who expressed his anguish at the prospect. The parallel is exact: ʿAlī was not abandoning his duty; he was registering the cost of keeping it.

**The Ḥadīth on Wishing for Death, and How to Reconcile It**

There is an authentic ḥadīth that appears, at first sight, to forbid exactly what Mary says — and the apparent tension is worth resolving carefully, because it is the kind of difficulty a reader notices and then cannot un-notice. The resolution the scholars gave turns on the difference between the grief itself and the *expression* of grief. What the ḥadīth prohibits is the specific practice of wailing, striking the face, tearing the garments, and protesting against the decree — the pre-Islamic *niyāḥah* — and not the sorrow that God has placed in the heart and that no one can simply switch off. The Prophet ﷺ himself wept at the death of his son Ibrāhīm and said that the eye weeps and the heart grieves while the tongue says only what pleases its Lord. Mary's words belong to the second category, which is why the sūrah records them without any hint of disapproval:

> *"None of you should wish for death because of harm that has befallen him. If he must do something, let him say: 'O God, give me life as long as life is good for me, and cause me to die when death is good for me.'"* (al-Bukhārī 6351; Muslim 2680, from Anas and Abū Hurayrah)

How is Mary's statement to be squared with this? The scholars' reconciliation runs along several lines, and they are cumulative. First, the prohibition concerns a *settled, deliberate wish* for death arising from impatience with one's lot; Mary's words were a cry uttered at the peak of physical agony and social dread, and the tradition has always distinguished a cry from a considered position. Second, the reports of what Mary said come to us as narrative within the Qur'an, and the Qur'an records it without censure — which is itself an indication that it was not a sin. Third, some commentators noted that Mary's wish was specifically that she had died *before this*, i.e., before the trial, not that she be killed now — a wish about the past, which is a different thing from a wish for the future. And there is a fourth consideration that matters most for readers, which is the one the tradition drew out of the *absence* of a rebuke. The Qur'an reports this sentence immediately before sending down an angel to comfort her, a stream to give her to drink, and a palm-tree that drops fresh dates into her lap. God's response to her cry was not correction but consolation. That tells the reader something essential about how God receives distress.

**The Psychology of the Cry, and Why the Qur'an Records It**

This verse is one of the strongest pieces of evidence against a misunderstanding of faith that causes enormous damage: the belief that a person of genuine faith does not experience despair, fear, or the wish to be elsewhere. The Qur'an is full of such moments, and it never edits them out. Jacob says of his grief: *"I only complain of my grief and my sorrow unto God"* (12:86). Moses, fleeing Egypt, says: *"My Lord, I am indeed in need of whatever good You send down to me"* (28:24). Jonah in the darkness says: *"There is no god but You; glory be to You; I have indeed been of the wrongdoers"* (21:87). The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself is addressed: *"Perhaps you will kill yourself with grief over them, if they do not believe in this message"* (18:6) — and again, *"We know that your breast is constricted by what they say"* (15:97). The Qur'an's God is not offended by distress. What He objects to is despair *of Him* — *"Despair not of the Mercy of God; verily God forgives all sins"* (39:53) — which is a different thing entirely. Mary's cry, in this reading, is not despair of God. It is despair of her situation, addressed to God. And that is precisely what *duʿāʾ* is. For anyone who has sat with a person in deep suffering, the practical implication is enormous, and it is worth stating as a sequence because the sequence is the teaching. The right response to a suffering believer is not "you shouldn't feel that way." God's response to Mary was: *grieve not; here is water; here is food; and here is your son, who will speak for you.* Note the order. God does not begin by correcting the feeling; He begins by naming it and setting it aside — *grieve not* — and only then attends to the material facts of her predicament. The water and the food come before the vindication, because a person in the extremity of labour and exposure cannot take in an argument. And the vindication, when it comes, is not a speech Mary must deliver but a son who delivers it for her.

**"A Thing Forgotten, Utterly Forgotten"**

The closing phrase contains a striking double: *nasyan mansiyyan*. Both words come from the root *n-s-y*, to forget; the first is a verbal noun, the second a passive participle. Together they intensify one another, and the commentators paraphrased the effect: she wished she had been completely erased from memory — that no one would look for her, that every trace of her would be gone, and that it would be as though she had never dwelt upon the earth (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī). The doubling is expressive of a specific kind of pain — not simply the wish not to be seen, but the wish never to have been known. It is the difference between hiding and ceasing to exist. Mary is a woman of renowned piety, known to everyone in her community, whose name is now about to become a byword; and the extremity of that reversal produces the wish to have no name at all. The Sufi commentators, led by Ibn ʿAjībah, heard in the phrase a second and higher register: the ultimate victory against the ego. On this reading, Mary wished not only to withdraw from the world and forget it, but to be utterly forgotten *by* it — the annihilation of the self's claim to be noticed. That is a station of the spiritual path rather than an exegesis of the verse, and it should be read as such; but it is not foreign to the text, for the Qur'an elsewhere makes the erasure of the ego's demand its own theme — *"Say: Verily my prayer and my sacrifice, my living and my dying, are for God, Lord of the worlds"* (6:162).

**What Happens Next**

The verse ends with the cry hanging in the air, and the sūrah answers it in the very next line with a voice. That is the structure of the whole passage: extremity, then relief. The sūrah is teaching its readers something about the rhythm of divine help — that it often arrives at the point at which the servant has nothing left to manage with, which is why it is recognisably *divine* help when it comes. Mary's cry was not answered because she was faithless. It was answered because she was in need, and she addressed the need to God. The next three verses are, in effect, a demonstration of the promise stated elsewhere: *"And whoever is reverent toward God, He will make for him a way out, and will provide for him from whence he does not reckon"* (65:2–3).

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:24

> **So he called out to her from below her, "Grieve not! Thy Lord has placed a rivulet beneath thee.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Voice, and the Question of Who Speaks**

The answer to Mary's cry comes immediately, in the form of a voice: *fa-nādāhā min taḥtihā* — "So he called out to her from below her." Who called? The commentators recorded two views and, characteristically, declined to settle the matter dogmatically. The first is that the caller was the Archangel Gabriel. This is the straightforward reading, since Gabriel is the one who has been the agent throughout (vv. 17, 19, 21), and he is the one who ministers to Mary in this passage. The difficulty is the phrase *from below her*, since an angel might be expected to address her face to face. The second view — which al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Zamakhsharī all incline toward, and which they describe as more likely — is that the caller was the infant Jesus, speaking immediately after his birth. The argument is precisely the phrase *from below her*: a newborn delivered and lying at her feet would be physically below her, and the sūrah will narrate his speech at length only six verses later (vv. 30–33), so this verse would be its beginning. Several harmonising readings were also offered. Some said that if it is Gabriel, *from below her* may mean "in front of her" or "beneath where she sat" — the preposition *taḥta* being used loosely for a lower elevation, as when Gabriel stood in the valley or at the foot of the mountain while Mary was on the slope above (Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabrisī).
