The Islamic tradition preserves reports about Jesus' conduct that fill out this verse, and while they rest on transmitted narrative rather than authenticated Prophetic ḥadīth, they have shaped the Muslim imagination of him for centuries. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī transmit that Jesus lived with striking humility: he ate mostly plant foods, wore a coarse garment of hair, sat on the ground, and was itinerant, with no fixed residence. He is reported to have travelled constantly, with nowhere to lay his head — a detail with obvious resonance in the Gospel accounts and in the Islamic sources alike. Alongside this humility, the sources record the priority he gave to his mother precisely because of v. 32. He is said to have treasured those who followed his message above all others. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī transmit the account of a woman who, seeing Jesus raise the dead and heal the sick, cried out: *"Happy is the womb that bore thee and the breasts that nursed thee!"* And he replied: *"Happy are those who recite the Book of God and follow what is in it."* That exchange has a parallel in the Gospels (Mark 3:31–35; Matthew 12:46–50; Luke 8:19–21), where Jesus identifies his true family as those who hear the word of God and do it. Muslim commentators read it as consistent with v. 32 rather than in tension with it: filial duty is an obligation laid on him by God, and it is not diminished by his teaching that the bond of faith is deeper than the bond of blood. Both are true. The one who is *barr* toward his mother is the same one who says that those who follow the truth are his family — and in the Islamic reading, there is no contradiction, because both statements rank relationships by their nearness to God rather than by convention.

**"And He Has Not Made Me Domineering, Wretched"**

The verse closes with a double negation, matching the pattern used of John in v. 14: *wa-lam yajʿalnī jabbāran shaqiyyā* — "And He has not made me domineering, wretched." *Jabbār* — the tyrant, the one who compels others — was discussed at v. 14. The word is repeated here, and the repetition is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that this is a fixed feature of the prophetic type. Both of the sūrah's miraculous children are described as free of domination. The commentators elaborate: he was neither arrogant nor disobedient to God's commands (al-Ṭabarī), nor violent in anger (al-Qurṭubī). The consistent portrait is of one who possesses the greatest conceivable authority — authority over life and death, by God's permission — and who never once uses it to compel anyone. *Shaqiyy* — "wretched" — is the word that repays the closest attention, and it is the sūrah's own internal echo.

**The Word Shaqiyy, and Its Echo at Verse 4**

*Shaqiyy* comes from the root *sh-q-w*, denoting misery, wretchedness, and perdition. It appeared, in this very sūrah, at v. 4 — where Zachariah said: *"And in calling upon Thee, my Lord, I have never been wretched (*shaqiyyan*)."* And it will appear again, in a far more terrible sense, at v. 48, where Abraham says: *"And I call upon my Lord; it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched."* And at v. 86, where the guilty are driven into Hell. The sūrah is using one word across four very different registers, and the pattern is deliberate:

- Zachariah: *I have never been wretched in calling upon God* — answered prayer.
- Jesus: *He has not made me wretched* — a life preserved from perdition.
- Abraham: *I call upon my Lord; perhaps I shall not be wretched* — hope in the answer.
- The guilty (v. 86): driven to Hell as a thirsty herd — wretchedness realised.

The word's opposite in the Qur'an is *saʿīd*, "blessed, felicitous," and the two terms are the Qur'an's vocabulary for the final division of humanity — a division the Book presents as exhaustive and as settled at the point of death rather than negotiated afterwards, so that what a person is on the Day is not a new condition imposed on him but the condition he has been making for himself brought out into the open:

> *"On the Day it comes, no soul shall speak save by His leave; and some among them shall be wretched (*shaqiyy*) and some blessed (*saʿīd*)."* (Qur'an 11:105)

So when the infant Jesus says *"He has not made me wretched,"* he is declaring, in a single word, that he is among the *suʿadāʾ* — those whom God has made felicitous. And note once more the verb: *He has not made me.* Not "I am not," but "He has not made me." Felicity is a divine act, and Jesus attributes it entirely to God.

**The Two Negations as the Definition of Prophetic Character**

Taken together, the two negations define a type that the sūrah is recommending by contrast. The opposite of *jabbār* is gentleness toward those beneath one. The opposite of *shaqiyy* is well-being in the sight of God. So the verse is saying: he does not oppress others, and he is not himself ruined. Both matter, and the sūrah has set them side by side because they are more closely connected than they appear. The person who compels others usually does so out of a fear of his own ruin — the tyrant is, in the classical analysis, the most frightened person in the room, which is why he cannot tolerate the possibility of refusal. Conversely, the person who is secure in God's favour has no need to compel. That is the logic the sūrah has already established at v. 14, where John's freedom from domination and from rebellion were shown to be two faces of one disposition. For anyone who holds authority — and everyone holds authority over someone — this verse supplies the test. Two questions: *do I compel?* And *am I content with what God has given me?* The tyrant and the wretched person are, in the Qur'an's psychology, the same failure seen from the outside and from the inside.

**The Infant's Portrait, Complete**

With this verse, the image the infant has drawn of himself is finished, and it is worth pausing to take the whole of it in before the sūrah moves on to comment. In four verses he has said:

1. I am a servant of God (v. 30a).
2. He gave me the Book and made me a prophet (v. 30b).
3. He made me blessed wherever I am (v. 31a).
4. He charged me with prayer and almsgiving for as long as I live (v. 31b).
5. He made me dutiful toward my mother (v. 32a).
6. He did not make me a tyrant or one who is wretched (v. 32b).

Every clause has God as its subject. There is not one assertion of independent status, not one claim of self-sufficiency, not one suggestion of divinity. And the whole declaration is delivered by a newborn who should not be able to speak at all. The sūrah has now established everything it needs. The mother is vindicated. The child is identified. The doctrine is stated by the person it concerns. What remains is the sūrah's own commentary on what has just happened, which comes in vv. 33–40 — and it is severe, because the sūrah is about to say what the world subsequently did with this child.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:33

> **Peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Blessing, Now in the First Person**

The infant's speech closes with a benediction that is almost word-for-word the one pronounced over John in v. 15: *wa'l-salāmu ʿalayya yawma wulidtu wa-yawma amūtu wa-yawma ubʿathu ḥayyan* — "Peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive!" The differences from v. 15 are minimal and instructive. There, the blessing was pronounced *upon him* (*ʿalayhi*) by God; here it is pronounced *upon me* (*ʿalayya*) by the speaker. There, the verbs were third person (*wulida*, *yamūtu*, *yubʿathu*); here they are first person (*wulidtu*, *amūtu*, *ubʿathu*). The content and the rhythm are identical. The sūrah clearly intends the identity to be registered. John and Jesus are the two children of this chapter born outside the ordinary human pattern, and they are the two who are given this threefold peace. The pairing is one of the sūrah's structural signatures, and it serves a specific purpose: it places the two cousins in exactly the same category. Both are servants; both are prophets; both are born; both die; both are raised. Whatever separates them in the history of religion, they are, in the Qur'an's account, the same kind of thing.

**Why the Infant Says It Himself**

The change of person is theologically important, and the commentators drew out why. For John, the blessing came from God — a declaration about him. For Jesus, it comes from his own mouth — a declaration *by* him. And that self-declaration is the point, because it forecloses the interpretation that would later be placed on him. A newborn speaking at all is a sign. A newborn speaking these particular words is a sign with a specific content. He announces that he was born, that he will die, and that he will be raised. Beings who are born, die, and are raised are creatures. The infant is stating, in the cradle and before anyone can interpret him otherwise, that he is a mortal servant of God with a beginning and an end. This reading is reinforced by the whole architecture of the passage. The Qur'an's concern throughout the Mary-narrative is that the manner of Jesus' birth not be made the ground of a false conclusion about his nature. So it has the angel declare him "a pure boy" (v. 19), has Mary's people accuse her (vv. 27–28), has Mary point to him (v. 29), and then has him speak — and the first and last things he says are the two things that need saying: *I am a servant* (v. 30) and *I will die* (v. 33).

**The Day He Was Born**

The first of the three days is the day of birth, which the commentators — as at v. 15 — identify as the day of Satan's assault on the newborn. Al-Ṭabarī notes that Jesus was spared Satan's touch on the day he was born, and the repetition of exactly the same threefold formula for John and for Jesus is itself one of the sūrah's structural claims: the two cousins are being presented as a single phenomenon, two miraculous births framing the chapter's first movement and answering the same impossibility from two directions — one from a womb that was too old, and one from a womb that had not been touched at all. The proof-text is the same ḥadīth already cited:

> *"No child is born except that Satan touches it when it is born, and it cries out from the touch of Satan — except Mary and her son."* (al-Bukhārī 3431; Muslim 2366)

That ḥadīth names exactly two human beings as exempted, and they are the two whose mother is the subject of this sūrah. The Prophet ﷺ thus tied the exemption to the prayer of Mary's mother in 3:36 — *"I seek refuge for her and for her progeny with You from Satan, the accursed"* — and the commentators understood the exemption of "Mary and her son" to be the acceptance of that prayer across two generations.

**The Day He Dies, and the Islamic Teaching About It**

The second of the three days raises the question already addressed at v. 31: when does Jesus die? The Qur'an is explicit that he was neither killed nor crucified, and the position held by the commentators, with very little dissent, is that God raised him up, that he will return before the Hour, live an ordinary human life, die a natural death, and be resurrected with everyone else. That is why the three days here are not three figurative thresholds but three actual passages — birth, death, and resurrection — each of which Jesus will genuinely undergo, and each of which the sūrah asks that he be blessed in:

> *"And [for] their saying, 'We killed the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, the messenger of God' — but they did not kill him, nor did they crucify him; rather, it was made to appear so to them. And indeed those who differ concerning it are in doubt about it; they have no knowledge of it, only the following of conjecture; and they did not kill him, for certain. Rather, God raised him up unto Himself; and God is Mighty, Wise."* (Qur'an 4:157–58)

The mainstream Islamic position, held by the vast majority of commentators including al-Ṭabarī, is that Jesus was raised alive to God and that he will return to the earth before the Day of Resurrection, will live for a period, and will then die a natural death. It is that death to which this verse refers. The return of Jesus is established in the Sunnah with a frequency that places it among the most securely transmitted matters of Islamic eschatology. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, the son of Mary will soon descend among you as a just judge; he will break the cross, kill the swine, and abolish the jizyah; and wealth will abound until no one accepts it."* (al-Bukhārī 2222, 2476; Muslim 155)

The details of that return — its timing, its length, its circumstances — are matters about which the sources vary and on which the scholars have been appropriately careful. What is firmly established is the two facts: that he did not die on the cross, and that he will die before the Resurrection. It is worth noting the pastoral weight of this for the reading of v. 33. When the infant says *"the day I die,"* he is referring to an event still in the future at the moment he speaks, and to a death that will occur under circumstances wholly unlike any other human death. The miracle does not end at the cradle.

**The Day He Is Raised Alive**

The third day is the Resurrection. The verb is *ubʿathu* — "I am raised, sent forth" — the same verb discussed at v. 15, with the same implication: resurrection is a divine dispatch, not a self-reassembly. The addition of *ḥayyan* — "alive" — repeats the emphasis: raised *living*, bodily, in possession of himself. The commentators add a detail specific to Jesus: he will be raised with the rest of humanity (al-Ṭabarī), and he will be free of the fear and dread that will overcome others on that Day. That is the content of the "peace" invoked here — not merely safety from punishment, but safety from the terror of the assembly itself, in which, as the Qur'an says, *"a man shall flee from his brother, and his mother and his father"* (80:34–35).

**Why Peace, and Why Three Times**

The word *salām* appears three times in this single verse — once explicitly and twice understood, since the construction distributes the greeting across all three days. The repetition is emphatic, and it is worth asking what is being emphasised. The three days named are the three moments at which a human being is most passive. At birth, a person does nothing; at death, a person does nothing; at the Resurrection, a person does nothing. All agency is removed. And at precisely those three moments, the sūrah asks for *salām* — the peace that comes from Another. That is the sūrah's anthropology in miniature. Human beings are agents in the middle of life and patients at its edges, and the edges are the parts nobody controls. The blessing is an acknowledgment of that, and a plea that the uncontrollable parts be held by the One who does control them. For the reader, the verse is thus a complete prayer and one that can be said for oneself, for one's children, and for one's parents. It asks for nothing else — no wealth, no success, no length of days. Only safety at the three points where safety is not in one's own hands.

**The Close of the Infant's Speech**

With this verse the cradle-speech ends, and the sūrah turns immediately to its own commentary — which is worth noting as a feature of the Qur'an's method throughout. The Book rarely leaves a passage to stand alone where an interpretive conclusion needs to be drawn from it; having reported the words, it supplies the meaning, so that no reader can take the narrative in a direction the revelation does not intend. The commentary begins, moreover, with a verse of pure theology rather than with a moral, which tells the reader what is actually at stake in the story:

> *"That is Jesus son of Mary — a statement of the truth, which they doubt."* (19:34)

The sūrah's next move is to tell the reader that what has just been narrated *is* the truth about Jesus, and that others have doubted it. The blessing of v. 33 closes the testimony; v. 34 opens the argument. And the argument, as the sūrah will develop it over the next seven verses and then again in vv. 88–95, is directed at both errors at once: the error of those who rejected him, and the error of those who made him more than a servant.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:34

> **That is Jesus son of Mary—a statement of the truth, which they doubt.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Own Verdict on the Story It Has Just Told**

With this verse, the narrative breaks and the sūrah speaks directly: *dhālika ʿĪsā ibnu Maryama qawla al-ḥaqqi alladhī fīhi yamtarūn* — "That is Jesus son of Mary—a statement of the truth, which they doubt." The demonstrative *dhālika* ("that") points back at the entire preceding account — the withdrawal, the annunciation, the conception, the birth beneath the palm, the silence, the accusation, and the infant's testimony. All of it, the sūrah says, is *qawl al-ḥaqq*. The phrase has been read in three ways, and the commentators recorded all three without forcing a choice. First, that it describes the account itself: what the Qur'an has just narrated about Jesus *is* the truth, as opposed to what the Jews said about him and what the Christians said about him (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī). Second, that it refers to the words Jesus spoke in vv. 30–33: his own testimony is a statement of the truth (al-Qurṭubī). Third — and this is the reading with the deepest theological reach — that *qawl al-ḥaqq* is connected to the description of Jesus as *a Word from God* in 3:45, where he is announced as *"a Word from Him"* (*kalimah minhu*). The third reading requires care. Since *al-Ḥaqq*, "the Truth," is one of the Beautiful Names of God, some commentators proposed that *qawl al-ḥaqq* could be rendered "the Word of God," which would make it synonymous with the *kalimah* of 3:45. Al-Ṭabarī and others registered the possibility while noting that the words in the two verses are different — *qawl* here, *kalimah* there. The connection is real but must be stated precisely: both terms affirm that Jesus came into being by a divine utterance, and neither affirms that he is that utterance's Speaker.

**The Name Used: "Jesus Son of Mary"**

The sūrah says *ʿĪsā ibnu Maryam* — "Jesus son of Mary" — and this is the Qur'an's invariable way of naming him. The formula appears some twenty-three times in the Book, and never once is Jesus called "son of God" or "son of Joseph." The insistence is deliberate, and the commentators drew out its function. By naming him always through his mother, the Qur'an does two things at once. It *honours* Mary — no prophet in the Qur'an is identified by his mother, and this is her unique distinction. And it *settles* the question of his human descent: he has a mother, and no father. The patronymic that the Qur'an uses is the one that is factually true, and it is the one that makes his birth a sign rather than a puzzle. There is a further subtlety. In a tribal society, a person without a father has no lineage, no standing, and no name. The Qur'anic formula therefore does something socially radical: it grants Jesus an honoured identity precisely through his mother, in direct contradiction of the convention that would have left him nameless. The boy whom the village would have called "son of nobody" is named by God as "son of Mary."

**"Which They Doubt"**

The closing clause is *alladhī fīhi yamtarūn* — "concerning which they doubt." The verb *imtarā* (root *m-r-y*) means to doubt, to dispute, to be in sceptical contention. Its form implies an active, ongoing doubting — not a mere absence of certainty but a state of argument. Who are "they"? The commentators answered: the Jews and the Christians. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī read it as both communities — the Jews who rejected his prophethood and slandered his mother, and the Christians who elevated him beyond his station. The Qur'an states the Christian disagreement elsewhere with precision:

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a son.' You have indeed asserted a terrible thing."* (Qur'an 19:88)

And the Jewish rejection, which took the form of denying his prophethood altogether and, in the Qur'an's account, of a grave slander against his mother — the same slander this sūrah has already dramatised and answered at vv. 27–33, which is why the narrative and the refutation belong together as one argument rather than two:

> *"And [for] their disbelief, and their uttering against Mary a tremendous calumny, and their saying, 'We killed the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, the messenger of God'..."* (Qur'an 4:156–57)

And the persistence of the disagreement, which the Qur'an records without any suggestion that it will be resolved by argument in this life — the Book's consistent position being that the differences between the communities will be judged by God on the Day when they are gathered together rather than settled by debate before it:

> *"And indeed those who differ concerning it are in doubt about it; they have no knowledge of it, only the following of conjecture."* (Qur'an 4:157)

So the sūrah's claim here is that the truth about Jesus has been obscured from both directions — denied on one side, exceeded on the other — and that the account it has just given is the one that is correct. The observation is a remarkable one for a scripture to make about its predecessors, and it explains why the Qur'an devotes so much of itself to Jesus: the disagreement is not treated as a matter of marginal interest but as the central case of a recurring human failure, which is the failure to hold a created being exactly where God placed him. The sūrah's portrait is therefore not a compromise between two positions but the restoration of a position that both had abandoned, and the fact that it is neither Jewish nor Christian in its Christology is precisely what this verse announces that it will be.

**Why the Sūrah Insists on This Now**

The placement of this verse is worth considering. The sūrah has just completed an unusually tender narrative: a frightened girl, a miraculous birth, provision in the desert, an infant who defends his mother. A reader might expect the sūrah to pause in that register. Instead it states, in the plainest terms, that this account is contested and that the doubters are wrong. The reason is that the sūrah is not telling a sentimental story. It is building the evidential basis for the argument it will make in v. 35 and again at vv. 88–95. The tenderness and the polemic are the same project. The Qur'an's account of Jesus is not a devotional embellishment attached to a theological argument; the *narrative itself* is the argument, and this verse is where the sūrah says so explicitly.

**A Note on What "Truth" Means Here**

Finally, the word *ḥaqq* deserves a note, because it is one of the most important words in the Qur'an and its meaning here is precise. *Ḥaqq* means truth, reality, right, and that which is due. It is the opposite of *bāṭil*, the false or the vain. When the Qur'an calls something *ḥaqq*, it is asserting that the thing corresponds to reality as God has made it — not merely that it is morally preferable or rhetorically persuasive. That is why the sūrah can say, without qualification, that its account of Jesus *is* the truth. It is not offering an interpretation that competes with other interpretations on equal terms. It is claiming to describe what actually happened. And the sūrah's willingness to make that claim in the strongest possible terms — against the settled positions of two large religious communities — is itself part of the Qur'an's claim about itself:

> *"And the truth (*al-ḥaqq*) We send down, and with the truth it descends."* (Qur'an 17:105)

**The Reader's Position After This Verse**

The verse performs one further function: it places the reader. Having read the narrative and now heard the sūrah's verdict, the reader is invited to decide. The sūrah will spend the remainder of its first movement explaining why the doubt is culpable rather than reasonable — v. 35 gives the logical refutation, v. 37 the historical consequence, v. 38 the eschatological exposure, v. 39 the warning, and v. 40 the closing reminder of who will inherit the earth. From here to the end of the sūrah, the tone will be harder. It is worth registering that the hardness is not a change of subject. It is the same mercy, addressing the same people, in the register of warning rather than of comfort — and the sūrah will say as much at v. 97: *"that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people."*

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:35

> **It is not for God to take a child. Glory be to Him! When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, "Be!" and it is.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Central Theological Sentence**

This is the verse the entire sūrah has been moving toward since v. 16, and it is one of the most consequential sentences in the Qur'an: *mā kāna lillāhi an yattakhidha min waladin subḥānahu idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn* — "It is not for God to take a child. Glory be to Him! When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, 'Be!' and it is." Two movements. The first denies: God does not take a child. The second explains: God creates by fiat. And the connection between them is the whole argument.

**The Grammar of Impossibility**

The opening construction is emphatic: *mā kāna lillāhi an...* — literally, "It is not *for* God to..." — a form that in Arabic denotes something that is *incompatible with who He is*. This is not a statement that God lacks the power to do something. It is a statement that the thing in question is contrary to His Perfection, and that affirming it would be a denial of His divinity. The commentators were careful on this point. Al-Rāzī notes explicitly that the verse is to be understood as asserting that such a thing is *impossible and inconceivable* — not because God is deficient in power, but because the notion is internally incoherent when applied to God. That distinction, which was discussed at v. 9 in connection with omnipotence, is essential here. Theologians of the Sunni tradition — and Ibn Taymiyyah among them — insisted that God's omnipotence is not the power to do what is self-contradictory or what His own Perfection precludes. The question "can God do this?" is only meaningful if "this" is a coherent object. "A child of the Eternal" is not one.

**The Logical Case: Why a Child Is Incompatible With God**

The Qur'an's refutations of divine sonship are not bare assertions; they rest on arguments that the Book states explicitly in several places, and the presence of actual argument is itself significant, since a scripture that merely prohibited the belief without reasoning would leave the reader with a rule rather than a conviction. Collected, they run as follows. *First, a child requires a partner or a consort, and God has none.*

> *"The Originator of the heavens and the earth — how could He have a son when He has no consort, when He created all things, and He is Knower of all things?"* (Qur'an 6:101)

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a son.' Glory be to Him! Nay, they are [but] honoured servants."* (Qur'an 21:26, on the angels)

The logic is straightforward: a child is generated from two of a kind. God has no peer, no consort, nothing "like unto Him" (*"There is nothing whatsoever like unto Him"*, 42:11), and therefore the concept of His generating offspring has nothing to operate with. The argument does not say that God *chooses* not to have a child; it says that the notion cannot be formed without importing into God the very features — likeness, pairing, dependence — that the Qur'an's theology exists to deny of Him. A claim that cannot be formed is not a claim that can be true or false, and the Qur'an treats it accordingly. *Second, a child implies need, and God is self-sufficient.*

> *"Say: He is God, the One, God the Eternal Refuge (*al-Ṣamad*). He begets not, nor was He begotten, and there is none comparable to Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

*Al-Ṣamad* denotes the One to whom all things turn and who turns to none — the absolutely Self-Sufficient, without need and without internal composition. Begetting is what a living being does in order to perpetuate itself or to express its nature; both are functions of need. God has neither. *Third, a child implies temporality and succession, and God is eternal.* A son is posterior to his father and shares his father's nature. If God had a son, the son would either be eternal — in which case there are two eternals, which is not monotheism — or originated, in which case he is a creature. There is no third option, and Christian theology's own history of attempting to articulate a third option is precisely what the Qur'an is rejecting as incoherent. *Fourth, the claim is unsupported by evidence and dishonours the Creator.*

> *"And they say, 'God has taken a son.' Glory be to Him! He is the Self-Sufficient. To Him belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth. You have no warrant for this. Do you say about God that which you do not know?"* (Qur'an 10:68)

Note the forensic character of that verse: *you have no warrant for this.* The Qur'an's objection is not only that the doctrine is incoherent but that it is asserted without proof — and that the assertion is made about God, which makes it a grave matter. *Fifth, the claim is of a piece with the worst error in the Qur'an's catalogue.* The claim that God has offspring is repeatedly listed alongside the gravest sins:

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a child.' You have indeed asserted a terrible thing."* (Qur'an 19:88–89)

And the Qur'an records the same claim in several historical forms: of the angels as God's daughters (16:57; 37:149–153; 43:15–19; 52:39); of ʿUzayr (Ezra) as the son of God by some Jews (9:30); and of Jesus by the Christians (9:30; 4:171). The sūrah addresses all of them together, since the error is one error — and the refusal to distinguish between them is itself part of the argument. A reader attached to one version of the claim will regard the others as obviously absurd, and the Qur'an's strategy is to force him to see that the version he holds is structurally identical to the ones he rejects, differing only in which creature has been placed in the position of the child.

**"Glory Be to Him" — Why the Exclamation Always Accompanies the Refutation**

The phrase *subḥānahu* — "Glory be to Him" — appears at nearly every point in the Qur'an where divine sonship is denied, and the consistency is meaningful. *Tasbīḥ* (root *s-b-ḥ*), as noted at v. 11, means to declare God free from every defect, limitation, and association with the created. So the formula is not an interjection of surprise; it is the precise technical term for the act the verse is performing. To say "God has a child" is to attribute a defect to God — the defect of dependence, of composition, of temporality — and the correct response is to declare Him free of it. That is why the Qur'anic refutations of this claim are, almost without exception, accompanied by *subḥānahu* (2:116; 4:171; 6:100; 10:68; 19:35; 21:26; 23:91; 39:4; 43:82; 112:3). The pattern is so consistent that it functions as a rule: whenever the claim is mentioned, the declaration of God's freedom from it follows immediately.

**The Second Half: Creation by Fiat**

The verse's second movement supplies the positive doctrine that replaces the false one: *idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn* — "When He decrees a thing, He only says to it, 'Be!' and it is." This is the Qur'an's standard account of creation, discussed at v. 9. Its relevance here is precise and has two aspects. *First, it explains how Jesus came to be without diminishing God.* Jesus exists by a decree — *kun* — not by generation. The sūrah has just narrated the decree's accomplishment (vv. 17–22) and is now giving its mechanism. A child by generation requires a father; a being by decree requires only the One who decrees. So the virgin birth, far from requiring the conclusion that Jesus is God's son, requires the opposite conclusion: he is a creature brought into existence by command, exactly as everything else is. *Second, it makes the general point that creation needs no partners.* If God creates by saying "Be," then there is no role for a consort, no role for an intermediary, and no role for a son. The doctrine of *kun fa-yakūn* is the doctrine of *tawḥīd* stated in the language of cosmology: one Will, one decree, one Creator. The same formula recurs across the Qur'an — at 2:117; 3:47 (in the annunciation to Mary); 3:59 (specifically about Jesus); 6:73; 16:40; 36:82; 40:68 — and it appears in this very sūrah at v. 9 as well as here. The sūrah is thus framing its entire argument with one sentence repeated at both ends, and the framing is exact: at v. 9 the formula establishes that God can create what has not existed, and at v. 35 it establishes that the same God does not beget. Between those two claims lies everything the chapter has to say — every birth it narrates, every impossibility it dissolves, and every excess it refuses.

**Theological Boundaries: What the Verse Is Not Saying**

Three clarifications are necessary, because the verse is frequently misread by both critics and defenders. *It is not a denial of Jesus' honour.* Islam holds Jesus to be the Messiah, a messenger of God, His Word, a spirit from Him, born of a virgin, supported by the Holy Spirit, a worker of miracles by God's permission, and one of the five greatest messengers (*ulu al-ʿazm*). Denying that God has a child is not a demotion of Jesus; it is a protection of the doctrine of God, which Islam holds to be the more fundamental of the two. *It is not a claim about God's power.* As stressed above, "it is not for God to" is not "God cannot." Theologians were emphatic: God's power is absolute over everything that is possible, and the claim about a son is not in that category. *It is not an attack on Christians as people.* The Qur'an addresses the claim, and it addresses it with argument. Its tone toward the People of the Book elsewhere is strikingly generous — *"And you will find the nearest of them in affection to the believers those who say, 'We are Christians'"* (5:82) — and it commands Muslims to dispute with them *"in the fairest manner"* (29:46). The polemic in this sūrah is aimed at a doctrine, not at a community, and the distinction has been maintained by responsible Muslim writers throughout.

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter**

Verse 35 is the theological keystone of Sūrah Maryam. Everything before it has been preparation: the story of John established that God creates outside ordinary means; the story of Mary established that He creates without a father; the infant's speech established that Jesus is a servant who will die and be raised. Now the sūrah states the conclusion that all of it was assembled to support. And the sūrah is not finished with the argument. It will return to it at vv. 88–95 with the fiercest language in the chapter — the heavens nearly rent, the earth split, the mountains falling in ruins at the claim — so that no reader can mistake the gravity with which the Qur'an regards this doctrine. The claim is, in the Qur'an's view, not a minor difference of formulation but a distortion of the most serious kind, because it concerns who God is.

**What This Means for the Believer**

For the ordinary Muslim, the practical content of this verse is the doctrine of *tawḥīd* in its purest form, and it carries two consequences. The first is intellectual: the believer is required to hold a conception of God that is free of every created feature — no body, no need, no parentage, no composition, no likeness. The sūrah will state the same requirement negatively at v. 65, *"Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?"* — and positively at v. 93, *"There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the Compassionate as a servant."* The second is affective: the declaration *subḥānahu* is meant to be said. It is on the tongue of every Muslim in *rukūʿ* and *sujūd* — *subḥāna rabbiya al-aʿlā*, *subḥāna rabbiya al-ʿaẓīm* — and it is one of the four formulas the Prophet ﷺ described as most beloved to God:

> *"The most beloved speech to God is four: Glory be to God (*subḥān Allāh*), praise be to God (*al-ḥamdu lillāh*), there is no god but God (*lā ilāha illā Allāh*), and God is Greatest (*Allāhu akbar*)."* (Muslim 2137)

To recite *subḥān Allāh* is, in other words, to be doing exactly what this verse commands: declaring God free of the defect that the claim of a child would attribute to Him. The word *subḥān* carries the sense of distance — of removing God to the remove at which He belongs — and it is the standard idiom of the Qur'an whenever a creature is described in terms too small for the Creator. This is why the phrase appears so often at precisely the moments when something unworthy has been said about God, and why the believer who says it in his prayer is not merely repeating a formula but taking up the argument of this sūrah on his own tongue.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:36

> **"Truly God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him. This is a straight path."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whose Words Are These?**

The verse as it appears in the standard reading is a continuation of the infant's speech: *wa-inna Allāha rabbī wa-rabbukum fa-ʿbudūhu hādhā ṣirāṭun mustaqīm* — "Truly God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him. This is a straight path." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabrisī record this as the majority reading: the sentence continues the words Jesus spoke from the cradle (vv. 30–33), and its first clause is nearly identical to words attributed to Jesus elsewhere:

> *"I said not to them except what You commanded me: 'Worship God, my Lord and your Lord.' And I was a witness over them as long as I was among them..."* (Qur'an 5:117)

There is, however, a second reading, also recorded by al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabrisī, which takes v. 36 as resuming from v. 34 — so that it would be rendered: *"That is Jesus son of Mary, a statement of the truth... and that is God, my Lord and your Lord."* On this reading the verse is the sūrah's own address to the reader rather than Jesus' address to his people. Both readings are defensible, and the difference between them is not doctrinally significant. What matters is the content, which is identical on either reading: *God is my Lord and your Lord; worship Him.*

**"My Lord and Your Lord" — The Levelling Formula**

The phrase *rabbī wa-rabbukum* is one of the most important formulas in the Qur'an's theology of prophecy, and it deserves close attention, because the equality it asserts is the whole of what a prophet is sent to establish. The speaker places himself and his audience in a single grammatical category: one Lord, and two parties who stand before Him on the same footing. Whatever else a prophet is — however high his station, however undeniable his miracles — he is not on the far side of that line, and the formula exists precisely to keep him where he belongs. It appears in the mouths of several prophets. Jesus says it here and in 5:117; and in another passage his followers are made to say it in the same terms:

> *"And God said: 'O Jesus, son of Mary! Did you say to mankind, "Take me and my mother as gods apart from God?"' He said: 'Glory be to You! It is not for me to say what I have no right to. Had I said it, You would have known it... I said not to them except what You commanded me: "Worship God, my Lord and your Lord."'"* (Qur'an 5:116–17)

The formula's function is to place the prophet and his audience on the same side of a single line. The prophet is not the object of worship; he is a fellow-worshipper. He has a Lord, and that Lord is the same Lord his hearers have. This is why the Qur'an attaches so much weight to it. The claim to divinity, or to participation in divinity, is precisely the claim that some people have a different relationship to God than others do — that the prophet is on the other side of the line. The formula denies that in nine Arabic words. *My Lord and your Lord* — one Lord, two servants, no difference in kind. The same formula appears with reference to Moses (*"Verily I am your Lord"*, 20:12, spoken by God) and, most famously, in the address to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ:

> *"Say: O mankind! Verily I am the messenger of God to you all — [the messenger of] Him to Whom belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth. There is no god but He; He gives life and causes death. So believe in God and His messenger, the unlettered prophet, who believes in God and His words; and follow him, that you may be guided."* (Qur'an 7:158)

**"So Worship Him" — The Consequence**

The second clause is the necessary conclusion: *fa-ʿbudūhu* — "so worship Him." The particle *fa* indicates consequence: *because* He is my Lord and yours, *therefore* worship Him — and Him alone. The logic is the standard Qur'anic syllogism and is worth stating explicitly, because it is the engine of every prophetic call in the Book. What is worth noticing is that the conclusion is not an additional command bolted onto the premise; it is the premise's own content. To acknowledge someone as Lord and then worship another is not to disobey a second rule but to contradict the first statement — which is why the Qur'an can move from theology to obligation in a single particle, and why the call of every prophet begins with the former rather than the latter: The syllogism runs in three steps. First, there is one God, who is Lord of everything. Second, worship is owed to the Lord. Third, therefore worship is owed to Him alone. Nothing in the argument depends on revelation supplying a premise the mind could not reach on its own, and the Qur'an repeatedly appeals to just this reasoning — which is why the polytheists in the Qur'an are so rarely accused of failing to reason, and so often accused of reasoning badly or of abandoning their own conclusions under social pressure. And note that the command is addressed *by* Jesus *to* those who would later worship him. The sūrah has arranged for the refutation of the incarnation to be delivered by the person the incarnation is claimed about, in the cradle, before anyone could put words in his mouth. The commentators drew out the polemical force: the command *"worship Him"* is an instruction that those who worship Jesus should worship God instead, who is Jesus' Lord as well as their own (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī).

**"This Is a Straight Path"**

The closing phrase, *hādhā ṣirāṭun mustaqīm* — "This is a straight path" — uses one of the most loaded terms in the Qur'an. *Ṣirāṭ* denotes a way, a road, a course. *Mustaqīm* (root *q-w-m*) means straight, upright, standing, without crookedness or deviation. The phrase thus describes a way that does not bend — and by implication, one that reaches its destination. The term's most famous occurrence is in the Fātiḥah, the prayer every Muslim recites in every unit of every prayer:

> *"Guide us upon the straight path (*al-ṣirāṭa al-mustaqīm*) — the path of those upon whom You have bestowed favour, not of those who have earned wrath, nor of those who go astray."* (Qur'an 1:6–7)

The commentators noted the connection and drew the conclusion: the path Jesus calls people to is the same path the Fātiḥah asks for, which is the same path every prophet called to. There is one path, and the differences between the prophetic dispensations are differences of law, not of destination. The Qur'an states this unity as a command to the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And verily this is My straight path, so follow it; and follow not other paths, for they will separate you from His way. That is what He has enjoined upon you, that you may be reverent."* (Qur'an 6:153)

And it describes the straight path as the religion of all the prophets in a single verse, which settles the question of whether the messages differed in their substance. What differed was the law given to each community; what never differed was the path itself. The Qur'an's insistence on the point is what allows it to speak of a single religion running from the first prophet to the last rather than of a sequence of unrelated dispensations:

> *"He has ordained for you of religion what He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to thee, and what We enjoined upon Abraham and Moses and Jesus: establish the religion, and be not divided therein."* (Qur'an 42:13)

**Why "Straight" Rather Than "Easy"**

There is a small but important point in the choice of adjective. The path is described as *straight*, not as *easy, broad, or comfortable*. The distinction matters, and the Prophet ﷺ made it explicitly in a ḥadīth that has been repeated in every generation of Islamic teaching — repeated so often, in fact, precisely because it corrects an expectation that forms almost immediately in anyone who hears about a path. The word *mustaqīm* denotes directness, the absence of deviation, and says nothing at all about width, gradient, or ease. A straight road can be narrow and steep, and the quality that makes it the right road is that it arrives:

> *"The Garden is surrounded by hardships, and the Fire is surrounded by desires."* (Muslim 2822)

And in the famous image, which the scholars preserved not as a curiosity but as a working description of how a believer is meant to picture his own position — a line, with calls coming off it from either side, and the person's whole task being to stay on the line rather than to work out an adjudication between the calls:

> *"God has set forth a parable: a straight path, with walls on either side and open gates, with curtains hanging over the gates, and a caller at the head of the path calling: 'O mankind, enter the path all of you and do not deviate!'..."* (al-Tirmidhī 2859; Aḥmad; graded ḥasan)

The path is straight, which means it is unambiguous and it arrives. It is not necessarily pleasant, and it is certainly not wide. Its straightness is its truth, not its comfort — and the sūrah, which will warn in the next verses of a "Day of Regret," is not promising otherwise. The sequence is deliberate: the sūrah establishes the path in this verse and immediately follows it with the urgency of the warning, because a path that has been described honestly is a path that can be urged without misleading anyone. The Qur'an nowhere recruits by promising comfort, and the absence of that promise is one of the things that makes its call credible to a person who has noticed how rarely the world's other offers describe themselves in the same terms.

**The Verse's Function in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 36 completes the sūrah's case about Jesus in three coordinated moves, and it is worth seeing them together, because the order in which they are made is the order in which the sūrah's whole Christology has been built: first the identity of the Lord, then the obligation that follows from it, then the identification of the path. Nothing in the sequence is optional, and nothing in it requires a reader to accept a premise he has not been shown:

- *V. 30:* I am a servant of God. (His status.)
- *V. 33:* I will die and be raised. (His mortality.)
- *V. 35:* It is not for God to take a child. (The doctrine about God.)
- *V. 36:* Worship God — my Lord and yours. (The consequence for the hearer.)

Each is a distinct claim, and they are mutually supporting. A servant is not worshipped. One who dies is not God. God does not take a child. Therefore worship God alone. There is no gap in the argument, and it is delivered by the person whom the argument concerns. What follows in vv. 37–40 is the sūrah's account of what happened *after*: the sects differed, the doubters persisted, and the Day of Regret waits. The sūrah's movement from the cradle to the Resurrection in five verses is one of the most compressed transitions in the Qur'an, and it is deliberate. The story of Jesus is not a story with a moral attached; it is a story with a Judgement attached.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:37

> **Yet the parties differed among themselves, and woe unto those who disbelieve for the witnessing of a tremendous day!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Parties" — Who Differed, and About What**

The sūrah now reports the historical consequence: *fa-ikhtalafa al-aḥzābu min baynihim* — "Yet the parties differed among themselves." The word *aḥzāb* (singular *ḥizb*) means parties, factions, groups — those who band together around a position. It is the same word used for the confederate armies that besieged Madinah (33:9–22) and for the factions that the Qur'an repeatedly forbids the believers to become (*"and be not of those who associate partners with Him — those who have split their religion and become sects, each party rejoicing in what it has"*, 30:31–32). Who are the parties here, and what did they differ about? The commentators gave two answers, and they are not incompatible. The first is that they are the Jews and the Christians, who differ with each other over the status of Jesus — the Jews rejecting his prophethood and the Christians making him divine (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī). The second, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Zamakhsharī, is that they are the Christian sects themselves — the Jacobites and the Nestorians being named specifically, since these were the Christological parties dominant in the Near East at the time of the revelation. The Jacobites (Miaphysites) held to a single united nature in Christ; the Nestorians held to two natures and two persons; and the Chalcedonian orthodoxy of Byzantium held to two natures in one person. The differences were, and in some communions remain, matters of the most intense doctrinal and political consequence. The sūrah does not adjudicate between these positions. It states the fact of the division and moves to the judgement, and that silence is itself significant: from the Qur'an's vantage, the entire spectrum of post-biblical Christological dispute is conducted on ground the Qur'an has already rejected. Whether one says "one nature" or "two natures," the Qur'an's answer is the same — *he is a servant of God* (v. 30) — and the differences within a mistaken framework are, from that vantage, differences about the wrong question. This is not a hostile observation so much as a diagnosis, and the Qur'an makes it about every community:

> *"And this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so be reverent toward Me. But they split their affair among themselves into sects, each party rejoicing in what it has."* (Qur'an 23:52–53)

**The Prophetic Warning About Division**

The Islamic tradition took the warning about sectarian division with great seriousness, and the Prophet ﷺ addressed it directly, repeatedly, and in terms that left no doubt about where he placed the matter. He is reported to have said — and the report is one the scholars cited whenever the community's disagreements threatened to become the community's defining feature:

> *"The Jews split into seventy-one sects, and the Christians split into seventy-two sects, and this community will split into seventy-three sects — all of them in the Fire except one."* They said: "Who are they, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Those upon what I and my Companions are upon."* (Abū Dāwūd 4596; al-Tirmidhī 2640; graded ḥasan)

The ḥadīth is transmitted through several chains and is graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī and others, though some scholars have discussed particular transmitters in its chains. Its content, however, is corroborated by the Qur'an's own repeated warnings against division, and it has functioned throughout Islamic history as the community's standing self-criticism. Note what the ḥadīth identifies as the saved group: not the largest, not the most powerful, not the newest, but **"those upon what I and my Companions are upon"** — that is, the group defined by adherence to the original teaching rather than by any sociological characteristic. That is a devastating criterion, because it cannot be claimed by self-declaration; it has to be measured.

**"Woe Unto Those Who Disbelieve"**

The second half of the verse pronounces judgement: *fa-waylun lilladhīna kafarū min mashhadi yawmin ʿaẓīm* — "and woe unto those who disbelieve for the witnessing of a tremendous day!" The word *wayl* is one of the severest terms in the Qur'an's vocabulary of warning. The philologists derived it from a root meaning *destruction, ruin, woe*; some of the early authorities described it as a valley in Hell, though the more precise account is that it is an imprecation of ruin. It appears most often in the formula *waylun li...* — "woe to..." — and it is used with restraint in the Qur'an, which makes its appearance here notable. Who are *those who disbelieve* in this context? On the sūrah's own terms, they are the parties who differed about Jesus — those who rejected him and those who exceeded the truth about him. Both are covered by the warning, and that symmetry is one of the sūrah's most important features. The Qur'an does not excuse one error in order to condemn the other.

**"The Witnessing of a Tremendous Day"**

The final phrase is dense: *min mashhadi yawmin ʿaẓīm* — "for the witnessing of a tremendous day." *Mashhad* (root *sh-h-d*, the root of *shahādah*, witness and testimony) most literally denotes the *time and place* of witnessing — the scene, the assembly, the occasion at which something is witnessed. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that it denotes the time and place of the reckoning and judgement, and that the verse may thus allude to the sheer terror that the *setting* itself will induce. What is witnessed on that Day? The commentators, drawing on the Qur'an's own account, list several things, and they accumulate: *The witnessing of the reckoning itself.* Every soul will see its own record. *"And We have fastened every man's omen to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a book which he will find spread open: 'Read your book! Your own soul suffices this day as a reckoner against you'"* (17:13–14). *The testimony of the angels and the prophets.* The prophets will be asked what their communities did with their messages: *"And [mention] the Day when God will gather the messengers and say: 'What response did you receive?'"* (5:109). *The testimony of the leaders of misguidance* — those who led others astray, and the reciprocal cursing of the followers and their leaders (2:166–67; 33:67–68), in which the one who was followed disowns the one who followed him and the one who followed wishes he had never done so. *And most startlingly, the testimony of the sinner's own body*, which is the element the tradition has dwelt on most, because it removes the last remaining place where a person might have kept something to himself:

> *"On the Day when their tongues, their hands, and their feet will bear witness against them concerning what they used to do."* (Qur'an 24:24)

> *"This Day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will bear witness to what they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

> *"Until, when they reach it, their hearing, their eyes, and their skins will bear witness against them concerning what they used to do."* (Qur'an 41:20)

That last verse is often cited by the commentators in connection with *mashhad*, and it supplies the most vivid content for the word. The Day is called a *witnessing* because nothing will be hidden and nothing will need to be argued — the evidence will be given by the parties themselves, including by their own limbs. The commentators drew out what this means: the Day is not called a witnessing because God requires evidence, since He knows the matter already, but because the person will be confronted with what he did from a source he cannot dispute, having supplied it himself. The point of the testimony is not to inform the Judge but to close the mouth of the accused — which is why the Qur'an describes the sinners' response to their own limbs as one of protest rather than denial:

**The Terror of Exposure Rather Than of Punishment**

There is a psychological precision in the sūrah's choice of emphasis that deserves notice. The warning is not, in the first instance, about the *pain* of that Day. It is about the *exposure*. Most people can contemplate punishment abstractly. What they cannot contemplate is the removal of every private thing — every hidden motive, every secret act, every unspoken thought — into full public view before the assembly of all creation, in the presence of the One who knows it already. The Qur'an's descriptions of the Day return again and again to this dimension:

> *"On that Day you will be exposed; not one secret of yours will remain hidden."* (Qur'an 69:18)

> *"And they will be presented before your Lord in ranks: 'Now you have come to Us as We created you the first time...'"* (Qur'an 18:48)

The Prophet ﷺ referred to this in a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, in which he mentioned the one whom God will cover in this world and then expose in the Hereafter — and the ḥadīth is among the most frequently cited in the entire corpus on the ethics of concealment, because it states the reciprocity in the plainest possible terms. What a person does with another's fault is what God will do with his:

> *"A servant who conceals [the faults of] a servant in this world, God will conceal him on the Day of Resurrection... and whoever exposes the fault of his Muslim brother, God will expose his fault until He disgraces him with it in his own house."* (Muslim 2590)

The sūrah's warning thus has a direct ethical corollary that runs in the opposite direction to instinct: the person who covers the faults of others is the person who will be covered on the Day when everything is uncovered. The corollary runs against instinct, because the natural movement when a person's fault becomes known is to pass the information on, and the natural justification is that it is true. The tradition's answer is that truthfulness is not the relevant category. The fault's being real is exactly why covering it is a mercy rather than a falsehood, and the person who repeats it is not adding information to the world but adding exposure to a person.

**The Transition to the Next Verse**

Verse 37 declares that the Day will be *witnessed*. Verse 38 will describe the sensory acuity with which the disbelievers will witness it — *"How well they will hear and how well they will see on the Day they come unto Us"* — and verse 39 will name it the *Day of Regret*. The three verses form a single escalating movement: the scene, the perception, and the emotional result. What binds them is the word *mashhad*. In v. 37 it is the *witnessing*; in v. 38 it is the *seeing and hearing*; in v. 39 it is the *regret* at what is seen. The sūrah is not repeating itself. It is walking the reader through the experience in order.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:38

> **How well they will hear and how well they will see on the Day they come unto Us. But the wrongdoers, today, are in manifest error.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grammar of Astonishment**

The verse opens with an Arabic construction that has no smooth English equivalent: *asmiʿ bihim wa-abṣir yawma yaʾtūnanā* — "How well they will hear and how well they will see on the Day they come unto Us!" The forms *asmiʿ* and *abṣir* are imperatives in form but are used here in the classical idiom of *taʿajjub* — the expression of wonder. The sense is not "make them hear" but "how perfectly they will hear!" — an exclamation about the quality of their perception, as though the speaker is marvelling at the contrast with what has gone before. It is worth noting that the same construction is used in the Qur'an at 2:20 in the opposite direction, of the lightning that *"well-nigh snatches away their sight"* — and the rhetorical effect is the same in both places: the reader is being made to *feel* the strangeness rather than merely to be told it.

**The Reversal: From Spiritual Deafness to Physical Clarity**

The commentators were agreed on what is being reversed. Al-Ṭabrisī and al-Ṭabarī explained: on the Day of Resurrection, the disbelievers will hear and see keenly — *in contrast to* their spiritual blindness and deafness in earthly life toward the signs of God and the teachings of the scriptures and the prophets. That contrast is one of the Qur'an's most consistent themes. Throughout the Book, the disbelievers' failure to respond to revelation is described in sensory terms:

> *"Deaf, dumb, and blind — they do not understand."* (Qur'an 2:171)

> *"And We have certainly created for Hell many of the jinn and mankind: they have hearts with which they do not understand, they have eyes with which they do not see, and they have ears with which they do not hear. Those are like cattle; nay, they are further astray. Those are the heedless."* (Qur'an 7:179)

> *"And whosoever is blind in this life will be blind in the Hereafter, and further astray from the path."* (Qur'an 17:72)

The Qur'an is emphatic that this is not a physical defect, and the insistence matters because the alternative reading would make the verse's warning pointless. The people in question have working eyes and working ears; what is missing is the act of *attending* to what the senses deliver. The distinction is one the Qur'an makes over and over, and it is the difference between a person who cannot see and a person who will not look — the first is a misfortune, and the second is a decision. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The example of guidance and knowledge with which God has sent me is like abundant rain falling on the earth... [and the example of the one who hears and understands is like the one who benefits from the water]."* (al-Bukhārī 79; Muslim 2282)

The failure is in the reception, not in the organ. And that is precisely what makes it culpable, which is why the Qur'an consistently frames it as a moral condition rather than an intellectual misfortune. The reason is straightforward: a defect of the organ would excuse the person, and a defect of the will does not. What is being described is not a failure of evidence but a failure of attention, and attention is something a person directs. This is why the Qur'an so often pairs the description with a moral accusation rather than with an invitation to investigate further — the problem was never that the signs were insufficient:

**The Removal of the Cover**

The most explicit Qur'anic statement of the reversal is in Sūrah Qāf, and the commentators cited it in connection with this verse, because it supplies in a single line the mechanism by which a person who has spent a lifetime not seeing arrives at a Day on which he cannot stop seeing:

> *"You were indeed heedless of this. Now We have removed from you your cover; so today your sight is piercing."* (Qur'an 50:22)

Two things happen at once in that verse, and it is worth separating them because they are not the same event. The *cover* — the veil of heedlessness, self-deception, and preoccupation — is removed, which is something done to the person. And the sight becomes *ḥadīd*, piercing, sharp as iron, which is a change in the person. That is exactly the state described in our verse. The disbelievers on that Day will see everything, and the removal of the veil is not a reward but part of the exposure. The problem is that seeing it then does not help, because sight was never what was missing — and what was missing cannot be supplied by a Day, since a Day does not contain the interval in which it could have been chosen.

**Why Seeing Too Late Does Not Help**

This is the hardest and most important point in the verse, and it is worth stating plainly. The Qur'an is explicit that perception arriving after the deadline confers no benefit. Faith at the point of death is rejected; belief at the Resurrection is useless; and the reason is not vindictiveness but the nature of the thing being tested. A test is an exercise conducted under conditions of uncertainty, and certainty is not a state in which the thing being assessed can be performed at all. A person who believes when the veil has been lifted is not believing; he is assenting to what has become unavoidable, and assent of that kind carries none of the weight the test was designed to measure. This is why the Qur'an's descriptions of deathbed faith are so consistently severe, and why the tradition has always treated the moment of dying as the closure of the examination rather than as a last opportunity to sit it:

> *"But their faith, when they saw Our punishment, did not benefit them — the practice of God, which has passed concerning His servants. And thereupon the disbelievers were lost."* (Qur'an 40:85)

> *"The Day when some of the signs of your Lord come, no soul will benefit from its faith if it had not believed before, or earned good through its faith."* (Qur'an 6:158)

The logic is coherent and worth spelling out, because it directly answers the most common objection to the doctrine of judgement. The earthly life is a test conducted *under conditions of partial evidence*. God has made Himself known through signs, scriptures, and messengers — sufficiently that the refusal to believe is culpable, but not so overwhelmingly that belief is coerced. That is the whole design: *"And had your Lord willed, all those on earth would have believed, all of them together. Would you then compel mankind until they are believers?"* (10:99). It follows that when the veil is removed and the evidence becomes total, the test is over — not because God has changed, but because the exercise that constituted the test has concluded. A student who is handed the answer key after the examination does not thereby pass; the examination is finished. That is why the sūrah's exclamation in this verse is an exclamation of *wonder* rather than of good news. They will see perfectly. And it will not save them.

**A Different Blindness: The One That Endures**

The commentators noted a subtlety that is easy to miss. There are two distinct metaphors of blindness in the Qur'an, and they apply to different things. The first is the blindness of *this life* — spiritual deafness and blindness to the signs, which is removed at death and at the Resurrection. That is the blindness in view in our verse, and it is healed. The second is the blindness that is *given* in the Hereafter to those who were spiritually blind in this life, as a permanent condition and a testimony against them:

> *"And whoever turns away from My remembrance — for him is a straitened life, and We shall raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection. He will say: 'My Lord, why have You raised me blind, when I was seeing?' He will say: 'Thus Our signs came to you and you forgot them; and thus today you are forgotten.'"* (Qur'an 20:124–26)

The two are not contradictory. The first says: on that Day you will perceive the reality you denied. The second says: you will be raised in a condition that *manifests* the condition you chose. Both are true, and both are forms of the same principle — that the Hereafter makes visible what the earthly life concealed. The reconciliation is therefore not a matter of choosing between two readings but of recognising that the veil and the condition are the same fact seen from two sides. A person who has spent his life declining to look has, by exactly that, built the condition in which he will be raised; and the removal of the cover does not change him but reveals him, which is why the Qur'an can describe the same event as both an exposure and an arrival.

**"But the Wrongdoers, Today, Are in Manifest Error"**

The verse's second sentence turns abruptly from the future to the present: *wa-lākin al-ẓālimūna al-yawma fī ḍalālin mubīn* — "But the wrongdoers, today, are in manifest error." The word *al-yawma*, "today," means *now* — in this life, in the present moment of their existence. The sūrah is making a contrast in a single line: *then* they will see with perfect clarity; *now* they are in error so evident that it should be unmistakable. Two words carry the weight. *Ẓālimūn* — "wrongdoers" — from the root *ẓ-l-m*, meaning to wrong, to put something in the wrong place, to darken. In the Qur'an's usage, *ẓulm* encompasses both wronging others and, most seriously, wronging oneself by *shirk*: *"Verily shirk is a tremendous wrong (*ẓulm*)"* (31:13). And *ḍalāl mubīn* — "manifest error" — meaning error that is clear, evident, open. The point is that the error is not subtle. It is not the kind of mistake an honest person makes after careful thought. The sūrah has spent thirty-seven verses establishing that the signs are plain, the message is clear, and the truth about Jesus has been stated by the infant himself. To be in error after all of that is to be in error *without excuse*. That is a hard saying, and it should be stated carefully. The Qur'an's insistence that the error is "manifest" is a claim about the *availability* of the truth, not a claim about the psychology of every individual who has failed to arrive at it. The tradition has always distinguished between the objective clarity of the message and the subjective situation of the person who has not received it — a distinction the Qur'an itself makes:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"Whoever is guided is guided only for [the good of] his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only against it. And no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another."* (Qur'an 17:15)

**The Warning's Practical Edge**

For the reader, the verse delivers a specific and uncomfortable message, and it is not primarily about the Hereafter. It is about the gap between what a person *can* perceive and what a person *does* perceive. The disbelievers in this verse are not described as lacking evidence. They are described as having it and not attending to it. And the Qur'an's diagnosis of why is consistent throughout: preoccupation, conformity, arrogance, and the preference for a conclusion one likes over a conclusion one does not. The remedy the tradition prescribes is equally consistent, and it is not intellectual. It is the removal of the cover — the *ghaflah*, the heedlessness — through remembrance. The sūrah will name that condition explicitly in the very next verse: *"while they are in a state of heedlessness and believe not."* The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical measure of it in a ḥadīth:

> *"The difference between the one who remembers his Lord and the one who does not is like the difference between the living and the dead."* (al-Bukhārī 6407; Muslim 779)

That is the sūrah's point in a sentence, and it is the point on which the whole passage turns. The organs work either way: the disbeliever's eyes function perfectly in this life and will function more perfectly still on the Day. The question is whether the person is awake — and wakefulness, in the Qur'an's usage, is not an intellectual attainment but a moral one, produced by attention and maintained by it. This is why the sūrah places the warning here, immediately after the account of the path, and why it chooses the language of hearing and seeing rather than the language of argument. What is being asked for is not a conclusion but an act of looking.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:39

> **And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will have been decreed, while they are in a state of heedlessness and believe not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day That Has Only One Name in the Qur'an**

The sūrah instructs the Prophet ﷺ: *wa-andhirhum yawma al-ḥasrati idh quḍiya al-amru wa-hum fī ghaflatin wa-hum lā yuʾminūn* — "And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will have been decreed, while they are in a state of heedlessness and believe not." *Yawm al-ḥasrah*, "the Day of Regret," is a name for the Day of Resurrection that occurs nowhere else in the Qur'an in this exact form. That uniqueness gives the phrase a peculiar force: the sūrah is not describing one aspect of that Day among many, but naming the entire Day by its dominant emotional character. *Ḥasrah* (root *ḥ-s-r*) denotes a grief that is sharpened by the knowledge that something is now unrecoverable. It is the word for regret in its most acute form — not merely sorrow, but sorrow *at having lost a chance that cannot come again*. The lexicographers defined it as a grief over what has passed out of one's hands. The Qur'an uses the root elsewhere to convey exactly this:

> *"And you will see them brought before it [the Fire], humbled by abasement, looking with a furtive glance. And those who believe will say: 'Verily the losers are those who have lost themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection.' Unquestionably, the wrongdoers are in lasting punishment."* (Qur'an 42:45)

> *"And those who disbelieved will say [on that Day]: 'O woe to us, for our heedlessness concerning it! Nay, we were wrongdoers.'"* (Qur'an 6:31 — using a closely related construction)

And the classic description of the regret itself, in a verse the commentators quoted whenever this sūrah's warning was discussed, since it supplies what the word *ḥasrah* actually consists of — not a feeling imposed from outside but a recognition arrived at too late to be acted upon:

> *"And those who followed will say: 'If only we had another turn, then we would disassociate ourselves from them as they have disassociated themselves from us.' Thus will God show them their deeds as regrets (*ḥasarāt*) upon them, and they will never leave the Fire."* (Qur'an 2:167)

**What the Regret Consists In**

The commentators gave a specific and often-repeated account of what produces this regret, and it is transmitted from the early authorities through al-Ṭabarī. On that Day, those whose disbelief and evil deeds have earned them the Fire will first be shown the dwellings in the Garden that had been prepared for them *had they been believers* — and then those dwellings will be given to the believers who took their place. The sight of what was available, followed by the knowledge that it is now irrevocably another's, is the *ḥasrah*. The Prophet ﷺ described the transaction in a ḥadīth transmitted by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"There is no one among you but that he has his place in the Fire and his place in the Garden."* They said: "O Messenger of God, then why should we do anything?" He said: *"Do deeds, for everyone is facilitated toward what he was created for."* Then he recited: *"As for him who gives and is reverent, and believes in the fairest — We shall ease his way unto ease..."* (al-Bukhārī 4948; Muslim 2647)

And in another transmission, which the scholars noted for adding the element of the plea — the request for a second interval, made at the point where intervals have run out, and recorded in the Qur'an precisely so that a person still inside his own interval can hear what it sounds like from the other side:

> *"The people of the Fire will be shown their places in the Garden, and it will be said to them: 'Had you believed and done righteous deeds, you would have inherited this.' Then regret will befall them."* (reported in the commentaries on this verse; cf. al-Bukhārī 6574 on the inheritance of the people of the Garden from the people of the Fire)

The logic is worth stating plainly, because it is the sūrah's most pointed piece of psychology. Regret is proportional to the gap between what was possible and what was chosen. The greater the offer that was declined, the greater the grief at having declined it. That is why the Day is named for regret rather than for pain: the torment is not only in what is suffered but in what is known to have been forfeited. Anyone who has declined an opportunity and watched someone else take it has felt a faint version of this. The sūrah is describing that feeling without the possibility of consolation.

**"When the Matter Will Have Been Decreed"**

The second phrase is *idh quḍiya al-amru* — "when the matter will have been decreed." The verb *quḍiya* is passive perfect: the matter will *have been* settled, finished, decided. The same or similar phrases appear at several points in the Qur'an to mark the arrival of the Last Day and the Judgement. *"Do they await anything but that God should come to them in the shadows of clouds, and the angels — and the matter is decreed?"* (2:210). *"And they say: 'Why has an angel not been sent down to him?' But had We sent down an angel, the matter would have been decreed, and then they would not be reprieved"* (6:8). *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decreed: 'Verily God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you...'"* (14:22). What makes the matter "decreed" at that point? The sūrah's answer is implied in its own argument: at that moment the opportunity to repent and mend one's ways in a manner that would change one's fate has closed. That is the precise meaning, and it is worth being exact about it, because the tradition has been careful here. Repentance remains open to a human being until death — or, more precisely in the classical formulation, until the soul reaches the throat, or until the sun rises from the west. The Qur'an states the closing of the door in terms of *timing*, not of God's willingness:

> *"Repentance is not [accepted] for those who do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says: 'Now I repent' — nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:18)

And the Prophet ﷺ said — in a ḥadīth that supplies the practical corollary to everything above, since it tells the believer what the interval is actually for and what a person is meant to be doing with the period during which the exercise is still running:

> *"God accepts the repentance of His servant as long as the soul has not reached the throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537; Ibn Mājah 4253; graded ḥasan)

And, in a ḥadīth that describes the final closing — the point at which the interval ends not by a decision made on the Day but by a condition that has been reached, so that what shuts is not a door someone closed but a period that has simply elapsed:

> *"Whoever repents before the sun rises from its west, God will accept his repentance."* (Muslim 2703)

So the "decree" of this verse is not God's change of heart. It is the end of the appointed interval — the closure of the only period during which the exercise was taking place. The commentators were careful about this, because reading the verse as a shift in God's disposition would make the Divine will unstable and the warning arbitrary. What is described is instead the arrival of a term that was set from the beginning, and the term is not punitive in itself: it is simply what a bounded period does, which is end. The tragedy is not that the door was slammed but that the person standing outside it was the one who decided not to walk through it.

**"The Slaughter of Death": The Ḥadīth Associated With This Verse**

The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, associated with this verse one of the most striking ḥadīths in the entire corpus — the narration of the slaughter of death. It is recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, and its placement here is exact, since the ḥadīth is the tradition's most developed account of what happens *after* the interval has closed, when regret is no longer productive and the parties have been separated for good:

> *"Death will be brought on the Day of Resurrection in the form of a white ram and it will be made to stand between the Garden and the Fire.
