Then it will be said: 'O people of the Garden, do you recognise this?' They will raise their heads and look, and say: 'Yes, it is death.' And it will be said: 'O people of the Fire, do you recognise this?' They will raise their heads and look, and say: 'Yes, it is death.' Then the command will be given, and it will be slaughtered. Then it will be said: 'O people of the Garden, eternity and no death! O people of the Fire, eternity and no death!'"*

The Prophet ﷺ then recited — and the recitation is the report's own interpretation, which is why the narrators preserved it rather than leaving the parable to speak for itself. The verse supplies the meaning, and the meaning is that the warning being quoted is the warning that will in fact have been delivered:

> *"And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will have been decreed, while they are in a state of heedlessness and believe not."* (al-Bukhārī 4730; Muslim 2849, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The connection to the verse is explicit in the narration itself, and its meaning is exact. For the people of the Fire, the slaughter of death removes the last possible ending. There is no cessation, no fading, no relief — and therefore no possibility that the regret will ever grow dull. That is what makes the Day the Day of Regret: the grief is not only intense but permanent. For the people of the Garden, the same event is the removal of the last possible anxiety — that even this could end. The Prophet ﷺ described it by reciting the verse that follows:

> *"And warn them of the Day of Regret..."* — and the people of the Garden are told: *"eternity, and no death."*

**"While They Are in a State of Heedlessness"**

The third phrase is *wa-hum fī ghaflatin* — "while they are in a state of heedlessness." *Ghaflah* (root *gh-f-l*) means heedlessness, inattention, unawareness — the state of a person who is not thinking about what matters. It is one of the Qur'an's central diagnostic terms, and it is used consistently to describe the condition of those who are warned and do not respond:

> *"And the Hour is coming — no doubt about it — and God will resurrect those in the graves. And among mankind is he who disputes concerning God without knowledge, without guidance, and without an enlightening Book..."* (Qur'an 22:7–8)

> *"The reckoning of the people has drawn near, yet they are heedless, turning away."* (Qur'an 21:1)

The crucial point about *ghaflah* is that it is *not* ignorance. The heedless person may know all the facts. What he lacks is the *application* of his knowledge to his own case. He has not connected the general truth to the particular situation — "I will die; I will be raised; I will be judged" — and the failure to connect is the heedlessness. That is why the Qur'an's cure for it is *dhikr* — remembrance, the sūrah's own opening word (v. 2). Remembrance is not the acquisition of new information; it is the activation of what one already knows. And it is why the prophets are called *mudhakkirūn*, "reminders":

> *"So remind; you are only a reminder. You are not a controller over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

The word *ghaflah* also carries a specific warning for the believer, and the tradition took it seriously, because it is the one element of this passage that does not apply solely to those who rejected the message. Heedlessness is not the exclusive property of disbelievers; it is a state that afflicts everyone and must be repeatedly shaken off. The scholars wrote about it as a condition that returns rather than one that is cured, which is why the remedy prescribed for it is a practice repeated throughout the day rather than a single act of resolution. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Take advantage of five before five: your youth before your old age, your health before your illness, your wealth before your poverty, your free time before your preoccupation, and your life before your death."* (al-Ḥākim; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

**"And They Believe Not"**

The verse closes with *wa-hum lā yuʾminūn* — "and they believe not." The present-tense verb describes an ongoing state: they are, at this moment, in the condition of not believing. The sūrah puts the two conditions side by side without connecting them causally, and the omission is deliberate: heedlessness and unbelief reinforce each other but are not identical. A person can be heedless and believe; the Qur'an warns believers against exactly that (*"O you who believe! Let not your wealth or your children distract you from the remembrance of God"*, 63:9). And a person can, in principle, attend carefully and still not believe. But the two together — as here — produce the condition that the sūrah is warning about: a person who is not thinking about the matter *and* has not accepted it, being warned about a Day on which both will be forcibly corrected.

**Why the Prophet Is Told to Warn**

Finally, note the verb: *andhirhum* — "warn them." The sūrah is instructing the Prophet ﷺ in his function, and the word choice is significant. Islam pairs *bashīr* (bringer of glad tidings) with *nadhīr* (warner) throughout, and the two are never separated. The sūrah will state both at v. 97: *"that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people."* The pairing is not a stylistic tic; it reflects the two things a message must do. Good news that has no warning attached is flattery. Warning that has no good news attached is cruelty. The Qur'an refuses both. The Prophet ﷺ lived this pairing. When he first called his own clan, he said:

> *"O Assembly of Quraysh! Buy yourselves [from the Fire]; I cannot avail you anything against God. O sons of ʿAbd Manāf, I cannot avail you anything against God. O ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, I cannot avail you anything against God. O Ṣafiyyah, aunt of the Messenger of God, I cannot avail you anything against God. O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muhammad, ask me whatever you wish of my wealth — I cannot avail you anything against God."* (Muslim 206)

That is what warning looks like when it is done out of love rather than out of superiority: the warner includes his own dearest relations in the warning, because the Day of Regret does not exempt them and he cannot help them. The sūrah has been building toward this point since its opening, and the shape of the warning is the argument: a man who warns his own household first is not performing concern but discharging it, and the inclusion of the dearest relations is what distinguishes a warning from a reproach. It is also, in the sūrah's own structure, the bridge to the narratives that follow — Abraham warning his father, and the long sequence of prophets sent to people who did not want to hear them. The pattern of the warner who cannot help the ones he loves is the pattern of every prophet in this chapter, and it begins here.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:40

> **Surely We shall inherit the earth and whatsoever is upon it, and unto Us shall they be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Closes the Sūrah's First Movement**

This verse brings the sūrah's opening section to its conclusion and makes the transition to the second. It is short, but it is one of the most comprehensive statements of the Qur'an's metaphysics of ownership: *innā naḥnu narithu al-arḍa wa-man ʿalayhā wa-ilaynā yurjaʿūn* — "Surely We shall inherit the earth and whatsoever is upon it, and unto Us shall they be returned." Two clauses: God inherits everything, and everything returns to Him. The first deals with the *earth* and what is on it; the second deals with *they* — the human beings who were warned in the previous verse. The movement is from property to persons, and it covers both.

**Al-Wārith — "The Inheritor" Among the Names of God**

The verb *narithu* — "We inherit" — comes from the root *w-r-th*, the same root discussed at v. 6, where Zachariah prayed for an heir who would "inherit from me and inherit from the House of Jacob." That echo is deliberate, and it is one of the sūrah's most elegant pieces of wordplay. At v. 6, a mortal man worries about who will inherit his religious authority after he dies. At v. 40, God declares that *He* is the Inheritor — of the earth and everyone on it. The sūrah has thus moved the concept of inheritance from the human level, where it is anxious and uncertain, to the divine level, where it is absolute. Every human worry about succession is, in the end, a small version of a fact that belongs entirely to God. *Al-Wārith*, "The Inheritor," is one of the Beautiful Names of God, and the Qur'an applies it in several places:

> *"And verily it is We Who give life and cause death, and We are the Inheritor (*al-wārithūn*)."* (Qur'an 15:23)

> *"And how many a city We destroyed that exulted in its life [of ease]! There are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little; and it is We Who are the Inheritor."* (Qur'an 28:58)

And in this sūrah's own vocabulary, at v. 6 and v. 63, inheritance language is applied to the righteous — which is the point at which the metaphor turns from threat into promise, since the same verb that describes God's taking back the earth describes His giving the Garden. *"This is the Garden We shall bequeath (*nūrithu*) unto those among Our servants who were reverent"* (19:63). The mechanism is identical and the difference lies entirely in the recipient: what is taken from the earth and everyone on it is given to those who were reverent, and the two halves of the sūrah's teaching about inheritance are therefore one teaching:

**What Divine Inheritance Means — and What It Does Not**

The concept requires precision, because "inherit" applied to God could be misread. In human usage, inheritance is *acquisition after another's death* — the heir receives what the deceased left because the deceased is gone. Applied to God, that is impossible: nothing predeceases God, and nothing is outside His ownership at any moment. The commentators therefore explained *al-Wārith* in the sense that al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī give it: God is the Inheritor insofar as **all things belong to Him and thus return to Him in the end**. The "inheritance" is not a transfer of title that occurs at a point in time; it is the *manifestation*, at the end of time, of a title that always existed. That is why the second clause of the verse states the same thing in a form that cannot be misread: *"and unto Us shall they be returned."* Return implies a prior belonging. One returns to where one came from. The Qur'an states the principle in a number of places with perfect clarity:

> *"Unto Him belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and unto God are all matters returned."* (Qur'an 57:5)

> *"All upon it [the earth] passes away. And there remains the Face of thy Lord, Possessed of Majesty and Bounty."* (Qur'an 55:26–27)

That second verse is worth reading alongside ours, because together they contain the whole doctrine. Everything on the earth is perishing; only the Face of the Lord remains; therefore the earth and everyone on it returns to Him, and He remains. The doctrine is worth stating in its barest form, because everything the sūrah says about loss, bequest, and return depends on it. Ownership is God's, and a human being's possession of anything is a temporary trust rather than a title. What the verse removes, therefore, is not a thing a person actually had but the illusion that he had it — and the removal is mercy rather than loss for anyone who has understood what he was holding.

**The Theological Point: Ownership Is Not What We Think It Is**

The practical content of this verse is a radical reorientation toward property, and it is one of the Qur'an's most repeated teachings. Human beings experience themselves as owners. They hold land, wealth, position, family, and body, and the default assumption of the mind is that these are *theirs*. The sūrah's assertion is that this assumption is a description of a temporary custodianship, not of a title. The Qur'an makes the point in the sharpest possible terms elsewhere:

> *"And spend from what We have provided you before death comes to one of you and he says: 'My Lord, if only You would delay me for a short term, so I could give charity and be among the righteous.' But God will never delay a soul when its term has come."* (Qur'an 63:10–11)

And, most explicitly, in the formula the Prophet ﷺ taught and the community recites at every death — a sentence so compressed that its familiarity can conceal how much it asserts, and which the scholars described as the believer's own summary of the metaphysics of loss:

> *"Verily we belong to God, and verily unto Him we are returning."* (Qur'an 2:156)

The word used there for "returning" is the same root as in our verse. Every Muslim who hears of a death is instructed to say it, and the sūrah's verse is the metaphysical ground of that sentence. *Innā lillāhi wa-innā ilayhi rājiʿūn* — we are God's, and to Him we go back. The sentence is doing two things at once, and both are worth holding onto. The first clause states ownership: we belong to God, which means we were never ours to lose. The second states destination: to Him we return, which means the direction of the movement was always toward Him and death is simply the arrival. A person who has understood the first clause is not surprised by the second, and a person who has not will find both clauses unbearable — which is why the tradition placed this sentence on the tongue at the exact moment when it is hardest to say.

**The Second Clause: "Unto Us Shall They Be Returned"**

The shift from *the earth and whatsoever is upon it* to *they* is significant. The sūrah has been addressing *them* — the heedless, the unbelieving, the parties who differed — since v. 37. Now it says where they are going. The verb *yurjaʿūn* (root *r-j-ʿ*) means they will be returned, brought back. It implies that the journey outward from God and the journey back to Him are the two halves of a single arc, and that the second half is as certain as the first. The Qur'an uses this verb constantly in eschatological contexts (*"then unto your Lord you will be returned"*, 32:11; 39:7; 45:15), and the consistency is itself the argument: if a thing came from somewhere, it goes back there, and the return is not a matter of opinion.

**The Verse's Function as a Bridge**

Placed where it is, this verse performs a specific structural service. It closes the first movement of the sūrah — the narratives of Zachariah, John, Mary, and Jesus, with their warnings about sectarianism, heedlessness, and the Day of Regret — and it sets up the second movement, which opens at v. 41 with *"And remember Abraham in the Book."* The connection is the concept of inheritance. The second movement will be about the *gifts* God bestows on those who leave everything for Him: Isaac and Jacob given to Abraham after he withdraws from his people (v. 49), Aaron given to Moses (v. 53), prophethood given to Ishmael and Idrīs (vv. 54–57). The pattern announced here — *everything belongs to God and returns to Him* — is the premise of that entire sequence. A person who knows that he owns nothing can leave everything. And a God who inherits everything can give anything.

**A Closing Practical Word**

The verse invites one final application, and it is the one the tradition has most often drawn out. If God inherits the earth and everyone on it, then the only thing a human being can actually keep is what he has sent ahead. The Prophet ﷺ stated this with his customary economy, and in a form that makes the application impossible to forget, since it asks the question from inside the situation it describes rather than from outside it:

> *"When a person dies, his deeds come to an end except for three: ongoing charity, beneficial knowledge, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631)

Everything else is inherited by someone else, and ultimately by God. The sūrah's first movement, which opened with a barren old man praying for an heir (vv. 4–6), thus closes with the answer to that prayer in its widest form: the true inheritance belongs to God, and the only thing worth asking for is what survives the return to Him.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:41

> **And remember Abraham in the Book—verily he was truthful, a prophet—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third "And Remember"**

The sūrah's second movement opens with the same formula that introduced Mary: *wa-dhkur fī'l-kitābi Ibrāhīma* — "And remember Abraham in the Book." The repetition of the formula at v. 41, v. 51, and v. 56 establishes that what follows is a single series: the figures God blessed and chose (v. 58), whose stories are to be recited as scripture and held in the community's memory. The transition from the first movement to the second is worth noticing. The first movement closed on inheritance and return (v. 40). The second opens on Abraham, who is the figure in the Qur'an most closely associated with *leaving everything* — his father, his people, his city — and receiving everything in return. The movement from "unto Us shall they be returned" to "remember Abraham" is therefore not a change of subject but a continuation: having established that everything belongs to God, the sūrah now shows what a person does who has understood that.

**Ṣiddīq — The Title of the Utterly Truthful**

The verse's description of Abraham is two words: *innahu kāna ṣiddīqan nabiyyan* — "verily he was truthful, a prophet." *Ṣiddīq* comes from the root *ṣ-d-q*, the root of *ṣidq* (truthfulness), *taṣdīq* (confirming/affirming the truth), and *ṣadaqah* (charity, so called because it affirms the truth of one's faith). The intensive form *ṣiddīq* denotes one in whom truthfulness has become a settled, habitual, overwhelming characteristic — not merely a person who tells the truth, but a person whose entire being corresponds to the truth. The word is among the rarest and highest titles in the Qur'an. It is applied to only three people by name: Joseph (*"Joseph, O truthful one!"*, 12:46), Idrīs (19:56, in this sūrah), and — in a disputed reading — Mary, who is called *ṣiddīqah* at 5:75. And its plural, *al-ṣiddīqūn*, is used for the highest rank of believers after the prophets:

> *"And whosoever obeys God and the Messenger — those are with the ones God has blessed: the prophets, the truthful ones (*al-ṣiddīqīn*), the martyrs, and the righteous. And excellent are those as companions."* (Qur'an 4:69)

The commentators unpacked the title. Al-Rāzī noted that the term refers both to those who are honest by nature and habit *and* to those who constantly confirm (*yuṣaddiqūn*) the truth — the two senses belonging to the same root. And al-Ṭabarī specifies what Abraham's truthfulness consisted in: he was truthful in his speech, in his promises, and in his deeds. That threefold specification is worth dwelling on, because it defines the virtue precisely. Most people who think of themselves as honest are thinking only of the first item — not lying. The sūrah's portrait requires all three: a correspondence between what one says and what is true; a correspondence between what one promises and what one does; and a correspondence between what one professes and how one lives. A person can be truthful in the first sense and fail badly at the third, which is precisely the failure the Qur'an calls hypocrisy. Abraham's truthfulness was tested in all three registers, and the Qur'an records the tests: he spoke truth to a tyrant (21:57–63), to his father (19:42–45), and to his people (6:76–83); he kept his promise when God commanded the sacrifice (37:102–107); and his deeds matched his profession when he was cast into the fire (21:68–70).

**Nabī and Rasūl: The Distinction the Tradition Drew**

The second title is *nabiyy* — "a prophet." The word and its relationship to *rasūl* ("messenger") have already been touched on at v. 30, but this verse is the place where the distinction matters most, because Abraham is the test case. Islamic theology distinguishes them as follows: a *nabī* is one to whom God has given a message — of glad tidings or warning, or both — to deliver to his people. A *rasūl* is a prophet who has additionally been given a new revelation or a new religious law for his people. All messengers are prophets; not all prophets are messengers. In the Qur'an itself the two terms are used more loosely than in later technical theology, and the commentators noted this carefully. Certain Arabian prophets who brought no new scripture — Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb — are nevertheless called *rasūl* in the Qur'an (7:67; 26:125, 143). So the distinction is sharper in the scholarly tradition than in the revealed text. Abraham's case is interesting precisely because it falls between the two definitions. The Qur'an calls him *nabī* explicitly (here), *rasūl* implicitly in some readings (4:163–65 lists him among those to whom God sent revelation), and it credits him with scriptures: *"Or has he not been informed of what is in the scriptures of Moses, and of Abraham, who fulfilled [his covenant]?"* (53:36–37); and *"Verily this is in the former scriptures, the scriptures of Abraham and Moses"* (87:18–19). On that basis, Islamic tradition classifies Abraham as both a prophet and a messenger — a *rasūl* because he brought a new religious dispensation and was given scriptures, and a *nabī* because he conveyed God's message to his people. In this sūrah, only the title *nabī* appears, and the sūrah's purpose in using it is to place Abraham in the same category as the figures who came before him in the chapter: John (v. 12–15), Jesus (v. 30), and, coming later, Moses (v. 51), Ishmael (v. 54), and Idrīs (v. 56).

**Abraham in the Islamic Imagination**

No figure outside the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ occupies as much space in Islamic piety as Abraham, and it is worth stating why, because the sūrah assumes it. He is *Khalīl Allāh*, "the intimate friend of God": *"And God took Abraham as an intimate friend"* (4:125). He is the one whom God tested with words and made a leader of mankind: *"And [mention] when his Lord tested Abraham with certain words, and he fulfilled them. He said: 'Verily I am making you a leader (*imām*) for mankind'"* (2:124). He is, in the Qur'an's own declaration, the prototype of the Muslim:

> *"Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; rather, he was upright, a submitter (*muslim*), and he was not of those who associate partners with God."* (Qur'an 3:67)

He is the builder of the Kaʿbah with his son Ishmael (2:125–29), and therefore the founder of the ḥajj; the Prophet ﷺ described the rites as an inheritance from him. And he is the figure to whom the Qur'an most often appeals when it wants to refute the argument from ancestral custom, precisely because his own people relied on that argument against him. In the Islamic daily liturgy, Abraham is named in every unit of every prayer — in the *tašahhud*: *"O God, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad as You blessed Abraham and the family of Abraham; verily You are Praiseworthy, Glorious."* A Muslim who prays five times a day invokes Abraham by name at least forty times. That is the measure of his place.

**Why Abraham, and Why Here**

There is a specific reason this sūrah turns to Abraham immediately after the story of Mary and the warning about heedlessness, and it is the same reason the sūrah told the stories it told first. Abraham is the Qur'an's preeminent example of a man who *left*. He left his father, his people, and his city; he argued with the most powerful ruler of his region and was cast into a fire; he was commanded to sacrifice his son and prepared to do it. In every case the pattern is the sūrah's pattern: separation, then gift. The sūrah has already shown the pattern three times — Zachariah's withdrawal, Mary's withdrawal, and (implicitly) the flight of the believers to Abyssinia that this sūrah is associated with. Now it shows it in the life of the man the Qur'an calls *"a community, devoutly obedient to God, upright, and he was not of those who associate partners with Him"* (16:120). And there is a second reason, which is polemical and immediate. The Quraysh claimed descent from Abraham and invoked his religion as the warrant for their idolatry. The sūrah's answer, spread across vv. 42–50, is devastating: *here is what Abraham actually said to an idolater* — and the idolater in question was his own father. The sūrah thus takes the Quraysh's own claimed ancestor and sets him against them.

**The Verse as a Title-Page**

Finally, note that the verse is a title-page rather than a narrative. It states who Abraham is, and the story begins only in v. 42 with *when he said unto his father*. The ordering is deliberate: the character first, then the acts. The sūrah wants the reader to understand Abraham's speech to his father as the speech of a *ṣiddīq* and a *nabī* — that is, as truthful prophecy rather than as a son's insolence. The accusations that follow (v. 46: "Do you reject my gods?") will make sense only if the reader has first been told that the speaker is truthful and commissioned by God. That is a general principle of the Qur'an's narrative method, and it is worth carrying away: the Qur'an almost never presents a disputed act without first establishing the character of the actor. It knows that readers judge acts by what they already believe about the person — so it tells them first.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:42

> **when he said unto his father, "O my father! Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor can avail you in any way?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene: A Son Confronting a Father**

The story begins with a single word that sets the whole tone: *idh* — "when." *Idh qāla li-abīhi* — "when he said unto his father." The sūrah does not say "Abraham rebelled," or "Abraham argued," or "Abraham condemned." It says *when he said*. The neutral framing is itself an instruction in how to read what follows: this is a speech, addressed to a person, in a relationship. Who is the father? The Qur'an names him at 6:74: *"And [mention] when Abraham said to his father, Āzar: 'Do you take idols as gods?'"* Islamic sources describe him variously as Abraham's biological father, as an uncle who raised him, or as a maternal relative — the question arises because the Qur'anic genealogy is not spelled out in detail, and because the Qur'an elsewhere forbids a believer to seek forgiveness for a polytheist parent (9:113). The majority of commentators held that Āzar was his father in the ordinary sense, and the sūrah's own address (*yā abati*, "O my father") supports the plain reading.

**The Question Form: Why Ask a Question?**

Abraham's opening move is a question, not an assertion: *yā abati lima taʿbudu mā lā yasmaʿu wa-lā yubṣiru wa-lā yughnī ʿanka shayʾan* — "O my father! Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor can avail you in any way?" The sūrah will use the question form throughout vv. 42–45, and the choice is pedagogically significant. A question requires the hearer to supply the answer, and the answer here is available to anyone. If Abraham had opened by saying "your idols are worthless," his father could have defended himself. By asking *why*, he forces his father to produce a reason — and there is no good one. This is the Qur'an's characteristic method of argument, and it is worth noticing how often it appears in the arguments of the prophets. Moses asks Pharaoh: *"And what is the Lord of the worlds?"* (26:23) — inviting an answer that will expose Pharaoh's confusion. The Qur'an asks of the idolaters: *"Do they associate [with God] those who create nothing while they themselves are created, and who cannot help them nor help themselves?"* (7:191–92). The method is Socratic before Socrates: lead the interlocutor to the conclusion rather than asserting it over him.

**The Threefold Indictment**

The question contains three specific charges against the objects of worship, and each is a premise that any reasonable person would grant. *First: they do not hear.* An idol cannot hear a supplication. Al-Rāzī drew out the consequence with characteristic thoroughness: if the idols cannot hear, they cannot hear those who call upon them, and they cannot distinguish between those who worship them and those who do not. Worship addressed to something that cannot register it is, functionally, worship addressed to nothing. *Second: they do not see.* They cannot see their devotees, cannot see their need, cannot see the wrong done to them. A being that cannot perceive its worshippers cannot respond to them. *Third: they cannot avail in any way.* The verb *yughnī* (root *gh-n-y*) means to suffice, to enrich, to be of avail — to meet a need. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it as: they cannot repel harm nor bring benefit. The same charge is made elsewhere in the identical terms: *"Do they benefit you or harm you?"* (21:66; 26:73). The logic is compact and unanswerable, and it is worth stating as a syllogism: The argument is a functional one, and it runs in three steps. First, worship is an act of seeking benefit and averting harm from the one worshipped. Second, the idols can neither hear the request nor see the requester nor produce any effect. Third, therefore the worship cannot achieve what worship is for. The elegance of the approach is worth noting, because it explains why Abraham begins here rather than elsewhere. He does not open by asking whether the idols are gods, since that question admits of interminable dispute and commits his father to a defence. He asks instead what his father is actually doing when he prays to them — and on the functional question there is no disagreement available to be had.

There is an additional sting in the argument that al-Rāzī made explicit: to worship something that cannot hear or see is to worship something *less capable than oneself*. The worshipper can hear and see; the object of worship cannot. The act thus inverts the relationship it purports to establish — the greater serves the lesser. The Qur'an makes this point in a compressed and devastating image elsewhere:

> *"Verily those you call upon apart from God are servants like you. So call upon them and let them respond to you, if you are truthful. Do they have feet with which they walk, or hands with which they strike, or eyes with which they see, or ears with which they hear?"* (Qur'an 7:194–95)

**What the Idols Actually Were**

The commentators offered competing accounts of what Abraham's people were worshipping, and the disagreement is instructive because it changes the shape of the argument. Some held that they were simple carved idols (al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī). On this reading, Abraham's argument is a straightforward reductio: a carved stone cannot hear, so why pray to it? Al-Rāzī, however, proposed a more sophisticated reconstruction, and it is probably the more accurate one. He suggested that Abraham's people did not regard the idols as powerful in themselves. Rather, they worshipped them as *representatives* — either of the stars, which they believed to possess divine power, or of great and righteous people, whom they believed could intercede with God on their behalf. That reconstruction is strongly supported by the Qur'an's own account of Abraham's intellectual development in Sūrah al-Anʿām, where Abraham reasons his way from a star, to the moon, to the sun, and finally to the One who created them:

> *"Thus did We show Abraham the dominion of the heavens and the earth, that he might be among those who possess certainty. When the night covered him, he saw a star. He said: 'This is my Lord.' But when it set, he said: 'I love not those that set.' And when he saw the moon rising, he said: 'This is my Lord.' But when it set, he said: 'If my Lord does not guide me, I shall surely be among the people who go astray.' And when he saw the sun rising, he said: 'This is my Lord; this is greater.' But when it set, he said: 'O my people, verily I am innocent of what you associate with God. Verily I have turned my face toward Him Who originated the heavens and the earth, upright, and I am not of those who associate partners with Him.'"* (Qur'an 6:75–79)

That passage is the key to understanding this sūrah's version. Abraham is not addressing simple stone-worshippers; he is addressing people whose idolatry rests on a theory — a theory about intermediaries, celestial powers, and intercession. And his argument in v. 42 is aimed precisely at that theory: whatever the intermediary is supposed to do, a thing that cannot hear, see, or avail cannot do it.

**Why This Matters Now: The Continuing Relevance of the Argument**

It would be a mistake to read this verse as relevant only to seventh-century or ancient Near Eastern polytheism. The argument's structure applies to every form of reliance on something that cannot deliver. The Qur'an's definition of *shirk* is broader than idol-worship. It is the attribution to a created thing of what belongs only to the Creator — ultimate trust, ultimate fear, ultimate hope, ultimate obedience. The Prophet ﷺ identified the subtle form of it in a ḥadīth already cited at v. 18:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk."* They said: "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Riyāʾ (showing off). God will say on the Day of Resurrection, when people are recompensed for their deeds: 'Go to those for whom you used to show off in the world, and see if you find any reward with them.'"* (Aḥmad, *Musnad*; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The logic of Abraham's question applies directly. To seek one's worth from the approval of people who cannot see one's heart, to hope in a career or a sum of money that cannot hear a prayer, to fear a power that cannot actually determine one's fate — these are structurally the same error. The thing worshipped cannot hear, cannot see, and cannot avail. That is why the Qur'an treats *tawakkul* — reliance on God — as the positive counterpart to the rejection of *shirk*. The question "why do you worship that which cannot avail you?" has a built-in answer: worship the One who can.

**The Address: "O My Father"**

The final detail of the verse is the vocative: *yā abati* — "O my father." It will be repeated at the head of each of the four arguments in vv. 42–45, and al-Rāzī drew out the significance: the repetition of the tender address at every point indicates that everything Abraham says is said out of love and earnest filial concern for his father's spiritual well-being, not out of disrespect. This is a crucial point, and the sūrah insists on it. Abraham is doing something that in any traditional society is close to unforgivable: telling his father that his religion is false. The sūrah's framing makes clear that the manner of the correction is not the offence. The address is affectionate, the arguments are ordered, and the tone escalates only as the father hardens. The tradition has drawn a general rule from this passage: the obligation to speak the truth does not suspend the obligation of kindness, and the two are not in tension. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God is Gentle and loves gentleness in all matters."* (al-Bukhārī 6927; Muslim 2165)

And the Qur'an legislates the manner explicitly, which is the detail most often missed by readers who take their model of religious argument from somewhere else. The instruction is not simply to convey the truth but to convey it in a particular register, and the register specified is gentleness toward the person being addressed even where his position is being dismantled outright:

> *"Invite to the way of your Lord with wisdom and fair exhortation, and dispute with them in the fairest manner."* (Qur'an 16:125)

Abraham's four arguments to his father are, in the view of the commentators, the Qur'an's own model of how *daʿwah* is to be conducted with those closest to one — which is always the hardest case, because with family the correction is heard as a betrayal. The difficulty is structural rather than merely emotional: a stranger who disagrees with you is exchanging information, while a child who disagrees with a parent is, in the parent's own frame of reference, refusing a rank. This is why the sūrah has Abraham open with *yā abati* — "O my dear father" — and repeat the address at the head of every argument, so that the relationship is asserted in the same breath as the disagreement. Nothing in the address softens the content; what it does is locate that content inside a bond that is not being withdrawn.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:43

> **"O my father! Verily knowledge has come unto me that has not come unto you. So follow me, and I shall guide you upon a sound path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inversion of Authority**

The second argument opens with a claim that would have been scandalous in its setting: *yā abati innī qad jāʾanī min al-ʿilmi mā lam yaʾtika fa-ittabiʿnī ahdika ṣirāṭan sawiyyan* — "O my father! Verily knowledge has come unto me that has not come unto you. So follow me, and I shall guide you upon a sound path." A son telling his father that he knows something his father does not is, in a traditional hierarchy, a violation. The sūrah states it plainly anyway, and the commentators noted why: this is one of the places where the Qur'an establishes that **spiritual authority is not determined by age or lineage**. Al-Qurṭubī explains that the knowledge in question is the spiritual knowledge that has come to Abraham as a prophet, by which his father and his people might be guided — specifically, knowledge of the reality of God and of the Hereafter. So the claim is not a claim of general cleverness. It is a claim of *revelation*: knowledge has *come to* him (*jāʾanī*), passively, from a source outside himself. That grammatical point is what makes the statement humble rather than arrogant. Abraham does not say "I am cleverer than you." He says "knowledge has come to me" — and the implication throughout the Qur'an is that what comes to a prophet comes from God. The prophet is a receiver, and receiving is not a ground for pride.

**The Sūrah's Systematic Attack on the Argument From Age**

This verse is one instance of a pattern that runs through the whole chapter, and it is worth collecting the instances because together they constitute one of the sūrah's most distinctive teachings. The chapter is, among other things, an argument about who is entitled to speak, and it returns to the question often enough that the repetition itself becomes the answer:

- Zachariah is old and physically spent, and is given a son (vv. 4–9).
- John is given judgment *as a child* (v. 12).
- Mary is young, and is chosen above the women of the worlds (3:42; 19:16 ff.).
- Jesus speaks *as an infant* and corrects the theology of his elders (vv. 30–33).
- Abraham, the son, instructs his father (vv. 42–45).
- Moses is given Aaron as a helper, though Aaron is the elder (vv. 51–53).

In every case the natural hierarchy of age is inverted, and in every case the inversion is the sign. The sūrah is making an argument about where authority actually comes from: not from years, not from seniority, not from lineage, but from what God has given. The point is not that youth is preferable to age — the sūrah's elders are among the most honoured figures in it, and Zachariah's age is the very thing that makes his prayer remarkable — but that neither youth nor age is the source of the claim. What God gives, He gives to whom He wills, and a community that organises itself around seniority is organising itself around something the Qur'an does not recognise as a qualification.

**The Answer to the Argument From Ancestry**

The sūrah's insistence on this point had a direct polemical target in Makkah, and the commentators identified it. The Quraysh's standard objection to the Prophet ﷺ was not that his message was unintelligible; it was that it contradicted what they had inherited. The Qur'an quotes their objection repeatedly, and the consistency of the objection is itself instructive: at no point in the Makkan period is the Qur'an's message reported to have been refuted on its merits. What is reported instead is a claim of priority — that the fathers did otherwise, and that the fathers' practice settles the matter. The sūrah's whole apparatus of inverted authority aims directly at that claim, since the claim's entire force depends on age and inheritance being titles to truth:

> *"And when it is said to them: 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say: 'Nay, we will follow that which we found our fathers upon.' Even though their fathers understood nothing and were not guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

> *"And when it is said to them: 'Come to what God has sent down, and to the Messenger,' they say: 'Sufficient for us is that which we found our fathers upon.' Even though their fathers knew nothing and were not guided?"* (Qur'an 5:104)

The structure of the Qur'an's reply is instructive. It does not simply assert that the fathers were wrong. It asks a question: *even though their fathers knew nothing and were not guided?* The challenge is to the assumption that ancestry transmits truth. And the sūrah's answer here is the positive counterpart: if truth is what God gives, then the person who has it is the person to follow, regardless of whether he is older or younger than you. Abraham says *follow me* to his own father, and the Qur'an endorses the instruction — which means that paternal authority has a limit, and the limit is truth.

**The Limits of Filial Obedience**

This raises a question that the tradition has always handled carefully, because Islam places enormous emphasis on obedience to parents (see the commentary on v. 14). Is Abraham's instruction to his father a licence for children to disregard their parents? The answer is precise and important, and the jurists were emphatic about it because both of the alternative readings are live errors — one permitting rebellion under the cover of religion, and the other demanding obedience that extends into disobedience of God. The principle the jurists derived from the totality of the texts is this:

> *"There is no obedience to a created being in disobedience to the Creator."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad*; graded ṣaḥīḥ — reported from ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn and others)

Obedience to parents is absolute *within the sphere of what is permissible*, and it ceases exactly where the parents command disobedience to God. But two clarifications follow, and they are essential. *First, the disobedience is never in the manner.* The Prophet ﷺ taught that even where a parent commands something unlawful, the refusal must be courteous. The Qur'an's model is in this very passage: Abraham refuses his father's religion and continues to address him as "my father" four times, and when his father threatens him, he answers with *"Peace be upon you"* (v. 47). *Second, the parents retain every other right.* A parent who is wrong about religion does not thereby lose the right to be supported, visited, and treated with kindness. The clearest Qur'anic case is that of parents who pressure a child toward *shirk*:

> *"And if they strive to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them; but accompany them in this world with kindness, and follow the way of those who turn to Me."* (Qur'an 31:15)

Note the structure: *do not obey them* — and *accompany them with kindness*. Both halves are commands, and they are simultaneous. The sūrah's Abraham lives exactly this pattern: he will withdraw from his father's religion (v. 48) while continuing to pray for his father's forgiveness (v. 47). The simultaneity is the difficult part, and it is worth stating plainly because the two halves pull in opposite directions and a person under pressure will always be tempted to take the easier one. What the verse prohibits is a single thing: compliance in disobedience to God. What it demands is everything else — provision, company, gentleness, and the ordinary care a parent is owed regardless of the disagreement. The person who withholds obedience and withholds kindness has obeyed only the half that suited him, and the sūrah's Abraham is the model precisely because he manages both at once.

**"So Follow Me, and I Shall Guide You"**

The second half of the verse contains the instruction and the promise: *fa-ittabiʿnī ahdika* — "So follow me, and I shall guide you." Note what Abraham does *not* say. He does not say "believe in me." He says "follow me" — and then promises guidance, not salvation. The guidance is his to offer; the outcome is not his to guarantee. That distinction runs throughout the Qur'an's account of prophecy and is stated explicitly to the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Verily you do not guide whom you love; but God guides whom He wills."* (Qur'an 28:56)

> *"It is not for you to guide them; rather, God guides whom He wills."* (Qur'an 2:272)

The prophet's function is to point. The turning of the heart is God's. That is why the sūrah's Abraham can be both urgent and unanxious — he pleads with his father (vv. 42–45) and then, when his father refuses, he withdraws in peace (vv. 47–48) without any suggestion that he has failed. The distinction is one of the most practically useful things in the sūrah, because it addresses a burden that falls heavily on anyone who has tried to convey the religion to someone he loves. Urgency belongs to the pleading, and anxiety does not, because anxiety presupposes that the outcome rests in the pleader's hands. It does not. The messenger's task is delivery, and the measure of his success is the delivery rather than the response — which is why the sūrah can present Abraham as complete in this episode even though the episode ends with his father refusing him.

**"A Sound Path" — The Third Appearance of the Root S-W-Y**

The final phrase is *ṣirāṭan sawiyyan* — "a sound path," or more literally "an even, level, rightly-proportioned path." Al-Qurṭubī glosses it as the path of correct religion by which salvation is attained. But the word choice deserves a note, because it is the third occurrence of the root *s-w-y* in this sūrah, and the sūrah has been using it with remarkable consistency:

- **v. 10:** Zachariah was unable to speak *sawiyyan* — "while sound" — perfectly healthy, yet unable to speak.
- **v. 17:** The angel appeared as a *basharan sawiyyan* — a "perfect man" — perfectly formed, yet not human.
- **v. 43:** Abraham promises to guide his father on a *ṣirāṭ sawiyy* — a "sound path."

The thread connecting them is the idea of *rightness of proportion* — a thing that is straight, even, and without defect. A path that is *sawiyy* is a path with no hidden bend in it, one that does not lead somewhere other than where it appears to lead. That is a precise description of what Abraham is offering, and it stands in deliberate contrast to what his father has. Idolatry, in the Qur'an's account, is a path that *looks* like a path but does not arrive. And the Qur'an's most repeated description of the mistaken ways is that they are crooked:

> *"And [saying]: 'Verily this is My straight path, so follow it; and follow not other ways, for they will scatter you from His path.'"* (Qur'an 6:153)

**The Pastoral Application**

For the reader, this verse carries a difficult and necessary instruction. The difficult part: there are cases in which a person must say to a parent, a teacher, or an elder, "on this matter, I know something you do not, and you should follow me." The sūrah legitimises that. It does not legitimise arrogance, and it does not legitimise the wholesale collapse of filial duty — but it legitimises the claim itself when the claim is true and the knowledge is real. The necessary part: the claim is only legitimate under Abraham's conditions. The knowledge must have *come* to one, not been generated by one. The offer must be accompanied by love, not contempt. And the goal must be guidance, not victory. A young person who instructs an elder in order to win is not doing what Abraham did; a young person who instructs an elder because the elder is walking toward a cliff is. The sūrah will show both sides of this in the next three verses — Abraham's escalating plea (vv. 44–45) and his father's furious refusal (v. 46) — and in the response that follows, which is one of the most beautiful passages in the entire Qur'an.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:44

> **"O my father! Worship not Satan; surely Satan is disobedient toward the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Deepens: From Idols to What Stands Behind Them**

Abraham's third argument moves behind the objects of worship to the agent behind them: *yā abati lā taʿbud al-Shayṭāna inna al-Shayṭāna kāna li'l-Raḥmāni ʿaṣiyyā* — "O my father! Worship not Satan; surely Satan is disobedient toward the Compassionate." The progression across vv. 42–45 is carefully constructed, and it is worth mapping because it is a model of how to argue:

- **v. 42:** The objects of worship are powerless. (A factual argument.)
- **v. 43:** True knowledge has come to me; follow it. (An epistemological argument.)
- **v. 44:** What is really being served is Satan, who is a rebel. (A moral argument.)
- **v. 45:** The consequence is punishment and alliance with Satan. (An eschatological argument.)

Each argument takes the matter a level deeper, and the sequence is worth tracing because it is a model of how the Qur'an reasons with a person rather than at him. Abraham begins with what his father can observe, moves to what he can know, then to what he is actually doing, and finally to where it leads. The escalation is not rhetorical — it is diagnostic. He is not repeating himself at greater volume but locating the problem more precisely each time, and the order moves from the object worshipped, to the knowledge available about it, to the worshipper's own action, to its end. Any one of the four would have been sufficient to state the conclusion; what the sequence does is leave his father without a place left to stand.

**What "Worshipping Satan" Means**

The commentators were quick to clarify, because on its face the verse attributes to Abraham's people a practice they did not have: nobody in that setting was performing rites to Satan. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Thaʿlabī all give the same explanation: "worship not Satan" means *do not obey Satan's promptings toward sin and evil*, since Abraham's people did not worship Satan directly. The principle behind the equation is stated explicitly by the commentators and is one of the most important in Islamic ethics: **to obey a being in an act of disobedience to God is to worship that being**. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Thaʿlabī both cite 9:31 in this connection, where the Qur'an makes the same move about a different object:

> *"They have taken their rabbis and their monks as lords apart from God, and [also] the Messiah, son of Mary — though they were commanded to worship only One God."* (Qur'an 9:31)

The sīrah supplies the gloss on that verse, and it is one of the most frequently cited episodes in the whole literature of *tafsīr*, because it settles a question that arises afresh in every generation. When ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim, a former Christian, heard the Prophet ﷺ recite it, he objected: *"But we do not worship them!"* — meaning that he and his people had never prostrated themselves to a rabbi or a monk. The objection is entirely reasonable on a ritual definition of worship, and the Prophet ﷺ replied by relocating the definition rather than by disputing the facts:

> *"Do they not forbid what God has permitted and permit what God has forbidden, and you follow them?"* He said: "Yes." He said: *"That is your worship of them."* (al-Tirmidhī 3095; graded ḥasan gharīb)

That exchange is the definitive Islamic statement on the matter, and it makes the logic of our verse exact. Worship, in the Qur'an's functional definition, is not exhausted by ritual. It is *ultimate obedience* — and whoever is ultimately obeyed is, functionally, worshipped. The consequence is uncomfortable, and the tradition has never softened it: a person may perform every prescribed act of worship to God and still be, in the functional sense this verse is using, a worshipper of something else, if the thing he will not disobey is not God. The same principle appears in the Qur'an's account of Satan's own programme:

> *"Did I not charge you, O children of Adam, that you should not worship Satan — for he is a manifest enemy to you — and that you should worship Me? This is a straight path. And he has certainly led astray a great multitude among you. Did you not then understand?"* (Qur'an 36:60–62)

**The Name and the Nature of Satan**

The word *Shayṭān* comes from a root whose senses include *to be distant, to be remote, to burn with rage*. The philologists derived it either from *shaṭana*, "he was distant" — because Satan is distant from all good — or from *shāṭa*, "it burned," because of the intensity of his enmity. The Islamic position on Satan, which this verse assumes, is specific and worth stating because it differs from several alternatives: *Satan is a creature, not a principle.* He is a jinn, created by God, not a co-eternal power of darkness. The Qur'an states this explicitly:

> *"And [mention] when We said to the angels: 'Prostrate before Adam,' and they prostrated, except Iblīs. He was of the jinn, and he rebelled against the Command of his Lord."* (Qur'an 18:50)

*His rebellion was a choice, not a nature.* This distinction carries the whole of the tradition's theology of Satan, and it is the reason Islam has never developed a dualistic strain. Satan is not a rival principle, not a dark counterpart to a light one, and not a necessary feature of the created order; he is a creature who was commanded and refused. The refusal is his, and so is the enmity, and the fact that he once stood in the company of the angels has never been read by the commentators as evidence about his kind:

> *"He said: 'What prevented you from prostrating when I commanded you?' He said: 'I am better than him; You created me from fire and You created him from clay.'"* (Qur'an 7:12)

*He has no power over God's servants except by permission and within limits.* The Qur'an is emphatic on this, and the point is pastorally vital, because the alternative — a Satan with an independent reach — produces either despair or superstition, and both are forms of the same mistake. The tradition's position is that Satan invites and cannot compel, that his access to a person runs through that person's own response, and that the servant who holds to God is not within his jurisdiction at all:

> *"Verily he has no authority over those who believe and rely upon their Lord. His authority is only over those who take him as an ally and who, because of him, associate partners with God."* (Qur'an 16:99–100)

**"Disobedient Toward the Compassionate"**

The verse's second clause gives the reason Satan must not be obeyed: *inna al-Shayṭāna kāna li'l-Raḥmāni ʿaṣiyyā* — "surely Satan is disobedient toward the Compassionate." Two things are notable. *First, the Name.* God is called *al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate — the sūrah's signature Name, appearing here for the first time in the Abraham passage (and again in v. 45, and then at vv. 61, 69, 75, 78, 85, 87, 88, 91, 92, 93, 96). Why name mercy here, in the middle of an argument about Satan? The commentators' answer is implicit in the sūrah's theology: Satan's rebellion is a rebellion *against mercy*. He refused to bow to the creature God had honoured, and his entire programme is to make human beings as wretched as he is. To obey him is to side with the enemy of the One whose mercy encompasses everything. The Name is thus not decorative; it identifies what is at stake. There is a second, sharper point. The Qur'an records that Satan's rebellion was, at root, a refusal of mercy toward another — he would not acknowledge the honour God had given to Adam. And his declared programme is to make human beings ungrateful:

> *"Then I will come to them from before them and from behind them, and from their right and from their left; and You will not find most of them grateful."* (Qur'an 7:17)

*Second, the word ʿaṣiyy.* As noted at v. 14 (where John is described as *not* ʿaṣiyy), the root *ʿ-ṣ-y* denotes persistent, habitual disobedience. The intensive form here is strong: Satan is *constitutionally* disobedient. And the construction *kāna... ʿaṣiyyā* implies a settled, continuing state. So the argument of the verse is: *do not ally yourself with a being whose defining characteristic is rebellion against the Compassionate.* The logic is one of consequence. If you follow a rebel, you will end up where the rebel ends up — which is precisely the argument of the next verse.

**The Psychology of Following a Rebel**

There is a psychological insight in this verse that is worth drawing out, because it explains why human beings obey Satan at all. Nobody sets out to serve a being they know to be their enemy. The Qur'an is explicit that Satan is a manifest enemy (2:168; 6:142; 7:22; 12:5; 17:53; 35:6; 36:60; 43:62), and yet people follow him constantly. How? The answer the Qur'an gives is that the following is always *mediated*. People do not obey Satan; they obey a desire, a habit, a social expectation, a resentment, a sloth — and the desire is what Satan works through. That is why the sūrah's formulation matters: *worship not Satan* is simultaneously *do not follow the promptings that lead to sin*. The enemy is almost never visible as an enemy at the moment of obedience. The tradition's practical conclusion is that the defence is not heroic confrontation but *recognition* — learning to identify the prompting early, when it is still small and still recognisable. The Prophet ﷺ taught the specific response:

> *"If one of you is afflicted by a whisper, let him say: 'I believe in God and His messengers,' and let him seek refuge in God — for that will remove it from him."* (reported in the commentaries; cf. Muslim 134 on the virtues of seeking refuge)

And God's own instruction, quoted earlier, which supplies the practical form the refusal takes — not an argument, not a confrontation, and not an act of interior strength, but a request made to the very One in whom refuge is being sought:

> *"And if an incitement from Satan should incite thee, then seek refuge in God. Verily He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing."* (Qur'an 7:200)

**The Place of This Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Finally, it is worth noting that this verse supplies the missing term in the sūrah's moral psychology. The sūrah has, up to this point, described the righteous as servants (vv. 2, 30, 93), as reverent (vv. 13, 18, 63, 72, 85, 96), and as blessed (vv. 31, 50). It has described the opponents as disbelievers (v. 37), wrongdoers (v. 38), and the heedless (v. 39). What has been missing is the *agency* behind the opposition — the explanation of why the heedlessness is organised rather than random. Verse 44 supplies it: there is an agent, he has a programme, and his programme is rebellion against the Compassionate. That is why the sūrah's cosmology is not dualistic: Satan is a created rebel, not a rival power, and his authority is limited to those who take him as an ally. The sūrah will return to this at v. 68 (the satans gathered with the disbelievers around Hell), at v. 83 (the satans unleashed upon the disbelievers), and at v. 45 (the punishment that makes a person a *friend of Satan*). And it will close the whole matter at vv. 85–86, where the two processions are separated: the reverent gathered to the Compassionate as an honoured delegation, and the guilty driven to Hell as a thirsty herd.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:45

> **"O my father! Truly I fear that a punishment from the Compassionate will befall you, such that you will become a friend of Satan."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Argument: The Consequence**

Abraham's final argument is the heaviest, and it closes the series: *yā abati innī akhāfu an yamassaka ʿadhābun min al-Raḥmāni fa-takūna li'l-Shayṭāni waliyyā* — "O my father! Truly I fear that a punishment from the Compassionate will befall you, such that you will become a friend of Satan." The choice of the divine Name in this closing argument is the detail the commentators have most often dwelt on. Abraham is warning his father of punishment, but he frames it as punishment from *al-Raḥmān*, the Compassionate — which is to say that the severity of the warning is placed inside the Name of mercy rather than set against it. The fear he expresses is fear on his father's behalf rather than against him, and the word he uses is *akhāfu*, "I fear," not "you will be punished." The structure across the four arguments now stands complete:

- **v. 42:** What you worship cannot help you. (*What is false.*)
- **v. 43:** True knowledge has come to me. (*What is true.*)
- **v. 44:** What you are really serving is a rebel. (*What is happening.*)
- **v. 45:** The end of it is punishment and alliance with Satan. (*Where it ends.*)

The move from v. 44 to v. 45 is a move from diagnosis to prognosis, and it is the standard shape of Qur'anic warning everywhere: state the truth, name the danger, and leave the decision to the hearer. What is worth noticing is what the shape excludes. There is no third element — no compulsion, no enforcement, and no attempt to remove from the hearer his capacity to refuse. The warning is complete when it has been delivered accurately, and a warning that has been delivered accurately has done its entire job whether or not it is heeded.

**"Truly I Fear" — The Prophets' Knowledge and Their Fear**

The commentators noted an apparent tension in the verse. Abraham *fears* a punishment; but a prophet, who receives revelation, would know. And indeed some commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Thaʿlabī, record that the meaning is that Abraham *knew* it would befall his father if he persisted. How are the two to be reconciled? The answer lies in the narrative development of the Qur'an's own account of Abraham and his father. At this point in the story, Abraham does not yet know the outcome. He is pleading with a living man whose response is not yet determined, and the fear is real. It is only later — when his father dies in his disbelief, or when it becomes clear to Abraham that his father is an enemy of God — that knowledge replaces fear, and Abraham dissociates himself:

> *"And Abraham's seeking forgiveness for his father was only because of a promise he had made to him. But when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he dissociated himself from him. Verily Abraham was most tender-hearted, forbearing."* (Qur'an 9:114)

That verse is the key to the whole passage, and it resolves the tension exactly. Fear and knowledge are not the same thing, and a prophet's knowledge is of what God has told him — which, in the case of a living person's final state, is not generally disclosed. So Abraham feared while his father lived, and knew only when the matter was closed. The commentators were also careful about a second point: Abraham's *fear* is itself evidence of his love. Al-Rāzī, who noted at v. 42 that each argument is prefaced by "O my father," makes the same observation here — the fear is proof that the warning is not a threat but a plea. A person who is indifferent does not fear.

**"A Punishment From the Compassionate"**

The punishment is described as coming *min al-Raḥmāni* — "from the Compassionate." This is the second consecutive use of the Name (v. 44, v. 45), and the repetition is deliberate and theologically pointed. Why describe punishment as coming from the Compassionate? The question is one the Qur'an's readers have always asked, and the answer the tradition gives has several layers. *First, punishment in the Qur'an is never separate from mercy.* The God who punishes is the same God whose mercy encompasses everything (*"My mercy encompasses all things"*, 7:156). Punishment is not the failure of mercy but its corollary: it is what mercy looks like when it is finally refused by its object. The Prophet ﷺ expressed this in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"Verily My mercy precedes My wrath."* (al-Bukhārī 7454; Muslim 2751)

*Second, the warning is itself mercy.* The punishment described in this verse is announced in advance by a man who loves the person he is warning. That is the structure of the entire passage: a son pleading with his father to avoid a catastrophe. Nothing about that arrangement suggests a God eager to punish. *Third, the Name disciplines the reader against a false image of God.* The sūrah has used *al-Raḥmān* sixteen times, and it will not permit the reader to construct a God who is merciful in the abstract and vindictive in practice. The punishment comes from the Compassionate, which means it is just, measured, and — in the strictest sense — not incompatible with His mercy, whatever else must be said about it.

**The Word Walī: Friendship as Allegiance**

The verse's most discussed term is *waliyyan* — "friend," in the phrase *fa-takūna li'l-Shayṭāni waliyyā*, "such that you will become a friend of Satan." The root *w-l-y* — the same root encountered at v. 5 (*mawālī*) and v. 6 (*waliyyan*) — denotes nearness, allegiance, protection, and authority. The commentators were precise about what it means here, and their analysis runs in two directions. *The negative direction: what it does not mean.* It does not mean that Satan will befriend him in any affectionate sense. The Qur'an is unambiguous that Satan is the enemy of mankind (*"Verily Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy"*, 35:6) and that on the Last Day he will abandon his followers utterly:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decreed: 'Verily God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you. I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me. So do not blame me, but blame yourselves. I cannot help you, nor can you help me. Verily I disbelieved in your associating me with God before.' Verily for the wrongdoers is a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 14:22)

That verse is the definitive statement of what alliance with Satan actually yields: nothing. The ally deserts, disclaims, and disowns. *The positive direction: what it does mean.* Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī give two readings, both true. The first is that it means *companionship in punishment* — that he will be a companion and cohabitant with Satan in the Fire (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī). The second, and deeper, is that he will become one of those through whom Satan's influence operates in the world. That second reading deserves expansion, because it is the sūrah's most searching insight. The Qur'an's portrait of the *walī* is essentially one of *channel*. A *walī Allāh* — a friend of God — is one through whom God's grace flows to others. Conversely, a *walī* of Satan is one through whom Satan's influence flows. The alliance is not merely a destination; it is a function. The Qur'an states the positive counterpart in the verses that define sainthood in Islam:

> *"Unquestionably, the friends of God (*awliyāʾ Allāh*) — no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve: those who believe and are reverent. For them are glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter. There is no changing the Words of God; that is the supreme triumph."* (Qur'an 10:62–64)

The symmetry is exact, and the sūrah is asking its reader to consider which channel he is. The question is not whether a person considers himself religious, since both parties in the verse would have described themselves that way. The question is which way the influence runs — whether he is a channel through which God's mercy reaches others, or a channel through which Satan's invitation does. The image admits of no neutral position, and that is precisely the sūrah's point: a person is always transmitting something, and the only question available to him is what.

**Theological Precision: Neither Dualism Nor Diminishment**

The verse raises a question that Islamic theology has always handled with care: does describing Satan as having "friends" imply two rival powers? The answer is no, and the Qur'an's framework makes this clear on several grounds. Satan is a creature (18:50). He has no authority over those who believe and rely on God (16:99). His authority is *only* over those who take him as an ally (16:100) — which means the alliance is established by the human being, not imposed by Satan. And on the Last Day he disclaims his followers entirely (14:22). So the relationship is not symmetrical. God is the Lord; Satan is a rebel with limited, delegated, and temporary scope. The "friendship" is real in its consequences and unreal in its promises. The Prophet ﷺ warned about the practical danger in a ḥadīth that captures the asymmetry precisely:

> *"Satan flows through the son of Adam like blood."* (al-Bukhārī 7171; Muslim 2174)

The image is of something penetrating and pervasive — and the Prophet ﷺ paired the warning with the remedy, which is the consistent pattern of his teaching: he never described a danger without supplying what to do about it, and the remedy he supplied here is not a technique of resistance but an occupation of the space. Remembrance of God, the recitation of the Qur'an, and the prescribed supplications — recited in the morning and the evening, at sleep, on entering and leaving, and at the thresholds of the day — fill the very intervals in which the invitation would otherwise arrive. The tradition's insight is that a space already occupied is a space not available, and that the defence against a whisper is not silence but a different sound.

**The Verse's Practical Use for the Reader**

Two applications suggest themselves, and both are direct. *The first is about how to warn.* Abraham's fear is stated in the first person: *I fear for you.* Not "you will be punished," not "God will destroy you," but *I am afraid for you*. That is the difference between a warning and a threat. A threat is delivered from a position of safety; a warning is delivered from a position of shared risk. The Prophet ﷺ embodied it:

> *"The believers, in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion, are like one body: if one part of it complains, the rest of the body is afflicted with sleeplessness and fever."* (al-Bukhārī 6011; Muslim 2586)

*The second is about what to fear for others.* Abraham's fear is not that his father will be poor, disgraced, or unsuccessful. It is that he will end up allied with a rebel against the Compassionate. The sūrah is thus setting the hierarchy of concerns: what matters about a person is not their circumstances but their allegiance. That is a corrective to a very common inversion. Most parents fear the wrong things for their children — failure, poverty, discomfort — and rarely fear the thing Abraham feared. The sūrah's scale of value puts allegiance first, and everything else at a considerable distance.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:46

> **He said, "Do you reject my gods, O Abraham? If you cease not, I shall surely stone you. Take leave of me for a long while!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Father's Answer: Four Short Sentences**

The sūrah gives the father's entire response in one verse, and every element of it is revealing: *qāla a-rāghibun anta ʿan ālihatī yā Ibrāhīmu la-in lam tantahi la-arjumannaka wa-hjurnī maliyyā* — "He said, 'Do you reject my gods, O Abraham? If you cease not, I shall surely stone you. Take leave of me for a long while!'" There are four moves: a question, a threat, and a banishment, with the son's name inserted in the middle.

**"Do You Reject My Gods?" — The Possessive**

The first word of the reply is the tell: *ālihatī* — "**my** gods." The father does not defend the gods. He does not argue that they hear, or see, or avail, or are worthy. He does not answer any of Abraham's four arguments. He says: *my* gods. The commentators noticed this, and it is one of the most psychologically acute moments in the sūrah. The father's attachment is not intellectual; it is possessive. The gods are his — his inheritance, his community's, his identity's. Abraham's arguments were about truth; his father's response is about ownership. And the two are operating on entirely different planes, which is why no resolution is possible. This is a pattern the sūrah will record again at v. 81, of those who *"have taken gods apart from God, that they might be a strength for them"* — the language of what the gods do for the worshipper, not of whether they are true. The possessive also explains the intensity of the reaction. A person who is wrong about a matter of fact can be corrected without loss of face. A person whose identity is invested in the error cannot, which is why the response to correction is rage rather than argument. The Qur'an records the same possessive logic in the responses of other communities, and the recurrence is the point: what is being defended is never a proposition but a possession. The objector does not say that the claim is false; he says that abandoning it would cost him what is his — his fathers' religion, his standing, his people. The argument from inherited ownership appears wherever the Qur'an reports a rejection, and its consistency is what allows a reader to identify it when he meets it in himself:

> *"And they said: 'O Shuʿayb! Do your prayers command you that we should abandon what our fathers worshipped, or that we should not do with our wealth what we please?'"* (Qur'an 11:87)

> *"They said: 'Have you come to turn us away from what we found our fathers upon?'"* (Qur'an 10:78)

In every case the objection is to being turned away from *ours* — never a defence of *truth*. The observation is worth holding onto, because it explains why the Qur'an so rarely answers the rejections it records with evidence. The rejection was not produced by a deficit of evidence and cannot be repaired by a surplus of it. What is being protected is an identity, and an identity cannot be argued with; it can only be outgrown, and outgrowing it is precisely the thing the objector is refusing to do. This is also why the Qur'an's responses so often take the form of warning rather than demonstration — the obstacle is not in the mind.

**"O Abraham" — The Naming**

The father addresses his son by his proper name: *yā Ibrāhīmu*. It is the only time in this passage that he does so, and the effect is chilling precisely because of the contrast with what Abraham has been saying. Abraham has said *yā abati* — "O my father" — four times (vv. 42, 43, 44, 45). Each address was affectionate, and each was an appeal to the relationship. The father's single address is formal, nominal, and, in the Arabic rhetorical tradition, distancing. He does not say "my son." He says "Abraham." The sūrah is showing the reader the moment the relationship is severed — and it is severed by the father, not by the son. Abraham will continue to address him as "my father" in his own response (implicitly, in the content of v. 47) and will continue to pray for him. The rupture is unilateral.

**"I Shall Surely Stone You"**

The threat is *la-arjumannaka* — "I shall surely stone you." The verb comes from the root *r-j-m*, meaning to stone, to pelt with stones. The commentators were nearly unanimous on what is meant here, and their reading is careful. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Thaʿlabī all state that the phrase is understood by most to mean that he would *curse* him or "stone him with words" — that is, to revile him and speak evil of him — rather than to stone him physically. Some, however, recorded the physical possibility as well (al-Qurṭubī). Two considerations support the non-literal reading. First, there is a well-established Arabic idiom: *rajama bi'l-ghayb* (to throw stones at the unseen, i.e., to speak in conjecture) and *rajama fulānan bi-kalāmih* (to assail someone with speech). Second, the same verb is used elsewhere in the Qur'an in clearly non-physical senses: *"And they [the jinn] thought no evil of them, but they stoned [i.e., slandered] them with a violent stoning"* — and, most tellingly, of Satan: *"And verily We have made the sky a guarded ceiling, but they turn away from its signs... and [they say] 'a man possessed' — and they stoned [slandered] him"* (cf. 15:17–18; 26:116). The Qur'an will record a similar threat against Noah, in a passage that makes the non-literal sense almost certain, since the punishment described is one a community can actually inflict while the one a reader expects it to mean is not. The commentators were uniform on the reading, and the reason is textual rather than merely cautious:

> *"They said: 'If you do not desist, O Noah, you will surely be among those who are stoned.'"* (Qur'an 26:116)

Commentators read that as a threat of being reviled and ostracised — the punishment of the outcast in a small community, and in such a community a punishment more severe than anything the state could inflict. The same reading applies here, and it fits the third element of the verse: *take leave of me for a long while.* The father is not announcing that he will kill his son; he is announcing that he will stop speaking to him, and the fact that this is what he reaches for as a threat tells the reader what the relationship consisted of. Exile from a household is the severest available sentence in a world where the household is the whole of a person's standing.

**"Take Leave of Me for a Long While"**

The final sentence is *wa-hjurnī maliyyā* — "Take leave of me / avoid me for a long while." The imperative *hjurnī* (root *h-j-r*) means to abandon, to separate from, to cut off. It is the root of *hijrah* — the emigration that would become the defining event of the Islamic calendar — and of *hajara*, to boycott. The instruction is a complete severance of social relations. The word *maliyyan* means "for a long time," "for a good while." Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the commentators' gloss on the intent: *"Take leave of me so that you will be safe from my punishment"* — that is, the banishment is presented as a protection. Whether or not that was his actual motive, the effect is the same: Abraham is to be cut off. The commentators also noted the irony, which the sūrah makes explicit in the next verse: Abraham *accepts* the banishment. He had been preparing to withdraw anyway (v. 48), and his father has now given him the instruction he needed. What the father intends as punishment, God uses as release.

**A Note on the Word Hijrah**

Because the root *h-j-r* appears here, it is worth pausing on its significance for the sūrah's original audience. The first Muslims had, at the time of this sūrah's revelation, already experienced a *hijrah* — the migration to Abyssinia, in which a group of the Prophet's followers left Makkah and took refuge under a Christian king. Sūrah Maryam, as the introduction noted, is the sūrah that was recited to that king. So when the Quraysh heard *wa-hjurnī maliyyā* — "take leave of me for a long while" — they were hearing the description of their own action toward their own children. The sūrah is holding up a mirror. Abraham's father banished him, and Abraham became the father of nations. The Quraysh banished their sons and daughters, and those sons and daughters found refuge with a foreign king. The sūrah's message to both sides is the same: exile for the sake of God is not a diminution but a migration, and God gives to those who leave what He does not give to those who stay.

**The Failure of the Argument, and What It Teaches**

The verse records the collapse of the conversation, and it is worth asking what went wrong, because the sūrah has shown Abraham doing everything right. He argued from observable fact (v. 42).
