He claimed revelation (v. 43). He identified the real agency behind the error (v. 44). He warned of the consequence out of love (v. 45). And he received, in return, a threat and a banishment. The sūrah's answer to that outcome is theological rather than therapeutic: guidance is not produced by the quality of the argument. The Qur'an states this to the Prophet ﷺ in the plainest terms:

> *"Verily you do not guide whom you love; but God guides whom He wills, and He knows best those who are guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

And the same limit is stated in a passage that addresses the Prophet ﷺ directly at a moment when his desire to see his people believe had become acute — which is to say that these verses are not a rebuke but a consolation, and one that anyone who has argued with a person he loves will recognise. The Prophet ﷺ is told that the outcome was never placed in his hands, and that his task is the delivery of the reminder rather than the production of the response:

> *"So remind, if the reminder should benefit; he who fears will be reminded, but the most wretched will avoid it..."* (Qur'an 87:9–11)

That is not a counsel of despair about *daʿwah*; it is a protection against burnout in it. The prophet's obligation is the message, not the result. And it is the ground of the remarkable response Abraham makes in the next verse: he does not repeat himself, does not escalate, does not argue further. He says "peace," promises to pray, and leaves. The practical lesson is one that anyone who has tried to reason with an entrenched relative will recognise. There is a point beyond which further argument is not persuasion but abrasion. Abraham reached it after four attempts and stopped. The sūrah presents that stopping as wisdom, not as failure.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:47

> **He said, "Peace be upon you! I shall seek forgiveness for you from my Lord. Verily He has been gracious unto me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response That Refuses to Escalate**

Abraham's answer to his father's threat and banishment is one of the most admired passages in the Qur'an, and its power lies entirely in what it does not do: *qāla salāmun ʿalayka sa-astaghfiru laka rabbī innahu kāna bī ḥafiyyā* — "He said, 'Peace be upon you! I shall seek forgiveness for you from my Lord. Verily He has been gracious unto me.'" He does not defend himself. He does not repeat his arguments. He does not threaten back, or express resentment, or point out that he is right. He says three things: peace, a promise to pray, and a statement about God's graciousness to him. The commentators were struck by the disproportion. Threatened with stoning and banished, Abraham replies with a blessing. The sūrah is presenting a model of response to hostility that the tradition has held up ever since as the Prophetic standard — and, indeed, the Qur'an prescribes it explicitly:

> *"And the servants of the Compassionate are those who walk humbly upon the earth, and when the ignorant address them, they say: 'Peace.'"* (Qur'an 25:63)

> *"And when they hear idle talk, they turn away therefrom and say: 'Unto us our deeds, and unto you your deeds. Peace be upon you! We do not seek out the ignorant.'"* (Qur'an 28:55)

**What "Peace Be Upon You" Means Here**

The commentators noted an important nuance: this is not a greeting. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Thaʿlabī all state that Abraham's response is not meant as a salutation but as an indication that he will discontinue the confrontation — that he does not intend to fight his father, and that his father has nothing to fear from him. That reading is essential, because it preserves the verse from a common misunderstanding. Muslims greet one another with *al-salāmu ʿalaykum*, and the phrase here is the same. But the function is different: it is a *declaration of non-aggression*. Abraham is saying, in effect: I am not your enemy. You may banish me, but I will not harm you, and you need not fear me. The distinction matters because it shows what the phrase is for. *Salām* — peace, safety, wholeness — is what a person offers when he has no intention of retaliation. It is the opposite of the curse, and in a tribal society in which a curse was a serious weapon, the refusal to curse was itself a statement. The Prophet ﷺ taught the same reflex in his own conduct, and the tradition records it. When the people of Ṭāʾif stoned him and drove him out, bleeding, the angel of the mountains came to him and offered to crush the town between two mountains. He replied:

> *"Nay; I hope that God will bring forth from their loins people who will worship God alone, associating nothing with Him."* (al-Bukhārī 3231; Muslim 1795)

That is Abraham's response, in a different century, from a different prophet, in the same register — and the sameness is the sūrah's argument. The register in question is the one the Qur'an gives every prophet at the moment of separation: the withdrawal of association, and with it an explicit refusal to assume the outcome. Abraham does not curse his father and does not predict his father's end. He states what he will do and leaves his father's standing with God, which is the only place it was ever going to be settled.

**"I Shall Seek Forgiveness for You"**

The second element is a promise: *sa-astaghfiru laka rabbī* — "I shall seek forgiveness for you from my Lord." Three things are worth noting. *The tense.* The prefixed *sa-* indicates the future: *I shall*. Abraham is committing to an ongoing action, not performing it on the spot. He will keep praying for his father. *The object.* He prays for *forgiveness* — that is, he asks God to pardon the man who has just threatened and banished him. There is no hint of "I hope you get what you deserve." *The complexity.* This is one of the most theologically delicate promises in the Qur'an, because the outcome is recorded elsewhere and it is not what Abraham hoped. The Qur'an states:

> *"And Abraham's seeking forgiveness for his father was only because of a promise he had made to him. But when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he dissociated himself from him. Verily Abraham was most tender-hearted, forbearing."* (Qur'an 9:114)

So there are two stages. First, Abraham promised and prayed. Second, when it became clear — through revelation, or through his father's persistence until death in disbelief — that his father was an enemy of God, he desisted. The sequence is important: Abraham's prayer was not a mistake, and his desisting was not a failure of love. Both were obedience at different moments. The tradition made this a general rule, and the jurists derived from it the prohibition of praying for the forgiveness of those who have died in disbelief:

> *"It is not for the Prophet and those who believe to ask forgiveness for those who associate partners with God, even if they are near of kin, after it has become clear to them that they are the inhabitants of Hellfire."* (Qur'an 9:113)

The verse is explicit that the prohibition applies *after it has become clear*, and the qualification is doing a great deal of work — enough that the jurists built rulings on it rather than treating the verse as a flat ban. Before that point, a believer may pray for a living relative's guidance, and the tradition's practice is full of such prayers; after it, the matter belongs to God. The distinction is between praying *for* a person and praying *about* a settled fact, and the prohibition attaches to the second. What has become clear has become clear, and asking God to reverse what He has declared is not intercession but contention.

**The Ḥadīth of the Meeting, and Why It Is Heartbreaking**

The commentators associated with this passage one of the most affecting narrations in the entire ḥadīth corpus, recorded by al-Bukhārī, and they did so because the narration supplies the emotional content of the principle this verse states. The legal rule is that a believer does not pray for a relative who died upon rejection; the narration is what that rule costs:

> *"Abraham will meet his father Āzar on the Day of Resurrection, and there will be dust and darkness upon Āzar's face. Abraham will say to him: 'Did I not say to you, do not disobey me?' His father will say to him: 'Today I will not disobey you.' And Abraham will say: 'O Lord, You promised me that You would not disgrace me on the Day they are resurrected — and what disgrace is greater than that my father should be far away [from mercy]?' So God will say: 'Verily I have forbidden the Garden to the disbelievers.' Then it will be said: 'O Abraham, what is beneath your feet?' And he will look, and there will be a [filthy] male hyena, which will be seized by its legs and thrown into the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 3350, from Abū Hurayrah)

The narration is frequently cited in connection with this verse, and its function is to show that God honoured Abraham's promise without violating His own justice: the disgrace was averted, the promise was kept, and the judgement stood. The commentators were careful to note that the transformation of Āzar's form is a mercy to Abraham rather than a deceit — a point the scholars discussed at length. For the reader, the narration gives the darkest possible content to the promise in this verse. Abraham will keep praying for his father right up to the Resurrection, and the prayer will not change the outcome. That is the hardest form of love there is: love that does not get what it asks for.

**"Verily He Has Been Gracious Unto Me"**

The third element is the most personal: *innahu kāna bī ḥafiyyā* — "Verily He has been gracious unto me." The word *ḥafiyy* comes from the root *ḥ-f-w*, whose senses include *to be kind, attentive, gracious, and to inquire solicitously after someone*. It is a word of intimacy: not merely "He has been good to me," but "He has been attentively, persistently gracious to me." Why does Abraham say this here, at the end of a sentence about praying for his father? The commentators gave the reason, and it is psychologically exact: it is the ground of his confidence. Abraham is promising to pray for someone else, and the basis of his expectation is his own experience of being answered. *God has been gracious to me; therefore I expect Him to be gracious to those I pray for.* That is the same logic Zachariah used at v. 4: *"And in calling upon Thee, my Lord, I have never been wretched."* Both prophets argue from the record of their own answered prayers to the hope of future answers. And both arguments are honoured by the Qur'an, which is to say that the tradition regards this as a legitimate and praiseworthy way to approach God. The practical conclusion is direct, and it is one of the most useful things in the sūrah. A person's confidence in prayer is built by *keeping a record*. The believer who has noticed what God has done before is the believer who can pray with expectation now. The one who has kept no such record prays, if at all, with the flatness of someone addressing a possibility. The Prophet ﷺ taught the same discipline:

> *"Be mindful of God in times of ease and He will be mindful of you in times of hardship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Sūrah's Portrait of a Prophet's Manners**

Taken together, vv. 42–47 constitute the Qur'an's most complete single lesson in how to disagree with someone you love about something that matters — and the completeness is worth remarking on, because the situation is the one the tradition's literature returns to most often and the one its readers find hardest. What the passage supplies is not a tone but a sequence, and the sequence is what keeps a person from collapsing into either of the two failures: harshness, which abandons the relationship, or silence, which abandons the truth. Abraham's method, extracted from these six verses, runs as follows:

1. **Address the person, not the position.** He says "O my father" four times and never once uses a hostile epithet.
2. **Begin with what is observable.** He starts with the fact that the idols cannot see or hear — something his father cannot deny.
3. **Claim revelation humbly.** "Knowledge has come to me" — received, not earned.
4. **Name the real stakes.** Not "you are wrong" but "the end of this is ruin."
5. **Stop when the argument is spent.** He does not repeat himself after the refusal.
6. **Refuse retaliation.** "Peace be upon you."
7. **Pray for the person who wronged you.** And keep praying.
8. **Do not take responsibility for the outcome.**

That last item is what v. 48 will make explicit, and it is what makes the whole sequence bearable, because without it the preceding five items would simply be a description of a man losing his father. Abraham does what he can do and leaves the rest to God — and the leaving is not resignation but a recognition of where the matter actually sits. The distinction matters for anyone in this position: a person who has delivered the message and then stops worrying is not a person who has stopped caring, and a person who keeps worrying after the delivery is not being more faithful. He is carrying a weight that was never assigned to him.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:48

> **"And I withdraw myself from you and that which you call upon apart from God. And I call upon my Lord; it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Withdrawal Accepted, and Transformed**

Abraham's speech closes with a declaration that takes his father's curse and converts it into a blessing: *wa-aʿtazilukum wa-mā tadʿūna min dūni Allāhi wa-adʿū rabbī ʿasā allā akūna bi-duʿāʾi rabbī shaqiyyā* — "And I withdraw myself from you and that which you call upon apart from God. And I call upon my Lord; it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched." The verb *aʿtazilukum* (root *ʿ-z-l*) means "I separate myself from you," "I withdraw from you." It is the same root as *iʿtizāl*, and it is the fourth occurrence of the sūrah's withdrawal-motif — after Zachariah's seclusion (vv. 2–3), Mary's withdrawal east (v. 16), and Mary's withdrawal far off (v. 22). Note precisely what is being withdrawn from: *you* — the father and the people — *and that which you call upon apart from God*. The separation is from two things at once: the persons and their objects of worship. The Qur'an's formulation is careful, because a person could leave his people and remain attached to their beliefs, or renounce the beliefs and remain entangled with the people. Abraham does both, and the sūrah insists on both.

**The Banishment Becomes a Migration**

There is a quiet transformation in this verse that deserves to be noticed. In v. 46, the father commanded: *wa-hjurnī maliyyā* — "Take leave of me for a long while." In v. 48, Abraham says: *wa-aʿtazilukum* — "I withdraw from you." The same act, but the agency has changed. What was an expulsion has become a decision. What was a punishment has become an act of worship. That is one of the sūrah's most consoling patterns, and it recurs throughout the Qur'an and the Sunnah. God takes what people intend as harm and converts it into the means of His servant's deliverance. The brothers' plot against Joseph became the path to Egypt (12:15–21). Pharaoh's pursuit of the Israelites became the occasion of his own destruction and their salvation (26:52–68). The Quraysh's boycott of the Prophet's clan became the pressure that opened Makkah. The commentators, including al-Thaʿlabī, understood this verse as Abraham's departure from his family to migrate to the Holy Land — that is, as the beginning of the *hijrah* that would take him to Palestine and eventually, through Ishmael, to Makkah. The sūrah thus treats the banishment as the moment of commissioning.

**"And I Call Upon My Lord"**

The second clause is the positive counterpart: *wa-adʿū rabbī* — "And I call upon my Lord." The structure of the verse is a complete statement of *tawḥīd* in two sentences. *I withdraw from you and from what you invoke besides God* — the negation. *And I call upon my Lord* — the affirmation. Every prophet in the Qur'an says both halves, and the Qur'an makes the pattern the formula of the religion itself:

> *"And [mention] when Abraham said to his father and his people: 'Verily I am innocent of what you worship, except Him Who created me; and verily He will guide me.' And he made it a word enduring among his progeny, that they might return."* (Qur'an 43:26–28)

> *"There is no compulsion in religion. Sound judgment has become clear from error: whoever disbelieves in false deities and believes in God has taken hold of the firmest handhold, which will never break."* (Qur'an 2:256)

The order is significant: dissociation first, then affirmation. One cannot genuinely turn to God while still holding on to what competes with Him — which is why every conversion in the Qur'an has this two-part shape, and why the two parts always appear in this order. The negative is not a preliminary purification that makes the positive possible; it is simply what the positive means. To turn to God is, by definition, to turn away from what one was facing, and a conversion that has not yet named what it has left behind has not yet happened.

**"It May Be That... I Will Not Be Wretched"**

The verse closes with a phrase that the sūrah has deliberately prepared, and the preparation is the reason the phrase lands with the force it does. *ʿasā allā akūna bi-duʿāʾi rabbī shaqiyyā* — "it may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched." These are the exact words Zachariah used at v. 4, placed here in the mouth of Abraham, and the repetition across the centuries is the sūrah's most compact statement that the religion is one thing. The word *shaqiyyā* — "wretched" — is the sūrah's third use of the root *sh-q-w*, and the pattern is now complete:

- **v. 4 (Zachariah):** *"And in calling upon Thee, my Lord, I have never been wretched."* — the past: answered.
- **v. 32 (Jesus):** *"And He has not made me domineering, wretched."* — the condition: preserved.
- **v. 48 (Abraham):** *"It may be that in calling upon my Lord, I will not be wretched."* — the future: hoped for.

Three tenses of one confidence. And note that in all three cases the word is attached to *calling upon God* — *bi-duʿāʾi rabbī*, "in my supplication to my Lord." The sūrah has woven a single doctrine across the chapter: **supplication is the thing that saves a person from wretchedness.** The commentators, led by al-Ṭabarī, took the meaning here to be what it was at v. 4: that God would answer his prayers and supplications, not merely that he would escape perdition. The two are connected, since unanswered prayer in the Qur'anic frame is the condition of the wretched: *"And your Lord says: 'Call upon Me and I will respond to you. Verily those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, humiliated'"* (40:60).

**The Word ʿAsā — "It May Be"**

There is a subtlety in Abraham's wording that the commentators noticed and that repays attention. He says *ʿasā* — "perhaps," "it may be that" — rather than a confident assertion. Why? Two readings were offered, and they are complementary. The first is that it expresses *humility before God*. Even a prophet who has received revelation does not presume upon the outcome. The sūrah's Abraham has just been promised nothing about his father, and he is about to leave everything he has; *ʿasā* is the language of a servant who knows that God is not obliged. The second is that it expresses *the limits of his knowledge*. He does not know what will happen to him. He is walking into the unknown with a promise he has not yet seen fulfilled. Both readings converge on the same practical point, which is that faith is not the absence of uncertainty about outcomes. It is the presence of trust in the One who determines them. The Qur'an's own vocabulary makes room for this: *ʿasā* appears in the speech of the righteous throughout the Book — *"Perhaps my Lord will guide me to a sounder way than this"* (18:24); *"Perhaps my Lord will forgive us our sins"* (in the supplications of the believers). The word is not doubt; it is the grammar of hope.

**What Abraham Prayed For**

The commentators offered two accounts of what Abraham's supplication actually was, and the second is better supported. Some held that he prayed for his father's guidance (al-Qurṭubī). Others — and al-Qurṭubī prefers this — held that he called upon his Lord to grant him family and children who would strengthen him, now that he was alone and separated from his father and his people. The second reading is almost certainly correct, because the very next verse records the answer: *"So when he had withdrawn from them... We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob"* (v. 49). The sequence is explicit: withdrawal, supplication, gift. And it fits the sūrah's architecture perfectly. This is a chapter about God's compassion in bestowing blessed children upon prophets and holy people — John to Zachariah (vv. 7–9), Jesus to Mary (vv. 19–21), and now Isaac and Jacob to Abraham (v. 49). Abraham, having lost his father and his people, is given a family. That is the sūrah's answer to exile.

**The Sūrah's Practical Teaching on Separation**

For the reader, this verse addresses one of the most painful human experiences: the necessity of separating from people one loves. Abraham's situation is the hardest version of it. He was not leaving because he had stopped caring. He was leaving *because* he cared — because staying would require him to affirm what he knew to be false, and affirming it would have destroyed him. And he leaves without rancour, without a final denunciation, and with a promise to keep praying. The sūrah's distinction is worth stating carefully, because it is often abused in both directions. On the one hand, the Qur'an does not license withdrawal as a punishment, a power-play, or an expression of superiority. Abraham withdraws only after four attempts at persuasion, only when the refusal is total, and only from the *belief and its practice* — not from the duties of love. On the other hand, it does not permit a person to remain in a situation that requires him to affirm what he believes to be false, simply because leaving is painful. The Prophet ﷺ drew the line in a ḥadīth that the tradition has always cited in this connection:

> *"There is no obedience to a created being in disobedience to the Creator."* (Aḥmad; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And the Qur'an supplies the accompanying obligation, quoted earlier at v. 43: *"accompany them in this world with kindness"* (31:15). Both together: separation from the error, continued kindness to the person. The pairing is the whole of the tradition's position on the subject, and it is worth stating as a single rule because each half is routinely detached from the other. Separation without kindness produces the harshness that drives people away; kindness without separation produces the compliance that quietly concedes the point. The sūrah's Abraham does both, and the sūrah's next verse will show what that separation produced.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:49

> **So when he had withdrawn from them and that which they called upon apart from God, We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob, and each We made a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sequence the Sūrah Has Been Building**

Here is the sūrah's thesis in a single verse: *fa-lammā iʿtazalahum wa-mā yaʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi wahabnā lahu Isḥāqa wa-Yaʿqūba wa-kullan jaʿalnā nabiyyan* — "So when he had withdrawn from them and that which they called upon apart from God, We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob, and each We made a prophet." The Arabic opens with *fa-lammā* — "so when," "and then when" — a construction that binds the gift to the withdrawal as its consequence. The sūrah has now stated the pattern three times in explicit causal form:

- Mary withdrew, and God sent His Spirit (vv. 16–17).
- Mary withdrew with him to a place far off, and God provided (vv. 22–26).
- Abraham withdrew, and God bestowed Isaac and Jacob (vv. 48–49).

The principle is not that withdrawal is a technique for obtaining gifts — that would make it a transaction, and the sūrah's theology is not transactional. The principle is that *the removal of what competes with God creates the space in which God gives*. A heart fully occupied cannot receive, and a life fully entangled cannot be redirected. The withdrawal is not the price of the gift; it is the vacating of the room.

**Isaac and Jacob: The Gift in Detail**

Isaac (*Isḥāq*) is the son of Abraham and Sarah, born to them in their old age, as the Qur'an narrates in several places — and the fact of the old age is the point the sūrah has been constructing since its first narrative. The child is given to a woman who had despaired of children and a man long past expecting them, which places Isaac in the same category as John and Jesus rather than in the category of ordinary sons:

> *"And his wife was standing, and she laughed; so We gave her glad tidings of Isaac, and after Isaac, of Jacob."* (Qur'an 11:71–72)

Jacob (*Yaʿqūb*), also called Israel, is Isaac's son and Abraham's grandson — the father of the twelve tribes, and the ancestor of Moses, Aaron, David, Solomon, Zachariah, John, Mary, and Jesus. The gift is thus not one child but *a lineage*. Al-Ṭabarī noted that the gift of these two prophets was far better than what Abraham had lost — he had lost a father and a people, and gained a prophetic line that would produce most of the prophets of Israel. The Qur'an states the same gift in several other passages, and the accumulation is itself testimony to its importance:

> *"And We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob — each of them We guided; and Noah We guided before..."* (Qur'an 6:84)

> *"And We bestowed upon him Isaac, and after Isaac, Jacob — and each of them We made righteous."* (Qur'an 21:72–73)

> *"And We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob, and We placed among his progeny prophethood and the Book..."* (Qur'an 29:27)

That last verse is the key to the whole gift, and it explains why the sūrah treats the birth of a son as an answer rather than merely as a comfort. God did not merely give Abraham sons; He placed *prophethood and scripture* in his line, and it remained there for fifteen centuries. The gift is therefore not personal but historical: what is being granted is a succession, and the child is the vehicle of it. This is why the sūrah moves immediately from the naming of Isaac and Jacob to the statement that God made each of them a prophet — the prophecy is the substance and the sonship is the delivery.

**"Each We Made a Prophet" — A Grammatical Detail**

The phrase *kullan jaʿalnā nabiyyan* — "each We made a prophet" — raises a small question the commentators addressed: does "each" include Abraham? Al-Ṭabarī states that the statement is meant to include Abraham as well as Isaac and Jacob. The logic is that Abraham was, after all, a prophet before either of them existed, and the verse's purpose is to affirm that the whole family stood in the same relation to God. The Qur'an confirms both Isaac's and Jacob's prophethood elsewhere:

> *"And We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob — each of them We guided; and Noah We guided before, and among his progeny David and Solomon, Job and Joseph, Moses and Aaron... and Zachariah and John and Jesus and Elias — each was of the righteous."* (Qur'an 6:84–85)

The sūrah's emphasis on "each" is significant for a further reason, and the significance is grammatical as much as theological: the word is distributive rather than collective, so that Jacob is not included by implication through his father but named as a recipient in his own right. Prophethood in Abraham's line is not a single event but a *succession*. God gave Abraham not one prophet son but a prophetic house, and the sūrah's catalogue of prophets in vv. 51–57 will trace exactly that succession through Moses, Aaron, Ishmael, and Idrīs. The chapter is thereby presenting prophethood as a lineage rather than as a series of unrelated appointments, which is the ground of its claim that the religion of all of them is one religion.

**The Ordering of the Sūrah's Gifts: A Theological Reading**

It is worth stepping back to see the sūrah's complete list of gifts, because they are arranged in a deliberate ascent, and the arrangement is easier to miss than the individual items. The chapter does not present a set of blessings side by side; it presents a sequence in which each gift is larger than the one before it, and a reader who follows the sequence is being led somewhere rather than merely informed:

- **John:** given to a man whose bones had failed and a woman who was barren (vv. 4–9). Impossible by *age*.
- **Jesus:** given to a woman no man had touched (vv. 19–22). Impossible by *category*.
- **Isaac and Jacob:** given to a man who had lost everything (vv. 48–49). Impossible by *circumstance*.
- **Aaron:** given to Moses as a helper (v. 53). A gift of *support*.
- **Prophethood and renown:** given to Ishmael and Idrīs (vv. 54–57). Gifts of *station*.

The sūrah is showing that God gives to every kind of impossibility, and that the pattern in every case is the same: human capacity is exhausted, and then God gives. The uniformity is the teaching. What varies is only which capacity runs out — a womb, a marriage, a body, a lineage, a childhood — and the variation is what allows a reader to locate his own impossibility somewhere in the list. The sūrah's argument is not that these things happen but that they happen to the exhausted, and that exhaustion is therefore not a disqualification but the condition in which the gift arrives.

**The Withdrawal's Practical Meaning**

The verse has been used throughout Islamic history as the textual basis for *ʿuzlah* — temporary withdrawal from society for the sake of worship and clarity — and, in the Sufi tradition, for the discipline of *khalwah*. The commentators were careful about its limits, and their care should be noted. Ibn ʿAjībah, commenting on this very sūrah, stated the balanced position with unusual clarity: withdrawal from creatures is a necessary condition *at the beginning* of the path, but once the seeker has reached a certain station it becomes incumbent upon him to return and mingle with people — because at that point his encounters with others increase his knowledge and complete his character. The Prophet ﷺ's own practice embodies the balance. He withdrew to the cave of Ḥirāʾ *before* the revelation (al-Bukhārī 3) and performed *iʿtikāf* in the mosque every Ramaḍān (al-Bukhārī 2026) — but he was, by every account, continuously among his Companions, teaching, judging, marrying, fighting, and grieving with them. The withdrawal was periodic; the engagement was total. The practical rule the tradition derived: **withdraw in order to return.** Solitude that produces clarity for service is Abraham's solitude. Solitude that produces contempt for people is not, and the Prophet ﷺ warned against it:

> *"The believer who mixes with people and is patient with their harm is better than the one who does not mix with them and is not patient with their harm."* (al-Tirmidhī 2507; graded ḥasan)

**The Immediate Lesson: Loss and Replacement**

Finally, the verse speaks directly to anyone who has lost something — a relationship, a community, a position — for the sake of what they believe. Abraham lost his father and his people. The sūrah does not minimise the loss; it records the threat, the banishment, and the departure. But it also records, in the very next clause, what God gave. And al-Ṭabarī's comment is the sūrah's own verdict: **what he received was far better than what he lost.** That is not a promise that every loss is followed by an equivalent replacement in this life. The Qur'an makes no such promise, and the tradition has never taught that it does. What it does assert is that the one who leaves something for God's sake is not left with nothing — and that the reckoning, when it comes, is generous beyond the scale of what was surrendered. The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in the most frequently cited ḥadīth on the subject:

> *"Whoever gives up something for the sake of God, God will replace it with something better."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad* 21961; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And the Qur'an states the general rule, in a verse the commentators cited as the principle underlying every particular promise in the Book — that the reward is not proportional to the ease of the situation but to what was borne in it:

> *"And whoever is reverent toward God, He will make for him a way out, and will provide for him from whence he does not reckon."* (Qur'an 65:2–3)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:50

> **And We bestowed upon them something of Our Mercy, and ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Gift: Mercy**

The verse adds a second bestowal to the first: *wa-wahabnā lahum min raḥmatinā wa-jaʿalnā lahum lisāna ṣidqin ʿaliyyā* — "And We bestowed upon them something of Our Mercy, and ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown." The commentators offered two readings of what "Our Mercy" refers to here, and they are not exclusive. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Thaʿlabī record that some take it to mean bounty and provision in this world — wealth, children, and the like. Others take it to include the gifts of prophethood and revelation (al-Thaʿlabī). The second reading is broader and fits the sūrah better, since the sūrah's entire economy of gifts is spiritual before it is material. Note the preposition: *min raḥmatinā* — "**something** of Our Mercy," not "Our Mercy." The partitive *min* is significant. God gives His servants a portion, not the whole; the whole belongs to Him and is not transferable. That is the same theological restraint noted at v. 17 regarding *Rūḥanā* ("Our Spirit"): God honours what He creates, but He does not transfer His attributes. And note the Name-context. This is the sūrah's fourth use of *raḥmah* in exactly the register established at v. 2 — "a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord." The sūrah has been tracing mercy from an old man's prayer (v. 2) through a virginal conception (v. 21) to a prophetic lineage (v. 50), and it will continue to the promise of love in v. 96. Mercy is the sūrah's through-line.

**"A Sublime, Faithful Renown"**

The second half of the verse contains one of the most famous idioms in the Qur'an: *lisāna ṣidqin ʿaliyyā* — literally, "a sublime tongue of truthfulness." The phrase requires unpacking, because the literal rendering is meaningless in English. *Lisān* means tongue, and by extension *speech, language, reputation* — the thing by which a person is spoken of. *Ṣidq* — the root encountered at v. 41 — means truthfulness, and by extension *sincere praise*. *ʿAliyy* (root *ʿ-l-w*) means high, exalted, sublime. So the idiom means: *a reputation that is sincerely and enduringly good, and of the highest order.* The commentators were unanimous on this, though they phrased it variously. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī, and al-Ṭabrisī all state that the phrase refers to the high reputation and sincere praise that Abraham and his family enjoy, and continue to enjoy, among people of many creeds — including not only Jews, Christians, and Muslims, but also the pre-Islamic pagan Arabs, who revered Abraham as their forefather.

**The Universality of Abraham's Reputation**

That last point deserves expansion, because it is one of the most remarkable facts about Abraham and one the sūrah is drawing attention to. No other figure in religious history is claimed by so many. Jews call him their patriarch. Christians call him the father of faith and cite him in the canon of the Mass. Muslims call him *Khalīl Allāh*, pray for him in every prayer, and trace the Kaʿbah to him. The Qur'an's own claim is that they are all, in a sense, right — and that the differences came later:

> *"O People of the Book! Why do you dispute concerning Abraham? The Torah and the Gospel were not sent down except after him. Do you not understand?... Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; rather, he was upright, a submitter, and he was not of those who associate partners with God."* (Qur'an 3:65, 67)

The sūrah's claim in this verse is that the universality of Abraham's good name is not an accident of history but a divine ordination: *We ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown.* God decreed that this man would be spoken well of, and the decree has held for four thousand years — and the phenomenon is without parallel. Abraham is claimed as an ancestor by Jews, Christians, and Muslims, and is spoken well of by almost everyone who has heard of him; no other figure in religious history is venerated across such a range of traditions. The sūrah's explanation for that fact is that it was decreed rather than achieved.

**The Connection to Abraham's Own Prayer**

The commentators noted that this verse is the answer to a specific supplication that the Qur'an records elsewhere, in Abraham's own words — which is to say that the renown described here is not a reward imposed from outside but the granting of something that was asked for. The pairing is one of the clearest instances in the Qur'an of a prayer recorded in one sūrah and its answer recorded in another, and the commentators used it to establish that the two are to be read together:

> *"And make for me faithful renown (*lisāna ṣidqin*) among later generations."* (Qur'an 26:84)

The phrase is identical. Abraham asked for it at 26:84; the sūrah reports at 19:50 that it was granted. That correspondence is one of the clearest instances in the Qur'an of a prayer and its answer recorded in two different chapters, and the commentators made much of it. It also establishes something important about the nature of reputation. Abraham did not ask for wealth, long life, victory over his enemies, or a large following. He asked for a *truthful tongue* among those who would come after him — that is, that he be remembered well and truthfully. And the Qur'an honours the request as a legitimate and high one. That is worth noting in an age in which reputation is often pursued as an end in itself. The distinction the Qur'an draws is between *lisān ṣidq* — a good name that is genuinely deserved and is a means of continued good — and the pursuit of fame, which the tradition has always regarded as corrupting. The first is a gift from God; the second is a disease. The Prophet ﷺ drew the distinction in a ḥadīth:

> *"A man may do the deeds of the people of the Fire while, in God's sight, he is among the people of the Garden; and a man may do the deeds of the people of the Garden while, in God's sight, he is among the people of the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 6493; Muslim 112)

What matters is not the reputation but the reality behind it. *Lisān ṣidq* is valuable precisely because it is *ṣidq* — true. A good name that rests on a false record is not a good name but a deception that happens to favour its subject, and the Qur'an's promise here is not that Abraham would be praised but that he would be praised for what he actually did. This is why the tradition has always treated reputation as something to be earned rather than managed: the only durable kind is the kind that survives scrutiny, and the kind that does not is not worth having.

**The Sufi Reading: Solitude and Its Fruit**

The Sufi commentators, led by Ibn ʿAjībah, read this verse as a statement about the fruits of withdrawal. His formulation is worth quoting because it has shaped Islamic devotional practice: Those who remove themselves from worldly and created things and withdraw to the "realm of Truth" in order to witness the Truth will be rewarded with spiritual knowledge and "holy gifts." And he cites his own shaykh, Abu'l-Ḥasan al-Shādhilī (d. 656/1258), who commented on this verse that the practice of solitude leads to *"the lifting of veils, the descent of Mercy, the realization of love, and 'a truthful tongue' in speech."* Note the sequence: veils lifted, mercy descends, love is realised, and *then* speech becomes truthful. The Sufi claim is that truthful speech is a *consequence* of the other three, not a technique. A person who has not been alone with God cannot, in the long run, speak truthfully — because his speech will be shaped by what he wants from his audience rather than by what he has seen. That is a hard saying, and it is worth testing against experience. Most dishonesty is not calculated; it is reflexive speech shaped by the desire to be liked. The cure is not a rule about lying but a change in whose approval is being sought — which is, precisely, what solitude is for.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument About Gifts**

Placed as it is, v. 50 completes the Abraham passage with a twofold gift that matches the twofold structure of the whole sūrah: an *inner* gift (mercy) and an *outer* gift (renown). That pairing is worth noticing, because it recurs throughout the chapter. John is given judgment and tenderness (vv. 12–13). Jesus is given the Book and blessedness (vv. 30–31). Abraham is given mercy and renown (v. 50). Moses will be given his brother and the intimate discourse (vv. 52–53). In every case, what God gives is both what the servant *is* and what the servant *becomes known for*. The sūrah is making a quietly important point about the religious life: genuine religion produces both. A person who has received grace but whose reputation is foul has not integrated the gift; a person whose reputation is excellent but whose interior is empty is, in the Qur'an's vocabulary, a hypocrite. The sūrah holds the two together, as Abraham's *lisān ṣidq* was paired with his *raḥmah*.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:51

> **And remember Moses in the Book. Verily he was devoted, and he was a messenger, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third "And Remember"**

The sūrah turns to its fourth prophet: *wa-dhkur fī'l-kitābi Mūsā innahu kāna mukhlisan wa-kāna rasūlan nabiyyan* — "And remember Moses in the Book. Verily he was devoted, and he was a messenger, a prophet." Moses is the prophet most frequently mentioned in the Qur'an — named in well over a hundred verses, with his story told at greater length and in more separate accounts than any other figure. The major passages are 2:49–74; 5:20–26; 7:103–55; 10:75–93; 18:60–82; 20:9–97; 26:10–67; 27:7–14; 28:3–46; 43:46–56; and 79:15–26. There is a reason for this, and the sūrah is invoking it. Moses is the prophet whose career most closely parallels that of Muhammad ﷺ: a prophet sent to a ruling power that refused him, who led a community out of oppression, who was given a scripture and a law, who faced the recalcitrance of his own people, and who triumphed within his lifetime. The Qur'an draws the parallel explicitly:

> *"Verily We have sent unto you a messenger, a witness over you, just as We sent unto Pharaoh a messenger."* (Qur'an 73:15)

For the Quraysh, hearing this sūrah in Makkah, the parallel would have been unmistakable and unwelcome, since the man being described is a prophet sent to a people who did not want him, and the description is being recited to a people who did not want one either. The sūrah is not subtle about the correspondence, and the original audience would have had no difficulty locating themselves in the account. What is being offered them is the chance to notice that the role they are playing in this story is the role that never ends well, and that the man they are refusing is the man the whole chapter has been preparing them to recognise.

**Mukhliṣ or Mukhlaṣ? Two Readings, Both Preserved**

The description of Moses in this verse contains a word that the reciters transmit in two forms, and the commentators discussed both at length: *mukhliṣ* or *mukhlaṣ*. *Mukhliṣ* (the reading of Ḥafṣ, and the one in general use) is the active participle of the root *kh-l-ṣ*, meaning *to be pure, sincere, unadulterated*. A *mukhliṣ* is one who is sincere — who worships God alone, without any admixture of other motives. *Mukhlaṣ* (the reading of some of the other canonical transmitters) is the passive participle of the same root: one who has been *purified*, *made sincere*, *specially chosen*. Al-Ṭabarī states that both readings can be considered correct, and he is right, because the relationship between them is one of the central themes of Islamic theology. The *mukhliṣ* — the one who is sincere — is only sincere because God made him so; and the one God has purified (*mukhlaṣ*) is purified precisely in order to be sincere. Grace and effort are not competing explanations of a single life; they are the two ends of one process. The commentators glossed *mukhliṣ* in this verse as: devoted to the One God, worshipping Him alone (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī), without hypocrisy (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī). And Ibn ʿAjībah, as always, pushed the term to its interior limit: true sincerity means to worship God *for the sake of God*, not for the blessings of this world or the next that one might attain through such worship. The refinement is severe, and the tradition has never pretended otherwise, because it excludes a great deal of what passes for devotion. A person who obeys in order to be rewarded has made the reward his object and God his means, and what he is worshipping is the thing he is facing rather than the One he claims to be facing. This is why the scholars classified the desire for Paradise as a station below the desire for God, and why they were careful to say that the lower station is valid and salvific while the higher is the station of the *ṣiddīqūn* — those described in this very verse.

**The Definition of Ikhlāṣ**

That last gloss deserves expansion, because *ikhlāṣ* — the noun from the same root — is one of the most important and most demanding concepts in Islam. At its core, *ikhlāṣ* means the removal of admixture. Gold that is *khāliṣ* is gold without alloy. A deed that is done with *ikhlāṣ* is a deed without any motive other than God. The difficulty, which the tradition acknowledged with unusual honesty, is that the heart is not transparent to itself. A person can perform an act of worship, believe it to be purely for God, and discover — years later, or on the Day of Resurrection — that a significant part of his motive was the approval of others. The Prophet ﷺ named this danger as the thing he feared most for his community, in a ḥadīth cited earlier:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk... Riyāʾ (showing off)."* (Aḥmad; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The classical definition that the scholars settled on is functional rather than psychological, and the choice of a functional test is itself instructive. *Ikhlāṣ* is present when the motive of the act would be the same if no one else ever knew about it. A psychological definition would require a person to inspect his own interior, which is precisely the exercise the tradition distrusts, since the self is unreliable about itself and the inspection quickly becomes a form of self-regard. The functional test asks a question that can actually be answered: would I still do this if it were invisible? That is a test anyone can apply, and it is why the scholars preferred it. The Qur'an states the requirement in the sharpest terms:

> *"And they were not commanded except to worship God, sincere (*mukhliṣīn*) to Him in religion, upright, and to perform the prayer and give the alms — and that is the upright religion."* (Qur'an 98:5)

And it states the reward, which is worth attending to because of what it identifies as the thing that survives. The act's value is not denied, but the motive is what is described as the residue — everything else in the deed being perishable along with the world in which it was performed:

> *"Except those who repent, and believe, and work righteousness — for those, God will replace their evil deeds with good; and God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 25:70)

**"A Messenger, a Prophet" — The Double Title**

The verse closes with *wa-kāna rasūlan nabiyyan* — "and he was a messenger, a prophet." The two titles appear together, and the commentators explained the distinction as applied to Moses specifically. He is a *rasūl* — a messenger — because he brought his people a new scripture (the Torah) and through it a new divine law. He is a *nabī* — a prophet — because he conveyed messages of warning both to the Children of Israel and to Pharaoh's people. That dual function is Moses's distinguishing feature among the messengers. He was sent to two audiences simultaneously: to liberate and legislate for his own people, and to warn and argue with the ruling power that oppressed them. The Qur'an records both commissions in a single address:

> *"Go, both of you, unto Pharaoh; verily he has transgressed. And speak to him a gentle word, that perhaps he may take heed or fear."* (Qur'an 20:43–44)

The contrast with Abraham is instructive. Abraham was, on the majority reading, both a prophet and a messenger, but the Qur'an uses only *nabī* for him in this sūrah (v. 41). Moses is given both titles explicitly here, and Ishmael will be given both at v. 54. The sūrah is thus distinguishing between the figures it catalogues, and the distinction is functional: those who brought a scripture and a law are called *rasūl*.

**Moses in the Sunnah**

The Prophet ﷺ spoke of Moses frequently and with particular warmth, and two narrations are especially relevant to this verse, since together they establish both the closeness between the two prophets and the limit the community is placed under in speaking about them. The first establishes the bond between them:

> *"I am the closest of people to Jesus son of Mary... and between me and Moses there was no messenger; rather, the messengers were sent in succession."* (al-Bukhārī 3442)

The second is the remarkable ḥadīth in which the Prophet ﷺ forbade his community to rank the prophets against one another, and specifically to rank him above Moses — a prohibition that would be merely modest if it came from anyone else, and which carries a specific legal and theological weight precisely because it comes from the one everyone agrees is the greatest of them:

> *"Do not prefer me above Moses. For on the Day of Resurrection the people will be struck unconscious, and I will be the first to regain consciousness — and there I will find Moses holding onto one of the pillars of the Throne. I will not know whether he has regained consciousness before me, or whether he was among those whom God exempted."* (al-Bukhārī 2411, 3414; Muslim 2373)

The ḥadīth is a model of how the tradition handled the ranking of prophets: by declining to rank them, and by giving the benefit of the doubt to a fellow messenger in a matter about which revelation was silent. And the Prophet ﷺ described his own experience of the Ascension in a way that places Moses at the centre of it — Moses is the one who advised him to return to his Lord and ask for a reduction in the number of daily prayers, from fifty to five:

> *"Moses said: 'Your community will not be able to bear fifty prayers a day... Go back to your Lord and ask Him to lighten it.'"* (al-Bukhārī 349; Muslim 162)

The outcome — five prayers prescribed, fifty rewarded — is the most frequently cited example in the tradition of God's generosity beyond strict reckoning, and it is cited for a reason that goes beyond the numbers. What the episode establishes is that the obligation was reduced while the reward was not, which means the reward was never a wage calculated against the effort in the first place. A wage would have been halved with the work; a gift is not, and the tradition has consistently used this narration to teach that a person's standing with God is not a function of how much he was asked to carry.

**Why the Sūrah Names Him Briefly**

Finally, note how compressed the Moses passage is: two verses (vv. 51–53) for the prophet who occupies more of the Qur'an than any other. The sūrah is not attempting a summary of his career; it is selecting precisely the element that fits its theme. The theme is: *God gives gifts to those who withdraw.* And the aspect of Moses' life the sūrah selects is the moment at which he was called away from everything — from Egypt, from Pharaoh's court, from his own people — and brought to the side of a mountain to receive a revelation, a brother, and a commission. That is the next verse.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:52

> **We called out to him from the right side of the Mount, and drew him nigh in intimate discourse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call in the Wilderness**

The sūrah's account of Moses is a single scene: *wa-nādaynāhu min jānibi al-Ṭūri al-aymani wa-qarrabnāhu najiyyā* — "We called out to him from the right side of the Mount, and drew him nigh in intimate discourse." The Mount (*al-Ṭūr*) is Mount Sinai — the mountain at which Moses received the revelation and the tablets, which the Qur'an names in several places:

> *"By the fig and the olive, and Mount Sinai, and this secure city."* (Qur'an 95:1–3)

> *"And by the Mount, and a Book inscribed..."* (Qur'an 52:1–2)

The historical setting is well established from the Qur'an's own accounts, and the details are worth holding in mind because the sūrah's silence about them is itself a choice. Moses had fled Egypt after killing a man (28:15–21), had travelled to Midian, had married and worked there for a period (28:22–28), and was journeying with his family when he saw a fire and turned aside toward it:

> *"And [mention] when Moses said to his family: 'Verily I perceive a fire. I will bring you news from it, or a burning brand, that you may warm yourselves.' And when he came to it, he was called from the right side of the valley, in the blessed spot, from the tree: 'O Moses! Verily I am God, the Lord of the worlds.'"* (Qur'an 28:29–30)

Note the sūrah's selectivity, because it is the clearest evidence that this chapter's narratives are not excerpts. It does not mention the fire, the family, the journey, or the commission. It mentions only two things: *the place* and *the nearness*. That is because those are the two things the sūrah's argument requires — and the argument, as the surrounding verses have been establishing, is about withdrawal as the condition of encounter. Every other sūrah that tells this story selects differently, and the variation is the reader's clue that each account is answering a different question rather than abbreviating the same one.

**"The Right Side of the Mount"**

The commentators gave a straightforward explanation. Al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Ṭabrisī all state that "the right side of the Mount" refers to the side of the mountain on Moses' right as he approached it from Midian and saw the burning bush in the distance (cf. 20:10; 27:7; 28:29). Alongside that literal reading, the Sufi commentators developed a symbolic one that the tradition has preserved. Al-Qushayrī and others took the *right side* as a symbol for the innermost dimension of the heart — the place Sufis describe as the locus of divine self-disclosure and of intimate discourse with God. The symbolic reading is not an alternative to the literal one, and the tradition treated it as an additional layer rather than a replacement. But it is worth understanding why it arose, because the Qur'an itself invites it. The *right* in the Qur'an is associated with honour, blessing, and the reception of the record:

> *"Then as for him who is given his book in his right hand, he will say: 'Here, read my book!'"* (Qur'an 69:19)

And the *side* (*jānib*) of the mountain is the place of turning aside — of leaving the road, of being out of the way, of no longer being on anyone's route. The combined image is of a person who steps off the path and finds that the place he stepped toward is the place of encounter. The sūrah has been building this image since its opening, and the consistency is deliberate: Zachariah withdraws to the sanctuary, Mary withdraws to an eastern place, Abraham withdraws from his father and his people, and Moses is called at the side of a mountain. In every case the withdrawal precedes the address, and the sūrah is proposing that this is not incidental but structural.

**"And Drew Him Nigh"**

The second clause is the heart of the verse: *wa-qarrabnāhu najiyyā* — "and drew him nigh in intimate discourse." The verb *qarrabnā* (root *q-r-b*, the root of *qurb*, nearness) means We brought him close. The word *najiyy* (root *n-j-w*) denotes one who is in confidential, private conversation with another — an intimate interlocutor. What kind of nearness is this? The commentators were careful, because the question touches the doctrine of God. Some of the reports describe the nearness in sensory terms: that God drew him near until he could hear the scratching of the pen on the tablets of the Torah, or until he could hear God's speech directly (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī). Others describe it in mystical terms: that there are seventy thousand veils of light and darkness between the Throne and the earth, and Moses came so close that only a single veil remained between him and his Lord (al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī). And others, notably al-Ṭabrisī, insisted on a reading that avoids the theological difficulty entirely: that the nearness means God *raised him to a station of nobility and purity*, or to the noble station in which God spoke to him outside the context of ordinary revelation. Al-Ṭabrisī expressly prefers this reading, *since it avoids the theological impossibility of discussing physical distance in relation to God, as God does not reside or inhere in any place or location*. That caveat is essential and is the settled position of Sunni theology, arrived at not by dismissing the verse's plain wording but by holding it together with the verses that preclude locating God. God is not in a place, and nearness to Him is a matter of *rank and relation*, not of metres. Theologians from the earliest period insisted on this, and the reason they insisted so firmly is that the alternative — a nearness measured in distance — requires God to be somewhere, and a God who is somewhere is a God with an extent, a direction, and a boundary. The tradition's formulation preserves the reality of the nearness while denying it a geometry:

> *"There is nothing whatsoever like unto Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

> *"And God has encompassed all things in knowledge."* (Qur'an 65:12)

The nearness of Moses was therefore a nearness of honour, of address, and of unveiled communication — not a shortening of a spatial interval, since no spatial interval was ever involved. The distinction matters practically as well as doctrinally, because it tells a person what he is seeking when he seeks closeness to God. What is available is not proximity but attention: God's turning toward the servant, and the servant's being addressed. This is why the tradition has consistently located the experience of nearness in the act of worship rather than in any place, and why the private supplication is described in the sources as the closest a person comes to his Lord.

**Kalīm Allāh: The One to Whom God Spoke**

The phrase "in intimate discourse" is the basis of Moses' title in Islam: *Kalīm Allāh*, "the one to whom God spoke" — and the title is one every Muslim knows, since it appears in the creedal formulas and in the ordinary speech of the community, recited and heard far more often than the narrative that explains it. The Qur'an states it directly:

> *"And God spoke to Moses directly (*wa-kallama Allāhu Mūsā taklīman*)."* (Qur'an 4:164)

That verse is doctrinally significant beyond its immediate reference, because it was one of the proof-texts deployed in the classical debates about whether the Qur'an is the *speech* of God. The Sunni position — articulated most fully by Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal and defended at length by Ibn Taymiyyah — is that God speaks with a real speech, in a manner befitting Him, without that speech being a created thing and without its being like the speech of creatures. Moses' title thus became a technical term in Islamic theology as well as an honourific. The Qur'an's account of the encounter is given at greater length in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf:

> *"And when Moses came at Our appointed time, and his Lord spoke to him, he said: 'My Lord, show me [Yourself], that I may look upon You.' He said: 'You will not see Me; but look at the mountain — if it remains in its place, then you will see Me.' And when his Lord revealed Himself to the mountain, He made it crumble to dust, and Moses fell down thunderstruck. Then when he recovered, he said: 'Glory be to You! I turn to You in repentance, and I am the first of the believers.'"* (Qur'an 7:143)

That passage is the complement to ours, and the two together define the limit precisely. It establishes that even the prophet whom God addressed directly could not see Him. Speech is possible; vision, in this life, is not. The pairing is doctrinally load-bearing, because it excludes both extremes — the claim that God can be seen in this world, and the claim that He cannot be truly addressed. Moses is the proof of the second and the disproof of the first, and the tradition has held both together without resolving the tension by diminishing either.

**The Sūrah's Point: Another Withdrawal, Another Gift**

It is worth registering, once more, that this verse is another instance of the sūrah's master-pattern. Moses is in the wilderness. He is not in Egypt, where he grew up in a palace; not in Midian, where he has a wife, a job, and a home; not among his people, who are in bondage. He is at a mountain, alone, in the desert, on his way to somewhere else — and that is where God calls him and gives him everything: the commission, the signs, the scripture, and his brother (v. 53). The sūrah has now made the point five times. Zachariah in the sanctuary. Mary to the east. Mary to a distant place. Abraham leaving his people. Moses at the foot of a mountain. And the next verse will complete the pattern by naming the gift that followed the call.

**A Practical Word About Intimate Discourse**

There is a devotional application of this verse that the tradition has never stopped drawing, and it is available to every reader. Moses' *najwā* — his private converse with God — is the model for the believer's own private supplication. The Prophet ﷺ taught that the closest state of a servant to his Lord is in prostration, and he encouraged his community to use it for exactly this:

> *"The closest a servant is to his Lord is while he is prostrating, so increase in supplication therein."* (Muslim 482)

And he described a category of people God favours specifically for private converse, in a ḥadīth the scholars cited whenever the question arose of who inherits the stations of the prophets — since the sūrah has just named the station, and a listener who has heard it reasonably wants to know whether it is closed to him:

> *"Seven will God shade on the Day when there is no shade but His shade... and a man who remembered God in solitude and his eyes overflowed [with tears]."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

The conjunction of *solitude* and *remembrance* in that ḥadīth is the sūrah's own conjunction, and it is the reason the chapter has placed withdrawal at the centre of every narrative it tells. The mountain is not available to everyone; the private hour is. What Moses had at the side of a mountain is offered to every person in the last third of the night, or in any interval he chooses to close, and the sūrah's argument throughout is that the geography was never the point. The withdrawal is the condition, and the condition is reproducible.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:53

> **And We bestowed upon him, from Our Mercy, his brother, Aaron, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Gift in the Sūrah's Chain of Gifts**

The Moses passage closes as the Abraham passage did — with a bestowal: *wa-wahabnā lahu min raḥmatinā akhāhu Hārūna nabiyyan* — "And We bestowed upon him, from Our Mercy, his brother, Aaron, a prophet." The sūrah's chain is now explicit and complete. John was a mercy to Zachariah (vv. 2–3, 7). Isaac and Jacob were gifts to Abraham (vv. 49–50). Aaron is a mercy to Moses (v. 53). In every case the gift is a *person*, not a thing; in every case the gift follows a withdrawal; and in every case the gift is described as coming from God's mercy. The phrase *min raḥmatinā* — "from Our Mercy" — is the same phrase used at v. 50 of Abraham's family. The sūrah is insisting that the people God places in a prophet's life are themselves acts of mercy, not merely provisions. That is a revaluation of an ordinary assumption: most people regard the people around them as circumstances to be managed. The sūrah regards them as gifts to be recognised.

**Why Aaron Was a Gift**

The commentators noted a striking detail: Aaron was *older* than Moses. The reports give him three years' seniority, and in the normal course of a traditional society the elder brother would be the one with precedence. So God's giving of Aaron to Moses is not the giving of a subordinate; it is the giving of an elder brother as a helper. That is why the commentators understood the gift to mean that God gave Moses *the gift of Aaron's prophethood* (al-Ṭabarī) — that is, that Aaron was made a prophet so that Moses would have a prophet beside him, rather than merely a supporter. Aaron's qualifications are stated in the Qur'an's own account of Moses' supplication:

> *"And appoint for me a helper from among my family — Aaron, my brother. Strengthen me through him, and let him share my task, that we may glorify You much and remember You much. Verily You are ever seeing us."* (Qur'an 20:29–35)

And in Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ, Moses gives the specific reason Aaron is needed, in a request whose candour is one of the most human moments in the Qur'an — a man asking not to be given power, and not to be given eloquence, but to be given his brother:

> *"And my brother, Aaron, is more eloquent than me in speech. So send him with me as a helper, to confirm me; verily I fear that they will deny me."* (Qur'an 28:34)

The gift was thus precisely tailored. Moses had, by his own account, a speech difficulty (*"and my breast is constricted and my tongue is not fluent"*, 26:13). Aaron was eloquent. God gave the stammering prophet the eloquent brother — which is to say that God answered the prayer in the exact shape it was asked. That is a pattern worth noting for its own sake. The sūrah has recorded God giving Isaac and Jacob to a man who had lost his family, a son to a man whose bones had failed, and an eloquent brother to a man whose tongue was tied. The gifts correspond to the deficiencies. The sūrah's God is not a dispenser of generic blessings; He gives what the specific servant lacks.

**Aaron's Rank: Prophet, Not Messenger**

The verse calls Aaron *nabiyyā* — "a prophet." It does not call him *rasūl*, "messenger," and the tradition drew the distinction carefully. Aaron was a prophet, but not a messenger in the technical sense, because he did not bring a separate revelation or a separate law. He was sent to *support and confirm* the revelation given to Moses, not to add to it. The Qur'an's depiction of him is consistently as Moses' partner in the same mission:

> *"Then We sent Moses and his brother Aaron with Our signs and a manifest authority, to Pharaoh and his notables..."* (Qur'an 23:45)

> *"And verily We gave Moses and Aaron the Criterion, and a radiant light and a reminder for the reverent."* (Qur'an 21:48)

> *"And We favoured them both, and We gave the two of them the Book that makes clear, and guided them both upon the straight path."* (Qur'an 37:117–18)

Those three verses are worth reading together, because they establish that Aaron shared in the scripture, in the authority, and in the guidance — but always as the second of two, never as the bringer of a second message. The distinction is doctrinally precise and the tradition has maintained it carefully: Aaron is a prophet, and his prophethood is not a diminished or derivative thing, but his message is Moses' message. He is sent to the same people with the same book, and the unity of the two is the Qur'an's own model for a kind of shared authority that does not divide what it serves.

**The ʿAlī–Aaron Ḥadīth and Its Significance**

No discussion of Aaron in Islamic sources is complete without the ḥadīth that connects him to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, because it is one of the most consequential texts in Islamic history and the source of a great deal of subsequent theology. The Prophet ﷺ said to ʿAlī, on the occasion of the expedition to Tabūk, when ʿAlī had been left in charge of Madinah:

> *"Are you not pleased to be to me as Aaron was to Moses, except that there is no prophet after me?"* (al-Bukhārī 3706, 4416; Muslim 2404)

The ḥadīth is rigorously authenticated — it is in both Ṣaḥīḥs through multiple chains — and its meaning is contested between Sunni and Shia interpretations. *The Shia reading* takes it as a proof-text for ʿAlī's designation as the Prophet's immediate successor: Aaron was Moses' designated deputy and, had Moses died, would have succeeded him. On this reading, the exception ("except that there is no prophet after me") removes only prophethood, leaving the deputyship and succession intact. *The Sunni reading* takes it as a statement about a specific, temporary deputyship: just as Moses left Aaron in charge when he went up the mountain, so the Prophet ﷺ left ʿAlī in charge when he went on campaign. The ḥadīth establishes ʿAlī's rank, his proximity, and his qualification for leadership, but it does not, on this reading, constitute a designation of succession — and it is raised by Sunni scholars against any claim that it does. The commentators on this sūrah noted the parallel because of the verse's own logic: God gave Moses his brother as a *helper*, and the Arabic *wazīr* ("helper, minister, one who bears the burden") is the word used in 20:29. Aaron's role was to bear part of the load with his brother, and that is the aspect of the relationship the ḥadīth invokes.

**The Harder Episode: Aaron and the Calf**

Intellectual honesty requires including what the sūrah omits, because it is the passage that defines Aaron's vulnerability, and it would be dishonest to present him only as the figure this verse praises. When Moses went up the mountain for forty nights, he left Aaron in charge of the Children of Israel. During his absence, a man named al-Sāmirī made a calf from the people's gold ornaments, and they worshipped it. When Moses returned — and the account that follows is the one the commentators had to reconcile with everything the Qur'an says in Aaron's praise:

> *"He said: 'O Aaron! What prevented you, when you saw them going astray, from following me? Did you then disobey my command?' He said: 'O son of my mother! Seize not my beard nor my head. I feared that you would say: "You have caused division among the Children of Israel, and you did not heed my word."'"* (Qur'an 20:92–94)

Aaron's defence is that he *did* warn them — the Qur'an records his warning at 20:90, *"O my people! You are only being tested by it; and verily your Lord is the Compassionate, so follow me and obey my command"* — but that he was outnumbered and feared a worse outcome if he had split the community by force. The passage is one of the most human in the Qur'an, and the tradition has generally read it sympathetically: Aaron was not an apostate, he did not participate, he warned them, and he was caught in an impossible situation. Moses' anger was real, and his prayer afterwards included his brother: *"My Lord, forgive me and my brother, and admit us into Your mercy"* (7:151). The relevance to this verse is direct. God's gift to Moses was a brother who was himself a prophet and who was also flawed, overmatched, and in need of the same mercy as everyone else. The gift was not a perfect instrument but a companion — and that, the sūrah suggests, is what God usually sends.

**The Esoteric Reading**

The Sufi commentators, as so often, proposed a further layer. Rūzbihān al-Baqlī read Aaron as representing the *esoteric* dimension of the revelation given to Moses — the inner meaning beneath the law, just as Moses represents the law's outward form. The two are inseparable and neither is complete without the other. That reading harmonises with the ḥadīth about ʿAlī, since Shia and Sufi sources alike treat ʿAlī as the bearer of the Prophet's esoteric knowledge. Whatever one makes of the claim, its value here is to draw attention to something the verse itself supports: Aaron was given to Moses as a *completion*, not merely as an assistant.

**The Practical Lesson**

For the reader, the verse makes a simple and important point about how God answers the prayers of people who feel inadequate. Moses did not ask to be made eloquent. He asked for a helper. And God gave him one — which is to say that God's answer to a genuine deficiency is not always the removal of the deficiency. Sometimes it is the provision of a person who supplies what is missing. That is a considerable comfort to anyone who has prayed to be freed from a limitation and found the limitation still there. The sūrah's testimony is that the remedy may arrive in the form of a brother rather than a cure — and that the brother is *from Our Mercy* just as much as the cure would have been.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:54

> **And remember Ishmael in the Book. Verily he was true to the promise, and he was a messenger, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth "And Remember"**

The sūrah continues its catalogue: *wa-dhkur fī'l-kitābi Ismāʿīla innahu kāna ṣādiqa al-waʿdi wa-kāna rasūlan nabiyyan* — "And remember Ishmael in the Book. Verily he was true to the promise, and he was a messenger, a prophet." Ishmael is the son of Abraham and Hājar, the ancestor of the Arabs, and — in Islamic scripture and law — the builder of the Kaʿbah with his father and the progenitor of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. His place in Islam is therefore not marginal but central: the ḥajj, the well of Zamzam, the station of Abraham, and the *saʿī* between Ṣafā and Marwah are all traced to him and his mother. The sūrah gives him two titles: *true to the promise* and *messenger, prophet*. Both deserve attention.

**Ṣādiq al-Waʿd — True to the Promise**

The Arabic phrase is *ṣādiqa al-waʿd* — literally "truthful of the promise," meaning one who keeps his word, who fulfils what he has pledged. The root *w-ʿ-d* denotes a promise or an appointment; the root *ṣ-d-q* is the same root as *ṣiddīq*, discussed at v. 41. The commentators illustrate the quality with a story that appears in several of the classical works. Ishmael made an appointment to meet someone at a particular place. The man forgot, and did not come. Ishmael waited — the reports vary between several days and an entire year — without leaving the spot. The account is transmitted by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Zamakhsharī, and a very similar story is told of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ before his mission (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī). Two remarks are necessary about such reports. The first is about their status. These narratives reach the commentaries through transmitted reports whose chains are not of the ṣaḥīḥ grade; they are edifying *akhbār*, not law. The tradition has always received them as illustrations of a quality the Qur'an itself asserts in this verse, rather than as independent evidence. The second is about their content, and it is more interesting. The story's point is not punctuality. It is that Ishmael treated a promise as *binding on him* even when the other party had abandoned it. The other man's failure did not release him, because the obligation was not owed to the other man's convenience; it was owed to the word he had given.

That is the heart of *ṣidq* in the Islamic understanding, and it is why the Prophet ﷺ treated the keeping of promises as a criterion of faith itself — not as one virtue among others but as the thing whose absence indicates that faith is not present at all. The logic is that *ṣidq* is not primarily a property of speech; it is the correspondence between what a person says and what he is. A person whose word cannot be relied upon has given evidence about his interior rather than merely about his punctuality:

> *"The signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks it, and when he is entrusted he betrays."* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59)

And, in a ḥadīth qudsī, God describes those who will be with the prophets on the Day of Resurrection — a ḥadīth the scholars cited in connection with this verse because it supplies the company the *ṣiddīq* keeps, and because what is named there is the company rather than the reward:

> *"Verily, truthfulness (*ṣidq*) leads to righteousness (*birr*), and righteousness leads to the Garden. A man continues to be truthful and to seek truthfulness until he is recorded with God as a ṣiddīq. And beware of lying, for lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 6094; Muslim 2607)

**The Other Reading: "True to the Promise" as Steadfastness**

Some commentators pointed to a second and deeper application of the phrase, connected to a question that divides the early authorities and that the tradition has never closed: who was the son Abraham was commanded to sacrifice? The Qur'an does not name him, and the silence is itself the reason the disagreement has persisted for fourteen centuries — a scripture that had wanted the matter settled would have settled it in a single word:

> *"And when he reached with him the age of striving, he said: 'O my son! Verily I have seen in a dream that I am sacrificing you. So look — what do you think?' He said: 'O my father! Do what you are commanded; you will find me, if God wills, among the steadfast.'"* (Qur'an 37:102)

The name is not given in that passage, but the immediate context resolves it: the announcement of Isaac's birth follows at 37:112–13, after the sacrifice episode. On that basis, the majority of the early authorities — including Ibn ʿAbbās, and the position favoured by many classical commentators — held that the son was Ishmael. A minority, including some reports from Ibn ʿAbbās as well, held that it was Isaac. The question matters to this verse because of what al-Qurṭubī noted: for those who take the son to be Ishmael, the description *true to the promise* alludes specifically to his remaining steadfast during that ordeal. The promise he kept was not an appointment with a man but the promise he made to his father — *"you will find me, if God wills, among the steadfast."* Whichever reading one prefers, the quality is the same and the sūrah is praising it: Ishmael says what he will do and does it, including when the cost is himself.

**"A Messenger, a Prophet"**

The second title places Ishmael among the messengers. The commentators explained why: he was responsible for bringing Abrahamic monotheism to the people of Arabia, and especially to the tribe of Jurhum, among whom he and his mother settled in Makkah (al-Ṭabrisī). That is a significant claim, and it has a direct bearing on the Qur'an's argument with the Quraysh. If Ishmael was a prophet sent to the Arabs of Makkah, then the Arabians are not a people to whom no warner came. They received the same monotheism as everyone else, in their own language, from one of their own ancestors — and the idolatry of the Quraysh is therefore an apostasy from their own inheritance rather than an innocence that had never been addressed. The Prophet ﷺ confirmed the descent of the Arabs from Ishmael, and the sīrah literature traces his lineage to Ishmael through a chain of ancestors whose names are preserved.
