The tradition's earliest sources report him saying:

> *"God chose Kinānah from the descendants of Ishmael, and He chose Quraysh from Kinānah..."* (Muslim 2276)

**Ishmael in the Sunnah and the Rites**

The Islamic rites most closely associated with Ishmael are three, and all are live in the practice of every Muslim who performs the ḥajj or ʿumrah, which is the reason the disagreement about the sacrifice has never detached itself from ritual. *The Kaʿbah.* Father and son raised its foundations together, and the Qur'an records their prayer:

> *"And [mention] when Abraham raised the foundations of the House with Ishmael: 'Our Lord, accept it from us; verily You are the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing. Our Lord, and make us submitters to You, and from our progeny a community submitting to You. And show us our rites, and turn to us; verily You are the Relenting, the Merciful.'"* (Qur'an 2:127–28)

*Zamzam.* The well that gushed at the infant Ishmael's feet when his mother Hājar ran between Ṣafā and Marwah searching for water — and the well is still there, and every pilgrim drinks from it, which means the miracle is not a report about the past but a fixture that millions of people walk past every year. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"May God have mercy on the mother of Ishmael! Had she left it [the water] as it was, it would have been a flowing stream."* (al-Bukhārī 3364)

*The sacrifice.* The *udḥiyah* performed by Muslims at ʿĪd al-Aḍḥā commemorates the ram substituted for Abraham's son, and the *saʿī* between the two hills commemorates Hājar's search. What is worth noticing is that the rite preserves both parents: the father's obedience is commemorated in the animal, and the mother's running is commemorated in the walking. A family is being remembered rather than an individual, and the ritual has the shape of the whole episode rather than only of its climax.

**The Qualities the Sūrah Selects**

Note what the sūrah praises in Ishmael, and compare it with what it praises in the others, because the variation is deliberate and a reader who expects the same epithet each time will miss what the chapter is doing. Each prophet is described by the quality his own story demonstrates, and the qualities are not interchangeable:

- Zachariah: a hidden cry (v. 3).
- John: tenderness, purity, reverence, filial duty (vv. 13–14).
- Jesus: servanthood, prayer, almsgiving (vv. 30–31).
- Abraham: truthfulness (v. 41).
- Moses: sincerity (v. 51).
- Ishmael: keeping his word (v. 54), commanding prayer and charity (v. 55).
- Idrīs: truthfulness (v. 56).

The sūrah is assembling a complete moral portrait out of its catalogue, and each figure contributes a distinct virtue. Taken together they constitute the Qur'an's model of character: sincerity, truthfulness, reliability, worship, and generosity. That is worth noting, because it is easy to read a list of prophets as a list of offices. The sūrah reads it as a list of qualities. What made these men great was not that they were prophets; it is that they were these kinds of people, and God made them prophets.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:55

> **He used to bid his people to prayer and almsgiving, and he was pleasing unto his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Pillars, in the Mouth of Ishmael**

The verse adds Ishmael's practice to his character: *wa-kāna yaʾmuru ahlahu bi'l-ṣalāti wa'l-zakāti wa-kāna ʿinda rabbihi marḍiyyā* — "He used to bid his people to prayer and almsgiving, and he was pleasing unto his Lord." The verse begins with *wa-kāna* — "and he used to" — a construction that in Arabic denotes a settled, habitual practice. Ishmael did not command prayer once; prayer and almsgiving were the standing content of his instruction to his household and his people. The word *ahlahu* means his people, his household, his family — the ones for whom he bore responsibility. Some commentators specified that Ishmael used to command his people to pray at night and to give charity during the day (al-Ṭabrisī). Others noted the parallel instruction given to the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And bid your family to prayer, and be steadfast therein. We do not ask you for provision; We provide for you. And the [good] end is for the reverent."* (Qur'an 20:132)

That parallel is significant, because it establishes the scope of the duty and, more importantly, its direction. The prophet's obligation begins at home. A man who instructs the world and neglects his own household has not fulfilled it — and the tradition states the corollary with equal firmness, which is that the household is the first and hardest audience, since it is the one that has seen the man without his public manner. The commentators' observation is that a prophet who cannot reach his own family has no lever anywhere else, not because his family is especially stubborn but because proximity exposes the difference between a message held and a message performed.

**Prayer and Almsgiving — The Constant Pair**

The pairing of *ṣalāh* and *zakāh* has already been discussed at v. 31, where the infant Jesus declared them to be the charge laid on him. It appears again here, for Ishmael, and it will be worth noting how often the Qur'an uses the pair. The recurrence establishes that these two are not specific to the law given to Muhammad ﷺ. They belong to every dispensation: commanded to the Muslims (2:43, 83, 110, 177, 277; 4:77; 9:71), to the People of the Book (2:43; 5:12), to Jesus (19:31), and now to Ishmael (19:55). The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Islam is built upon five: the testimony that there is no god but God and that Muhammad is the Messenger of God, the establishment of the prayer, the giving of zakāh, the pilgrimage to the House, and fasting in Ramaḍān."* (al-Bukhārī 8; Muslim 16)

The logic of the pairing, as noted at v. 31, is that the two together structure the whole of a religious life: prayer governs a person's relation to God, and almsgiving governs his relation to everyone else. Neither alone suffices, and the Qur'an's indictment of the religion that prays without giving is among the most frightening passages in the Book (107:1–7).

**"He Was Pleasing Unto His Lord"**

The verse closes with a description that the sūrah has carefully prepared: *wa-kāna ʿinda rabbihi marḍiyyā* — "and he was pleasing unto his Lord." The word *marḍiyy* comes from the root *r-ḍ-y*, the root of *riḍā* — contentment, acceptance, approval. And this is the sūrah's third and final use of that root, completing a movement that began at the very beginning of the chapter:

- **v. 6 (Zachariah's prayer):** *"And make him, my Lord, well-pleasing (*raḍiyyā*)."* — the request.
- **v. 55 (Ishmael):** *"and he was pleasing (*marḍiyyā*) unto his Lord."* — the fulfilment.

The two words are from the same root and the same morphological family: *raḍiyy* is the active intensive (one who pleases), *marḍiyy* the passive (one who is pleased with). Zachariah asked for a son who would be pleasing; the sūrah reports that Ishmael was. The sūrah is thus doing something quietly beautiful. It takes the prayer of the man it opened with and shows that prayer answered in the figure of another prophet several verses later. The reader who remembers v. 6 will hear the echo at v. 55, and the effect is to bind the whole catalogue of prophets into a single story of one prayer. The commentators also spelled out what made Ishmael *marḍiyy*: his obedience, his righteousness, his purity (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī), and his avoidance of everything ugly in his conduct (al-Ṭabrisī).

**Al-Riḍā — The Station of Contentment**

The root *r-ḍ-y* deserves a further note, because *riḍā* is one of the great technical terms of Islamic spirituality and one of the highest stations on the path. *Riḍā* has two directions, and the tradition insisted on both: *God's pleasure with the servant.* This is the sense in our verse, and it is the Qur'an's stated goal of the religious life:

> *"And God is well-pleased with them, and they are well-pleased with Him. That is the great triumph."* (Qur'an 5:119; cf. 9:100; 58:22; 98:8)

*The servant's contentment with God.* This is the harder direction, and it is the station the Sufis described as the summit below love: to be content with whatever God decrees, not because one enjoys it, but because it comes from Him. The distinction is worth insisting on, because contentment is routinely confused with either passivity or cheerfulness, and it is neither. The contented person does not pretend that what happened was pleasant, and he does not stop acting to change what can be changed; he has stopped contending with the fact that it happened at all. The Prophet ﷺ taught both directions. Of God's pleasure:

> *"God has decreed: 'Whoever shows enmity to a friend (*walī*) of Mine, I have declared war against him. My servant does not draw near to Me with anything more beloved to Me than what I have made obligatory upon him; and My servant continues to draw near to Me with supererogatory acts until I love him...'"* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

And of the servant's contentment — in a ḥadīth whose structure is the argument, since it describes the same circumstances producing opposite outcomes for two different people and locates the difference entirely in the response:

> *"How wonderful is the affair of the believer! All of his affairs are good for him, and this is not the case for anyone except the believer: if something good happens to him, he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if something harmful happens to him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

That ḥadīth is the practical definition of *riḍā*: not a feeling of delight at whatever happens, but the recognition that every circumstance can be converted into good by the right response to it. The definition is practical in the strictest sense, because it removes contentment from the category of emotion — where it would be unavailable to anyone not currently feeling it — and places it in the category of action, where it is available to everyone. A person who cannot feel content can still behave as one, and the tradition's consistent teaching is that the behaviour is the substance while the feeling, when it comes, is a gift added.

**A Practical Word on Pleasing God**

Finally, note what the verse does *not* say. It does not say that Ishmael was pleasing to his people, or successful, or wealthy, or famous. It says he was pleasing *unto his Lord* — *ʿinda rabbihi*, "in the sight of his Lord." The preposition matters. The Qur'an's vocabulary of approval is almost always framed this way: what matters is the standing a person has with God, not the standing he has with others. The two can diverge sharply, and the Qur'an repeatedly warns that they often do — the prophets were, as a rule, rejected by their own people and approved by God. That is a considerable comfort to anyone whose sincerity has been doubted by the people around them. The sūrah's measure is not the community's verdict. It is *ʿinda rabbihi*. And the practical corollary is one of the most liberating in the entire tradition: the approval of people is not something one can secure by trying, because it depends on their judgment, not on one's conduct. The approval of God is something one can seek directly, and the two pillars of seeking it are named in this verse — prayer, and giving.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:56

> **And remember Idrīs in the Book. Verily he was truthful, a prophet.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth and Final "And Remember"**

The sūrah's catalogue of prophets closes with its most mysterious figure, and the choice is worth pausing over, because a list that has been moving through the most familiar names in the tradition ends on one about whom almost nothing is known. *wa-dhkur fī'l-kitābi Idrīsa innahu kāna ṣiddīqan nabiyyan* — "And remember Idrīs in the Book. Verily he was truthful, a prophet." Idrīs is named only twice in the Qur'an — here and at 21:85, where he appears alongside Ishmael and Dhu'l-Kifl:

> *"And [mention] Ishmael and Idrīs and Dhu'l-Kifl; all were of the patient."* (Qur'an 21:85)

That is the sum of the Qur'an's information about him. Everything else in the Islamic tradition about Idrīs comes from exegetical and historical reports, and the commentators were candid about the limits of what can be known — which is itself a lesson the sūrah seems intent on teaching at the point where its catalogue closes. The chapter has named prophets about whom volumes could be written and ends with one about whom the revelation has said two sentences, and the placement suggests that what is required of a reader is not information about Idrīs but the acknowledgement that he is named, that he is a prophet, and that he was truthful. That is sufficient, and the tradition's willingness to stop where the revelation stopped is a discipline rather than a defeat.

**The Identification With Enoch**

The most widely held view among the commentators is that Idrīs is to be identified with the biblical Enoch (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Thaʿlabī) — the antediluvian figure of Genesis 5, described there as walking with God and being "taken" by Him. On this reading Idrīs was the great-grandfather of Noah, and he lived before the Flood. Some authorities also identified him with the Qur'anic Elias (Ilyās) — a view recorded by the commentators but not widely adopted, since the Qur'an appears to treat Ilyās as a separate figure (6:85; 37:123). The identification with Enoch is not certain, and it is worth saying so plainly. The Qur'an does not make the connection, and the connection rests on the similarity of the narratives rather than on any revealed statement. Muslim scholars have generally treated it as probable rather than established, and the sūrah's own reticence supports that caution.

**The Etymology: What the Name Might Mean**

Several commentators proposed that the name *Idrīs* derives from the Arabic root *d-r-s*, meaning *to study, to learn, to con a text* — the same root as *madrasah*, "school," and *dars*, "lesson." On this reading, the name was given to him because of his devotion to study (al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Ṭabrisī). Al-Zamakhsharī, characteristically sceptical about etymologies of non-Arabic names in the Qur'an, cast doubt on this. His objection is philologically sound: if Idrīs is Enoch, the name is not Arabic at all, and deriving it from an Arabic root is a coincidence of resemblance rather than an etymology. The disagreement is methodologically instructive. The early commentators were willing to find Arabic roots for foreign names and to draw devotional meaning from them; the later philologists insisted that a non-Arabic proper name has no Arabic etymology. Both positions have defenders, and the responsible position is the one the tradition ultimately reached: the derivations are edifying possibilities, not established meanings.

**The Reports About His Life**

What the tradition reports about Idrīs is substantial, though much of it rests on reports from the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* corpus and from early historians rather than on authenticated Prophetic ḥadīth. He is said to have been the first human being to write with a pen; to have possessed knowledge of astronomy and mathematics; to have made sewn clothing from animal skins; and to have been the first prophet among the children of Adam (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). He is said to have been given thirty scrolls of revelation (al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī). And in Islamic philosophical and metaphysical literature he is called *Abu'l-Ḥukamāʾ*, "the father of the sages" — a title reflecting the tradition's placement of him at the origin of the sciences and of philosophy. These reports should be received with the standard caution about *Isrāʾīliyyāt*: what agrees with the Qur'an is accepted, what contradicts it is rejected, and the remainder is neither affirmed nor denied as doctrine. What the Qur'an itself asserts about Idrīs is exactly two things — he was truthful, and he was a prophet — and those are the two things Muslims are obliged to believe.

**"Truthful" — The Third of Three**

The title *ṣiddīq* was discussed at length at v. 41, where it was applied to Abraham. It now appears for the second time in this sūrah, and for the third time in the Qur'an as a whole (Joseph at 12:46, Abraham at 19:41, Idrīs at 19:56). The recurrence is worth noting, because it tells the reader something about the sūrah's construction. Of the seven figures the sūrah catalogues, the ones it singles out with this specific title are Abraham and Idrīs — the two whom the tradition credits with the earliest, most foundational transmissions of religion after Adam. The sūrah is, in effect, marking them as the sources from whom the stream began. And note that the title precedes the office here, exactly as it did for Abraham: *ṣiddīqan nabiyyan* — truthful, a prophet. The sūrah consistently puts the moral quality before the office, as if to say that the quality is the substance and the office the confirmation.

**Why Such a Brief Notice?**

The sūrah's treatment of Idrīs is a single verse of description plus a single verse about his station. That brevity is itself worth considering. There is a Qur'anic principle at work here that the tradition has often noted: the Qur'an tells us what we need, not what we want to know. The reader's curiosity about Idrīs — who he was, when he lived, what he taught — is not satisfied by the revelation, and the revelation does not treat that as a defect. The Qur'an says the same thing about the Companions of the Cave:

> *"They will say: 'Three, and their fourth was their dog'... [Say:] 'My Lord knows best their number.' None knows them except a few. So do not argue concerning them except with an obvious argument, and do not inquire concerning them among them [the People of the Book] about any of them."* (Qur'an 18:22)

The principle is that the Qur'an's purpose is guidance, not antiquarianism, and that an unresolved historical question is not a spiritual obstacle. A Muslim is not required to know who Idrīs was in order to believe what the Qur'an says about him. That is a bracing corrective in an age of unlimited information. Most religious people who spend their energy on speculation about obscure figures would do better with the two facts the sūrah supplies: he was truthful, and he was a prophet.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:57

> **And We raised him to a sublime station.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Raised Him" — The Verb and Its Debates**

The verse is four Arabic words long and has generated a large and careful body of commentary: *wa-rafaʿnāhu makānan ʿaliyyā* — "And We raised him to a sublime station." The verb *rafaʿnā* (root *r-f-ʿ*) means We raised, lifted, elevated. The noun *makān* means place, station, rank. The adjective *ʿaliyy* (root *ʿ-l-w*) means high, lofty, exalted. The question the commentators addressed is what the raising consisted of, and they recorded three principal answers.

**The First Reading: Raised Bodily or Spiritually to the Heavens**

The most widely transmitted report is that God raised Idrīs to one of the heavens — the reports differ as to whether it was the fourth or the sixth (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). The support for this reading comes from the authentic ḥadīth of the Ascension, in which the Prophet ﷺ described passing through the seven heavens and meeting the prophets in them. The narration, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, includes the encounter with Idrīs:

> *"...and he passed by Idrīs, and [Gabriel] said: 'This is Idrīs; greet him.' So he greeted him, and he returned the greeting, and said: 'Welcome, righteous brother and righteous prophet.' Then he ascended with him to the fourth heaven..."* (al-Bukhārī 3207; Muslim 164 — part of the long ḥadīth of the Isrāʾ and Miʿrāj)

The ḥadīth thus confirms that Idrīs is in the fourth heaven — which does not by itself establish how he got there, but does support the reading of this verse as a bodily or spiritual elevation. Some of the reports state that Idrīs has not yet died — that he was raised alive, or taken directly, by God (al-Ṭabarī), just as Jesus was (4:158). This view has defenders in the tradition, though it is not the majority position.

**The Second Reading: Raised in Prophetic Station**

A second reading, recorded by al-Zamakhsharī among others, takes the verse simply to mean that God raised him to the station of prophethood. On this reading *makānan ʿaliyyā* — "a high station" — is the station of being a messenger of God, which is, after all, the highest station a human being can occupy. This reading has the advantage of requiring no supernatural claim beyond what the verse states, and it is the reading the more austere commentators preferred. It does not exclude the first reading; a person can be raised both to prophethood *and* to a heavenly station.

**The Third Reading: A Station in the Garden**

A third reading takes the "high station" as the station Idrīs occupies in the Hereafter rather than as anything that occurred during his life, and the reading has the advantage of not requiring the commentator to explain an event the Qur'an does not narrate. The commentators noted the Qur'an's description of the elevated ranks of the Garden:

> *"And whoever obeys God and the Messenger — those are with the ones God has blessed: the prophets, the truthful ones, the martyrs, and the righteous."* (Qur'an 4:69)

All three readings are coherent, and the classical method was to hold them together rather than choose: God raised Idrīs in station, in nearness, and in the Hereafter, and the precise mechanism is known to Him. The method is worth noticing, because it is the opposite of what a modern reader expects from a commentary. The expectation is that an interpreter will adjudicate and eliminate; the practice of al-Ṭabarī and those after him was to retain every reading the Arabic can bear and the transmitted sources support, on the principle that an ambiguity in the revelation is richer than any resolution a commentator might substitute for it.

**Theological Boundaries: How Not to Read "We Raised Him"**

Because the verse speaks of God raising a person, it is worth stating what the tradition refused to draw from it — the more so because similar language is used of Jesus at 4:158 and has generated comparable confusion. *The raising does not imply divinity.* To be raised by God is to be acted upon; God is not acted upon. The Qur'an uses the same verb for God's raising of the mountains (2:63) and of the heavens (88:18), and in every case the direction of the action is from Creator to creature. *The raising does not imply that God has a location to which things are brought.* As noted at v. 52 in connection with Moses' nearness: God is not in a place, and "heaven" in these texts denotes an order of creation, not a distance from the divine essence. The tradition's formulation, repeated by theologians from the earliest period, is that God is *above* His Throne in a manner befitting Him, without His being contained by it and without the "above" being spatial in the created sense. *The raising does not imply deathlessness as a matter of doctrine.* The question of whether Idrīs died is one about which the sources differ, and no creedal article depends on it. What is established is that he was raised to a high station, and that is what the verse asks the reader to believe.

**The Miʿrāj Ḥadīth in Detail**

Since the ḥadīth of the Ascension is the primary external evidence for this verse, it is worth quoting its relevant portion more fully, because it is one of the most-read texts in Islam. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record, from Anas ibn Mālik, the account of the night journey and the ascent through the heavens. At each heaven the Prophet ﷺ knocked, was asked who he was, was admitted, and met a prophet:

> *"...Then he ascended and we came to the lowest heaven... [and he met Adam]. Then he ascended and we came to the second heaven... [and he met Jesus son of Mary and John]. Then he ascended and we came to the third heaven... [and he met Joseph]. Then he ascended and we came to the fourth heaven, and [Gabriel] asked to be admitted. It was said: 'Who are you?' He said: 'Gabriel.' It was said: 'And who is with you?' He said: 'Muhammad.' It was said: 'Has he been sent for?' He said: 'He has been sent for.' So it was opened for us, and there was Idrīs. He welcomed me and prayed for me [with good], and God, Exalted is He, has said: 'And We raised him to a sublime station.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3207; Muslim 164)

The narration explicitly connects the encounter to this very verse, which is why the commentators treated it as the definitive gloss rather than as one report among many — the Prophet ﷺ himself supplying the reference removes any question about whether the identification is correct:

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

For the reader, the practical content of this verse is not a puzzle to be solved but a promise to be registered. Idrīs is the most obscure figure in the sūrah's catalogue — the one about whom least is known and about whom the revelation says almost nothing. And he is the one about whom the sūrah says: *We raised him to a sublime station.* The sūrah has thus arranged its catalogue so that the least-known figure receives one of the highest tributes. That is the sūrah's final word on the subject of reputation, and it connects directly back to v. 50, where God "ordained for them a sublime, faithful renown." The logic is: God raises whom He wills, and the raising does not depend on being known. A person written about in one verse of one scripture and forgotten by history can stand, in reality, at a rank that the famous would envy. That is a considerable comfort, and a considerable warning. The comfort is for everyone whose work is unseen. The warning is for everyone whose renown is secure: the sūrah has just told you, in the same breath as it announced Abraham's enduring good name, that the two things are not the same.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:58

> **They are those whom God has blessed among the prophets of the progeny of Adam, and of those whom We carried with Noah, and of the progeny of Abraham and Israel, and of those whom We guided and chose. When the signs of the Compassionate were recited unto them, they would fall down, prostrate and weeping.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Sums Up the Whole Catalogue**

This is the summit of the sūrah's second movement, and one of the most remarkable verses in the Qur'an: *ūlāʾika alladhīna anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhim min al-nabiyyīna min dhurriyyati Ādama wa-mimman ḥamalnā maʿa Nūḥin wa-min dhurriyyati Ibrāhīma wa-Isrāʾīla wa-mimman hadaynā wa-jtabaynā idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātu al-Raḥmāni kharrū sujjadan wa-bukiyyā* — "They are those whom God has blessed among the prophets of the progeny of Adam, and of those whom We carried with Noah, and of the progeny of Abraham and Israel, and of those whom We guided and chose. When the signs of the Compassionate were recited unto them, they would fall down, prostrate and weeping." The verse does two things: it classifies the prophets the sūrah has named, and it describes their defining response to revelation. Both halves repay close attention.

**The Four Classifications**

The verse places the sūrah's figures into four categories, and the commentators worked out which figure belongs to which (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī): *The progeny of Adam* — this is taken to refer specifically to Idrīs, who otherwise would fall into none of the other categories, since he predated both Noah and Abraham. (Some commentators, however, read it as referring to all of them, since all human beings are of Adam's progeny.) *Those whom We carried with Noah* — that is, the descendants of those who were in the Ark. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī take this to refer to Abraham and his progeny: Isaac, Ishmael, and Jacob, all of whom descend from the survivors of the Flood. *The progeny of Abraham and Israel* — "Israel" is Jacob, so this category covers the Israelite figures named in the sūrah: Moses, Aaron, Zachariah, John, Mary, and Jesus. *Those whom We guided and chose* — this is the general category, covering all of them, and it supplies the principle of election: they were not great by their own achievement; they were *guided* (*hadaynā*) and *chosen* (*ijtabaynā*). That last pair of verbs is the theological heart of the classification. *Hidāyah* is God's guidance; *ijtabāʾ* (root *j-b-w*) is His choosing, His selecting, His electing. The two together state the Islamic doctrine of prophethood precisely: God guides whom He wills and chooses whom He wills, and the choice is His act, not the prophet's attainment:

> *"Your Lord creates what He wills and chooses; they have no choice. Glory be to God, and exalted is He above what they associate with Him."* (Qur'an 28:68)

**The Description: They Fell Down Prostrate and Weeping**

The verse's second half is what has made it one of the most-loved passages in the Book: *idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātu al-Raḥmāni kharrū sujjadan wa-bukiyyā* — "When the signs of the Compassionate were recited unto them, they would fall down, prostrate and weeping." Three words carry the description, and each is chosen. *Kharrū* (root *kh-r-r*) means they *fell* — not knelt, not bowed, but fell. The word denotes a sudden collapse, as of something giving way under a weight. The image is of a person overcome. *Sujjadan* — "prostrate" — the posture of the forehead on the ground, which is the physical posture of the Islamic prayer and the posture of maximum humility in the Qur'an's vocabulary (*"Nay, prostrate and draw near"*, 96:19). *Bukiyyā* (root *b-k-y*, weeping) — an intensive plural form denoting those who weep abundantly. These are not people wiping a tear; they are people overcome. And the conjunction is important: they fell prostrate *and* weeping. Not one or the other. The body goes down and the eyes overflow, because the whole person — body and heart — has registered what was recited.

**What Moved Them**

The commentators asked the obvious question: why did the prophets weep at the recitation of God's signs? They were, after all, the recipients of revelation themselves; the signs were addressed to them before they were addressed to anyone. Al-Rāzī's answer is that they fell in submission and humility before the signs — that the weeping was the response of a creature to the speech of its Creator. And the Sufi commentators went deeper. Al-Qushayrī wrote that the bodily prostration of the prophets was a reflection of the prostration of their *inner selves* before the spiritual truths they had realised. The outer act was the visible form of an inward state. Ibn ʿAjībah placed the response on the map of the spiritual path, identifying it as the first stage of *maḥabbah* — the love of God — which is characterised by weeping at the words of the Beloved, and which is later replaced by joy as the servant draws nearer. The tradition also noted that the weeping was not the response of fear alone. The word used for the recited material is *āyātu al-Raḥmāni* — "the signs of the *Compassionate*." The Name appears again, for the ninth time in the sūrah, and its presence here is decisive: what overcame them was not terror at a threat but the weight of mercy.

**The Prostration of Recitation**

This verse is one of fifteen passages in the Qur'an known as *āyāt al-sajdah*, the verses of prostration, upon hearing which Muslims prostrate physically. The fifteen are, in the traditional enumeration: 7:206; 13:15; 16:49–50; 17:107–9; 19:58; 22:18; 22:77; 25:60; 27:26; 32:15; 38:24; 41:37–38; 53:62; 84:21; and 96:19. There is some disagreement about a few: whether the prostration at 41:37–38 and 53:62 is obligatory or supererogatory, and whether the prostration at 38:24 is one of thanksgiving (*shukr*) rather than of recitation. The ruling, according to the majority of the jurists, is that prostration upon reciting or hearing one of these verses is a confirmed Sunnah for both the reciter and the listener. The Prophet ﷺ taught the significance of the act in a ḥadīth:

> *"When the son of Adam recites a verse of prostration and prostrates, Satan withdraws weeping, saying: 'Woe to me! The son of Adam was commanded to prostrate and he prostrated, so for him is the Garden; I was commanded to prostrate and I refused, so for me is the Fire.'"* (Muslim 133)

That ḥadīth explains why the act matters so much, and the explanation is more specific than a general commendation of humility. The prostration is the exact posture that Iblīs refused, and performing it is therefore a declaration of alignment with the obedient rather than the rebel — a declaration made by the body rather than by the tongue, and renewed in every unit of every prayer. The tradition's reading is that the refusal and the performance are the same act performed by two different parties, and that the creature who prostrates is answering a question that was posed before he existed.

**Weeping as a Religious Practice**

The verse is also the primary textual basis for the Islamic valuation of weeping in worship, a theme that runs through the Sunnah with a frequency that surprises modern readers, and that sits oddly with the reputation the tradition has acquired in some quarters for dry legalism. The weeping described here is not a technique and not a display: it is the ordinary physical response of a person who has understood something, and the Qur'an records it as the mark of the prophets rather than as a failure of composure. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Recite the Qur'an and weep. And if you do not weep, then make yourself weep."* (Ibn Mājah 4196; graded ḥasan)

That instruction — *make yourself weep if you cannot weep* — is one of the most psychologically acute statements in the entire ḥadīth corpus. It recognises that the feeling and the posture are connected in both directions: weeping produces tenderness, and assuming the posture of weeping can produce the feeling. The tradition has always understood this as encouragement to the discipline, not as licence for display. The Prophet ﷺ also identified weeping from the fear of God as one of the seven forms of divine shelter:

> *"...and a man who remembered God in solitude, and his eyes overflowed."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

And his own practice is recorded, which is the element that settles the matter for a community that takes its Prophet ﷺ as its model. A teaching about weeping could be read as encouragement; a report that he wept, and wept until those around him heard it, establishes that this is not an exaggeration introduced by later generations but the example itself:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ stood [in prayer] until his feet became swollen. It was said to him: 'Does God not forgive you your past and future sins?' He said: 'Should I not be a grateful servant?'"* (al-Bukhārī 4836; Muslim 2819)

**Tenderness of Heart and Its Loss**

The commentators gave the quality a name: *riqqat al-qalb*, tenderness of heart. Al-Qushayrī observed that it was shared by all the prophets, and the tradition has treated its absence as a spiritual disease requiring treatment. That diagnosis is worth taking seriously, because the loss of *riqqah* is one of the most common complaints of religious people in every generation: the recitation no longer moves, the prayer is dry, and the heart is unmoved by what once moved it. The classical remedies the tradition prescribed are direct and few: keeping company with those who weep; sitting with the poor and the sick; contemplating death and the grave; reciting slowly (*tartīl*) with attention to the meanings; and — above all — asking God for it, since the softening of the heart is His to give. The Prophet ﷺ used to pray:

> *"O Turner of the hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140; graded ḥasan)

The phrase "make my heart firm" and the request for tenderness are the same request in different vocabulary, and the identity is worth dwelling on because the two sound opposed. Firmness suggests hardness, and tenderness suggests its opposite, yet both are being asked for at once — because what is wanted is a heart that holds its position and still reacts. A heart that is firm but unreactive has become a stone, and the Qur'an uses exactly that image for the condition it warns against. A heart that reacts but does not hold is at the mercy of every impression. The prayer is for neither: it is for a heart that is steady *and* alive.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah**

Verse 58 is the hinge of the sūrah's second movement. Everything before it in this movement has been a catalogue of individual prophets; everything after it is the *sequel* — what happened to the generations that came after them (v. 59), the door of repentance that remains open (v. 60), and the Gardens promised to those who return (vv. 61–63). The sūrah's structure is thus: *here is what the best of humanity looked like* — men and women who fell down weeping at the recitation of God's signs — *and here is what came after them.* The contrast is the argument of the next five verses, and it is deliberately jarring.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:59

> **Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed base desires. So they shall meet [the reward of] error,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fall That Follows the Summit**

Having described the prophets who fell down weeping at the recitation of God's signs (v. 58), the sūrah turns immediately to what came after: *fa-khalafa min baʿdihim khalfun aḍāʿū al-ṣalāta wa-ittabaʿū al-shahawāti fa-sawfa yalqawna ghayyan* — "Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed base desires. So they shall meet [the reward of] error." The transition is one of the most abrupt in the Qur'an, and it is meant to be. The verse before it ends on prostration and weeping; this one opens on a generation that abandoned the prayer. The sūrah places the two side by side without commentary, and the contrast does the work. The word *khalf* (root *kh-l-f*, the root of *khalīfah*, "successor") means a succeeding generation, that which comes after. The verb *khalafa* means "they came after [them]" — and the same root carries the sense of *replacing* and, in another form, of *corrupting*. The sūrah is describing a succession that is also a decline. Who is this generation? The commentators recorded two views. Ibn ʿAbbās, transmitted by al-Ṭabrisī and al-Zamakhsharī, held that it refers to the Jews — that is, the later generations of Israel who inherited the revelation of Moses and Aaron and did not keep it. Mujāhid and Qatādah held that it refers to the Muslim community at the end of time (al-Ṭabrisī). The second reading is the one Muslim preachers have returned to most often across the centuries, and with good reason: the sūrah's description is not tied to a specific community, and the features it names are precisely the features that afflict religious communities in their late stages. The tradition has always read such verses as warnings addressed to itself, and the habit is not a homiletic device but a principle of reading. A verse that describes the failure of an earlier people is, on the Qur'an's own terms, a description of a recurring pattern — and a reader who applies it only to the people it was originally about has not understood what kind of text he is holding.

**"They Neglected the Prayer"**

The first failing is *aḍāʿū al-ṣalāta* — "they neglected the prayer," more literally, "they caused the prayer to be lost." The verb *aḍāʿa* (root *ḍ-y-ʿ*) means to let something go to waste, to lose it, to allow it to perish. It is stronger than "they missed the prayer." It implies a process by which something is allowed to decay. The commentators specified the two forms that decay takes, and both are recorded (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). Some said it means they abandoned the prayer altogether. Others said it means they delayed it beyond its appointed time, or performed it carelessly at the edge of its window. Both are forms of *iḍāʿah*, and the second is the more insidious, because it permits a person to believe he is praying while the prayer is dying of neglect. A person who abandons the prayer knows where he stands. A person who prays it late, hurriedly, and without attention can go for years believing he is among those who pray. The Prophet ﷺ described the two failure-modes precisely:

> *"That is the prayer of the hypocrite: he sits watching the sun until it is between the horns of Satan, then he rises and pecks at it four times, remembering God hardly at all therein."* (Muslim 622)

And he warned of the intermediate stages, which is the detail that makes the warning useful rather than merely alarming. A person is not told simply that decline is possible; he is told what it looks like while it is still reversible:

> *"Between a man and shirk and kufr stands the abandonment of the prayer."* (Muslim 82, from Jābir)

And he identified the prayer as the first item of the reckoning, which supplies the reason the sūrah places the prayer at the head of its own anatomy of decline. If the first thing examined on the Day is the prayer, then the prayer is not one obligation among many but the one by which the others are ordered:

> *"The first thing for which the servant will be called to account on the Day of Resurrection is the prayer. If it is sound, the rest of his deeds will be sound; and if it is corrupt, the rest of his deeds will be corrupt."* (al-Tirmidhī 413; Abū Dāwūd 864; graded ḥasan)

**"And Followed Base Desires"**

The second failing is *wa-ittabaʿū al-shahawāt* — "and followed base desires." The word *shahawāt* (singular *shahwah*) denotes appetites, cravings, passionate desires. In the Qur'an's usage it is not intrinsically evil — the Qur'an acknowledges that human beings have desires for spouses, children, wealth, and comfort, and calls these *"the adornment of the life of this world"* (18:46). What is condemned is not having the desire but *following* it — *ittibāʿ*, making it the directing principle of one's life:

> *"Then We caused the Book to be inherited by those We chose from among Our servants. But among them is he who wrongs himself, among them is he who is moderate, and among them is he who outstrips [in good] by God's leave."* (Qur'an 35:32)

> *"But as for him who feared the standing before his Lord and restrained himself from base desire (*al-hawā*) — verily the Garden will be his abode."* (Qur'an 79:40–41)

**The Causal Link Between the Two Failings**

The sūrah's most important insight in this verse is the *order* of the two failings, and the tradition has always read it as causal. Neglect of the prayer comes first; following the desires comes second. The sūrah is asserting that the second is a consequence of the first, and the logic is exact. The prayer is, among other things, a scheduled interruption of the pursuit of desire. Five times a day, a person stops what he wants to do and does what God has commanded. That interruption is not incidental to the prayer's purpose; it is the mechanism by which a person practises being governed rather than being driven. The Qur'an states this as the prayer's function:

> *"Recite what has been revealed to you of the Book and establish the prayer; verily the prayer forbids indecency and wrongdoing. And the remembrance of God is greater; and God knows what you do."* (Qur'an 29:45)

Remove the interruption and the pursuit runs uninterrupted. That is why, in the classical analysis, the loss of the prayer is never the *first* thing to go wrong; it is the first thing to be *noticed*. By the time a community stops praying, the deeper failure — the loss of the sense that one is governed — has already occurred, and what follows it is the uninhibited pursuit of appetite. That is a diagnosis worth applying beyond the individual. A community that has stopped praying together is a community whose members have stopped interrupting their own desires, and the social consequences follow with the regularity of physics.

**"So They Shall Meet Error"**

The verse closes with the consequence: *fa-sawfa yalqawna ghayyan* — "So they shall meet [the reward of] error." The word *ghayy* (root *gh-w-y*) means error, going astray, ruin — the opposite of *rushd*, right guidance. It is the word used in the Qur'an for the state of those who have lost their way, and notably in the description of Satan's own claim over humanity. The commentators offered two readings of the phrase, and they are complementary. The majority take it to mean that they will meet the *requital* or punishment for their error — the word *ghayy* standing metonymically for what error earns (al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). Some gloss *ghayy* in this context as "evil" or "loss" (al-Ṭabrisī). A second reading, transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, takes *Ghayy* as a proper name — the name of a valley, well, or river in Hell (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). On this reading the verse says that they will meet *Ghayy* itself, the place prepared for those who went astray. Both readings converge on the same point, and it is worth stating because it is the verse's core assertion: **error has a destination.** Going astray is not a neutral condition; it is a direction, and directions arrive somewhere.

**The Structure of the Warning**

It is worth noting the verse's three-part structure, because it is the sūrah's standard anatomy of decline and it recurs throughout the Qur'an with a consistency that suggests it is being offered as a diagnostic instrument rather than as a description of one group. The structure is a sequence and not a list, and the sequence is what makes it useful — a reader can locate himself in it at a stage before the last one: The three stages are these: first, a loss of discipline, which the verse names as neglecting the prayer; second, a surrender to appetite, named as following desires; third, an arrival, named as meeting error. The order is not arbitrary, and the third stage is not a punishment imposed from outside but the natural terminus of the first two — a person who has stopped maintaining the barrier and has begun following appetite arrives somewhere, and error is simply where that road ends. Every community that has collapsed religiously has passed through those three stages, and the order is invariable, because the prayer is the thing that stands between the person and the appetite. Remove the barrier and the river takes its course. The sūrah's remedy, which comes in the very next verse, begins where the disease began — and the correspondence between the diagnosis and the remedy is one of the chapter's most practical features. It does not prescribe an intensity of devotion greater than the one that was abandoned; it prescribes the return of the thing whose absence started the decline.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:60

> **save for those who repent and believe and work righteousness. It is they who shall enter the Garden, and they shall not be wronged in the least:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Saves the Verse From Despair**

The sūrah follows its severest diagnosis with the widest possible door: *illā man tāba wa-āmana wa-ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-ulāʾika yadkhulūna al-jannata wa-lā yuẓlamūna shayʾan* — "save for those who repent and believe and work righteousness. It is they who shall enter the Garden, and they shall not be wronged in the least." The word *illā* — "except," "save for" — is one of the most important words in the Qur'an, and it appears at the hinge of the sūrah's argument. Nothing in the previous verse is retracted. The generation that neglected the prayer and followed its desires will meet error. But the door is not closed *to any person in it*, and the sūrah says so in the same breath.

**The Three Conditions**

The sūrah names three things, and the tradition has always read them as a single integrated response rather than as three separate items: *Repentance (*tawbah*).* The root *t-w-b* means to return. Repentance in Islam is not an emotion; it is a turning around. Its conditions, as the scholars enumerated them, are four: to stop the sin, to regret it, to resolve not to return to it, and — where the sin involved another person's right — to restore it. *Faith (*īmān*).* Belief in God, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day — the content of faith being defined by the Qur'an and the famous ḥadīth of Gabriel (Muslim 8). *Righteous action (*ʿamal ṣāliḥ*).* Deeds that are sound in themselves and done for God. The order is deliberate and matches the structure of the disease in v. 59. The disease was: neglect of the prayer (a failure of action), followed by the pursuit of desire (a failure of the heart's orientation). The cure reverses it: turn back, believe, and act.

**Faith and Works: The Qur'an's Consistent Pairing**

The pairing of belief and righteous action is the single most repeated formula in the Qur'an, and the sheer frequency is the argument. It appears in some fifty verses, addressed to Muslims and to the People of the Book alike, and a formula repeated that often across that range of audiences is not a stylistic habit but a definition. Faith, in the Qur'an's usage, is not a state that exists prior to action and is then expressed by it; it is the name of a thing that includes its own expression, and a claim to the first that is unaccompanied by the second is described throughout the Book as a claim rather than as a fact:

> *"Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds — for them are Gardens beneath which rivers flow. That is the great triumph."* (Qur'an 85:11)

> *"Whoever does righteousness, whether male or female, while being a believer — We will surely cause him to live a good life, and We will surely give them their reward according to the best of what they used to do."* (Qur'an 16:97)

> *"By time! Verily mankind is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds, and enjoin truth upon one another and enjoin patience upon one another."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

The insistence is not incidental. The Qur'an's refusal to separate the two is aimed at two opposite errors: *Against the claim that belief alone suffices,* the Qur'an insists that faith which produces no obedience is not faith: *"O you who believe! Why do you say what you do not do? It is most hateful to God that you say what you do not do"* (61:2–3). *Against the claim that works alone suffice,* the Qur'an insists that a deed is only accepted if it is grounded in faith and done for God: *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they did, and We shall make them as scattered dust"* (25:23) — said of the deeds of those who disbelieved. The Sunni position, articulated against both the Khārijites (who held that a major sin expels a person from faith entirely) and the Murjiʾites (who held that works are irrelevant to faith), is the middle one: faith is *statement in the heart, affirmation by the tongue, and action by the limbs*; it increases with obedience and decreases with sin; and a believer who commits a major sin does not leave the fold of faith but is deficient in it, entrusted to God's will.

**The Door of Repentance: How Long It Stays Open**

The commentators were emphatic about one thing, and the emphasis is pastoral as much as legal: the door described in this verse remains open until death. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabrisī both cite the relevant texts, and they cite them with visible relief, because the verse's promise of transformation is the tradition's answer to the person who has concluded that his history disqualifies him. The limit is real, and it is stated; but it is a limit in time rather than a limit in the degree of sin:

> *"Repentance is only [accepted] from those who do evil in ignorance, then repent soon after. It is those to whom God turns in mercy... But repentance is not [accepted] for those who do evil until, when death comes to one of them, he says: 'Now I repent' — nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:17–18)

And the Prophet ﷺ stated the timing precisely, which closes the one loophole a reader might otherwise exploit — the assumption that there will be a later opportunity to make the turn, and that the present one can therefore be postponed without cost:

> *"Verily God extends His Hand during the night so that the sinner of the day may repent, and He extends His Hand during the day so that the sinner of the night may repent — until the sun rises from its west."* (Muslim 2759)

> *"God accepts the repentance of His servant as long as the soul has not reached the throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537; Ibn Mājah 4253; graded ḥasan)

And the most famous statement of all, which the tradition has repeated to the despairing and to the complacent alike, and which has carried more people back than any argument in the corpus:

> *"God, Exalted is He, says: 'O son of Adam, so long as you call upon Me and place your hope in Me, I will forgive you for whatever you have done, and I will not mind. O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky and then you sought My forgiveness, I would forgive you. O son of Adam, were you to come to Me with sins nearly as great as the earth, and then you met Me not associating anything with Me, I would come to you with nearly as much forgiveness.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3540; graded ḥasan)

That ḥadīth is the single most consoling text in the corpus, and it is the tradition's standing answer to the person who has concluded that his case is beyond repair. What makes it consoling is not that it is vague — the wording is precise, and the numbers in it are enormous rather than infinite. What makes it consoling is that it relocates the matter entirely: the question is not whether the sin is large but whether the mercy is larger, and the ḥadīth's answer is that it is, by an amount the Prophet ﷺ described in terms that exceed anything a person could have accumulated against himself.

**"They Shall Not Be Wronged in the Least"**

The verse closes with a promise of exact justice: *wa-lā yuẓlamūna shayʾan* — "and they shall not be wronged in the least." The Qur'an asserts God's refusal to wrong anyone with a frequency that is clearly deliberate, as though the point needed to be made repeatedly because human beings find it hard to believe — and the repetition is diagnostic. People who have been treated unjustly by human authorities develop a settled expectation of being wronged, and the expectation is then projected onto God. The Qur'an's insistence answers that projection rather than a doctrine, since no one argues that God *may* wrong; what people do is live as though He will.

> *"...and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 2:281; 3:25; 3:161; 4:49; 4:77; 4:124; 6:160; 8:60; 16:111; 17:71; 23:62; 36:54; 39:69; 46:19)

The word *shayʾan* — "a thing," "anything at all" — is an indefinite noun used for emphatic negation: they will not be wronged *by so much as a thing*. The theological point is precise. God does not punish anyone beyond what he deserves, and He does not diminish anyone's reward. What He does beyond that — forgiveness of the repentant, multiplication of good deeds tenfold or more — is bounty, not obligation. The distinction is one the tradition maintained carefully: justice is what is owed; grace is what is given; and both are real. The Prophet ﷺ stated it in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden wrongdoing to Myself, and I have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another... O My servants, these are your deeds; I record them for you and then recompense you for them. Whoever finds good, let him praise God; and whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself."* (Muslim 2577)

**The Pastoral Structure of the Two Verses**

Read vv. 59 and 60 together and a pattern emerges that is worth naming, because it is the Qur'an's characteristic method and the modern reader will often misjudge it. The pattern is: *state the ruin in full, then open the door in full.* The Qur'an does not soften the diagnosis in order to make the cure seem plausible, and it does not qualify the cure in order to make the diagnosis seem serious. Both are stated absolutely, and the reader is left to hold both at once. That is harder than either alternative. It is much easier to say either "the situation is hopeless" or "it does not really matter." The Qur'an refuses both, and the refusal is psychologically accurate: despair produces paralysis, and reassurance produces complacency. Only the two together produce movement — which is precisely what repentance is.

**Repentance as the Sūrah's Answer to Neglect**

Finally, note the specific remedy the sūrah prescribes for the specific disease of v. 59. That generation neglected the prayer and followed its desires. The cure is not "start praying again" — though that is certainly required. It is *repent*, which is a change of direction rather than a change of schedule. The sūrah is making the same point the Prophet ﷺ made:

> *"Actions are but by intentions, and every man shall have only what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

A person who resumes the prayer without turning will be back where he started within a month, because he has treated the symptom and left the cause in place. A person who turns will find that the prayer resumes itself, because the prayer is where a turned person goes — and the sūrah's order throughout has been exactly this. Repentance is presented as the first stage rather than as the consequence of reform, and a person who reverses the sequence is attempting to behave his way into a change of direction rather than changing direction and then behaving accordingly.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:61

> **Gardens of Eden, those which the Compassionate promised His servants in the Unseen. Verily His Promise shall come to pass.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Gardens of Eden" — The Word and What It Means**

The verse elaborates on the Garden promised in v. 60: *jannāti ʿadnin allatī waʿada al-Raḥmānu ʿibādahu bi'l-ghaybi innahu kāna waʿduhu maʿtiyyā* — "Gardens of Eden, those which the Compassionate promised His servants in the Unseen. Verily His Promise shall come to pass." *Jannāt ʿadn* — "Gardens of Eden" — is one of the Qur'an's standard names for the abode of the blessed, appearing in 9:72; 13:23; 16:31; 18:31; 19:61; 20:76; 35:33; 38:50; 40:8; and 98:8. The word *ʿadn* comes from a root meaning *to remain, to settle, to abide permanently*. So *ʿadn* denotes a place in which one settles and does not move on — the opposite of the transient encampments of this world. The Qur'an's use of the term thus carries within it the promise of permanence, which is one of the things the Garden is for: nothing in this world stays, and the human heart was made for something that does. The word *jannah* itself (root *j-n-n*, to conceal, to cover) denotes a garden — an enclosed place, shaded and planted. The image is deliberately the opposite of the desert that was the sūrah's original audience's environment: enclosure, shade, water, and permanence.

**"Promised... in the Unseen"**

The phrase *bi'l-ghayb* — "in the Unseen" — is the theologically loaded part of the verse, and the commentators unpacked it in two related ways. The first, given by al-Ṭabarī, is that the Gardens are *in the Unseen* in the sense that they remain hidden from human beings while they are in this world. No one has seen them; no one can inspect them; they are known only by report. The second, which follows from the first, is more demanding: the servants believed in what they had not seen. That is the definition of faith that the Qur'an opens Sūrah al-Baqarah with:

> *"Who believe in the Unseen, and establish the prayer, and spend from what We have provided them."* (Qur'an 2:3)

So the verse is also a statement about the *quality* of the believers' response. The promise is made in the Unseen, and it is believed in the Unseen. Nothing is produced for inspection; the entire transaction rests on the trustworthiness of the Promiser. That is the whole point of the earthly life as a test, and it is why the Garden is described this way. If the Garden were visible, believing in it would be like believing in a chair. The test consists precisely in the fact that what is promised has not been seen.

**The Hidden Reward: The Ḥadīth That Defines It**

The commentators on this verse consistently cite the ḥadīth qudsī that is the definitive statement of what God has prepared, and they do so because the verse itself describes the reward only by negation — that it is unseen and unimagined — which leaves the reader wanting precisely what the ḥadīth supplies:

> *"God, Exalted is He, says: 'I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and what has never occurred to any human heart.' Recite, if you wish: 'No soul knows what comfort is kept hidden for them as a recompense for that which they used to do.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3072; Muslim 2824 — citing Qur'an 32:17)

The verse the Prophet ﷺ pointed to is 32:17, and the pointing matters, because it establishes that the ḥadīth is not an independent report about the Garden but the Prophet's own interpretation of a Qur'anic verse. The two are to be read as a single text:

> *"No soul knows what comfort is kept hidden for them (*qurratu aʿyunin*) as a recompense for that which they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:17)

The commentators noted the phrase *qurratu aʿyunin* — "a cooling of the eyes" — which is the same idiom used of Mary's comfort at v. 26, and the repetition is one of the sūrah's characteristic echoes of its own vocabulary. The sūrah is thus connecting the comfort God gave Mary in her extremity to the comfort God will give the believers in the Hereafter. Both are *qurrat ʿayn*, and the connection is the argument: the God who sent water and food to a woman in labour is the God who will give the believers what no eye has seen, and the continuity between the two is the continuity of a single Giver rather than a resemblance between two events.

**"Verily His Promise Shall Come to Pass"**

The verse closes with an emphatic statement: *innahu kāna waʿduhu maʿtiyyā* — "Verily His Promise shall come to pass." The grammar here is unusual and was discussed by the commentators and the grammarians. The word *maʿtiyyā* is a passive participle of the root *ʾ-t-y* (to come): literally, "that which is come to," "that which is arrived at." Since a promise is not a place, the passive form is striking; some grammarians explained it as an emphatic idiom — the promise is *so certainly fulfilled that it is as though it has already been arrived at* — while others took it actively, meaning "His promise comes," or "He brings His promise to pass." Either way the force is the same, and it is emphatic: the promise is not a possibility. The particle *inna*, the verb *kāna* (denoting an established, continuing state), and the passive participle together produce one of the strongest possible assertions of certainty in Arabic. The Qur'an repeats this assertion with the same confidence:

> *"Verily the Promise of God is true, so let not the life of this world delude you..."* (Qur'an 31:33)

> *"God — there is no god but He. He will surely gather you unto the Day of Resurrection, in which there is no doubt. And who is truer than God in statement?"* (Qur'an 4:87)

That last verse supplies the argument, and it is worth stating in full because it is the thing that distinguishes a divine promise from every other kind. A promise is only as reliable as the promiser, and God is the only promiser who cannot be incapacitated, cannot forget, cannot change His mind, and cannot be prevented. Every human promise fails for one of those four reasons, and the removal of all four is what the tradition means by describing God as sufficient: the gap between a promise and its fulfilment, which in human affairs is where trust has to live, does not exist.

**The Names in This Verse**

It is worth noting the two Names the verse uses, because the sūrah has been deliberate about them throughout. *Al-Raḥmān* — "the Compassionate" — is now used for the tenth time in this sūrah. The promise of the Garden is attached explicitly to the mercy-Name, which is to say that the Garden is not a wage earned but a mercy given. The Prophet ﷺ stated this in a ḥadīth that no Muslim should misread:

> *"None of you will enter the Garden by his deeds."* They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Not even me — unless God envelops me in His Mercy. But know that the most beloved of deeds to God is that which is done consistently, even if it is small."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

*ʿIbādahu* — "His servants" — is the word the sūrah has used throughout for the relationship between God and His chosen: Zachariah (v. 2), Jesus (vv. 30, 93), and now the believers generally. The sūrah's vocabulary is consistent: the people who receive the promise are the people who belong to Him. The consistency is the chapter's answer to any reader who had supposed that servanthood was a low station reserved for those who could attain nothing better. It is the word used for the prophets at their highest moments and the word used for the believers at theirs, and the sūrah has been arguing since v. 30 that there is nothing above it.

**The Practical Weight of an Unseen Promise**

There is a final practical dimension to this verse that deserves to be stated, because it addresses one of the most common experiences of religious life. The promise is *bi'l-ghayb* — in the Unseen. That means the believer's entire orientation is toward something he cannot currently verify. Every day, therefore, involves a choice between what can be verified now (comfort, advantage, approval, money) and what cannot (the Garden, the reckoning, the Face of God). That is not an occasional dilemma; it is the structure of every decision. The tradition's answer is not to pretend that the visible is unreal. It is to hold the two in the correct relation, which the Qur'an states plainly:

> *"Nay, you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more enduring."* (Qur'an 87:16–17)

The word *abqā* — "more enduring" — is the operative one. The visible is real but temporary; the promised is unseen but permanent. The comparison is not between real and unreal but between what lasts and what does not. And the sūrah's own closing movement will press exactly this point: the disbelievers boast of what they can see (v. 73: "Which of the two groups is better in station or fairer in company?"), and the sūrah answers by pointing to what cannot be seen and cannot be lost.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:62

> **They shall hear no idle talk therein, but only "Peace!" And therein they shall have their provision, morning and evening.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Negative Description: What Is Not There**

The sūrah's description of the Garden proceeds by negation before it proceeds by affirmation: *lā yasmaʿūna fīhā laghwan illā salāman wa-lahum rizquhum fīhā bukratan wa-ʿashiyyan* — "They shall hear no idle talk therein, but only 'Peace!' And therein they shall have their provision, morning and evening." The first thing promised about the Garden is an *absence*. That is worth pausing on, because it is not what most people expect from a description of paradise. Most people imagine a superabundance of pleasant things. The Qur'an's first move is to tell them what will not be there. The word *laghw* (root *l-gh-w*) means idle, vain, purposeless, or harmful speech. The lexicographers defined it as speech that has no point — and the Qur'an uses it for exactly the kind of talk that fills most of human conversation:

> *"And those who turn away from idle talk (*al-laghw*), and those who are active in giving zakāh."* (Qur'an 23:3)

> *"And when they hear idle talk, they turn away therefrom and say: 'Unto us our deeds, and unto you your deeds. Peace be upon you! We do not seek out the ignorant.'"* (Qur'an 28:55)

The same promise is repeated in several passages describing the Garden, and the repetition establishes it as one of the Garden's defining features rather than as an incidental detail. What the Qur'an chooses to repeat about the next life is what the reader is meant to want, and the frequency of this particular promise is therefore an instruction about what the Garden is:

> *"Therein they shall hear no idle talk nor any sin, but only the saying: 'Peace, peace!'"* (Qur'an 56:25–26)

> *"They shall not hear therein idle talk or falsehood, but only [the greeting of] 'Peace!'"* (Qur'an 78:35; 88:11; 52:23)

**Why the Removal of Idle Talk Is the First Promise**

The tradition read these verses as a statement about what most exhausts human beings, and the reading is psychologically acute. Most of the suffering of social life is verbal. Gossip, insult, insinuation, boasting, argument, complaint, flattery, and the low-grade background noise of people competing for standing — these are the things that make human company tiring. The Qur'an's promise is not merely that the Garden has no pain, but that it has no *noise* — no speech that carries no good and does damage. The Prophet ﷺ stated the corresponding obligation directly:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

That ḥadīth is, in effect, the worldly counterpart of this verse. The believer's discipline in this life — speak good or be silent — is preparation for the place where nothing else exists. There is also a subtle symmetry with the sūrah's own opening movement. Mary was slandered by her people (vv. 27–28) — accused, in the strongest possible terms, on the basis of appearances. The Garden is promised as the place in which no such speech exists. The two are connected: the woman who was destroyed by words in this world is honoured in a place where only *salām* is spoken.

**"But Only: Peace!"**

The exception is a single word: *illā salāman* — "but only 'Peace!'" *Salām* — the sūrah's recurring word — means peace, safety, wholeness, and freedom from defect. It is the greeting of the believers in this world (*al-salāmu ʿalaykum*), the greeting of the angels to the people of the Garden, and the greeting with which the people of the Garden greet one another. The Qur'an describes it in several places:

> *"And those who were reverent will be led to the Garden in companies... and the angels will enter upon them from every gate [saying]: 'Peace be upon you, for that you patiently persevered. How excellent is the final abode!'"* (Qur'an 13:23–24)

> *"They will not hear therein idle talk, but only [the greeting of] 'Peace!' And they will have their provision therein, morning and evening."* (Qur'an 19:62)

> *"Their greeting therein will be: 'Glory be to You, O God!' And their greeting therein will be: 'Peace!' And the end of their call will be: 'Praise be to God, the Lord of the worlds!'"* (Qur'an 10:10)

> *"Their greeting on the Day they meet Him will be: 'Peace'; and He has prepared for them a noble reward."* (Qur'an 33:44)

The word is doing more than describing a pleasant atmosphere, and the theological weight of it is easy to miss. *Salām* is one of the Beautiful Names of God (*"He is God, than Whom there is no other god, the Sovereign, the Holy, the Peace (*al-Salām*)..."*, 59:23), and the Qur'an states that God will Himself greet the people of the Garden:

> *"Peace! — a word from a Merciful Lord."* (Qur'an 36:58)

**"Their Provision, Morning and Evening"**

The verse's second promise is provision at two times: *bukratan wa-ʿashiyyan* — "morning and evening." The sūrah has used this exact pair once before, at v. 11, where Zachariah came out of the sanctuary and signalled to his people *"that they should glorify morning and evening."* The repetition is deliberate and is one of the sūrah's most elegant structural devices: the same pair of times that marked the worship of the prophets on earth now marks the provision of the believers in the Garden. The rhythm of the day is thus carried over. What was the rhythm of *dhikr* becomes the rhythm of *rizq* — remembrance answered with provision. There is, however, an obvious difficulty. Morning and evening imply a sun that rises and sets, and the Qur'an states that the Garden has no night. The commentators addressed this directly. Al-Ṭabarī transmits the explanation that there is no night or midday in the Garden, since there is no darkness; rather, "night" and "day" there are measured by the lowering and lifting of veils and the closing and opening of gates. The more general answer is that the Qur'an consistently uses the language of this world to describe the next, because no other language is available. The sūrah will do the same at v. 73, where the disbelievers boast of their "station" and "company," and the sūrah answers in kind. Description by analogy is not literalism; it is the only way to speak of what has not been experienced.

**The Provision Itself**

What is the provision? The commentators answered: food and drink, and everything they desire (al-Ṭabarī), and the generality of the answer is deliberate, since the verse promises provision without specifying it and the specification is supplied elsewhere in the Book rather than here. The Qur'an describes it in more detail in other passages:

> *"And We shall provide them with fruit and meat, such as they desire."* (Qur'an 52:22)

> *"And We shall give them to drink of a pure drink."* (Qur'an 76:21)

> *"Therein is whatever the souls desire and the eyes delight in, and you will abide therein forever."* (Qur'an 43:71)

The last of those verses is the summary: whatever the soul desires and whatever delights the eye. The Garden's provision is not a fixed menu but the removal of the gap between what a person wants and what is good for him. In this life the two diverge constantly, which is the source of most human misery. In the Garden they coincide.

**A Note on the Word Rizq**

The word *rizq* — provision, sustenance — is theologically significant in Islam and deserves a note. The Qur'an attributes *rizq* to God alone, and it uses the word for everything a person is given: wealth, children, knowledge, opportunity, health, and — as here — the food of the Garden. The theological consequence, which the tradition drew out at length, is that provision is not earned but allotted, and that the pursuit of it is therefore to be conducted within the limits God has set rather than by any means available. The Prophet ﷺ taught this in a ḥadīth of unusual beauty:

> *"The Holy Spirit [Gabriel] has inspired into my heart that no soul will die until it has completed its allotted term and exhausted its allotted provision. So be reverent toward God and be moderate in seeking [provision]; and let not the delay of something you are due cause you to seek it through disobedience to God — for what is with God is not attained except through obedience to Him."* (al-Ḥākim; al-Bayhaqī; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

That ḥadīth is the practical corollary of this verse, and it resolves a tension that readers have sometimes manufactured between reliance and effort. The provision is promised; the means of seeking it are regulated; and the two are not in tension, because the Provider is One. A person who has been promised provision does not thereby learn that seeking it is pointless; he learns that seeking it is not what feeds him. The distinction is the same one the sūrah drew at v. 25 with the birds, and the chapter has been consistent about it from the beginning.

**The Verse's Place in the Sequence**

Verses 60–63 form a single description of the Garden, and it is worth seeing their order, because the sequence is arranged rather than merely cumulative. The chapter does not list features; it takes the reader through the thing in the order in which it would be encountered:

- **v. 60:** Who enters it — the repentant, the believers, the doers of good.
- **v. 61:** What it is — Gardens of Eden, promised in the Unseen.
- **v. 62:** What is *not* in it — idle talk — and what is: peace and provision.
- **v. 63:** Whose it is — the reverent, by inheritance.

The sequence moves from the people, to the place, to the experience, to the title-deed — and the last of the four is the one the sūrah has been preparing since its opening verses. The sūrah will close the description at v. 63 with a word that ties the whole passage back to v. 40 and v. 6: *nūrithu* — "We bequeath." The circle is thereby closed: God inherits the earth and everyone on it (v. 40), and God bequeaths the Garden to those of His servants who were reverent (v. 63). What is taken with one hand is given with the other, and the two movements are a single act.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:63

> **This is the Garden We shall bequeath unto those among Our servants who were reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Title-Deed**

The sūrah's description of the Garden closes with a statement of ownership: *tilka al-jannatu allatī nūrithu min ʿibādinā man kāna taqiyyan* — "This is the Garden We shall bequeath unto those among Our servants who were reverent." The verb *nūrithu* (root *w-r-th*) is the same root used at v. 6 (Zachariah's prayer for an heir), at v. 40 (*"Surely We shall inherit the earth"*), and at v. 50 (the bestowal of mercy). The sūrah has been building an argument with this root from the beginning, and this verse is its completion. The sequence is worth laying out, because the sūrah has constructed it carefully:

- **v. 6:** A mortal prays for an heir, worried about who will inherit his authority.
- **v. 40:** God answers: *I* am the Inheritor — of the earth and everyone on it.
- **v. 50:** God bestows "something of Our Mercy" on Abraham's family.
- **v. 63:** God bequeaths the Garden to His reverent servants.

The movement is from anxiety about inheritance, to the declaration that all inheritance is God's, to the promise that God's servants inherit from Him. What began as an old man's worry about succession ends as the believers' title to Paradise, and the arc is one of the sūrah's most deliberate pieces of composition. Zachariah's prayer at v. 5 was, on its face, a private request about who would carry his knowledge after him; the sūrah has taken that request and enlarged it until it contains everyone. The reader who noticed the elder's worry at the beginning is meant to recognise it at the end and see what it has become.

**The Direction of the Inheritance**

There is a profound reversal in this verse, and the commentators noted it. In human affairs, inheritance flows one way: the dead leave to the living. In the divine economy described here, the Living bequeaths to the mortal — God, who never dies, gives an inheritance to those who do. The theologians were careful about the direction because the language could be misread. God does not die, and therefore nothing "passes" from Him in the human sense. The commentators' explanation, consistent with what was said at v. 40, is that *nūrithu* here means: We give it to them as a permanent, un-revocable possession, in the manner of an inheritance — that is, as something that will never be taken back. That is the emphasis: not the transfer but the permanence. Human bequests can be contested, diminished, and lost. This one cannot.

**"Those Among Our Servants Who Were Reverent"**

The recipients are described with two terms the sūrah has used throughout. *ʿIbādinā* — "Our servants." The sūrah's term for the relation between God and those He honours: Zachariah (v. 2), the believers (v. 61), every creature (v. 93), and now the people of the Garden. To be a *ʿabd* is, as established at v. 2 and v. 30, the highest station, not the lowest. *Man kāna taqiyyan* — "whoever was reverent."
