The word *taqiyy* (root *w-q-y*) was discussed at v. 13 and v. 18, where it was applied to John and invoked by Mary. *Taqwā* — the guarding awareness of God — is the single most repeated condition of salvation in the Qur'an:

> *"And whoever is reverent toward God, He will make for him a way out, and will provide for him from whence he does not reckon."* (Qur'an 65:2–3)

> *"And the Hereafter, with your Lord, is for the reverent."* (Qur'an 43:35)

> *"And [mention] when it will be said to the reverent: 'What has your Lord sent down?' They will say: 'Good.'"* (Qur'an 16:30)

The sūrah's own closing movement will be built on this term, and a reader who tracks it will find that the chapter's third movement is held together by it. The reverent are gathered "to the Compassionate as an honoured delegation" (v. 85), and God saves "those who are reverent" from the Fire (v. 72). The word is therefore not a description attached to one group but the thread by which the sūrah ties its beginning to its end — the quality Zachariah was described as having at v. 4 being the same quality that is gathered, saved, and given the Garden at the close.

**"This Is the Garden" — The Demonstrative**

The verse opens with *tilka* — "that," used here for emphasis and elevation: *That* is the Garden. The demonstrative points back at everything described in vv. 60–62 — the entry, the Eden, the absence of idle talk, the peace, the provision — and pronounces it to be the inheritance of the reverent. The sūrah's use of *tilka* here is worth comparing with its use of *dhālika* at v. 34 ("That is Jesus son of Mary"). In both cases the demonstrative performs the same function: it gathers a long description into a single identified object and hands it to the reader. The sūrah is not describing things vaguely; it is pointing at them.

**The Garden as Inheritance, Not Wage**

The choice of the inheritance-word carries a doctrinal point that the tradition insisted upon. If the Garden were a *wage*, it would be owed in proportion to work done — and the implication would be that a person can, by sufficient effort, place God in his debt. The Qur'an's language of *inheritance* and *mercy* rules that out. The Garden is called an inheritance because it is *given*, and it is given to servants who are described not by what they accomplished but by what they were: *reverent*. That is not a denial that deeds matter — v. 60 has just named "righteous deeds" as a condition. It is a statement about proportionality. No quantity of human obedience is equivalent to eternal felicity. The disproportion is bridged by grace, and the tradition's formula is: *entry by mercy, degrees by deeds*. The Prophet ﷺ stated it directly:

> *"None of you will enter the Garden by his deeds... unless God envelops me in His Mercy."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

And the Qur'an states the grading, which is the detail that makes the inheritance metaphor exact rather than merely consoling. An inheritance is distributed, and the distribution is not uniform — a fact the sūrah preserves rather than flattens, since a promise of identical reward for every degree of striving would not be justice but its opposite:

> *"And for all are degrees according to what they did, that He may recompense them for their deeds; and they will not be wronged."* (Qur'an 46:19)

**The Closing of the Sūrah's Garden Passage**

With this verse, the sūrah's second movement reaches its conclusion, and the ending is worth marking because it is not where a reader would have expected the passage to stop. The structure from v. 41 to v. 63 now stands complete:

- **vv. 41–57:** The catalogue of the prophets — Abraham, Moses, Aaron, Ishmael, Idrīs — and the gifts given to each after withdrawal.
- **v. 58:** The summary: they are those whom God blessed, and they fell down weeping at His signs.
- **vv. 59:** The decline of the generations after them.
- **vv. 60–63:** The door that remains open, and the Garden promised to those who return.

What follows at v. 64 is a bridge — a verse of a different kind entirely, addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in his own circumstance rather than to the narratives that have preceded it. The shift is abrupt and deliberate: having carried the reader through the stories of the prophets, the sūrah turns to the man who is being addressed and speaks to him about his own situation. Then the sūrah's third and final movement begins at v. 65, and the bridge is what makes the transition possible — the reader has just been shown how God dealt with the prophets, and is now being shown that the same dealing applies to the one receiving this.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:64

> **"We descend not, save by the Command of thy Lord. Unto Him belongs that which is before us and that which is behind us, and whatsoever lies between that, and thy Lord is not forgetful—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Breaks the Pattern**

Verse 64 is unlike anything around it, and the sūrah's readers have always noticed. The catalogue of prophets has closed; the promise of the Garden has been made; and then, without warning, the sūrah records a sentence that appears to be spoken by someone other than God to the Prophet ﷺ: *wa-mā natanazzalu illā bi-amri rabbika lahu mā bayna aydīnā wa-mā khalfanā wa-mā bayna dhālika wa-mā kāna rabbuka nasiyyā* — "We descend not, save by the Command of thy Lord..." The commentators explained the interruption by reference to its occasion, and the occasion is one of the most human episodes in the entire revelation.

**The Occasion of Revelation**

The reports, transmitted by al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Zamakhsharī, agree on the essentials. There was a period during which no revelation came to the Prophet ﷺ through Gabriel. The reports vary as to its length — twelve nights, fifteen nights, or forty nights. During this interval the Quraysh had been questioning the Prophet about three matters: the Companions of the Cave, Dhu'l-Qarnayn, and the Spirit — the questions whose answers appear in Sūrah al-Kahf (18:9–26, 83–98, 85). The Prophet did not know the answers and was waiting for revelation to guide him. Two things made the delay painful. The first was the external pressure: the idolaters began to say that his Lord had abandoned him (*"His Lord has not forsaken him, nor is He displeased"*, 93:3, is the Qur'an's answer to exactly this taunt). The second was internal: the Prophet ﷺ wanted to answer his questioners and could not, and the silence was, for him, a trial. When Gabriel finally came, the Prophet ﷺ said to him — in the words the reports transmit: *"What prevented you from visiting us more than you do?"* And this verse was the answer. The first-person plural — *We descend not* — thus belongs to Gabriel, speaking on behalf of the angels. That is the reading the commentators adopted (al-Zamakhsharī, al-Ṭabrisī), and it is supported by the verse's own logic: angels do not act on their own initiative.

**"We Descend Not Save by the Command of thy Lord"**

The angel's answer is, in the first instance, an explanation of angelic procedure: *We descend only when commanded.* The theological content is significant, and it is being stated here rather than elsewhere because it is the answer to the anxiety underneath the question. Angels in Islam are not independent agents. They do not choose when to act, what to convey, or whom to visit. The Qur'an states this explicitly:

> *"They do not precede Him in speech, and they act by His Command."* (Qur'an 21:27)

> *"And they do not descend except by the command of thy Lord. To Him belongs what is before us and what is behind us and what is between that; and thy Lord is not forgetful."* (Qur'an 19:64)

> *"The angels and the Spirit descend therein, by the leave of their Lord, for every matter."* (Qur'an 97:4)

So the first lesson of the verse is that the *timing* of revelation is not the angel's to determine. Gabriel did not stay away out of reluctance or neglect. He was not sent — and the negation is complete, because it removes every possible reading of the delay as something personal. The delay was not a decision made about the Prophet ﷺ by a messenger who had been free to come sooner; it was a decision made by God, transmitted by a messenger who had no discretion in it whatever.

**The Three Dimensions of Divine Knowledge**

The verse's second clause is one of the most comprehensive statements of God's knowledge in the Qur'an: *lahu mā bayna aydīnā wa-mā khalfanā wa-mā bayna dhālika* — "Unto Him belongs that which is before us and that which is behind us, and whatsoever lies between that." The commentators offered several readings of the three terms, and the accumulation is itself the point — the phrase is constructed to exhaust every dimension: *Temporal readings:* the affairs of this world and the Hereafter; the time before the creation of human beings and the time after their annihilation; the past and the future (al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabrisī). *Spatial readings:* the earth and the heaven; what is in front of the angels and what is behind them (al-Ṭabrisī). *Intermediate readings:* the period between the two blastings of the Trumpet — the first of which ends the world and the second of which raises the dead (39:68) — or the span of the angels' own existence, or the period of human earthly life (al-Ṭabrisī). The phrase recurs in the Qur'an in nearly identical form, which confirms that it is a formula for totality:

> *"He knows what is before them and what is behind them, while they cannot encompass anything of His Knowledge except what He wills."* (Qur'an 2:255)

The point the verse is making to the Prophet ﷺ is this: *nothing is outside His knowledge or His authority — not what has passed, not what is to come, not what lies in between. Therefore nothing about the timing of your revelation is accidental.* The threefold division is exhaustive, and the exhaustiveness is the comfort. What lies between our hands — the present interval, the thing happening now, the delay itself — is named explicitly alongside the past and the future, because that is the category in which the anxiety lives. A person is rarely troubled by what has been or by what will be; he is troubled by what is happening now and seems to have no meaning.

**"And Thy Lord Is Not Forgetful"**

The verse closes with the reassurance that the whole episode was about: *wa-mā kāna rabbuka nasiyyā* — "and thy Lord is not forgetful." The word *nasiyy* (root *n-s-y*, to forget) is an intensive form: your Lord is *not one who forgets*. The statement is addressed to a fear that every believer has felt: the fear of being forgotten by God. The Qur'an answers it elsewhere in terms almost identical to these, in the sūrah revealed on an earlier occasion of the same kind:

> *"By the morning brightness, and by the night when it is still — thy Lord has not forsaken thee, nor is He displeased. And the Hereafter will be better for thee than the present. And thy Lord will surely give unto thee, and thou shalt be satisfied."* (Qur'an 93:1–5)

Note the parallel structure, because it is the sūrah's answer to a specific kind of distress rather than merely a repeated formula. In both cases the Qur'an responds to the *inner* fear — "my Lord has abandoned me" — with a direct denial, and then follows it not with an explanation but with a promise. God does not explain the delay. He says: I have not forgotten you, and what is coming is better. The order matters enormously for anyone who has been in that condition: the denial comes first, because a person who believes he has been abandoned cannot take in an explanation, and the promise comes second, because a person who has been reassured can wait.

**The Minority Reading**

The commentators also recorded a second reading of the verse, which they noted but did not prefer: that the words are spoken by the people of the Garden, since they do not settle in the Garden except by God's command (al-Ṭabrisī). The reading is grammatically possible — the verse follows the description of the Garden — but it fits the vocabulary poorly. "We descend" (*natanazzalu*) is not how the inhabitants of the Garden would describe their arrival, and the verse's closing reassurance about God's not forgetting is addressed to someone who had been waiting in distress. The majority reading — Gabriel speaking to the Prophet — is the one the tradition adopted.

**The Theology of Delay**

This verse is the Qur'an's most direct treatment of a universal human experience, and it deserves to be drawn out beyond its historical occasion. The experience is: *God has stopped answering, and I do not know why.* The silence may be about a prayer, a direction, a relief, or a sense of nearness that has disappeared. And the conclusions the human mind draws from silence are almost always worse than the truth: I have been rejected; I have done something wrong; I was never really heard. The sūrah's answer has three parts, and each is worth holding onto: *First, the silence is not neglect.* God is not forgetful. The delay is not an oversight. *Second, the silence is not arbitrary.* Nothing is outside His knowledge. He knows what is before, behind, and between. *Third, the silence is ordered by command.* Gabriel did not come because he was not sent — which means the delay belongs to a plan, even though its reasons are not disclosed. The Prophet ﷺ taught the same in a ḥadīth that every Muslim is encouraged to memorise:

> *"Know that there is much good in patience concerning what you dislike, and that victory comes with patience, relief comes with distress, and ease comes with hardship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And the Prophet ﷺ gave the companion-truth to that promise of relief in a ḥadīth that removes the anxiety which delay always produces — the suspicion that what has not yet arrived has been withheld by someone, or has been lost in the interval, or depends on causes that are quietly failing. The ḥadīth excludes all three, and it does so by relocating the entire matter to a decree that was written before the pen was lifted:

> *"Know that if the whole nation were to gather together to benefit you with something, they would not benefit you except with something God has already written for you..."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516)

**A Final Note on the "Spirit" Question**

It is worth adding one detail about the occasion, because it has a practical bearing on how the Qur'an handles questions. The three questions the Quraysh asked — the Cave, Dhu'l-Qarnayn, and the Spirit — were not answered immediately, and when the answers came, one of them was answered in a way that declined to answer it. Of the Spirit, the Qur'an says:

> *"And they ask thee concerning the Spirit. Say: 'The Spirit is of the Command of my Lord; and you have not been given of knowledge save a little.'"* (Qur'an 17:85)

That is instructive, and it is one of the most practically useful things in the sūrah for a reader who has prayed for something and received silence. God answered the question by placing it beyond the questioner's competence rather than by supplying a fact. And it tells the reader something important: not every silence is followed by a disclosure, and some delays end in a lesson rather than an answer. The distinction is between being told what one wanted to know and being given what one needed — and the tradition's consistent teaching is that the second is what is actually being arranged, even where it does not feel like it.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:65

> **the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them. So worship Him and be steadfast in His worship. Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Movement Begins**

Verse 65 opens the sūrah's final movement, and it does so by establishing God: *rabbu al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa-mā baynahumā fa-ʿbudhu wa-ṣṭabir li-ʿibādatihi hal taʿlamu lahu samiyyā* — "the Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them. So worship Him and be steadfast in His worship. Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?" The verse is continuous with v. 64 — "thy Lord is not forgetful — the Lord of the heavens and the earth" — and the grammatical connection is significant. The reassurance of v. 64 is grounded in the description of v. 65: *He has not forgotten you, because He is the Lord of everything, and nothing escapes Him.*

**"Lord of the Heavens and the Earth"**

The title *Rabb al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* is one of the most frequently used in the Qur'an — appearing at 13:16; 17:102; 18:14; 21:56; 26:24; 37:5; 38:66; 43:82; 44:7; 45:36; and 78:37, among others. Its function in argument is consistent: it establishes God's lordship over the totality of what exists, and thereby establishes that there is nothing outside His domain to which appeal could be made. The same title is the premise of Abraham's argument in Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ and of Moses' address to Pharaoh:

> *"He said: 'The Lord of the heavens and the earth and whatsoever is between them, if you are certain.'"* (Qur'an 26:24)

The addition of *wa-mā baynahumā* — "and whatsoever is between them" — is not redundant. It forecloses the possibility that the space between heaven and earth is an unclaimed region belonging to some intermediate power — a conceit of several ancient cosmologies, and of the Arabian one, in which the jinn and the intermediaries were thought to occupy the middle air.

**"So Worship Him"**

The consequence is stated in a single imperative: *fa-ʿbudhu* — "so worship Him." The particle *fa* makes the inference explicit: *because* He is the Lord of everything, *therefore* worship Him. This is the same syllogism the sūrah placed in the mouth of the infant Jesus at v. 36 (*"Truly God is my Lord and your Lord; so worship Him"*), and it is the argument that structures every prophetic call in the Qur'an:

> *"O mankind! Worship your Lord, Who created you and those before you, that you may become reverent — Who made the earth a bed for you and the sky a canopy, and sent down water from the sky and brought forth thereby fruits as provision for you. So do not set up rivals to God while you know."* (Qur'an 2:21–22)

**"And Be Steadfast in His Worship"**

The second imperative is *wa-ṣṭabir li-ʿibādatihi* — "and be steadfast (*iṣṭabir*) in His worship." The verb is Form VIII of the root *ṣ-b-r*, and the Form VIII carries a sense of *taking upon oneself, persisting, sustaining effort*. The commentators noted that the worship described here is not a single act but a continuous one, and the command is not to perform it but to *sustain* it. That is a deeply practical instruction, and it addresses the actual difficulty of religious life. Most people do not abandon worship because they have concluded it is false; they abandon it because it is *tiresome*. The prayer at dawn is easy to perform for a week and hard to perform for forty years. The Qur'an's command is aimed exactly at that: *iṣṭabir* — hold the line. The Prophet ﷺ identified the quality as the secret of religious consistency:

> *"The most beloved of deeds to God is that which is done consistently, even if it is small."* (al-Bukhārī 6465; Muslim 782)

And he warned against the failure-mode that undoes most people — and the warning is directed at the shape of a practice rather than at its intensity, which is why it has been repeated in every generation of the tradition's teaching on worship:

> *"O people, take on only what you are able to do. For God does not tire until you tire — and the most beloved of deeds to God is that which is done consistently, even if it is small."* (al-Bukhārī 43; Muslim 785)

The pairing of the two statements is the sūrah's teaching in miniature: steadfastness is not achieved by intensity but by sustainability. The point is counter-intuitive and worth stating as plainly as possible, because most people who fail at a religious practice fail at the point of enthusiasm rather than at the point of reluctance. They begin with more than they can maintain, maintain it until they cannot, and then stop — whereas the practice the tradition commends is the one that can be carried for forty years.

**"Dost Thou Know Any Who Can Be Named Alongside Him?"**

The verse's closing question is the sūrah's sharpest formulation of *tawḥīd*: *hal taʿlamu lahu samiyyā* — "Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?" The word *samiyy* (root *s-m-w*, related to *ism*, "name") means a namesake, one who shares a name; and by extension, one who is a peer, a match, an equal. The commentators gave the sense precisely. Al-Ṭabarī: *"Is there any who is His equal or His like in generosity and bounty?"* and alternatively *"Is there any partner or associate who can be named alongside Him?"* Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical character of the question: it is purely interrogative in form but negative in meaning — no one should be called "god" except Him, and therefore it is not right that idols should be referred to as "gods." Al-Rāzī added a specific application: that no one other than God should be called *al-Raḥmān* — a Name that, as already noted, appears sixteen times in this sūrah.

**The Sūrah's Internal Echo: Two "Namesakes"**

There is a literary connection here that repays attention, because the sūrah has built it deliberately and a reader who misses it will misread the verse. The word *samiyy* appears exactly twice in the Qur'an, and both occurrences are in this sūrah — which is to say that the chapter is using the term to answer itself:

- **v. 7:** Of John — *"We have assigned no namesake (*samiyyā*) to him heretofore."*
- **v. 65:** Of God — *"Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him (*samiyyā*)?"*

The sūrah is doing something quite extraordinary with this pairing. It has taken a word used to describe the uniqueness of a created servant — John, who had no namesake — and applied it, thirty verses later, to the Creator, who has no namesake. The effect is to measure the distance between them. Even the most unique of creatures is unique by *concession*, by an act of divine singling-out; God is unique by *nature*. John had no namesake because God made him so. God has no namesake because there cannot be one. That is the sūrah's theology of *tawḥīd* compressed into a single repeated word, and it is characteristic of the Qur'an's method: the same term applied to Creator and creature, with the difference between them carried by the context rather than by the vocabulary.

**The Doctrinal Point: Names Are Not Shared**

Al-Rāzī's application — that no one should be called *al-Raḥmān* except God — deserves expansion, because it reflects a real and carefully maintained principle in Islamic law and theology. The divine Names divide into categories. Some Names are used for both God and creatures, with the meaning appropriate to each: *al-Raḥīm* (merciful) is used of the Prophet ﷺ in the Qur'an (*"a messenger... full of pity, merciful (*raḥīm*) to the believers"*, 9:128), and *al-ʿAlīm* (knowing) and *al-Samīʿ* (hearing) are similarly applied. But these are applied to creatures *qualified and limited*, never absolutely. Other Names belong to God alone and may not be used for a creature without qualification: *Allāh*, *al-Raḥmān*, *Rabb al-ʿālamīn*, *al-Khāliq*, and others. The jurists derived from this the ruling that a person may be called *ʿAbd al-Raḥmān* ("servant of the Compassionate") but not *Raḥmān* itself, and that the divine Names are to be used in supplication with the acknowledgement that they belong to God alone:

> *"And to God belong the Most Beautiful Names, so call upon Him by them, and leave the company of those who deviate concerning His Names. They will be recompensed for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 7:180)

**The Bridge Into the Final Movement**

Verse 65 completes the sūrah's transition and sets up everything that follows. The remaining thirty-three verses will address, in order: the denial of resurrection (vv. 66–72), the boasts of the wealthy (vv. 73–76), the man who claimed he would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter (vv. 77–80), the futility of false gods (vv. 81–82), the satans unleashed on the disbelievers (vv. 83–84), the two processions (vv. 85–86), intercession (v. 87), the refutation of divine sonship (vv. 88–95), the promise of love (v. 96), the ease of the Qur'an (v. 97), and the silence of the destroyed generations (v. 98). Every one of those passages is an application of this verse. The resurrection is denied by people who have not reckoned with a Lord who owns everything. Wealth is boasted of by people who have not asked whether anyone can be named alongside Him. Intercession is sought from beings who are themselves servants. And the claim that God has a child is, at bottom, the claim that someone *can* be named alongside Him. The sūrah has stated its premise. What follows is its working out.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:66

> **Man says, "When I am dead, shall I be brought forth alive?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection, Stated in Its Own Words**

The sūrah's third movement opens with the objection it is about to answer, quoted verbatim from the mouths of the objectors: *wa-yaqūlu al-insānu a-idhā mā mittu la-sawfa ukhrāju ḥayyan* — "Man says, 'When I am dead, shall I be brought forth alive?'" The Qur'an uses the definite *al-insān* — "man," humankind, the human being as such. The commentators noted that while the phrasing is generic, the reference in context is to the idolaters of Makkah who rejected the Resurrection (al-Ṭabarī). The generality of the wording is itself part of the argument: this is the standard human objection, in every age, and the Qur'an answers it as such. The question is framed as a question but is not one. It is a dismissal. The form *a-idhā* — "when...? shall...?" — is the Arabic idiom for expressing incredulity about a consequence. The speaker is not asking for information; he is saying: *this is absurd.*

**The Objection Appears Throughout the Qur'an**

The same objection, in nearly identical words, is quoted and answered in several places, and the repetition is one of the most striking features of the Qur'an's argument for the Resurrection. A modern reader tends to find the repetition redundant and to wonder why the Book returns to it; the sūrah's own logic explains why. The objection is not an argument that can be answered once and disposed of, because it is not really an argument — it is a reflex of the imagination, and a reflex has to be answered every time it appears:

> *"And they say: 'When we are bones and fragments, shall we really be raised up as a new creation?' Say: 'Be you stones or iron, or some created thing that is yet greater in your breasts!' Then they will say: 'Who will bring us back?' Say: 'He Who originated you the first time.'"* (Qur'an 17:49–51)

> *"And he strikes for Us a comparison, and forgets his own creation. He says: 'Who will give life to bones when they are decayed?' Say: 'He will give them life Who produced them the first time, and He is Knower of every creation.'"* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

> *"Does man reckon that We will not assemble his bones? Nay — We are able to restore [even] his fingertips."* (Qur'an 75:3–4)

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'The Hour will not come to us.' Say: 'Nay, by my Lord, it will surely come to you...'"* (Qur'an 34:3)

The recurrence tells the reader something important: the objection is not treated as a difficulty to be accommodated but as a fixed and predictable response. The Qur'an quotes it, and then it answers it, in the same terms, every time. What is being modelled, therefore, is not a piece of information but a posture — the reader is being shown how to handle this objection when he meets it, in others or in himself, and the consistency of the answer is the lesson. A different answer at each occurrence would suggest that the answer depended on the objector; the same answer every time suggests that it does not.

**Why the Objection Is Rational — and Why It Is Still Wrong**

It is worth taking the objection seriously, because the Qur'an does, and because dismissiveness toward it would make the answer seem cheaper than it is. The objection rests on observation, and the observation is accurate. Human beings do die. Bodies decay. Bones crumble. Nothing has ever been observed to reassemble itself. A person who generalises from every available instance will conclude that death is terminal — and the Qur'an does not deny the observation. What it denies is the *inference from the observation to the impossibility of resurrection*, and there are two distinct moves in that denial. *The first move concerns the scope of the uniform.* The regularity of nature is real, but it is a description of what God ordinarily does, not a constraint upon what He can do. The person who says "this has never happened" is describing a pattern, and a pattern is not a law unless something enforces it. If the pattern is God's habit, then God can depart from it, and the report of a departure cannot be ruled out in advance by the regularity of the pattern. *The second move concerns the authority of the reporter.* The claim that a departure has occurred comes from a messenger, attested by signs, whose truthfulness is the question actually at issue. Once that is established, the resurrection is no longer an improbable event being proposed on no evidence; it is a reported event proposed on the testimony of a witness whose reliability has been demonstrated.

That is the structure of the Qur'an's argument, and it is why the Qur'an spends so much of its energy on the credibility of the messenger rather than on the plausibility of the Resurrection. The Resurrection is not intrinsically improbable; it is merely unprecedented, and unprecedented things are credible when reported by a credible source. The observation is worth holding onto, because it explains a feature of the Qur'an that otherwise looks like a disproportion: the Book argues endlessly for the truthfulness of the Prophet ﷺ and comparatively rarely for the mechanics of the Resurrection, and it does so because the second question is settled by the first.

**The Word Ukhrāju — "Brought Forth"**

The verb *ukhrāju* (root *kh-r-j*) is passive: "shall I be brought forth." The passive is significant. The speaker is not asking whether he will *come back*; he is asking whether someone will *bring him out*. And the passive voice, in the Arabic idiom, often leaves the agent unnamed — which is precisely the point. The denier's question contains its own answer. To be "brought forth" requires a bringer. The question is not whether resurrection is possible in the abstract but whether the One who claims He will do it can do it. And the answer to that is the subject of the next verse.

**The Modern Forms of the Objection**

It is worth noting that the objection in this verse has not aged. Its contemporary forms are different in vocabulary and identical in structure. The most common modern version is not "bones cannot reassemble" but "consciousness cannot survive the brain." The argument runs: mind depends on brain; brain dies; therefore mind ceases. And the same two moves apply. First, the dependence of mind on brain is a description of how God ordinarily operates, not a constraint on what He can do — a person who grants that God created mind-brain dependence in the first place can hardly maintain that He cannot sustain mind without brain. Second, the uniformity of the observation does not preclude a reported departure, and the reliability of the reporter is the real question. A second modern form is moral rather than metaphysical: the sense that judgement is too grand, too retributive, or too anthropomorphic to be credible. The Qur'an's answer to that is not an argument but a warning, and it appears repeatedly in this sūrah's closing movement: the objection is not really about the physics of resurrection but about the discomfort of being accountable. That is why the sūrah pairs the intellectual objection (vv. 66–67) immediately with the scene of judgement (vv. 68–72).

**The Sūrah's Placement of the Objection**

Note where the objection is placed. It comes immediately after *"Dost thou know any who can be named alongside Him?"* (v. 65) — that is, immediately after the sūrah's statement of God's absolute uniqueness and absolute power. The sequence is deliberate: *He is the Lord of everything with no peer; and you ask whether He can raise the dead?* The objection is thus exposed as not merely mistaken but inconsistent with what the objector would himself concede. Nobody who grants that God is the Lord of the heavens and the earth can maintain that the same God is unable to reconstitute a human body. That is why the sūrah's answer in v. 67 is so short. It does not argue. It asks a single question that dissolves the objection from the inside.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:67

> **Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was naught?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Is a Question About Memory**

The sūrah's response to the objection is one sentence: *a-wa-lā yadhkuru al-insānu annā khalaqnāhu min qablu wa-lam yaku shayʾan* — "Does man not remember that We created him before, when he was naught?" Two features of this verse deserve immediate attention. *The first is the verb: yadhkuru.* The root *dh-k-r* — remembrance — is the sūrah's opening word (*dhikru raḥmati rabbika*, v. 2) and its organizing concept. The sūrah began by commanding remembrance and now, sixty-five verses later, asks why the remembering is not happening. The objection to the Resurrection is thus diagnosed not as an intellectual failure but as a failure of *dhikr* — a failure to hold in mind what one already knows. *The second is the evidence: his own origin.* The argument is not cosmological. It is not "look at the universe." It is: *look at yourself.* You did not exist. Then you did. Who did that?

**The Argument Stated Formally**

The verse's argument is one of the most frequently used in the Qur'an, and it is worth stating in its logical form because its force is often missed by readers who take it as rhetoric. The argument is an inference from an admitted fact to a denied conclusion, which is the strongest available form — the objection's own premise is used to establish what the objector is denying:

1. Creating a thing from nothing is, by any human reckoning, more difficult than reconstituting or altering a thing that already exists.
2. God has already done the more difficult thing to this very objector: He created him when he was *nothing*.
3. Therefore the reconstitution of the same person after death is not a greater act than the one already performed.

The argument appears in this exact form repeatedly, and the repetition across different contexts is what allows the reader to abstract it. He is not being given a set of answers to a set of questions but a single instrument applied to many cases:

> *"And he strikes for Us a comparison, and forgets his own creation. He says: 'Who will give life to bones when they are decayed?' Say: 'He will give them life Who produced them the first time.'"* (Qur'an 36:78–79)

> *"And it is He Who originates creation, then brings it back again — and that is easier for Him."* (Qur'an 30:27)

> *"Were We then worn out by the first creation? Nay, but they are in doubt about a new creation."* (Qur'an 50:15)

That last verse is especially pointed, because it identifies with unusual precision what the objection actually consists of. The objection is not that God lacks the power; it is that the objector cannot picture it. And the Qur'an's answer throughout is that the difficulty is imaginative rather than real — which is a diagnosis rather than a refutation, and is far more useful for that reason. A person cannot be argued out of a failure of imagination, but he can be shown that his inability to picture a thing is not evidence about it.

**"When He Was Naught" — The Phrase the Sūrah Uses Twice**

The phrase *wa-lam yaku shayʾan* — "when he was naught" — is identical to the phrase God used to Zachariah at v. 9: *"I had created thee before, when thou wast nothing."* The repetition is one of the sūrah's central structural devices, and its effect is to place Zachariah and the resurrection-denier in the same argument. Zachariah was told: you were nothing, and I made you; therefore I can give a child to a barren woman. The denier is told: you were nothing, and I made you; therefore I can raise you after you have decayed. Both are instances of the same principle, and the sūrah has deliberately used the same five Arabic words for both. The reader who notices the echo has understood the sūrah's entire theology of creation: **there is only one act of creation, and it is repeated every moment; resurrection is not a second act but the same act applied again.**

**The Anthropology: What It Means That You Were Nothing**

There is a deeper point embedded in the phrase, and the tradition has always drawn it out. If a human being was *nothing* before God made him something, then existence is not a possession but a continuous gift. The person who exists does not hold existence the way a man holds a stone; he holds it the way a room holds light — by the light's continuing to be given. Remove the giving and the room goes dark instantly. The Qur'an states this in several registers. Of the soul's dependence moment by moment:

> *"O mankind! You are the ones in need of God, and God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy."* (Qur'an 35:15)

And of God's sustaining act, which is the premise the whole argument rests upon and which the Qur'an states in terms that make creation a present-tense verb rather than a completed one:

> *"Of everyone in the heavens and the earth, whatever is with Him is not too great for Him — He knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth... His Throne extends over the heavens and the earth, and preserving them does not weary Him."* (Qur'an 2:255)

That last clause — *"preserving them does not weary Him"* — is the metaphysical premise of the whole argument, and it is worth isolating because everything else in the passage depends on it. Creation is not a finished event in the past; it is a sustained act in the present. And an act that is being sustained can be resumed — which dissolves the objection entirely, since the objector has been treating resurrection as the restarting of something that stopped, when it is in fact the continuation of something that has never stopped happening. The universe is not a machine that God built and left; it is an act He is performing, and the performer of an act is not inconvenienced by its next movement.

**Why "Remember" Rather Than "Know"**

The choice of *yadhkuru* rather than a verb of knowing or reasoning is significant, and the commentators noted it. The objector *knows* that he was created. He is not ignorant of it. Nobody needs to be told that he did not exist a hundred years ago. What is missing is not the information but the *holding of it in mind* — the connecting of a known fact to a present conclusion. That is a precise diagnosis of a very common kind of error. Most wrong reasoning is not the result of missing data; it is the result of failing to apply data one already has. The person who denies the Resurrection has every fact he needs and has simply never assembled them. The person who knows he will die and lives as though he will not has not failed to learn; he has failed to remember. This is why *dhikr* — remembrance — occupies the place it does in Islam. It is not a devotional extra; it is the mechanism by which a person keeps the known facts assembled in front of his mind. The Qur'an states the purpose of the revelation in these terms:

> *"And remind, for verily the reminder benefits the believers."* (Qur'an 51:55)

And it names the Qur'an itself by that function, which is the point at which the argument returns to the reader and asks what he intends to do with it: *"And this is a blessed Reminder (*dhikr*) which We have sent down"* (21:50). The identification matters, because a Reminder presupposes something that has been forgotten rather than something that was never known — and the Qur'an's consistent claim is that the truths it conveys are ones the human being already recognises when he hears them.

**The Practical Answer to the Denial of Resurrection**

For a reader who already believes in the Resurrection, this verse has a different and equally important use. It supplies the answer to the question that the doctrine of resurrection actually raises in daily life: *if this life is not all, how should it be lived?* The sūrah's answer is embedded in the argument. Since you were nothing and were given everything, you are not your own. Since you will be raised, you are accountable. And since the One who made you from nothing can make you again, nothing that He asks of you is beyond what He can enable. The Prophet ﷺ compressed the whole orientation into a single ḥadīth:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveller."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

And Ibn ʿUmar, who transmitted it, used to say — and the fact that the transmitter's own practice is recorded alongside the report is what gives the report its force, since it shows what the teaching produced in the man who heard it:

> *"When you reach the evening, do not expect [to see] the morning; and when you reach the morning, do not expect [to see] the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

That is *dhikr* in practice: the known facts, deliberately reassembled, every morning and every evening. The definition is worth carrying away, because it removes the mystification that often attaches to the word. *Dhikr* is not a technique for producing unusual states; it is the deliberate re-assembly of what a person already knows, performed at set intervals because knowledge that is not revisited behaves exactly as though it had been forgotten. The tradition's insight is that forgetting is not the loss of information but the loss of its availability, and that availability is maintained by use.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:68

> **And by thy Lord, We shall surely gather them and the satans, and We shall surely bring them around Hell on their knees.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Oath**

The verse opens with one of the Qur'an's most solemn oaths: *fa-wa-rabbika* — "And by thy Lord." God swears by His own Lordship — and note the construction: *rabbika*, "thy Lord," addressing the Prophet ﷺ. The choice is significant. God swears by His relationship to His messenger, which is to say by His nurture, His care, and His ownership of him. The oath thus combines two things: the certainty of the assertion, and the intimacy of the One who asserts it. The commentators noted that oaths in the Qur'an signal emphasis at points where the reader might be inclined to doubt. The Resurrection is one of those points, which is why the Qur'an's affirmations of it are so often introduced with *"By your Lord, it will surely come to you"* (34:3) or *"And by thy Lord, We shall surely gather them..."*

**"We Shall Surely Gather Them and the Satans"**

The object of the gathering is *them* — the deniers — *and the satans*. The verb *naḥshuruhum* (root *ḥ-sh-r*) is the standard Qur'anic term for the eschatological assembly: the gathering of all creatures on the Day of Resurrection. It is the root of *al-Ḥashr*, one of the names of that Day (*"And We shall gather them on a Day about which there is no doubt"*, 3:9, 25; 4:87). The inclusion of *al-shayāṭīn* — the satans — is theologically pointed. The Qur'an describes the relationship between the disbelievers and the satans as one of alliance:

> *"And [mention] when We said to the angels: 'Prostrate before Adam,' and they prostrated, except Iblīs... He said: 'Have You seen this one whom You have honoured above me? If You delay me until the Day of Resurrection, I will surely destroy his progeny, except a few.'"* (Qur'an 17:61–62)

> *"And whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Compassionate, We appoint for him a satan, who becomes a companion to him."* (Qur'an 43:36)

> *"Verily he [Satan] has no authority over those who believe and rely upon their Lord. His authority is only over those who take him as an ally and who, because of him, associate partners with God."* (Qur'an 16:99–100)

So the gathering together of a person and the satan who accompanied him through life is the public manifestation of an alliance that was chosen in private, and the publicity is the point. What was a private companionship — unremarked, largely unnoticed, and frequently denied by the person himself — is made into a public fact. The Qur'an states the outcome:

> *"And God will say: 'O company of jinn and mankind! Did there not come to you messengers from among you, relating to you My signs and warning you of the meeting of this Day of yours?' They will say: 'We bear witness against ourselves.' And the life of this world deluded them, and they will bear witness against themselves that they were disbelievers."* (Qur'an 6:130)

The commentators noted that the satans here may be either jinn or human beings, since the Qur'an uses the word *shayāṭīn* for both — *"And thus We have made for every prophet an enemy — satans from among mankind and jinn"* (6:112). The two readings are complementary: a person who spent his life following the inciters will be gathered with the inciters, whether they were jinn or men.

**"Around Hell"**

The prepositional phrase *ḥawla jahannama* — "around Hell" — places them at the perimeter, not yet inside. The commentators and the tradition generally understood this to be the scene before the final separation: the disbelievers are brought to the edge of the Fire, in the condition described, and it is only afterwards that the sentence is carried out and the final sorting takes place (vv. 71–72). The distinction matters because the sūrah will say at v. 71 that *everyone* will approach it, and at v. 72 that only the wrongdoers are left in it. The sequence is: gathering, approach, separation.

**"On Their Knees"**

The final word is *jithiyyan* (root *j-th-w*), which means kneeling, crouching, huddled on the knees — and the posture is significant because it is the opposite of everything the gathering was expected to be. The word is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for the condition of creatures on that Day:

> *"And you will see every community upon its knees (*jāthiyah*); every community will be summoned to its record. 'This Day you will be recompensed for what you used to do.'"* (Qur'an 45:28)

The commentators, including al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī, explained the posture as expressing fear, lowliness, and utter powerlessness in the face of the impending judgement. It is worth noting what is *not* said. They are not described as fighting, arguing, pleading their case, or denying the charge. They are kneeling. The sūrah has elsewhere described the arguments the disbelievers will make on that Day — *"Our Lord, these are the ones who led us astray..."* (33:67); *"Our Lord, send us back, that we may do righteousness"* (23:99–100) — but at this moment in the sequence, argument has not yet begun. There is only the posture.

**The Scene's Function in the Sūrah's Argument**

This verse and the three that follow (vv. 69–72) constitute the sūrah's depiction of the Judgement, and it is worth noticing the order in which the sūrah presents it, because the sequence is argumentative rather than merely narrative:

- **v. 68:** The disbelievers and their satans are gathered around Hell on their knees.
- **vv. 69–70:** The most insolent are plucked out from each group, and those who most deserve the Fire are identified.
- **v. 71:** Everyone approaches it — a decree determined.
- **v. 72:** The reverent are saved and the wrongdoers left inside, kneeling.

The sequence thus moves from the general gathering, to the specific identification of the worst offenders, to the universal approach, to the final separation. And the sūrah's emphasis throughout is on *precision*: God does not punish indiscriminately. He knows exactly who deserves what, and the next two verses make that explicit. The emphasis is deliberate, and it answers an objection the reader may not have formed consciously — that a Judgement on this scale must be crude, and that individuals will be lost in it. The sūrah's answer is that the scale is exactly why precision is asserted so often.

**A Note on the Justice of the Scene**

There is a question that readers sometimes raise about this verse, and it deserves an answer: how can a just God gather people with the beings who misled them, as though their being misled were not a mitigating circumstance? The tradition's answer rests on a premise the Qur'an states repeatedly — that the satan's authority over a human being is *granted by the human being*, not exercised unilaterally:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decreed: 'Verily God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you. I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me. So do not blame me, but blame yourselves.'"* (Qur'an 14:22)

The satan did not have power; he had a voice, and the person followed it. That is why the gathering is just: the allies are assembled with the one they chose to be allied with, and the choice is theirs. That is a hard doctrine, and it should be stated gently. But its practical edge is real and is one of the sūrah's purposes: the person who is being misled by a companion, a teacher, a crowd, or a voice in his own head is not a passive object. He is responding, and the response is his.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:69

> **Then indeed We shall pluck out from every group whosoever among them was most insolent toward the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sorting Begins**

Having gathered them, God proceeds to sort them: *thumma la-nanziʿanna min kulli shīʿatin ayyuhum ashaddu ʿalā al-Raḥmāni ʿitiyyan* — "Then indeed We shall pluck out from every group whosoever among them was most insolent toward the Compassionate." The verb *nanziʿanna* (root *n-z-ʿ*) means to pluck out, to extract, to pull forth — the word used for drawing a tooth, removing a garment, or extracting something embedded. The image is of a hand reaching into a mass and removing a specific item. The object of the plucking is *min kulli shīʿatin* — "from every group." The word *shīʿah* (root *sh-y-ʿ*) means a party, a faction, a following, a sect — the same word that became the proper name of the Shia, and which the Qur'an uses generally for religious and social factions:

> *"And verily among his [Abraham's] party (*shīʿatihi*) is Abraham [himself]."* (Qur'an 37:83)

> *"Verily those who have divided their religion and become sects (*shīʿan*) — you have no part with them in any way."* (Qur'an 6:159)

The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī, took *shīʿah* here to mean every religious community or nation (*ummah*) that had fallen into error or schism, and the generality of the reading is what makes the verse as uncomfortable as it is. The word does not designate a particular sect; it designates any group gathered around a leader in error — a description that fits almost every religious community at some point in its history, including the one the reader belongs to:

**Who Is "Most Insolent"?**

The criterion is *ashaddu ʿalā al-Raḥmāni ʿitiyyan* — "most intense in insolence toward the Compassionate." The commentators gave two answers, which are complementary: *The majority reading* is that the most insolent are the **leaders** of those communities in error — those who are not merely astray themselves but who lead others astray, and who therefore deserve greater punishment (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Zamakhsharī). The supporting texts are explicit:

> *"Verily those who disbelieve and turn [people] away from the way of God, and then die as disbelievers — God will never forgive them."* (Qur'an 47:34)

> *"Those who disbelieve and turn [people] away from the way of God — We shall increase them in punishment upon their punishment, for they used to spread corruption."* (Qur'an 16:88)

> *"They will bear their own burdens in full on the Day of Resurrection, and also of the burdens of those whom they misled without knowledge. Unquestionably, evil is what they bear."* (Qur'an 16:25)

*A second reading* is that the most insolent are those most stubborn in their rejection of God and in disobedience to Him (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī), or those guilty of the worst offences — idolatry and disbelief — who will enter the Fire first (al-Ṭabarī). Both readings converge on the same principle: **punishment is proportionate, and one of the factors in the proportion is the harm a person did to others**.

**The Word ʿItiyyan — The Sūrah's Second Use**

The final word of the verse, *ʿitiyyan*, is the sūrah's second use of the root *ʿ-t-w*, and the echo is one of the most striking in the chapter. At **v. 8**, Zachariah said: *"and I have grown decrepit with old age"* — *wa-qad balaghtu min al-kibari ʿitiyyan*. There the word described a body that had become dry, hard, and unyielding with age. Here, at **v. 69**, the same word describes insolence, arrogance, rebellion — a *will* that has become dry, hard, and unyielding. The sūrah is using one word for two kinds of hardening, and it is inviting the reader to see the connection. Zachariah's *ʿitiyy* was physical and innocent — a body wearing out, described with honesty and used as the premise of a prayer. The disbelievers' *ʿitiyy* is spiritual and culpable — a will that has hardened against the truth. The contrast is instructive. The old man's decrepitude drove him to God. The rebel's decrepitude drives him away. Both are forms of being unable to move; the difference is which direction the immobility was chosen.

**Why the Leaders Are Singled Out**

The singling out of the leaders for the worst punishment is one of the most socially pointed doctrines in the Qur'an, and it deserves emphasis, because it runs directly against the way influence is usually discussed. The Qur'an's consistent teaching is that misguidance has a hierarchy of responsibility, and that those who mislead bear a burden proportionate to the number of people they took with them — which means that prominence is not a neutral fact about a person but a liability he is carrying, and that the more he is followed, the larger the account:

> *"And they will say: 'Our Lord! Verily we obeyed our chiefs and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way. Our Lord, give them double the punishment and curse them with a great curse.'"* (Qur'an 33:67–68)

> *"On the Day when their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say: 'Would that we had obeyed God and obeyed the Messenger!' And they will say: 'Our Lord! We obeyed our masters and our great men, and they led us astray from the way.'"* (Qur'an 33:66–67)

That is a sobering text for anyone in a position of influence — a parent, a teacher, an imam, a writer, a public figure, an employer — and the list is worth stating at length because most people on it do not think of themselves as leaders. The Qur'an holds such people accountable not only for their own error but for the error of those who followed them. The Prophet ﷺ stated the positive counterpart, and the fact that he stated both is what makes the doctrine complete: influence is not condemned, but it is accounted:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance will have a reward like the rewards of all who follow him, without diminishing their rewards at all; and whoever calls to misguidance will bear a sin like the sins of all who follow him, without diminishing their sins at all."* (Muslim 2674)

And, more sobering still — because the report removes the last comfort available to a person who has led others into error, which is the assumption that his own account is settled at his death and that whatever follows him is no longer his business:

> *"Whoever is given authority over any affair of the Muslims and then does not strive for them and is not sincere to them will not enter the Garden with them."* (Muslim 142)

**The Name al-Raḥmān Again**

Note that the insolence is described as being *against the Compassionate* — *ʿalā al-Raḥmāni*. The sūrah's signature Name appears for the eleventh time, and its placement here is deliberate. Insolence is measured by the One it is against. Rebellion against a tyrant is one thing; insolence toward the Being whose mercy encompasses everything is quite another. The Name that should have drawn the servant near is the Name he set himself against — and the sūrah is noting the enormity of that.

**The Precision of the Judgement**

Finally, the verse establishes a principle that the next verse will make explicit: the judgement is not a mass verdict. God does not condemn "communities" in the abstract. He plucks out *from every group* the individuals who were most responsible, and He distinguishes them from the followers who were led. That is the point of the preposition *min* — "from." The worst are extracted from within the group, which means the group was not uniform. There were degrees of guilt, and the degrees are known to the One who judges.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:70

> **Then We shall surely know those who most deserve to burn therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Surely Know" — The Nature of Divine Knowledge**

The verse is short but doctrinally dense: *thumma la-naḥnu aʿlamu bi'lladhīna hum awlā bihā ṣiliyyan* — "Then We shall surely know those who most deserve to burn therein." The phrase *la-naḥnu aʿlamu* — "We surely know best" — raises an obvious question, and the commentators addressed it directly: God already knows. Why does the verse speak of knowing in the future? The answer the tradition gives rests on a distinction that runs throughout Islamic theology between two modes of divine knowledge. *God's eternal knowledge* is not acquired. He knows everything — who will enter the Fire and who will enter the Garden — before He creates them. The Qur'an states this plainly: *"Does He not know what He created? And He is the Subtle, the Aware"* (67:14). *God's knowledge as manifested judgement* is the knowledge that is displayed to creation on the Day of Resurrection. On that Day what God has always known becomes *known to everyone else*, and the judgement follows it. Theologians described this as the difference between God's knowledge and His *recompense upon* that knowledge. So the verse means: *on that Day We shall manifest, by Our judgement, who most deserves the Fire* — and everyone present will see it. The commentators noted the parallel construction elsewhere:

> *"And We shall surely test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient, and We shall test your reported conduct."* (Qur'an 47:31)

> *"And We did not make the qiblah which you used to face except that We might know who follows the Messenger from him who turns back on his heels."* (Qur'an 2:143)

In both cases, "that We might know" cannot mean the acquisition of information; it means the bringing of the matter into the open where judgement can rest upon it. The point is a standard one in the tradition's theology, but it is worth stating carefully because the wording of such verses is often quoted by people who assume it means what it would mean if a human being had said it. Knowledge that is acquired is knowledge that was absent, and absence of knowledge is a defect; God is not acquiring anything. What is happening is that the thing becomes public, and judgement requires publicity rather than information.

**"Most Deserve to Burn"**

The criterion is *awlā bihā ṣiliyyan* — "most deserving to burn therein." The word *awlā* means more entitled, more fitting, more deserving. The word *ṣiliyy* (root *ṣ-l-y*) means roasting, burning, exposure to fire. The comparative form is significant. The verse is not saying that some deserve the Fire and some do not; it is saying that *within* those who deserve it, there are degrees, and God knows the degrees exactly. That principle — gradation within punishment as within reward — is one of the fixed doctrines of Islamic eschatology:

> *"Verily the hypocrites will be in the lowest depth of the Fire, and you will not find for them any helper."* (Qur'an 4:145)

> *"The Fire — they are exposed to it morning and evening; and on the Day the Hour is established: 'Cause Pharaoh's people to enter the severest punishment.'"* (Qur'an 40:46)

The Prophet ﷺ spoke of the gradation directly, and the directness matters because the alternative — a flat description of punishment — would produce in a listener either despair or indifference, neither of which is the intended effect:

> *"Verily the one who will be punished most lightly among the people of the Fire on the Day of Resurrection will be a man under whose feet two embers are placed, from which his brain boils."* (al-Bukhārī 6562; Muslim 213)

That ḥadīth is one of the most sobering in the corpus, and the tradition has always read it alongside the verses about God's mercy — as a warning, not as a description to be contemplated with anything but fear. The pairing is not optional, and the scholars were emphatic about it: a person who reads the descriptions of punishment without the descriptions of mercy, or the reverse, has read half a text and will draw conclusions the revelation does not support. The two bodies of texts qualify one another rather than cancel one another, and the intended state of the reader is the one the Qur'an itself names — hope and fear together.

**The Justice of Graded Punishment**

There is a philosophical point embedded in this verse that is worth making explicit, because objections to the doctrine of Hell often rest on a misunderstanding of it. The objection runs: how can a finite sin deserve infinite punishment? The tradition's answer has several parts, and this verse supplies one of them: justice requires *proportion*, and the verse asserts that the proportion is exact. God does not punish everyone alike; He punishes each according to what he deserves, and the degrees are known to Him. The classical discussions, from al-Ghazālī to Ibn Taymiyyah to Ibn al-Qayyim, developed this in detail. Their arguments included: that the gravity of an offence is measured by the dignity of the One against whom it is committed, and rejection of the Creator is therefore not a finite matter; that the punishment of Hell, in the classical Sunni view, is not uniformly eternal for all its inhabitants, with the tradition recording positions on the eventual annihilation of the Fire and on the temporary punishment of sinful believers; and that divine justice is not to be measured by human intuitions about proportion, since the human being is not the one who owes. Whatever position one takes within the range the tradition permits — and the range is wider than is often assumed, the early authorities having differed on questions that later writers sometimes treat as settled — the verse's assertion is non-negotiable: **God knows exactly who deserves what, and no one is punished beyond his desert.** That is the one claim in this passage that admits of no disagreement, and it is the claim the sūrah has been constructing since v. 68. Everything else is debated; this is not.

**The Connection to the Previous Verse**

Verses 69 and 70 form a pair, and they should be read together, because each supplies what the other leaves out and reading either alone produces a distorted conclusion:

- **v. 69:** The most insolent are *plucked out* from every group. (The extraction.)
- **v. 70:** God *knows* those who most deserve to burn. (The knowledge behind the extraction.)

The order matters. The extraction is not arbitrary; it rests on knowledge. And the knowledge is not general; it is knowledge of the exact degree of desert. The sūrah is thus making a point about the character of the Judgement that is easy to miss in the terribleness of the imagery: **this is not a riot. It is a procedure.** People are gathered, sorted by precise criteria, and assigned according to exact desert. The horror of the scene lies not in its arbitrariness but in its accuracy.

**A Practical Word About Leadership**

For the reader, this verse is the sūrah's most severe warning to anyone whose example is followed. The verse follows directly on v. 69's singling out of the leaders of misguidance, and the two together establish the principle: influence is a liability as well as an asset, and the person who uses it to lead others away from God will be held to account for the leading as well as for the going. That is not a comfortable thing for anyone who writes, preaches, teaches, or parents. But the sūrah's purpose in stating it is protective rather than punitive: it is given so that a person will take the weight of his influence seriously before the Day on which it is weighed. The Prophet ﷺ gave the measure of it:

> *"All of you are shepherds, and each of you is responsible for his flock."* (al-Bukhārī 853; Muslim 1829)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:71

> **And there is not one of you, but that he will approach it. It is, with thy Lord, a decree determined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Has Perplexed Readers**

This is one of the most discussed verses in the Qur'an: *wa-in minkum illā wāriduhā kāna ʿalā rabbika ḥatman maqḍiyyā* — "And there is not one of you, but that he will approach it. It is, with thy Lord, a decree determined." The difficulty is apparent. Verse 69 spoke of the guilty being brought around Hell. Verse 72 will speak of the reverent being saved. But v. 71 says that *not one of you* will fail to approach it — apparently including the believers, the prophets, and everyone else. The word *wāriduhā* (root *w-r-d*) is the crux. The root means *to come to a place of water*, to arrive at a watering-hole — and by extension simply *to approach, to arrive at, to come to*. Crucially, *wurūd* does not mean *entering*. A person who comes to a river has *warada* the river; whether he drinks, wades, or turns back is a separate matter.

**The Classical Interpretations**

The commentators recorded several readings, and they are not all mutually exclusive. *The first: the crossing of the Bridge.* The most widely held interpretation among the later authorities is that the "approach" refers to the crossing of the *ṣirāṭ*, the bridge laid over Hell over which all people must pass on the Day of Resurrection. The believers cross it swiftly according to their deeds; others fall from it. The Prophet ﷺ described it:

> *"Then the bridge will be laid across Hell... It is slimmer than a hair and sharper than a sword, and it has hooks and thorns... The believers will cross it: some in the blink of an eye, some like lightning, some like the wind, some like birds, some running, and some walking — according to their deeds."* (al-Bukhārī 7439; Muslim 193 — part of the long ḥadīth of intercession)

On this reading, the "approach" is the passage over the Fire, and the salvation of v. 72 is the crossing. *The second: the Fire is cooled for the righteous.* Several commentators transmitted that when the righteous pass through, the flames subside or become cool for them, so that they will not even be aware of having passed through them (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī). This reading draws on the pattern of the fire of Abraham:

> *"We said: 'O Fire! Be coolness and safety for Abraham.'"* (Qur'an 21:69)

*The third: entering and emerging.* Some held that the believers will enter the Fire but emerge from it because of their good deeds (al-Ṭabarī). This reading is connected to the doctrine of the intercession for sinful believers, who are punished for a period and then removed. *The fourth: only disbelievers are addressed.* Al-Ṭabarī records that some took the "you" to be addressed specifically to the disbelievers, on the grounds that the surrounding passage is about them. *The fifth: the Fire of illness.* Some held that the approach refers to the fevers and illnesses of this world, which are described as a portion of the Fire's heat (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī). The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Fever is from the heat of Hellfire, so cool it with water."* (al-Bukhārī 3262; Muslim 2209)

*The sixth: so that the believers may know the extent of God's bounty.* Al-Ṭabrisī transmitted that the righteous are made to pass through so that they may know the magnitude of what they were spared, and so enjoy the Garden more fully. The reasoning is psychologically exact: a deliverance that has never been seen as a danger produces no gratitude, because a person cannot be thankful for what he was spared if he has no notion of what it was. The passing-through is therefore not a remnant of punishment but the completion of the mercy — the point at which the saved are shown what the rescue actually rescued them from.

**The Ḥadīth of Ḥafṣah**

The commentators associated with this verse a ḥadīth that resolves the difficulty in the most economical way possible, and it is recorded by Muslim. The difficulty is real: the verse says that every one of you will approach it, while dozens of others promise the believers safety from the Fire, and a reader who holds the two together wants to know how both can be true. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"None of those who were present at Badr and at Ḥudaybiyyah will enter the Fire."*

Ḥafṣah, the Prophet's wife and the daughter of ʿUmar, said: "But does God not say: *And there is not one of you, but that he will approach it*?" The question is a good one, and it is recorded because the answer is a method rather than a fact. The Prophet ﷺ replied by reciting the following verse:

> *"Then We shall save those who are reverent and leave the wrongdoers therein, on their knees."* (Qur'an 19:72) — (Muslim 2496)

The method is instructive and is exactly the method the sūrah itself uses throughout. The Prophet ﷺ did not explain the verse; he recited the next one. The approach and the salvation are two halves of one statement, and the second half defines what the first means. The principle at work is that the Qur'an is its own commentary, and that a difficulty raised by one verse is answered by another rather than by reasoning imported from outside it — which is why the scholars made the pairing of verses the first operation of *tafsīr* and placed speculation last.

**"A Decree Determined"**

The verse closes with *kāna ʿalā rabbika ḥatman maqḍiyyā* — "It is, with thy Lord, a decree determined." Two words intensify one another. *Ḥatm* (root *ḥ-t-m*) means a settled, binding, unbreakable determination — the word is used for a debt that has fallen due and for a sentence that cannot be appealed. *Maqḍiyy* (root *q-ḍ-y*) means decreed, finished, settled. The phrase *ʿalā rabbika* — "with thy Lord" / "incumbent upon thy Lord" — is the striking part. Some commentators noted the apparent oddity of describing something as incumbent *upon God*, and the tradition's answer is that God has written it upon Himself as a matter of His own decree:

> *"Your Lord has decreed upon Himself mercy: that whoever among you does evil out of ignorance and then repents and amends — then He is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 6:54)

Theological precision requires noting that this is not an obligation imposed on God from outside, and the tradition has insisted on the point because the wording, read carelessly, suggests a constraint God is operating under. God is not subject to a law above Him. What the phrase means is that God's decree is not subject to revision — He has willed it, and His Will is not frustrated. The distinction matters: an obligation would imply a higher authority, while the description here is of God's own consistency. He has bound Himself, and the binding is His act rather than something done to Him.

**What the Verse Means for the Believer**

Taken with v. 72, the verse teaches something specific and comforting. Every human being will pass through the eschatological passage. No one is exempt by rank. The Prophet ﷺ, the prophets, the martyrs, the righteous — all will approach. And the believers will be delivered, not because they were exempted from the passage but because God saves them *in* it. That is a pattern that runs through the Qur'an's account of salvation, and it is worth naming: God does not usually remove His servants from trials; He brings them through trials. Abraham was not spared the fire; he was kept safe in it. Moses was not spared the sea; it was parted for him. Mary was not spared the labour; she was provided for in it. The believers are not spared the approach; they are saved in the approach. The practical conclusion is one of the most sustaining things in the entire tradition. A believer facing a trial should not expect exemption and should not interpret the trial as abandonment. The promise is not that the fire will not be there. It is that the fire will not consume him.

**The Sūrah's Pattern of Reckoning**

Finally, note how this verse fits the sūrah's eschatological sequence as a whole, because the chapter has been constructing the sequence across many verses and it is easy to experience it as a series of separate scenes rather than as an ordered account:

- **v. 68:** Gathering — the disbelievers and satans around Hell, kneeling.
- **v. 69:** Extraction — the most insolent plucked out from every group.
- **v. 70:** Knowledge — God knows who most deserves to burn.
- **v. 71:** Approach — not one of you but will come to it; a determined decree.
- **v. 72:** Separation — the reverent saved; the wrongdoers left kneeling.

The sequence is complete and orderly, and its shape is worth carrying: gathering, sorting, knowing, approaching, separating. The sūrah's God is not a force but a judge, and judgement has a procedure — a point the chapter makes by arranging the events rather than by asserting it. What the reader is given is an order of operations, and the orderliness is the argument against the charge that the Hereafter is arbitrary. Nothing in the sequence is improvised, and every stage follows from the one before it.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:72

> **Then We shall save those who are reverent and leave the wrongdoers therein, on their knees.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Separation**

The sūrah's eschatological sequence reaches its conclusion in six Arabic words: *thumma nunajjī alladhīna ittaqaw wa-nadharu al-ẓālimīna fīhā jithiyyan* — "Then We shall save those who are reverent and leave the wrongdoers therein, on their knees." The verse is the counterpart to v. 71 and completes its meaning. Everyone approaches; then the separation occurs. The reverent are delivered; the wrongdoers are left.

**The Verb: Najjā — Salvation and Intimate Discourse Share a Root**

The verb *nunajjī* (root *n-j-w*) means We save, We deliver, We rescue. And it is the same root as *najiyy* — the word used at v. 52, where God *"drew him [Moses] nigh in intimate discourse"* (*najiyyā*). That is not a coincidence, and the connection is worth drawing because it is one of the sūrah's most delicate pieces of wordplay. The root *n-j-w* carries the sense of *escaping, being delivered, and being raised above what would engulf one*. *Najwā* — from the same root — means a private, confidential conversation, because it is speech that is *set apart* from others; and *najiyy* is the one with whom such conversation is held, the intimate who is singled out. So the sūrah has used the same root twice, once for the highest intimacy (Moses brought near for private converse) and once for the final deliverance (the reverent saved from the Fire). The two are related in the sūrah's theology: **nearness to God is what saves.** The person who has been brought near in this life is the person who is brought out on that Day.

**"Those Who Are Reverent" — The Recurring Condition**

The saved are described with the sūrah's recurring term: *alladhīna ittaqaw* — "those who are reverent," from *taqwā*, the guarding awareness of God discussed at v. 13 and v. 18. It is worth registering how consistently the sūrah uses this one condition as the criterion, since the consistency is what allows the reader to stop guessing. The chapter does not distribute its promises across a range of qualifications — wealth, lineage, knowledge, station, or intensity of practice — but attaches them to a single quality, and then defines that quality by what it does rather than by what it feels like:

- **v. 13:** John was *taqiyy* — reverent.
- **v. 18:** Mary appealed to the intruder's *taqwā* — "if you are reverent."
- **v. 63:** The Garden is bequeathed to those who were *taqiyy*.
- **v. 72:** The saved are those who have *taqwā*.
- **v. 85:** The reverent are gathered to the Compassionate as an honoured delegation.
- **v. 96:** God will grant love to those who believe and do righteous deeds.

The sūrah is making a single point across ninety verses: the thing that distinguishes the saved from the lost is not knowledge, not lineage, not achievement, and not even the quantity of one's works, but the quality of one's awareness of God. The word also appeared, significantly, in the *opposite* direction at v. 48 — where Abraham hoped that in calling upon his Lord he would not be *shaqiyy* — wretched — which is the sūrah's antonym of the blessed state.

**"And Leave the Wrongdoers Therein"**

The second half of the verse is stark: *wa-nadharu al-ẓālimīna fīhā* — "and We leave the wrongdoers therein." The verb *nadharu* (root *w-dh-r*) means We leave, We abandon, We forsake. It is a hard word, and its hardness is the point. God does not push them in; He leaves them there. The movement into the Fire is the consequence of what they chose, and the "leaving" is the ratification of the choice. The word *ẓālimīn* — "wrongdoers" — is the Qur'an's standard term for those who are lost, from the root *ẓ-l-m* discussed at v. 38. The root's core meaning — *to put something in the wrong place* — is exactly right here: these are people who have placed worship, trust, fear, and love in the wrong objects, and the Fire is the natural terminus of that misplacement.

**"On Their Knees" — The Closing Image**

The verse ends where v. 68 ended: *jithiyyan* — "on their knees." The word repeats, and the repetition is deliberate. At v. 68, the disbelievers and their satans were *brought around* Hell on their knees. At v. 72, the wrongdoers are *left in* it on their knees. The posture has not changed, and that is the tragedy: the same helplessness that characterised their arrival characterises their permanent state. There is a subtle but devastating literary effect here. The sūrah has enclosed the entire judgement scene between two identical words describing the same posture. What happened between them — the extraction of the leaders, the knowledge of desert, the universal approach, the saving of the reverent — did not change the position of those who remain. They arrived kneeling, and they remain kneeling.

**Theological Balance: Salvation by Mercy, Not by Merit**

The verse's structure invites one further observation. The reverent are *saved* — a verb of deliverance, not of reward. Nothing in the verse says they earned their escape. The deliverance is an act performed upon them, by the One who performs it. That is consistent with everything the sūrah has said. The Garden is an inheritance (v. 63), not a wage. The promise is made by the Compassionate (v. 61), not by a debtor. And the Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in the ḥadīth already cited:

> *"None of you will enter the Garden by his deeds... unless God envelops me in His Mercy."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)
