Note also what the verse does *not* say. It does not say "those who never sinned." It says "those who were reverent" — a condition of orientation, not of perfection. The tradition has always held this: the reverent include people who sinned, repented, and were forgiven; people whose bad deeds were outweighed; and people who were punished for a time and then removed by intercession. *Taqwā* is not sinlessness. It is the direction in which a person is facing.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:73

> **And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs, those who disbelieve say unto those who believe, "Which of the two groups is better in station or fairer in company?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nature of the Objection: Social, Not Intellectual**

The sūrah now records the actual argument the Quraysh made, and it is not the argument a modern reader would expect: *wa-idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātunā bayyinātin qāla alladhīna kafarū lilladhīna āmanū ayyu al-farīqayni khayrun maqāman wa-aḥsanu nadiyyan* — "And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs, those who disbelieve say unto those who believe, 'Which of the two groups is better in station or fairer in company?'" Note what has happened. The signs have been recited *bayyinātin* — "as clear proofs," self-evident, manifest. There is no intellectual difficulty left. The objection that follows is therefore not about the evidence. It is about *appearances*. The disbelievers do not say: "your argument is flawed." They say: **look at us.** Which group is better off? Which has the finer homes and the better company? That is a rhetorical question with an obvious answer, and it is the wrong answer. The Quraysh had wealth, position, and the best of Makkan society. The early Muslims were largely poor, young, and — in the case of the slaves and clients among them — socially marginal. On the visible evidence, the disbelievers won the comparison easily.

**The Word Nadiyy — The Gentlemen's Club**

The second term of the boast is *nadiyyan*, from *nādin* — a gathering, an assembly, and specifically the place where the leading men of a tribe sat to talk, conduct business, and make decisions. The word carries a specific social charge in seventh-century Makkah. The *nādin* was the club room of the elite — the place where a man's standing was displayed by who would sit with him. To ask "which group is fairer in *nadiyy*?" is to ask: whose company is more distinguished? Who are the people worth knowing? That is an argument from prestige, and it is worth naming plainly because it is the most durable of all the arguments against religion. It does not run "your claims are false" but "your people are unimpressive."

**The Two Criteria, and Why Both Are Wrong**

The verse names two criteria: *maqām* — station, position, dwelling — and *nadiyy* — company, assembly. The first is about what you have; the second is about who knows you. Together they cover the whole of social standing. Both are irrelevant to truth, and the sūrah's answer in v. 75 will demonstrate precisely that, by turning the same two criteria back on the objectors in the Hereafter. The error has a name in the Islamic tradition and elsewhere: judging a claim by the standing of its adherents rather than by its evidence. It is an extraordinarily common failure, because it is cognitively cheap. Evaluating an argument requires effort; assessing the social position of the people making it requires none. The Qur'an records the same argument in other forms:

> *"And they say: 'Why was this Qur'an not sent down upon some great man from the two cities?'"* (Qur'an 43:31) — the objection that the messenger is not grand enough.

> *"And he entered his garden while he was wronging himself; he said: 'I do not think that this will ever perish, and I do not think the Hour will come. And if indeed I am returned to my Lord, I will surely find better than this as a resort.'"* (Qur'an 18:35–36) — the objection from present prosperity to future favour.

> *"And they said: 'We are more abundant in wealth and children, and we are not going to be punished.'"* (Qur'an 34:35)

That last verse is the explicit form of the argument, and it is worth stating in full because the sūrah will spend three verses dismantling it: *we are rich, therefore God is pleased with us, therefore we are safe.* The inference is worth examining closely, because it is not stupid. It rests on a premise almost everyone holds — that God rewards those He favours — and its error lies entirely in the second step, which treats the presence of a blessing as evidence about the recipient's standing rather than as a test of it.

**The Qur'an's Answer to the Argument From Prosperity**

The sūrah will answer this objection in the next two verses, but the answer is worth stating now because it runs throughout the Qur'an and is one of its most important teachings — important precisely because it is counter-intuitive, and because getting it wrong corrupts a person's reading of his own life. *Prosperity is not a sign of divine approval.* The Qur'an is emphatic:

> *"Do they think that in whatever wealth and children We provide them, We are hastening to them good things? Nay, they do not perceive."* (Qur'an 23:55–56)

> *"So as for man, when his Lord tries him and honours him and grants him ease, he says: 'My Lord has honoured me.' But when He tries him and stints his provision, he says: 'My Lord has humiliated me.' Nay!..."* (Qur'an 89:15–17)

That sūrah's *Nay!* is the Qur'an's correction of the most natural human inference, and the abruptness of the word is the point — the inference is not gently qualified but flatly refused. Prosperity is a test, and so is hardship. Neither is a verdict. *And hardship is not a sign of divine disapproval*, which is the harder half for most people and the one the tradition has had to repeat more often. The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in a ḥadīth that has sustained believers in every generation:

> *"If God loves a people, He tries them."* (al-Tirmidhī 2396; graded ḥasan)

> *"The greatest of rewards comes with the greatest of trials. When God loves a people, He tests them; whoever is content, for him is contentment, and whoever is angry, for him is wrath."* (al-Tirmidhī 2396)

**The Contemporary Forms of the Argument**

This verse is, of all the verses in the sūrah, the one that has aged least. The argument from *maqām* and *nadiyy* is the dominant argument of the modern world. It appears as: religion is for the uneducated. Believers are on the wrong side of history. The people who take this seriously are not the people anyone admires. The sophisticated, the successful, and the culturally central have moved on — so the claim must be false. The sūrah's answer to all of these is the same, and it is v. 75: *wait.* The comparison being made is between two groups at one moment of a story that is not over. And the standard by which the comparison will finally be made is not the one being used.

**The Recitation as the Occasion**

Finally, note the setting: *when Our signs are recited unto them.* This is an argument made *in response to the recitation of the Qur'an*, not in a vacuum. That detail matters. The disbelievers were not presented with a philosophical claim to evaluate calmly. They were presented with the Qur'an, which they found beautiful, unsettling, and impossible to ignore — and their response was to change the subject to their own social standing. That is a recognisable defence mechanism, and the tradition has named it: when an argument cannot be answered, the arguer attacks the standing of the person making it. The sūrah is exposing the mechanism by recording the exchange verbatim, with no commentary.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:74

> **And how many a generation We destroyed before them, who were better in possessions and outward appearance!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Historical Refutation**

The sūrah answers the boast of v. 73 with history: *wa-kam ahlaknā qablahum min qarnin hum aḥsanu athāthan wa-riʾyan* — "And how many a generation We destroyed before them, who were better in possessions and outward appearance!" The construction *wa-kam* — "and how many!" — is the Qur'an's standard idiom for introducing the historical argument. It appears dozens of times, always with the same function: to remind the reader that the present configuration of the world is not the only one that has ever obtained. The word *qarn* means a generation, a people, an age. The verb *ahlaknā* — "We destroyed" — is the past tense of a completed act. These are not hypotheticals; they happened.

**The Two Criteria, Reversed**

The verse's precision is worth noting. The disbelievers boasted of two things: *maqām* (station) and *nadiyy* (company). The sūrah's answer names two: *athāth* and *riʾy*. *ʿAthāth* denotes furniture, household goods, possessions, chattels — the accumulated stuff of a comfortable life. *Riʾy* (root *r-ʾ-y*, to see) denotes outward appearance, the look of a thing, what meets the eye. (Some of the canonical reciters read *riyyan*, from a related root, with the sense of a pleasing or impressive sight.) So the sūrah's response is exact: you boast of your possessions and your appearance, and We destroyed generations *better in possessions and in appearance than you*. The comparison is not evaded; it is conceded and then rendered irrelevant by the fact that those who won it perished anyway. That is a complete answer, and it is worth restating in its logical form: The argument runs in three steps, and it is a straightforward refutation by counter-example. First, you claim that prosperity indicates divine favour and therefore safety. Second, We destroyed generations more prosperous than you. Third, therefore prosperity does not indicate favour or safety. What makes the refutation decisive is that it grants the objector's own premise. The disbelievers were, in fact, better off, and the sūrah never disputes it; what it disputes is the inference drawn from the fact, and a single historical instance in which prosperity and destruction coincide is sufficient to break that inference permanently.

**The Same Argument Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The sūrah's argument here is one of the Qur'an's most repeated, and the repetition is itself evidence of how hard human beings find it to absorb. A teaching that could be learned once would not need to be restated across dozens of passages, in different vocabularies and different historical settings. The Qur'an returns to it because the error it corrects regenerates itself in every generation and in every individual life:

> *"Have they not travelled through the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were greater than them in power, and they tilled the earth and inhabited it more than they have inhabited it..."* (Qur'an 30:9)

> *"Did We not destroy the ancients? Then We shall follow them with the later ones."* (Qur'an 77:16–17)

> *"And how many a city We destroyed that exulted in its life [of ease]! There are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little; and it is We Who are the Inheritor."* (Qur'an 28:58)

> *"Have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them, whom We had established in the earth as We have not established you...? But they denied the messengers, so there came upon them what they used to deny."* (Qur'an 6:6)

The last of those verses contains the crucial qualifier, and it is the point of the whole argument: the destroyed generations were not destroyed for being prosperous. They were destroyed *because they denied the messengers*. Prosperity did not save them, and it did not condemn them. What condemned them was the rejection of guidance — and prosperity merely made the rejection harder to recognise as dangerous.

**The Mechanism: Why Prosperity Is Spiritually Dangerous**

There is a psychological observation embedded in this verse that the tradition has developed at length, and it deserves stating clearly. Prosperity is not merely irrelevant to one's standing with God; it is actively hazardous, because it removes the pressures that drive a person to God in the first place. Hardship is unpleasant but clarifying: it makes the fragility of the world obvious and forces the question of what is reliable. Prosperity is pleasant but anaesthetising: it makes the world seem self-sufficient and defers the question indefinitely. The Prophet ﷺ stated this with his customary directness:

> *"By God, it is not poverty that I fear for you; rather, I fear that the world will be spread out before you as it was spread out before those before you, and you will compete for it as they competed for it, and it will destroy you as it destroyed them."* (al-Bukhārī 4015; Muslim 2961)

That ḥadīth is one of the most important in the entire corpus for anyone living in a wealthy society, and it is a direct commentary on this verse. The Prophet ﷺ did not say that wealth is forbidden; he said that his fear for his community was not deprivation but abundance — and he gave the destruction of previous generations as the precedent. And the Qur'an states the mechanism:

> *"Nay, verily man transgresses, in that he deems himself self-sufficient."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

That is the precise diagnosis, and its precision is what makes it useful. The root of rebellion, in the Qur'an's account, is not malice but *istighnāʾ* — the sense of being self-sufficient, of not needing anything outside oneself. Prosperity produces that sense, and that sense produces transgression. The sequence matters: wealth does not produce rebellion directly, and the Qur'an nowhere teaches that money is evil. What it produces is a *feeling* of independence, and the feeling is what does the damage, because a person who does not experience himself as needing anything does not pray, and a person who does not pray has removed the one thing that would have corrected the feeling.

**"Outward Appearance" — The Superficiality of the Standard**

The second criterion in the verse, *riʾy* — outward appearance — deserves a final note, because it is the sūrah's sharpest insight into the objectors' psychology. The word names exactly what is wrong with their standard: they are judging by what can be *seen*. And what can be seen is the least reliable guide to what is real. A person's wealth, home, clothing, and company are visible; his standing with God is not. The entire argument of the sūrah's third movement is that the visible is a poor basis for inference about the invisible. That is the same point the sūrah made at v. 10 and v. 17, with the word *sawiyyan* — Zachariah looking sound but unable to speak, the angel looking perfectly human and being an angel. In both cases the appearance was real and the inference from it was wrong. The sūrah has thus been preparing this argument from its opening verses. The whole chapter is a sustained argument that appearance is not a reliable guide to reality, and this verse is where that argument is applied to the most consequential case.

**The Practical Use**

For the reader, this verse supplies the correction to one of the most seductive errors in human life: the inference from *how things are going* to *how one stands with God*. The correction is not that prosperity is bad — the Qur'an nowhere teaches that, and Islam has never been a religion of poverty. The correction is that prosperity is *information-neutral*. It tells you nothing about your standing, and it is dangerous precisely because people insist on reading it as though it did. The practical discipline the tradition recommends is to treat both states identically: gratitude in prosperity, patience in hardship, and the same religious obligations in both. The Prophet ﷺ taught his daughter's household the discipline in a ḥadīth that has come down through ʿAlī:

> *"Fāṭimah asked the Prophet ﷺ for a servant, and he said: 'Shall I not tell you of what is better for you than that? When you go to bed, say: "Glory be to God" thirty-three times, "Praise be to God" thirty-three times, and "God is Greatest" thirty-four times.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3113; Muslim 2727)

The point of that instruction is that the remedy for exhaustion and want is remembrance, not personnel — and the discipline is the same whether one is rich or poor. The observation applies in both directions, which is why the tradition has always read it as a warning to the prosperous rather than as comfort for the poor alone. A person who is wealthy and anxious reaches for rearrangement; a person who is poor and anxious does the same. The prescription is identical in both cases, and the sūrah's insistence on it is the chapter's standing refusal to let circumstance become the explanation for a person's interior state.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:75

> **Say, "Whosoever is in error, let the Compassionate prolong his term—until, when they see what they are promised, whether it be the punishment or the Hour, they will know who is worse in position and weaker in forces."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Boast**

The sūrah gives the Prophet ﷺ his reply: *qul man kāna fī'l-ḍalālati fa-l-yamdud lahu al-Raḥmānu maddā ḥattā idhā raʾaw mā yūʿadūna immā al-ʿadhāba wa-immā al-sāʿata fa-sayaʿlamūna man huwa sharrun makānan wa-aḍʿafu jundā* — "Say, 'Whosoever is in error, let the Compassionate prolong his term—until, when they see what they are promised, whether it be the punishment or the Hour, they will know who is worse in position and weaker in forces.'" The reply does not deny that the disbelievers are better off. It relocates the comparison in time.

**"Let the Compassionate Prolong His Term"**

The opening clause is one of the most unusual in the Qur'an: *fa-l-yamdud lahu al-Raḥmānu maddā* — "let the Compassionate prolong for him a prolonging." The verb *yamddu* (root *m-d-d*) means to extend, to stretch out, to prolong. The object *maddā* is a cognate accusative, an emphatic construction: "let Him extend for him an extension." The surface meaning is a curse in the form of a prayer: *let the deluded man be given more rope.* But the commentators were careful about what it implies, because the Agent named is *al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate. Two readings were recorded, and they are complementary: *The first is that this is a statement of fact dressed as a wish.* God *does* extend the term of those in error — He gives them long life, wealth, and continued freedom — and the extension is itself part of the test. The Qur'an calls this *istidrāj*, the gradual leading-on of those who persist:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that Our respite is better for them. We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin; and for them is a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"And those who deny Our signs — We shall lead them on gradually from whence they know not. And I grant them respite; verily My plan is firm."* (Qur'an 7:182–83)

> *"So leave Me with those who deny this discourse. We shall lead them on gradually from whence they know not."* (Qur'an 68:44–45)

*The second is that it is an expression of the gravity of the matter.* Al-Rāzī noted that the phrase is a form of threat: the prolonging is not a gift but a deferral of a reckoning that is certain to arrive. Both readings converge. The extension is real, it is granted by the Compassionate, and it is a test rather than a blessing.

**Why the Compassionate Extends the Term of the Deluded**

This is a question the tradition has taken seriously, because naming *al-Raḥmān* as the Agent is deliberate and the sūrah has used the Name eleven times already. The answer the commentators give is that the respite is itself a form of mercy. A person who is given time is given the possibility of repentance. No one is cut off while he still breathes, and the delay that the deluded experience as impunity is, in reality, the space in which turning back remains possible. That is why the Prophet ﷺ described God's patience in terms that are almost unbearable:

> *"Verily God grants respite to the wrongdoer until, when He seizes him, He does not let him escape."* Then he recited: *"And thus is the seizure of your Lord when He seizes the towns while they are doing wrong. Verily His seizure is painful, severe."* (al-Bukhārī 4686; Muslim 2583 — Qur'an 11:102)

The respite is mercy; the seizure is justice; and the two are not in tension because the respite had a purpose. The purpose is the whole point, and the sūrah states it rather than leaving the reader to infer it: the delay is not indifference and not approval but an interval in which the turn can still be made. A respite without a purpose would be either negligence or favour, and the verse excludes both — the first because God is not negligent, and the second because the seizure that follows proves the favour was never there.

**"Until, When They See What They Are Promised"**

The second half of the verse supplies the deadline: *ḥattā idhā raʾaw mā yūʿadūna* — "until, when they see what they are promised." The verb *raʾaw* — "they see" — is the pivot. The objectors were judging by what can be seen (v. 74: *riʾy*, outward appearance). The sūrah's answer is that there is a *future seeing*, and when it occurs the comparison will be settled. The promise is described as *either* the punishment *or* the Hour. The commentators explained the disjunction: for those who reject the Resurrection, the punishment may come in this world — as it came to previous generations (v. 74) — or the Hour may come upon them, and with it the full reckoning. Either way, the Seeing happens. Al-Zamakhsharī noted that the disjunction covers both possibilities exhaustively: whatever the deluded person is promised, he will see it, whether in this life or the next.

**The Reversal: "Worse in Position and Weaker in Forces"**

The verse's closing clause is a precise and devastating reversal of the boast in v. 73, and the precision is worth tracing term by term, because the sūrah is answering the objector in his own vocabulary rather than changing the subject:

| **v. 73 (their boast)** | **v. 75 (the reversal)** |
|---|---|
| "better in station" (*khayrun maqāman*) | "worse in position" (*sharrun makānan*) |
| "fairer in company" (*aḥsanu nadiyyan*) | "weaker in forces" (*aḍʿafu jundan*) |

The correspondence is exact and is meant to be seen. The first pair uses *maqām* and *makān* — two words from the same semantic field of station, place, and standing. They asked which group has the better *maqām*; the sūrah answers that on the Day they see, they will know who has the worse *makān*. The second pair answers *nadiyy* — company, the elite gathering — with *jund* — forces, troops, hosts. They asked whose company is more distinguished; the sūrah answers that the question that will matter is whose support is real. And the disbelievers' company will be no support at all. That is the sūrah's deepest answer to the argument from prestige: the thing that will matter on that Day is not who sat with you but what stands with you — and for those who set up partners to God, nothing will.

**The Theology of Respite**

The doctrine embedded in this verse — *imhāl*, the granting of respite — is one of the most pastorally useful in Islam, and it cuts both ways. *For the observer of injustice,* it explains why the wicked prosper. They are not being rewarded; they are being given rope. And the rope has an end, which is either their death, their punishment in this life, or the Hour. *For the sufferer of injustice,* it is the ground of patience. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"God grants respite to the wrongdoer..."* (al-Bukhārī 4686; Muslim 2583)

*And for the wrongdoer himself,* it is the most urgent warning in the Book, because the respite is not indefinite and its purpose is repentance rather than accumulation. The distinction between those two is what the verse turns on, and it is the one thing a person in a state of ease is least likely to grasp. He experiences the delay as confirmation and uses the interval to accumulate more, when the interval was given for the single purpose of turning back in it. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"This world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever."* (Muslim 2956)

That ḥadīth is the exact counterpart of this verse, and the parallel is worth holding onto because it completes the doctrine from both ends. The believer's hardship and the disbeliever's ease are both temporary and both misleading about the final state. What the two together establish is that this life's distribution of comfort carries no information at all about the next one — and that a person who treats his circumstances as a verdict has drawn a conclusion from evidence the Qur'an explicitly rules inadmissible.

**The Sūrah's Answer to the Argument From Success**

Read vv. 73, 74, and 75 together and the sūrah's full answer to the argument from social standing emerges in three steps — and it is worth seeing them as a single argument rather than as three separate replies, because each step does something the others cannot: The three steps are these. First, at v. 74, history refutes the claim: better-off generations were destroyed, so prosperity cannot be a verdict. Second, in the first half of v. 75, the apparent prosperity is identified as a granted respite rather than a judgement, which explains why it exists without conceding that it means anything. Third, in the closing clause, the comparison is re-run on the Day they see, and the result inverts. The three replies address three different questions — is the inference valid, why does the prosperity exist, and what will the final accounting show — and together they leave the position with nothing to stand on. That is a complete answer, and it is worth noting that it does not dispute the facts. The disbelievers *were* better off. The sūrah never claims otherwise. It relocates the comparison to a time at which the relevant facts are all visible, and it asserts that at that time the judgement will be reversed. The method is worth learning, because it is the opposite of the move a disputant usually makes. The instinct is to deny the opponent's premise — to argue that he is not really better off, or that his wealth is illusory. The sūrah grants the premise entirely and relocates the frame, which is both more honest and, for a reader who is genuinely in doubt, far more persuasive.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:76

> **And God increases in guidance those who are guided. And the enduring good deeds are better with thy Lord in reward, and better in return.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Statement: What Actually Increases**

Having answered the disbelievers' boast, the sūrah turns to the believers with a promise: *wa-yazīdu Allāhu alladhīna ihtadaw hudā wa'l-bāqiyātu al-ṣāliḥātu khayrun ʿinda rabbika thawāban wa-khayrun maraddā* — "And God increases in guidance those who are guided. And the enduring good deeds are better with thy Lord in reward, and better in return." The structure is a deliberate contrast with v. 75. There, the person in error is *prolonged* — given more time, more rope, more of the same. Here, the person who is guided is *increased* — given more guidance, more of what he has. The two are the same mechanism operating in opposite directions. What you choose, you are given more of.

**Guidance Increases — The Islamic Doctrine of Hidāyah**

The first clause states one of the most important and most consoling doctrines in Islam, and it is consoling precisely because it removes the despair that the alternative produces. **Guidance is not a fixed quantity; it grows.** A person is not assigned a measure of guidance at birth and left to manage with it; he is given more in proportion to what he does with what he has. The Qur'an states this repeatedly:

> *"And those who are guided — He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverent awareness."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"As for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverent awareness."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"And those who strive in Our cause, We shall surely guide them to Our ways; and verily God is with the doers of good."* (Qur'an 29:69)

> *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away."* (Qur'an 61:5)

The last of those verses states the obverse, and the two together constitute the doctrine: guidance and misguidance are both *dynamic*. A person does not stand still. Every act of obedience opens further light; every act of rejection closes it a little more. The practical consequence is that no one is ever at a fixed distance — which is the reason despair is irrational for the worst of people and complacency is irrational for the best of them. The Prophet ﷺ expressed the mechanics in a ḥadīth:

> *"Verily, when a servant commits a sin, a black spot appears upon his heart. If he desists, seeks forgiveness, and repents, his heart is polished; but if he returns [to sin], it increases until it covers his heart — and that is the 'rust' that God has mentioned: 'Nay! Rather, what they used to earn has rusted over their hearts.'"* (al-Tirmidhī 3334; Ibn Mājah 4244 — Qur'an 83:14; graded ḥasan)

The image is cumulative and physical: the heart acquires a coating, and the coating thickens with repetition and thins with repentance. What makes the image so effective is that it removes the matter from the category of verdict and places it in the category of accretion. A person does not become hardened by a single act or by a decision; he becomes hardened the way a surface becomes coated — gradually, by repeated exposure, and without noticing it happening. The same is true in the other direction, which is why the tradition prescribes small continuous acts rather than occasional large ones.

**The Practical Consequence: Start Small, Keep Going**

There is an enormously practical conclusion in this verse, and it is one of the most useful things in the entire sūrah for a person trying to change. Since guidance increases by being acted upon, the size of the beginning is not the critical variable. A person who takes one small step toward God receives an increase in guidance that makes the second step easier. A person who waits until he feels ready will wait forever, because the readiness is produced by the first step rather than preceding it. That is why the Prophet ﷺ's counsel was consistently about sustainability rather than intensity:

> *"Take on only what you are able to do, for God does not tire until you tire."* (al-Bukhārī 43; Muslim 785)

> *"The most beloved of deeds to God is that which is done consistently, even if it is small."* (al-Bukhārī 6465; Muslim 782)

And it is why the tradition treats small, regular acts of obedience — a fixed portion of Qur'an each day, the prescribed prayers at their times, a regular charity — as more valuable than sporadic heroism. The mechanism described in this verse rewards continuity, and the reason is not that God prefers moderation but that the heart is changed by accumulation rather than by intensity. A single enormous act produces a memory; a small repeated act produces a disposition. The tradition is not being cautious when it commends the latter; it is describing the mechanism the ḥadīth itself describes.

**"The Enduring Good Deeds"**

The second clause introduces a phrase that has become one of the most cherished in the Qur'an: *al-bāqiyātu al-ṣāliḥātu* — "the enduring good deeds." The word *bāqiyāt* (root *b-q-y*) means remaining, enduring, abiding. The word *ṣāliḥāt* means sound, righteous, good. So the phrase means: the good deeds that endure. What are they? The commentators transmitted several answers, and the tradition has held them together rather than choosing: *The five daily prayers.* This is the reading transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, and it fits the sūrah's context, since the preceding verses have been about the neglect of the prayer (v. 59). *The formula of remembrance.* This is the most widely known reading in the Sunnah. Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī reported:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ said: 'Hold fast to the enduring good deeds.' They said: 'O Messenger of God, what are the enduring good deeds?' He said: 'The tahlīl [saying lā ilāha illā Allāh], the takbīr [Allāhu akbar], the tasbīḥ [subḥān Allāh], and the ḥamd [al-ḥamdu lillāh] — for they come on the Day of Resurrection with speakers before them, faces, and tongues, calling [the people], while the people are standing for the reckoning.'"* (reported by al-Ṭabarī; cf. Aḥmad and al-Nasāʾī; graded ḥasan)

And in another transmission, which the scholars paired with the first because it supplies the mechanism from the other side — the same event described in terms of what accompanies a person rather than of what he leaves behind:

> *"Take to glorifying God, declaring His oneness, and praising Him, and do not be heedless — for they are the enduring good deeds."* (Aḥmad; al-Nasāʾī, *ʿAmal al-yawm wa'l-layla*; graded ḥasan)

*All righteous deeds generally.* Many commentators took the phrase comprehensively: the enduring good deeds are any deeds of obedience whose benefit outlasts the act itself — the knowledge taught, the charity that continues, the child raised, the prayer performed. The sūrah had prepared this comprehensive reading at v. 60, where the conditions of entering the Garden were named as *repentance, faith, and righteous deeds*. The "enduring" qualifier here adds the dimension of permanence: of all a person does, the part that survives him.

**"Better in Reward, and Better in Return"**

The verse closes with a double comparison: *khayrun ʿinda rabbika thawāban wa-khayrun maraddā* — "better with thy Lord in reward, and better in return." *Thawāb* means reward, recompense. *Maradd* (root *r-d-d*) means a return, a coming-back, an ultimate outcome — what the deed comes back to, what it yields in the end. So the verse makes two claims: the enduring good deeds are better in what they *earn*, and better in what they *are* — better as an investment and better as a possession. That is the sūrah's final answer to the argument from possessions. The disbelievers boasted of *athāth* — furniture, chattels, the accumulated stuff of a comfortable life. The sūrah answers with a category of possession that does not decay: deeds that survive their performer. And the Qur'an states the same comparison elsewhere in the sharpest terms:

> *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world. But the enduring good deeds (*al-bāqiyātu al-ṣāliḥātu*) are better with your Lord in reward, and better to hope for."* (Qur'an 18:46)

That verse, from the sūrah revealed shortly before this one, uses the identical phrase, and the repetition across two adjacent revelations is itself a signal that the reader is meant to connect them. The two together are the Qur'an's most compact statement of the difference between two kinds of wealth: the kind that ends with the owner, and the kind that accompanies him. The distinction is not between lawful and unlawful wealth but between wealth that has been spent and wealth that has been kept, and the Prophet ﷺ supplied the accounting:

> *"When a person dies, his deeds come to an end except for three: ongoing charity, beneficial knowledge, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631)

**What Lasts**

For the reader, the practical content of this verse is a single question, and it is worth asking seriously: *of everything I am acquiring, what will still be here when I am not?* The sūrah's answer is that what survives is what was done for God. That is not a pious platitude; it is a description of the mechanism. A deed done for God is, by its nature, addressed to the One who does not die, and it therefore attaches to something permanent. A deed done for any other audience attaches to that audience, which will perish. That is the sūrah's answer to the boast about *maqām* and *nadiyy*. The disbelievers' wealth and company will be gone. The believers' *bāqiyāt ṣāliḥāt* will be there when they arrive.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:77

> **Hast thou seen the one who disbelieved in Our signs and said, "Surely I shall be given wealth and children"?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the General to the Particular**

The sūrah now moves from the general argument against the disbelievers to a specific case: *a-fa-raʾayta alladhī kafara bi-āyātinā wa-qāla la-ūtayanna mālan wa-waladan* — "Hast thou seen the one who disbelieved in Our signs and said, 'Surely I shall be given wealth and children'?" The construction *a-fa-raʾayta* — "Hast thou seen...?" — is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic rhetorical devices. It is not a request for information; it is an invitation to *consider*, to look at a case and draw the obvious conclusion. The same construction introduces other striking parables: *"Hast thou seen him who takes his desire as his god?"* (45:23); *"Hast thou seen him who denies the religion?"* (107:1).

**The Occasion of Revelation**

The commentators were in near-unanimous agreement about who this was, and the occasion is recorded in the authentic ḥadīth collections — which is worth noting, because a verse that names no one is being given a specific referent by the transmitted sources rather than by conjecture. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record, from Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt — one of the earliest converts, a blacksmith by trade, and among those tortured for their faith in Makkah:

> *"I was a blacksmith in the Jāhiliyyah, and I had a debt owed to me by al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil. I went to him to ask for it, and he said: 'I will not give it to you until you deny Muhammad.' I said: 'By God, I will not deny him — neither while you live, nor while you die, nor when you are resurrected.' He said: 'Then when I am resurrected I will surely have wealth and children, and I will pay you then.'"* (al-Bukhārī 2091; Muslim 2795)

So this is al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī, one of the Quraysh's leading men and the father of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who would later become one of Islam's greatest generals. The story is worth pausing on, because it is one of the most revealing exchanges in the entire sīrah. Khabbāb was poor, a freedman, and a manual labourer. Al-ʿĀṣ was aristocratic, wealthy, and powerful. And al-ʿĀṣ used the debt as leverage: *deny Muhammad and I will pay you.* The offer was, in worldly terms, entirely reasonable — Khabbāb was owed money he needed, and the price was a sentence. Khabbāb's answer is one of the great statements in Islamic history: *By God, I will not deny him — not while you live, not while you die, and not when you are resurrected.* He refused on three temporal scales at once, which is to say he refused permanently. And al-ʿĀṣ's reply is the sentence quoted in this verse: *when I am resurrected I will have wealth and children, and I will pay you then.* It is a taunt. He does not believe in the Resurrection; he is using Khabbāb's belief in it as a way of refusing to pay, secure in the knowledge that the day of payment will never come. The sūrah records the taunt and answers it, and the recording is itself part of the answer. A taunt that is quoted has been taken seriously rather than dismissed, and the sūrah's method throughout is to state the opponent's case as strongly as it can be stated before responding to it. What is being modelled for the reader is a way of handling mockery: not to pretend it did not land, and not to answer it in kind, but to repeat it and then address the claim it actually makes.

**The Logic of the Claim**

The man's assertion is *la-ūtayanna mālan wa-waladan* — "I shall surely be given wealth and children." The commentators identified the assumption behind it, and it is the same assumption exposed in vv. 73–75: **that God's favour in this life is a guarantee of His favour in the next.** The man was wealthy and had sons. He reasoned: God has given me these here, so He will give me the same there, whether or not I believe. That is a claim about entitlement rather than about grace, and it is the error the sūrah is answering. The man does not say "perhaps God will be generous to me." He says *la-ūtayanna* — "I shall surely be given" — with the emphatic *la-*, as though it were a right. The Qur'an records the identical reasoning in the parable of the man with two gardens:

> *"And he entered his garden while he was wronging himself; he said: 'I do not think that this will ever perish, and I do not think the Hour will come. And if indeed I am returned to my Lord, I will surely find better than this as a resort.'"* (Qur'an 18:35–36)

That man's reasoning is exactly al-ʿĀṣ's: present bounty is evidence of future bounty. And the Qur'an's answer in that passage is equally direct, and it is worth setting the two side by side because the sūrah is showing the reader that this is a single recurring error rather than a set of separate mistakes. The man in the parable and the man in Makkah made the same inference centuries apart, which is the sūrah's evidence that the error is native to the human mind rather than acquired from a particular culture:

> *"His companion said to him, while he was conversing with him: 'Have you disbelieved in Him Who created you from dust, then from a drop, then proportioned you as a man? But as for me, He is God, my Lord, and I do not associate anyone with my Lord.'"* (Qur'an 18:37–38)

**A Reading of the Word Waladan**

The word rendered "children" is *waladan*, and some of the canonical reciters read it *wuldan*. The two forms are related but not identical: *walad* is the more general term for offspring, while the *wuld* form can carry the sense of a plurality or of male children specifically. The commentators noted the variant without making doctrinal use of it, and the difference does not affect the sense: the man was claiming that he would be given a family and wealth in the Hereafter regardless of his disbelief.

**The Two Errors, and Why They Are One**

The verse names two failures: *he disbelieved in Our signs*, and *he said "I shall be given."* They are not two separate faults. The second follows from the first, and the connection is worth stating because it is the sūrah's diagnosis of a very common condition. A person who does not reckon with God's signs — who does not treat the revelation as information about reality — has nothing left by which to form expectations except his own present circumstances. He has wealth; he assumes he will have wealth. He is healthy; he assumes he will remain healthy. He has never been held to account; he assumes he never will be. Every one of those assumptions is an inference from the present to the future, and every one of them is invalidated by the possibility that the future is not an extension of the present. That is precisely what the revelation is for: it tells a person about the parts of reality he cannot observe. To reject the revelation and then to make confident predictions about the unseen is not just wrong; it is self-contradictory. The sūrah states the contradiction explicitly in the next three verses, and it does so with a precision that is difficult to escape.

**The Contemporary Form of the Claim**

Like v. 73, this verse has aged very little. The claim *la-ūtayanna mālan wa-waladan* — "I shall surely be given" — is the standard posture of the modern secular imagination. It appears as: I have been successful, therefore I deserve to be successful. I have not been held to account, therefore there is no accounting. My life has been comfortable, therefore the universe is benign, or indifferent in a benign way, or at any rate not the sort of place where people are punished. The sūrah's answer is that this reasoning is not reasoning. It is the projection of a present condition into a future that has not been observed and about which a report has been given — and the report has been rejected. There is also a more personally applicable form. Most believers who read this verse are not al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil. But the underlying error — assuming that God's past generosity entitles one to future generosity, and that the absence of consequences means their absence is permanent — is common enough among religious people to be worth naming. The sūrah's correction applies: **grace received is not a right established.** The proper response to having been given much is gratitude and fear, not confidence. The Prophet ﷺ gave voice to exactly that combination in his own practice, and the practice is worth attending to because it answers the objection at the level of behaviour rather than of argument. A person who believes his blessings are a test behaves with them in a recognisable way, and the reports of what he did with what he had constitute a complete answer to anyone who claims that assurance about the next life is incompatible with effort in this one:

> *"The Prophet ﷺ stood [in prayer] until his feet became swollen. It was said to him: 'Does God not forgive you your past and future sins?' He said: 'Should I not be a grateful servant?'"* (al-Bukhārī 4836; Muslim 2819)

He had been given more than anyone — more than any human being has been given, by the consensus of the tradition — and his response was not entitlement but effort. The observation is the sūrah's closing argument against the claim of v. 77, and it is more effective than any reasoning would have been. If assurance produced ease, it would have produced ease in him; if there were a man entitled to rest, it was he. What the reports show instead is a person who stood in prayer until his feet swelled, and who, when asked about it, answered that he was a grateful servant.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:78

> **Has he gained knowledge of the Unseen, or taken a covenant from the Compassionate?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Questions, One Closed Door**

The sūrah responds to the claim of v. 77 with a pair of questions that leave no exit, and the form is worth noticing before the content: the sūrah does not contradict the man, it asks him how he knows. *a-ṭṭalaʿa al-ghayba ami ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "Has he gained knowledge of the Unseen, or taken a covenant from the Compassionate?" The shift from assertion to question is what makes the argument unanswerable, because a claim about the future cannot be refuted by evidence but can be dismantled by asking for its source. The logic is airtight and worth stating formally, because it is the sūrah's most rigorous piece of argument:

1. The man claims certain knowledge about a future state (that he will be given wealth and children in the Hereafter).
2. Knowledge of a future state can only come from one of two sources: either **observation of the Unseen** (*iṭṭilāʿ ʿalā al-ghayb*), or **a binding promise from the One who controls it** (*ʿahd ʿinda al-Raḥmān*).
3. He has neither.
4. Therefore his claim has no ground.

There is no third option, and the sūrah does not supply one, because there isn't one. A person can know the future only by seeing it or by being told it by someone who can bring it about. The man is neither a seer nor a recipient of a promise, and the sūrah's confidence on the point rests on the exhaustiveness of the disjunction rather than on any claim about what is possible. Knowledge of what has not yet happened has exactly two sources, and the sūrah asks which of them he is using.

**The First Question: Knowledge of the Unseen**

The verb *iṭṭalaʿa* (root *ṭ-l-ʿ*) means to ascend to a vantage point and look down — to gain a view of something from above, to become cognisant of it. The word carries the sense of *access*: has he been given a place from which the Unseen can be surveyed? The Qur'an's answer to that question, stated in several places, is that the Unseen belongs to God alone:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows what is on the land and in the sea; and no leaf falls but that He knows it."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"Knower of the Unseen and the witnessed, the Great, the Exalted."* (Qur'an 13:9)

> *"Say: None in the heavens and the earth knows the Unseen except God."* (Qur'an 27:65)

> *"Verily God, with Him is knowledge of the Hour; and He sends down the rain, and He knows what is in the wombs. And no soul knows what it will earn tomorrow, and no soul knows in what land it will die. Verily God is Knowing, Aware."* (Qur'an 31:34)

The last of those verses is the one the Prophet ﷺ recited when asked about the Hour, in the ḥadīth of Gabriel — and his choice of it, out of all the verses he might have quoted, is the tradition's clearest statement of how the question of the Unseen is to be handled:

> *"...He said: 'Then tell me about the Hour.' He [the Prophet ﷺ] said: 'The one questioned about it knows no more than the questioner.'"* (Muslim 8; al-Bukhārī 50)

That exchange is the definitive Islamic statement on the limits of creaturely knowledge, and it is definitive because of who is speaking. The greatest of the messengers, asked directly about the timing of the Hour, answered that he knew no more than the man asking him. The implication is total: if the Prophet ﷺ did not know, then no one in the community after him does, and any claim to knowledge of the Unseen — whether about the Hour, about a person's destiny, or about what the future holds — is a claim to something the revelation explicitly places beyond created beings.

**The Exception: What God Discloses**

There is one qualification, and it is important because the Qur'an states it itself rather than leaving it to be inferred, and because the absence of the qualification would make the doctrine indistinguishable from a bare denial. The Unseen is withheld from creation as a rule, and the rule has a stated exception:

> *"[He is] Knower of the Unseen, and He discloses not His Unseen to anyone, except to a messenger whom He has chosen — and then He sends a watcher before him and behind him."* (Qur'an 72:26–27)

So knowledge of the Unseen *is* possible — but only by disclosure, only to messengers, and only in the measure God wills. It is never *gained*, never *discovered*, and never *inferred*. That exception is what makes the sūrah's first question so devastating. The man in v. 77 is not a messenger. The revelation has not been given to him — he has rejected it (v. 77: *"the one who disbelieved in Our signs"*). So he is claiming knowledge of the Unseen while simultaneously rejecting the only channel through which such knowledge comes. That is the self-contradiction the sūrah is exposing, and it is worth naming: *he denies the revelation and then prophesies.*

**The Second Question: A Covenant**

The second possibility the sūrah considers is *ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "has he taken a covenant with the Compassionate?" The word *ʿahd* means a covenant, a pact, a binding promise, and the sūrah's use of it here is exacting: a covenant is the only means by which a person who cannot see the future could nonetheless be certain of it. The Qur'an uses the word for God's covenants with the prophets, with the Children of Israel, and with Adam:

> *"And [mention] when We took a covenant from the prophets, and from thee, and from Noah and Abraham and Moses and Jesus son of Mary; and We took from them a solemn covenant."* (Qur'an 33:7)

> *"And We had already taken a covenant from Adam before, but he forgot, and We found in him no resolve."* (Qur'an 20:115)

The sūrah's point is that if the man had been promised anything, it would have to be by such a covenant — and there is no covenant. God does not promise the Hereafter's rewards to those who reject Him. The idea is incoherent, since the reward is precisely for the relationship the man has refused. The commentators noted that the word *ʿinda* — "with," "in the presence of" — implies something deposited, held in trust. Has he a guarantee deposited with God? The question is rhetorical and the answer is no.

**The Name al-Raḥmān, Again**

It is worth noting that the sūrah names *al-Raḥmān* in both v. 75 and v. 78 — the One who prolongs the term of the deluded, and the One with whom the covenant would have to be made. The effect is to place the entire argument inside the sūrah's theology of mercy. The man is not dealing with a remote sovereign who might have made an arbitrary promise; he is dealing with the Compassionate, whose promises are recorded in the revelation he has rejected. The covenant he would need is available — it is the Qur'an itself, which promises everything to those who believe and warns those who do not. He has declined it and then claimed its benefits anyway.

**The Argument's Form: A Dilemma**

Logically, what the sūrah has constructed is a *dilemma* — a pair of exhaustive alternatives, each of which fails. It is worth naming the form, because the Qur'an uses it repeatedly and it is one of its most effective argumentative instruments. A dilemma does not require the respondent to be mistaken about a fact; it requires him to choose between two sources for his claim and then shows that neither is available to him. The argumentative advantage is that it cannot be answered by producing better evidence, since the question is not what he knows but how he came to know it:

> *"Say: 'Have you considered: if it [the Qur'an] is from God and you disbelieve in it, who is further astray than one who is in extreme opposition?'"* (Qur'an 41:52)

> *"Or were they created from nothing, or are they the creators? Or did they create the heavens and the earth? Nay, they are not certain."* (Qur'an 52:35–36)

> *"Say: 'To whom belongs the earth and whoever is in it, if you know?' They will say: 'To God.' Say: 'Will you not then be mindful?'"* (Qur'an 23:84–85)

In every case the structure is the same: state the exhaustive alternatives, let each fail, and leave the interlocutor with his claim unsupported. The pattern is worth carrying, because it is the form the Qur'an most often uses when the objection is not factual but epistemic — when the problem is not what a person believes but the ground on which he believes it. A reader who recognises the form will find it throughout the Book, and will find that it settles a class of objections that no amount of evidence would have touched.

**The Practical Application**

For the reader, this verse supplies a question worth asking of oneself, and it is one of the most useful intellectual disciplines in the tradition. *On what basis am I making this claim about the future?* Applied to religious matters, it is devastating. "God will forgive me" — on what basis? Knowledge of the Unseen? A covenant? "I will be fine" — how do you know? "Nothing bad will happen" — has that been promised? Applied properly, the question does not produce despair; it produces *humility about prediction*, which is the beginning of wisdom. The person who knows he cannot see the future prays rather than presumes, and the prayer is answered, while the presumption is not. And there is a second application, to the claims other people make. A great deal of what passes for religious teaching consists of confident assertions about the unseen — who is saved, who is not, what God will do, when the Hour will come. The sūrah's question is the correct test for all of it: *has this person been shown the Unseen, or has he a covenant?* If not, he is guessing, and the guess should not be treated as knowledge.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:79

> **Nay! We shall record what he says, and prolong for him the punishment extensively.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Nay!" — The Particle of Cessation**

The verse opens with one of the Qur'an's most emphatic words: *kallā* — "Nay!" / "No indeed!" / "Never!" The grammarians described *kallā* as a particle of *zajr wa-radʿ* — deterrence and rebuke. Its function is to cut off what precedes it and to deny it in the strongest terms. It appears thirty-three times in the Qur'an, almost always in passages addressing disbelief, arrogance, or a false claim about the Hereafter: 74:16; 75:11; 78:4; 80:11; 82:9; 83:7; 89:17; 90:4; 92:9; 96:6, 15, 19; 102:3–6; 104:4. Here it cuts off the man's claim at v. 77 and the two questions at v. 78. There is no discussion. The claim is refused.

**"We Shall Record What He Says"**

The first consequence is that the words are written down: *sa-naktubu mā yaqūlu* — "We shall record what he says." The Qur'an's doctrine of the recording of deeds is among the most insistent in the Book, and it is stated with an almost bureaucratic concreteness — scribes, registers, a record that nothing escapes, and a day on which it is produced. The concreteness is deliberate, and its purpose is to exclude the assumption that a careless word simply dissipates into the air. What a person says is retained, and the retention is described in terms that make it as public as a document and as durable as a book:

> *"Or do they think that We hear not their secrets and their private conversations? Nay, and Our messengers are with them, recording."* (Qur'an 43:80)

> *"He utters not a word, but that there is with him a watcher ready [to record]."* (Qur'an 50:18)

> *"And verily over you are keepers, noble and recording, who know whatever you do."* (Qur'an 82:10–12)

> *"And the Book will be placed, and you will see the guilty fearful of what is in it, and they will say: 'O woe to us! What a Book this is! It leaves out nothing, small or great, but that it has enumerated it.'"* (Qur'an 18:49)

The last of those verses contains the phrase that makes the doctrine vivid: *lā yughādiru ṣaghīratan wa-lā kabīratan illā aḥṣāhā* — "it leaves out nothing, small or great, but that it has enumerated it." Why is this stated so often? The tradition's answer is that the doctrine is the practical answer to heedlessness. A person who genuinely believes that every word he says is being written down will govern his speech differently, and the Qur'an is supplying the belief in order to produce the governance. The Prophet ﷺ stated the corollary in a ḥadīth that every Muslim is taught:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

**The Punishment Matches the Claim**

The second consequence is *wa-namuddu lahu min al-ʿadhābi maddā* — "and We shall prolong for him the punishment extensively." The verb *namuddu* is from the root *m-d-d* — the same root used at v. 75, where the sūrah said of the person in error: *fa-l-yamdud lahu al-Raḥmānu maddā* — "let the Compassionate prolong for him a prolonging." That is a deliberate and devastating correspondence, and it is one of the sūrah's finest literary effects:

- **v. 75:** God *prolongs* (*yamddu*) the deluded person's term of life and ease.
- **v. 79:** God *prolongs* (*namuddu*) that person's punishment.

The same verb, the same cognate accusative, the same construction. The measure has been matched exactly: the rope that was extended in this life is extended in the next, and what was given as time is returned as duration. This is an instance of a principle the tradition identified throughout the Qur'an and named *al-jazāʾ min jins al-ʿamal* — **recompense in kind**, the punishment or reward being of the same species as the deed. The principle appears explicitly in the Qur'an:

> *"And the recompense of an evil is an evil like it."* (Qur'an 42:40)

> *"Is the recompense of good anything but good?"* (Qur'an 55:60)

And the Prophet ﷺ stated it in a ḥadīth qudsī, which is the form the tradition reserves for the matters God speaks in the first person about, and which is therefore the closest the corpus comes to a direct statement of the principle from the One who is doing the recording:

> *"My mercy precedes My wrath."* (al-Bukhārī 7454; Muslim 2751)

That ḥadīth supplies the order of operations, and the order is the whole of the tradition's theology of divine justice. The recompense in kind is real, but it is mercy that has priority — which is why the punishment in v. 79 is preceded by the respite in v. 75 and the opportunity to turn back. A reader who takes the threats of the Qur'an without the ordering has misread them; the threat is real, and it is also downstream of a mercy that has already been extended and declined.

**What "Prolonging the Punishment" Means**

The commentators, including al-Zamakhsharī and al-Ṭabarī, noted that *maddā* here indicates an extension and increase — the punishment is not merely applied but *lengthened*, made continuous, drawn out rather than concluded. The verse does not specify the nature of the punishment, and the sūrah does not linger on it. The tradition has consistently discouraged speculation about the mechanics of the Fire, not out of squeamishness but because the Qur'an's purpose in mentioning it is to warn rather than to describe. What the verse asserts is three things: that the claim is false, that the claim will be recorded, and that the consequence will be severe and protracted.

**The Relationship Between the Two Halves**

It is worth noting how precisely the verse answers v. 77. *The man said something.* He said: "I shall surely be given wealth and children." The consequence: *We shall record what he says.* His words are not air; they are retained and will be produced. *The man claimed abundance.* He claimed he would be given more. The consequence: *We shall prolong for him the punishment extensively.* What he claimed as abundance is answered with abundance of a different kind. The symmetry is exact, and it is the sūrah's way of saying that the claim was not merely mistaken but *inverted*. He predicted a gift and received a sentence; he asserted entitlement and produced a record.

**A Word About Recording**

There is a pastoral dimension to the doctrine of recording that is often missed, because the doctrine is usually presented only as a threat. The same recording that retains the man's boast retains everything else — every word spoken for God, every private act, every hardship borne without complaint, and every intention that never reached the stage of being acted upon. The Qur'an states the positive side, and the sūrah's placement of it here is what keeps the verse from being merely menacing:

> *"And whosoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whosoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

And the Prophet ﷺ stated it in a ḥadīth that is among the most consoling in the corpus, and which the scholars cited as the companion-text to every verse about the recording of deeds — precisely because it supplies the asymmetry that the threat-verses omit:

> *"Whoever intends to do a good deed and does not do it, God records it with Him as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God records it with Him as ten good deeds, up to seven hundred times, up to many times over. And whoever intends to do an evil deed and does not do it, God records it with Him as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God records it as one evil deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131)

That is the same bookkeeping, and the asymmetry is deliberate: the recording mechanism is weighted overwhelmingly toward mercy. The man in this verse is recorded because he insisted on it — because his claim was public, his boast was public, and his rejection was public, and what is being written down is what he himself produced. The tradition's teaching is that a person is not recorded into perdition by a slip; he is recorded by what he persistently and publicly made himself to be.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:80

> **And We shall inherit from him that which he speaks of, and he shall come unto Us alone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Inheritance Verse**

The verse continues the reckoning: *wa-narithuhu mā yaqūlu wa-yaʾtīnā fardan* — "And We shall inherit from him that which he speaks of, and he shall come unto Us alone." The root *w-r-th* appears here for the fifth time in the sūrah, and the sūrah's argument with it is now complete. What began at v. 6 as Zachariah's anxiety about who would inherit from him has been taken up at v. 40 as the declaration that God inherits the earth, turned at v. 63 into the promise that the reverent inherit the Garden, and arrives here as the statement that a person's boast is inherited by God and the boaster arrives with nothing. The word has been carried the length of the chapter and used at every point at which the question of what a person actually keeps is raised:

- **v. 6:** Zachariah prays for an heir (*yarithu*).
- **v. 40:** God inherits the earth and everyone on it (*narithu*).
- **v. 50:** God bestows mercy on Abraham's family (*wahabnā*).
- **v. 63:** God bequeaths the Garden to the reverent (*nūrithu*).
- **v. 80:** God inherits from the boaster what he spoke of (*narithuhu*).

The sūrah has thus taken a single root and run it through the whole range of human anxiety about possession: the worry about who gets what after I die, the fact that everything returns to God anyway, the promise that the righteous will be given something permanent, and the warning that the arrogant will be stripped of everything they counted on.

**"That Which He Speaks Of"**

The phrase *mā yaqūlu* — "what he says" — has been read two ways, and the commentators recorded both. *The first reading* is that it means **what he speaks of**, i.e., the wealth and children he claimed he would be given. On this reading, God says: We shall inherit from him the very things he was boasting about — meaning that they will be taken from him, since God is the Inheritor of all things (v. 40). *The second reading* is that it means **what he says** — his words, his claims, his assertions. On this reading, the inheritance refers to the speech itself, which is retained against him as evidence (as in v. 79's "We shall record what he says"). Both readings are coherent, and the tradition has held them together: God takes from him everything he had, and God retains everything he said. The first strips him; the second convicts him. The commentators inclined to the first, understanding the verse to mean that God will take his wealth and children from him (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī), and that what he hoped for in the Hereafter will not be his.

**"And He Shall Come Unto Us Alone"**

The verse's closing clause is one of the most sobering in the Qur'an: *wa-yaʾtīnā fardan* — "and he shall come unto Us alone." The word *fard* (root *f-r-d*) means single, solitary, alone; the same root as *farīd*, "unique," and *fard*, "an individual." The intensive connotation is of someone stripped of everything that accompanied him in life — no wealth, no children, no tribe, no retainers, no reputation, no gods. The Qur'an states this with a frequency that suggests how badly human beings need to be told:

> *"And you have certainly come to Us alone, as We created you the first time, and you have left behind you all that We bestowed upon you. And We do not see with you your intercessors, those whom you claimed were partners among you. It has been severed between you, and what you used to claim has forsaken you."* (Qur'an 6:94)

> *"And every one of them will come to Him on the Day of Resurrection alone."* (Qur'an 19:95)

> *"And they will come forth before God all together, and the weak will say to those who were arrogant: 'Verily we were your followers...'"* (Qur'an 14:21)

The first of those verses is the fullest commentary on this one, and it names precisely what is absent: wealth, retinue, and the intercessors one claimed. The enumeration is worth attending to, because the three items are the three things that made the man's position feel secure to him while he held them — and the verse's method is to list them and then to state that he has arrived without any of them:

**The Psychology of the Word "Alone"**

There is a specific and powerful psychology in this verse, and it is worth drawing out because it addresses the deepest root of the behaviour described in vv. 77–79. The man's entire error was *social*. He had wealth and children — the two things that, in his world, made a man someone. He had standing, company, and a lineage that would continue after him. His confidence in the Hereafter was an extension of his confidence in his tribe: he assumed that what made him great here would make him great there. The verse's answer is that the transition to the Hereafter *dissolves the social*. What is carried across is not the network but the person. The man arrives as a single individual before his Lord — and everything that constituted his identity in this world has been declared irrelevant. That is both the most frightening and the most levelling statement in the sūrah. A person who has built his entire self on what others think of him has built on something that will not survive the journey. A person who has built on his standing with God has built on the only thing that arrives with him. The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in a ḥadīth that has been repeated in every generation, and whose endurance is explained by the fact that it addresses the exact delusion this verse describes — the assumption that what a person has accumulated is what he will arrive with:

> *"Verily God does not look at your bodies or your forms, nor at your wealth; rather, He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564)

**Theology: What Survives the Journey**

What, then, arrives with a person? The Qur'an and Sunnah are consistent on the answer, and the list is short: *One's deeds.* *"And We have fastened every man's omen to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a book which he will find spread open"* (17:13). *One's faith and its quality.* *"The Day when neither wealth nor children will avail, save him who comes to God with a sound heart"* (26:88–89). *One's lingering effects.* *"Ongoing charity, beneficial knowledge, or a righteous child who prays for him"* (Muslim 1631). And, by the sūrah's own final promise, something more: *"Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds — the Compassionate will grant them love"* (v. 96).

**The Irony of the Inheritance**

There is a final irony in the verse, and it is the sūrah's sharpest. The man said: *I shall surely be given wealth and children* — using the emphatic future of entitlement. And God answers: *We shall inherit from him what he speaks of* — using the same verb of transfer in the opposite direction. The gifts he claimed as a right become the things God takes back. He wanted to be a possessor, and the sūrah's answer is that possession was never his to begin with. God is the Inheritor (v. 40); everything the man had was on loan; and the loan's term expired with his breath.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:81

> **And they have taken gods apart from God, that they might be a strength for them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Returns to the Root Error**

The sūrah now turns from the individual case to the general error behind it: *wa-ittakhadhū min dūni Allāhi ālihatan li-yakūnū lahum ʿizzan* — "And they have taken gods apart from God, that they might be a strength for them." The verb *ittakhadhū* (root *a-kh-dh*, "to take") in its Form VIII means "they took for themselves," "they adopted." The word *ālihatan* is the plural of *ilāh* — a god, an object of worship. And the phrase *min dūni Allāh* — "apart from God," "besides God," "other than God" — is the Qur'an's standard formula for *shirk*. The construction *min dūni* is itself informative: it means "short of," "beneath," "in preference to." The idolater has not found another god; he has stopped short of the real one and taken something beneath Him in His place.

**The Stated Purpose: Strength**

The verse's most interesting element is the stated motive: *li-yakūnū lahum ʿizzan* — "that they might be a strength for them." The word *ʿizz* (root *ʿ-z-z*) means might, power, honour, glory, and invulnerability. It is the root of *al-ʿAzīz*, one of the Beautiful Names of God. To want *ʿizzah* is to want to be unassailable — to be beyond the reach of what can harm you. So the sūrah is diagnosing the motive behind idolatry with unusual precision. People do not adopt gods because they have concluded that the gods are true. They adopt them for *utility* — for what the gods can do for them. That diagnosis recurs throughout the Qur'an:

> *"And they worship, apart from God, that which neither harms them nor benefits them, and they say: 'These are our intercessors with God.'"* (Qur'an 10:18)

> *"Unquestionably, sincere religion is for God alone. And those who take allies apart from Him [say]: 'We only worship them that they may bring us nearer to God in nearness.'"* (Qur'an 39:3)

> *"And they have taken gods apart from Him, that are not created... and they can neither harm nor help themselves, nor can they control death, nor life, nor resurrection."* (Qur'an 25:3)

Those three verses together give the full account of what an idol is for, and the account is more charitable to the idolater than most modern readers expect: it is an intercessor, a means of nearness, and a source of protection. In every case the logic is transactional. The significant point is what the transaction is *not*: the Qur'an nowhere suggests that the idolaters believed a carved stone had created the heavens. What they believed is that it could obtain something for them from the One who had, and the Qur'an's objection is directed at that belief rather than at a cruder one the evidence does not support.

**The Two Motives Behind Shirk**

The tradition's analysis — developed by the commentators and by the theologians of *tawḥīd* — identifies two distinct motives behind the taking of partners with God, and both are named in the Qur'an. *The first is the desire for intercession.* The idolater believes that God is real but distant, and that access to Him requires an intermediary. The Qur'an quotes this reasoning directly, and answers it:

> *"Say: 'To God belongs intercession entirely. To Him belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth...'"* (Qur'an 39:44)

> *"Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

The answer is not that intercession does not exist; it is that intercession belongs to God and is granted on His terms. The sūrah will state this at v. 87. *The second is the desire for honour and protection* — the motive named in this verse. The idolater wants a patron, a backing, a power behind him. The Qur'an answers that honour belongs entirely to God:

> *"Those who take the disbelievers as allies apart from the believers — do they seek honour (*al-ʿizzah*) with them? Then verily all honour belongs to God."* (Qur'an 4:139)

That verse uses the same word, *ʿizzah*, and gives the same answer. There is no honour to be obtained from any source other than its Owner, and the attempt to obtain it elsewhere is not merely futile but degrading. The degradation is the point worth dwelling on, because it is the part the Qur'an presses hardest. The idolater is not presented as having made a mistake about a fact; he is presented as having diminished himself. A person who seeks honour from what he made rather than from the One who made him has inverted the relationship, and the sūrah's vocabulary for the transaction is consistently one of loss rather than of error:

**The Modern Forms of the Same Error**

Because the sūrah has diagnosed the *motive* rather than the *object*, its analysis transfers directly to cases that involve no statues. Anything a person relies on for ultimate security, in place of God, is functioning as *ʿizzah*. The Qur'an's own language permits the extension: what is trusted as one trusts God has been taken as a god in the functional sense. The candidates are the ones any honest person can name: wealth, career, social standing, a political cause, a family, a reputation, a nation, an ideology, a talent, a relationship. None of these is intrinsically wrong — the Qur'an lists wealth and children as an adornment of the worldly life (18:46). All of them become gods when they are relied upon for what only God can supply. The test the tradition derived is direct and practical: *what would have to happen for me to lose my footing?* The answer to that question names the thing. If the answer is "the loss of my money," "the loss of my reputation," or "the loss of this person," then that is where the reliance actually sits, whatever the profession of faith. The Prophet ﷺ gave a diagnostic in a ḥadīth of frightening accuracy:

> *"Wretched is the servant of the dinar, the dirham, and the velvet cloak; if he is given, he is pleased, and if he is not given, he is displeased. Wretched and doomed is he, and if he is pierced by a thorn, it is not removed."* (al-Bukhārī 2887)

The diagnostic in that ḥadīth is not the possession of money but the *dependence of one's state on it*: "if he is given, he is pleased, and if he is not given, he is displeased." That is the signature of worship, and the precision of the diagnostic is what makes the ḥadīth usable. A person cannot examine his heart directly and get a reliable answer, but he can observe what moves him — what depresses him when it is withdrawn and what elates him when it arrives — and that observation is available to anyone. The tradition's insight is that worship is detectable by its effects rather than by its claims.

**Why the Motive Matters More Than the Object**

There is a final point in this verse that deserves emphasis, because it distinguishes the Qur'an's account of idolatry from a merely legal one. The sūrah does not primarily criticise the *objects*. It criticises the *transaction*. The idolater is not wrong about the existence of his god in the way a person is wrong about the existence of a unicorn; he is wrong about where power lies, and he is wrong for a reason that is intelligible and human: he wants to be safe. That is why the Qur'an's cure for *shirk* is not only argument but reassurance. The positive counterpart of "do not take gods besides Me" is "I am sufficient for you." The sūrah has been saying exactly that since v. 2: the Name that recurs sixteen times is *al-Raḥmān*. The practical conclusion is worth stating: **people take false gods because they are afraid, and the cure for fear is not a better argument but a truer ground of confidence.** A person who genuinely believes that God suffices will not need the substitutes, and a person who does not will accumulate them no matter how well he has been instructed.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:82

> **Nay! They will deny their worship, and they will become an enemy to them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reversal**

The sūrah answers the idolaters' expectation with the same emphatic *kallā* that opened v. 79, and the repetition of the particle across consecutive verses is the sūrah's way of marking that a single claim is being refused from several directions. *kallā sa-yakfurūna bi-ʿibādatihim wa-yakūnūna ʿalayhim ḍiddan* — "Nay! They will deny their worship, and they will become an enemy to them." The reversal is total, and worth noting in its three stages: The reversal runs in three stages. First, they took them as gods (v. 81), and they did so for strength — for the advantages enumerated in the preceding verse. Second, they will deny their worship (v. 82a), which is to say that the objects of worship will reject the worship rather than merely fail to reward it. Third, they will become an enemy to them (v. 82b), the objects of worship turning against the worshippers. The progression matters: what is being described is not indifference but active opposition, and the sūrah is stating that the relationship itself inverts rather than simply dissolving. The thing sought as protection becomes the accuser. That is the sūrah's answer to the entire transaction described in v. 81, and it is a more precise answer than a denial of the transaction's premises would have been.
