The sūrah does not say that seeking protection was misguided; it says that the protection sought will be present on that Day and will be found on the other side. The transaction is not cancelled — it is completed, and what the worshipper receives from it is the opposite of what he contracted for.

**"They Will Deny Their Worship"**

The verb *yakfurūna* (root *k-f-r*) means they will disbelieve, disown, deny. The root's primary sense, as noted at v. 2 in the context of *kufr* as "concealing," is to cover over — and in this context it means to *disown*. The object is *bi-ʿibādatihim* — "their worship," i.e., the worship performed by the idolaters. So the gods will disown the worship that was offered to them. How? The commentators gave the mechanism, and the Qur'an supplies it in several places: the objects of worship — whether angels, prophets, jinn, or idols — will declare before God that they never asked for this and never accepted it:

> *"And [mention] the Day when He will gather them all together; then He will say to the angels: 'Was it you that these people used to worship?' They will say: 'Glory be to You! You are our Protector, not them. Nay, they used to worship the jinn; most of them were believers in them.'"* (Qur'an 34:40–41)

> *"And [mention] the Day when He will gather them and what they worship apart from God, and He will say: 'Was it you who led astray these servants of Mine, or did they go astray from the way themselves?' They will say: 'Glory be to You! It was not for us to take any protectors apart from You...'"* (Qur'an 25:17)

> *"And who is further astray than he who calls upon, apart from God, such as will not respond to him until the Day of Resurrection, and who are heedless of their call? And when mankind is gathered, they will be enemies to them, and will deny their worship."* (Qur'an 46:5–6)

That last verse is nearly identical to ours and uses the same word, *ḍiddan* ("enemies"). The two passages are the Qur'an's most complete statement of the reversal, and their near-identity across two different sūrahs is itself part of the emphasis — the Book states this outcome twice in almost the same terms, which is not the habit of a text that expects to be doubted on the point:

**"And They Will Become an Enemy to Them"**

The final word is *ḍiddan* (root *ḍ-d-d*), meaning an adversary, an opponent, a contrary. The word is singular in form but is used collectively here: they will be *an opposition* to them. The commentators explained what the enmity consists in. It is not that the idols will attack their worshippers; it is that they will *testify against them* and disassociate from them, and that this disassociation is precisely the opposite of what was sought. A person who took a being as a protector in order to be helped will find that being standing on the other side. The Qur'an states the same outcome for the human objects of misguided veneration:

> *"Then on the Day of Resurrection He will disgrace them, and say: 'Where are My partners, those for whose sake you used to oppose [the truth]?'"* (Qur'an 16:27)

And, most fully, in the account of the mutual cursing of the followers and their leaders — the passage in which the reversal is dramatised rather than merely stated, and in which the reader is shown what the enmity sounds like from both sides:

> *"On the Day when their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say: 'Would that we had obeyed God and obeyed the Messenger!'... Our Lord! Verily we obeyed our chiefs and our great ones, and they led us astray from the way."* (Qur'an 33:66–67)

**The Principle: The Betrayal Is Structural, Not Personal**

There is a specific logic in this reversal that is worth drawing out, because it is not arbitrary and it is not vindictive. The idolater's error was not merely that he worshipped the wrong object. It was that he *attributed to the object something it does not have* — the power to save, to intercede, to protect. On the Day when reality is manifest, that attribution collapses, and it collapses from *both* sides: the worshipper discovers the object cannot help, and the object disowns the worship the worshipper offered. The disassociation is therefore not a betrayal but a *correction*. The angel, the prophet, or the idol cannot accept what was never its due, and on the Day when everything is made true, it says so. That is why the Qur'an's descriptions of this scene are consistent and unsentimental. Nobody is being punished for a technicality. The relationship that the idolater constructed was founded on a falsehood, and on the Day when falsehood cannot survive, the relationship dissolves.

**A Special Case: The Worship of Jesus**

For Muslim readers, this verse has a particular application, because the sūrah has spent twenty verses on Jesus and will spend eight more on him from v. 88 — and the application is one the chapter has been constructing deliberately rather than incidentally. The Qur'an records the exchange that will take place, and it is the clearest instance of the reversal in the whole Book. What makes it clearest is that the object of worship in this case is a prophet who is innocent of that worship; the reversal is therefore not a punishment inflicted by the worshipped but a dissociation spoken by him, which is the most complete form the reversal can take:

> *"And [mention] when God said: 'O Jesus son of Mary! Did you say to mankind, "Take me and my mother as gods apart from God?"' He said: 'Glory be to You! It is not for me to say what I have no right to. Had I said it, You would have known it. You know what is in my soul, and I know not what is in Your soul. Verily You are the Knower of the Unseen. I said not to them except what You commanded me: "Worship God, my Lord and your Lord."'"* (Qur'an 5:116–17)

That is v. 82 enacted in the most consequential case. The one who is worshipped will disown the worship, in his own voice, before the assembly of all creation. And the sūrah's own contribution to that theme, already discussed, is that Jesus began doing this in the cradle — *"Truly I am a servant of God"* (v. 30) — four hundred years before the doctrine arose that would need refuting.

**The Practical Warning**

For the reader, this verse carries a warning that is not about idols. Anything a person has made the centre of his reliance will, at the moment of his greatest need, be discovered to have been incapable of helping him. That is not a threat pronounced from outside; it is the natural consequence of relying on what cannot bear the weight. Money cannot save at the reckoning; reputation cannot intercede; a career cannot answer for a person; a nation cannot; a talent cannot. The sūrah's closing promise at v. 96 supplies the positive counterpart: *"Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds — the Compassionate will grant them love."* That is the one thing that will not disassociate, because the One who gives it does not change.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:83

> **Hast thou not seen that We have sent the satans upon the disbelievers, inciting them [to sin]?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Agency Behind the Error**

The sūrah completes its diagnosis by naming the agent: *a-lam tara annā arsalnā al-shayāṭīna ʿalā al-kāfirīna taʾuzzuhum azzan* — "Hast thou not seen that We have sent the satans upon the disbelievers, inciting them [to sin]?" This verse is the counterpart to v. 44, where Abraham warned his father not to worship Satan, and v. 68, where the disbelievers and their satans were gathered together around Hell. The sūrah has now stated the full account: the idolatry has a motive (v. 81), a consequence (v. 82), and an agent (v. 83).

**"We Have Sent" — The Crucial Theological Claim**

The most important and most delicate element of the verse is its subject: *arsalnā* — "**We** have sent." God sends the satans. That is a hard saying, and the tradition has handled it with great care, because read carelessly it suggests that God is the author of the disbelief He punishes. The Sunni position, articulated with particular thoroughness by Ibn Taymiyyah, runs as follows: *God sent them as a trial and a punishment, not as a cause of the disbelief.* The sequence in the Qur'an is consistent: a person rejects, and God then *lets loose* upon him what confirms him in his rejection. The sending is a consequence and a seal, not an origin.

> *"And whoever turns away from the remembrance of the Compassionate, We appoint for him a satan, who becomes a companion to him."* (Qur'an 43:36)

Note the order in that verse: *whoever turns away* — then *We appoint*. The turning away is the person's; the appointment is God's, and it follows. *The sending is by way of divine decree, not by way of divine pleasure.* Nothing occurs in creation except by God's Will, and the satan's activity is not outside it. But willing something as part of a test is not the same as commanding or approving it. *The satan has no authority over the believer.* This is the decisive qualifier, and the Qur'an states it:

> *"Verily he [Satan] has no authority over those who believe and rely upon their Lord. His authority is only over those who take him as an ally and who, because of him, associate partners with God."* (Qur'an 16:99–100)

So the sending described in this verse applies *specifically* to *al-kāfirīn* — "the disbelievers" — those who have already taken the satan as an ally. The verse does not say that God sends satans upon people generally; it says He sends them upon those who have themselves chosen that company, and the distinction is doctrinally decisive. A God who incites everyone indiscriminately would be the author of the disobedience He punishes; a God who incites those who have already allied themselves against Him is describing a consequence rather than imposing one. Satan's own statement of his method confirms this reading:

> *"He said: 'My Lord, because You have put me in error, I will surely make [disobedience] fair-seeming to them on earth, and I will surely mislead them all, except Your sincere servants among them.' He said: 'This is a path [of return] to Me, binding [upon Satan]. Verily, as for My servants, you have no authority over them, except the deviant among those who follow you.'"* (Qur'an 15:39–42)

Satan's own admission is that his authority is limited to *those who follow him*, and the admission is quoted in the Qur'an rather than merely asserted by the commentators, which is what gives it its weight. That is the verse's safeguard, and it is why the sūrah's phrasing is precise. The tradition's position is therefore not that Satan is harmless but that his jurisdiction is conditional — he has authority over those who have granted it by following, and none at all over those who have not. The practical consequence is enormous, since it means the question is never whether a person is being tempted but whether he is following.

**The Verb: Taʾuzzuhum Azzan**

The final phrase describes the inciting: *taʾuzzuhum azzan* — "they incite them with an inciting." The verb (root *ʾ-z-z*) means to stir, to agitate, to goad, to urge vehemently. The lexicographers described it as stirring up a pot until it boils over — the image is of something being agitated from below until it cannot stay still. The cognate accusative *azzan* intensifies: "they goad them with a goading." The commentators glossed it as: they provoke them to acts of disobedience; they stir them up; they drive them toward sin (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī). The picture is of an internal agitation rather than an external command. The satan does not order; he agitates. He makes the wrong action seem urgent, the delay unbearable, the objection unbearable, the desire unignorable.

**"Satans" — Jinn or Men?**

The plural *al-shayāṭīn* has been read two ways, and the Qur'an supports both — which is why the commentators retained both rather than adjudicating between them. The plural form is what opens the readings, since the Qur'an elsewhere speaks of a single Satan while here it speaks of many:

> *"And thus We have made for every prophet an enemy — satans from among mankind and jinn, who inspire one another with ornate speech as delusion."* (Qur'an 6:112)

So there are *shayāṭīn* among humans as well as among jinn. The commentators noted that both readings apply here: God sends both the jinn-satans and the human inciters upon those who have turned away. That is a socially pointed reading, and it is worth stating. A community that has turned from God will find itself surrounded by voices — some invisible, some entirely embodied in people — that agitate it toward further error. The presence of such voices is not random; the sūrah presents it as a consequence.

**Satan's Proximity and the Remedy**

The tradition paired the warning with the remedy, as it does with every danger it describes, and the pairing is the reason a reader is not left with an anxiety he has no way to act on. The Prophet ﷺ described the danger in physical terms, and the physicality of the description is what makes it recognisable in a person's own experience:

> *"Satan flows through the son of Adam like blood."* (al-Bukhārī 7171; Muslim 2174)

The image is of something pervasive and penetrating, and the correct response is not to attempt to feel one's way free of it — an impossible task, and one that produces a worse condition than the one it was meant to escape. A person who tries to detect the whisper directly ends up in a state of permanent self-surveillance, which is itself a form of the occupation he was trying to avoid. What has been prescribed instead is a set of practices that do the work without the person having to monitor them:

> *"And if an incitement from Satan should incite thee, then seek refuge in God. Verily He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing."* (Qur'an 7:200)

> *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind, the King of mankind, the God of mankind, from the evil of the slinking whisperer, who whispers into the breasts of mankind, from among the jinn and mankind."* (Qur'an 114:1–6)

The last sūrah's closing phrase — *"from among the jinn and mankind"* — confirms that the whisperer operates through both, and the sūrah's remedy covers both. The confirmation matters practically, because the incitement a person actually experiences arrives far more often through other people than through any invisible agency — through company, through what is read and watched, through the opinions that surround him and make a thing seem normal. A reader who restricts the warning to the jinn has taken the less dangerous half of it and left the more dangerous half intact.

**The Removal of Excuse**

Finally, this verse performs one more function, and it is the most uncomfortable of all. A person might be tempted to say: *I was incited; what could I do?* The sūrah's answer is that the incitement is real and the responsibility is not diminished by it, because the inciter had no authority that was not granted. The Prophet ﷺ stated the matter in a ḥadīth that is among the most clarifying on the subject:

> *"There is none among you but that a companion has been appointed for him from among the jinn."* They said: "Even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Even me — except that God has helped me against him, and he has submitted, so he does not command me except with good."* (Muslim 2814)

That ḥadīth is the sūrah's teaching in miniature, and what it removes is the assumption that the accompanied person is the abandoned one. Everyone is accompanied. The difference between the believer and the disbeliever is not the presence of the inciter but whether the inciter has been brought into submission — and that is achieved through the same means the sūrah has been prescribing from v. 2: remembrance of the Mercy of the Lord. The chapter's argument thus closes its own circle. What Zachariah did in the sanctuary at the opening is what the believer does against the inciter at the close, and the sūrah has been one continuous instruction in a single practice.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:84

> **So hasten not against them. We are but numbering for them a number.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Grief, and God's Answer**

The verse addresses the Prophet ﷺ directly, and its occasion is his distress: *fa-lā taʿjal ʿalayhim innamā naʿuddu lahum ʿaddan* — "So hasten not against them. We are but numbering for them a number." The commentators explained the setting. The Prophet ﷺ was grieved by the Quraysh's rejection and by the slowness of the help he had been promised. He longed for the punishment to come — not out of vindictiveness but out of the intolerable strain of watching the people he loved walk toward ruin while being mocked for warning them. God's answer is not a promise of speed. It is a statement that the matter is being counted. The same consolation is given elsewhere in the Qur'an, and the consistency of the wording suggests how often the Prophet ﷺ needed it:

> *"We know well that thy breast is constricted by what they say. So glorify the praise of thy Lord, and be among those who prostrate."* (Qur'an 15:97–98)

> *"Perhaps you will kill yourself with grief over them, if they do not believe in this message."* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"And if their turning away is grievous to thee — then if you are able to seek a tunnel into the earth or a ladder into the sky, to bring them a sign... But had God willed, He would have gathered them to guidance. So be not among the ignorant."* (Qur'an 6:35)

**"We Are But Numbering for Them a Number"**

The phrase *innamā naʿuddu lahum ʿaddan* — "We are only numbering for them a number" — is one of the most linguistically interesting in the sūrah, and the commentators dwelt on it at length. The verb *naʿuddu* (root *ʿ-d-d*) means We count, We number, We enumerate. The cognate accusative *ʿaddan* intensifies it: *We are counting them out, a counting.* What is being counted? Two readings, and they are complementary: *The majority reading* is that it counts their **days, breaths, and deeds** — that is, the remainder of their allotted term. Every day that passes is one fewer. The commentators paraphrased the effect as: *do not be impatient, for their allotted span is running out; when it is finished, the matter will come to pass.* Al-Khalīl ibn Aḥmad al-Farāhīdī (d. 170/786), the founder of Arabic lexicography, made a famous grammatical observation on this verse, recorded by al-Rāzī, al-Thaʿlabī, and al-Zamakhsharī. He said that he had searched for a word in Arabic that a person "needs" and that God can "dispense with," and found only one: *days*. Because God says *We are counting for them a counting* — and counting is what a person does when he is *waiting for something to end*. God has no need to count, since He knows the number without counting; the expression is used for the sake of the hearer, to convey that the term is finite and is being exhausted. *A second reading* is that it counts **them** — that is, their number, person by person. On this reading the verse means: We are enumerating them individually, and not one of them will be missed. Both readings converge on the same consolation, and the convergence is worth remarking on, since the two readings differ about what is being counted while agreeing entirely about what the counting means: the matter is under exact administration. Nothing is slipping. For a community that was small, besieged, and easily overlooked by the standards of the world, the assurance is not abstract — it is addressed to people who had good reason to wonder whether anyone had noticed them.

**The Theological Point: Divine Timing Is Not Delay**

There is a doctrine embedded here that is worth naming, because it addresses one of the most common sources of religious distress. The believer prays, warns, strives, and nothing happens. The temptation is to conclude either that God is indifferent or that the effort was futile. The sūrah's answer is that *the appearance of inactivity is not inactivity*. The counting is real even when nothing visible is occurring. The Qur'an states the principle in general terms:

> *"And were it not for a word that had preceded from thy Lord, judgment would have been executed between them immediately."* (Qur'an 11:110)

> *"And verily a Day with thy Lord is as a thousand years of what you count."* (Qur'an 22:47)

And the Prophet ﷺ stated the practical corollary for the one who is waiting, which is the element that turns the doctrine from information into something a person can live inside. The counting is not only a threat to those being counted down; it is also the ground of the patience the sūrah has been commending since its opening:

> *"Know that there is much good in patience concerning what you dislike, and that victory comes with patience, relief comes with distress, and ease comes with hardship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**"Hasten Not Against Them" — The Discipline of Not Wishing Punishment**

The imperative *fa-lā taʿjal* — "so do not hasten" — carries a specific instruction that deserves attention, because it corrects a very natural religious emotion. It is one thing to want justice. It is another to want it *soon*, and the sūrah forbids the second. The commentators noted that the instruction is not a rebuke but a reassurance, and its form tells the reader something about how God speaks to His messenger in distress: He does not say "stop feeling that." He gives a reason — *We are counting* — which is the only thing that would actually settle the heart. That is a model of consolation worth noting. The person who is waiting is not helped by being told to be patient. He is helped by being shown that the waiting has an end that is known to Someone.

**The Sūrah's Own Instance of This Principle**

It is worth noticing that the sūrah has already dramatised this principle three times before stating it here. *Zachariah* waited a lifetime for a son, and God told him the sign would be three nights of silence (v. 10). The waiting had a term. *The revelation* was delayed while the Prophet ﷺ longed for it, and the answer was *"thy Lord is not forgetful"* (v. 64). The waiting had a term. *Mary* waited through labour and solitude, and the relief arrived at the point of extremity (vv. 23–26). The waiting had a term. Now the sūrah states the principle explicitly, and it applies it to the hardest case of all: the delay of justice itself.

**The Reckoning's Precision**

Finally, the verse asserts something about the character of the Judgement that is worth drawing out, because it distinguishes the Qur'an's account from every version of the afterlife that presents it as a general dissolution into something else. It is *counted*. The Qur'an's eschatology is not a general dissolution but an exact accounting, and the vocabulary of number, measure, and record runs through it — terms, tallies, scales, registers, and a reckoning exact to the weight of an atom:

> *"And We shall set up the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul will be wronged at all."* (Qur'an 21:47)

> *"And He has enumerated everything in number."* (Qur'an 72:28)

> *"And We shall bring forth a Book on the Day of Resurrection which he will find spread open: 'Read your book! Your own soul suffices this day as a reckoner against you.'"* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

For the wrongdoer, the counting is a threat: the term is running out. For the wronged, it is a promise: nothing has been missed, and every item will be produced. The same mechanism serves both, which is the point — a Judgement that is exact is only good news for the person whose account is clean, and the tradition has never presented it otherwise. What the doctrine removes is not the danger but the arbitrariness, and for anyone who has suffered at the hands of someone who appeared to prosper, the removal of arbitrariness is the whole of what is being offered. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Beware the supplication of the wronged, for there is no veil between it and God."* (al-Bukhārī 2448; Muslim 19)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:85

> **On the Day when We shall gather the reverent unto the Compassionate, as a delegation,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First of Two Processions**

The sūrah now paints its two final scenes, and they are among the most famous images in the Qur'an. The first: *yawma naḥshuru al-muttaqīna ilā al-Raḥmāni wafdan* — "On the Day when We shall gather the reverent unto the Compassionate, as a delegation." Three elements, each chosen with precision. *The verb: naḥshuru* — "We shall gather." The same verb used at v. 68 for the gathering of the disbelievers and their satans. The same event, the same verb — and then everything else differs. *The direction: ilā al-Raḥmāni* — "unto the Compassionate." They are gathered *to* a Person, not *to* a place. The sūrah, having used the Name sixteen times, now makes it the destination. *The manner: wafdan* — "as a delegation."

**The Word Wafd**

*Wafd* (root *w-f-d*) means a delegation, a deputation, an arriving party — specifically, a group that comes to a great personage as honoured guests. The word carries connotations of: arrival with dignity, reception, hospitality, and being *expected*. The commentators drew out the image. The reverent are gathered as the honoured guests of the Compassionate — arriving, not being driven; mounted, not on foot; received, not arraigned. The word's ordinary usage supports this. In pre-Islamic and early Islamic Arabia, when a tribe came to the Prophet ﷺ, it came as a *wafd* — a delegation received with hospitality, housed, fed, and given gifts. The year 9 AH is known in the sīrah as the *ʿām al-wufūd*, "the year of the delegations," because so many tribes came to Madinah to enter Islam. So the sūrah is describing the believing community's arrival at their Lord in the terms their own culture would have understood as the highest honour: a delegation, arriving at the court of a generous king, and received.

**The Reverent Are Gathered "Unto" a Person**

There is a theological precision in *ilā* — "unto" — that is worth dwelling on, because the sūrah's second scene will use a different preposition. The reverent are gathered *to the Compassionate*. The destination is named as a Person, and the Name used is the mercy-Name. That is the sūrah's entire theology in a preposition: the destination of the righteous is not a place but a Lord, and the Lord is the Compassionate. The Qur'an states the same elsewhere in terms that Muslims have found inexhaustibly consoling:

> *"And those who believed and did righteous deeds — We shall surely admit them among the righteous."* (Qur'an 29:9)

> *"O soul at peace! Return unto thy Lord, well-pleased and pleasing [unto Him]. So enter among My servants, and enter My Garden."* (Qur'an 89:27–30)

That last passage is the most intimate statement of the destination anywhere in the Qur'an, and its structure matches our verse exactly: return *to your Lord*, and then enter. The sequence is worth holding onto, because the destination is named as a Person before it is named as a place. The believers are not described primarily as arriving somewhere but as arriving to Someone, and the sūrah's insistence on the preposition is the chapter's final answer to any reading of the afterlife as a change of location rather than a change of relationship.

**The Condition: Al-Muttaqūn**

As throughout the sūrah, the people in this procession are identified by *taqwā* — *al-muttaqīn*, "the reverent." It is worth noting, once more, how consistently the sūrah has used this one word as the dividing criterion (vv. 13, 18, 63, 72, 85), and it will use it again in the final verse of the sūrah's argument (v. 97: *"glad tidings unto the reverent"*). The word's recurrence across ninety verses is itself the sūrah's answer to every competing criterion: not wealth (vv. 73–80), not lineage (vv. 41–57), not knowledge (vv. 66–67), not social standing (v. 74), and not even the quantity of one's works (v. 76, where what matters is what *endures*). The criterion is the quality of one's awareness of God.

**A Note on the Gathering's Physical Description**

The commentators and the ḥadīth literature supply details about the manner of the gathering that are worth recording, with the usual caveat about which reports are of Prophetic origin and which are Israelite or exegetical. The distinction matters here more than usual, because the details are vivid and a reader will be tempted to treat all of them as equally authoritative. The authentic ḥadīth describes the general gathering in stark terms:

> *"You will be gathered [on the Day of Resurrection] barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised."* (al-Bukhārī 6527; Muslim 2859, from ʿĀʾishah and Ibn ʿAbbās)

That ḥadīth describes humanity generally. The specific claim that the reverent will arrive *mounted* — which the word *wafd* implies, since a delegation in that culture arrived on camels — is transmitted in the commentaries (al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī) and is consistent with the word's ordinary sense, though the details rest on transmitted reports rather than on ṣaḥīḥ ḥadīth. The tradition's careful distinction should be preserved: the *image* of honour is established by the Qur'an's own word; the *mechanics* are not.

**The Verse's Pastoral Weight**

For the reader, this verse is one of the sūrah's great gifts, and it is worth saying why. Most people's imagination of the Day of Resurrection is dominated by its terror — and the Qur'an does present that terror unflinchingly. But the sūrah's balance is deliberate: it depicts the two processions side by side, and the first one it describes is the gathering of the righteous, arriving as honoured guests. The order is significant. When the Qur'an wants to encourage the believer, it begins with the promise; when it wants to warn, it begins with the threat. Here, at the end of a sūrah that has spent most of its length consoling a persecuted community, it begins with the consolation. And there is a specific comfort in the word *wafd*. The believer does not arrive as a criminal to a courtroom or as a supplicant to a gate. He arrives as a guest to a host who invited him, and the host is the Compassionate. The Prophet ﷺ gave the same image in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"God says: 'I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me... and if he comes to Me walking, I go to him at speed.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:86

> **and We shall drive the guilty into Hell as a thirsty herd—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Procession**

The sūrah places its two scenes in immediate juxtaposition, and the contrast is constructed with a precision that is almost architectural — the two verses are built from the same grammatical slots, and the reader is meant to fill each pair and feel what has changed. *wa-nasūqu al-mujrimīna ilā jahannama wirdan* — "and We shall drive the guilty into Hell as a thirsty herd." Compare the two verses word by word:

| **v. 85 (the reverent)** | **v. 86 (the guilty)** |
|---|---|
| *naḥshuru* — We gather | *nasūqu* — We drive |
| *al-muttaqīn* — the reverent | *al-mujrimīn* — the guilty |
| *ilā al-Raḥmāni* — to the Compassionate | *ilā jahannama* — to Hell |
| *wafdan* — as an honoured delegation | *wirdan* — as a thirsty herd |

Every element is inverted, and the inversion is total rather than partial. The verb changes from gathering, which preserves the dignity of the one being gathered, to driving, which removes it. The destination changes from a Person to a place. And the manner changes from *wafd* — an arriving delegation, received, expected, and honoured — to *wird* — a herd coming to water, driven by thirst rather than by invitation. The two words differ by a single letter, and the sūrah is relying on the reader to hear the difference: the same consonants, and an entirely opposite condition.

**The Verb: Nasūqu**

*Nasūqu* (root *s-w-q*) means We drive, We urge forward, We herd. It is the word used for driving animals — *sāqa al-ghanam*, "he drove the sheep." The verb's object is a human being, and the dehumanisation is the point. These are people who were given the choice of whether to be gathered or driven, and who chose, repeatedly, in the direction that led here. The contrast with v. 85 is deliberately jarring. The reverent are gathered; the guilty are driven. The difference is not in the destination alone but in the manner of arrival, and the manner reflects what the person has made of himself.

**The Word Wird — A Herd Coming to Water**

*Wird* (root *w-r-d*) means a group coming to a watering-place — a herd arriving at water to drink. It is the same root as *wāriduhā* in v. 71 (*"there is not one of you but that he will approach it"*). The verbal noun *wurūd* means arrival at water; hence *wārid* — one who arrives at water; and *wird* — the herd that arrives, or the act of arriving. The commentators explained the image: the guilty are driven to Hell as a thirsty herd is driven to water — that is, they are *parched with thirst*, and the thing they are driven toward is not relief. The Qur'an uses thirst as one of its principal images for the condition of the damned:

> *"And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces — blind, dumb, and deaf. Their abode is Hell; whenever it abates, We shall increase the flame for them."* (Qur'an 17:97)

> *"Then, moreover, you, O those who are astray, who deny [the Resurrection], will surely eat from a tree of Zaqqūm, and fill your bellies therewith, and drink on top of it scalding water, and you will drink as drinks the thirsty camel."* (Qur'an 56:51–55)

That last verse uses the same image — *"you will drink as drinks the thirsty camel"* — and the word for the parched animal there is from the same semantic field as *wird*, which is to say that the Qur'an's depictions of the Fire consistently use thirst rather than burning as their primary register. And the Qur'an contrasts their thirst with the drink of the righteous, and the contrast is established by the same vocabulary rather than by a different one:

> *"Verily the righteous shall drink from a cup whereof the mixture is camphor, a spring wherefrom the servants of God drink, making it gush forth abundantly."* (Qur'an 76:5–6)

**The Word Mujrimīn**

The guilty are called *al-mujrimīn* (root *j-r-m*), meaning criminals, those who have incurred guilt. The root's primary sense is *to cut off, to commit an offence, to earn* — and the Qur'an's usage makes the person one who has *earned* his condition. That is the sūrah's consistent emphasis, and it is worth noting because objections to the doctrine of Hell usually rest on a different picture. The guilty are not victims of a sentence imposed from outside; they are people who arrived somewhere, having been warned (vv. 66–77), having been given the evidence (v. 73: *"when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs"*), and having chosen. The sūrah has spent eighty-five verses establishing that the warning was clear, the evidence was manifest, and the door was open. This verse is the consequence, and it is stated without embellishment.

**The Destination Is a Place**

Note the asymmetry in the two verses' destinations, because it is theologically deliberate. The reverent are gathered *to the Compassionate* — to a Person. The guilty are driven *to Hell* — to a place. The sūrah is making a point that runs through the whole of Islamic eschatology: the punishment of the damned is, in the first instance, *the absence of God*, and their destination is described as a location because a location is all that remains to them. The reward of the blessed is described as a Person because a Person is what they wanted. The tradition has expressed this in various formulations. The essential insight is that Heaven and Hell are not two symmetrical destinations assigned by decree; they are the natural termini of two orientations. A person who spent his life turning toward God arrives at God. A person who spent his life turning away arrives at the place where God is not turned toward. The Prophet ﷺ expressed the asymmetry in a ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"God says: 'I am as My servant thinks of Me.'"* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

**The Sūrah's Refusal to Explain**

It should be noted how brief this verse is. The sūrah has given the scene of the damned six words and has moved on. That restraint is characteristic, and the tradition has followed it. Classical Islamic theology discusses the nature of Hell at length where the texts require it — its eternity, its inhabitants, its gradations — but it has consistently discouraged *speculation* about its mechanics, and it has treated descriptions of the Fire as warnings rather than as information to be elaborated upon. The reason is not squeamishness. It is that the Qur'an's purpose in mentioning Hell is to warn and thereby to save, and elaboration serves curiosity rather than warning.

**A Final Pastoral Note**

There is one further point to be made, and it is pastorally essential, because without it the verse is read as a description of a fixed class of people rather than as a warning to the reader. The sūrah's description of the guilty being driven as a thirsty herd is a warning addressed to people who are still alive and still able to change. The word *mujrimīn* designates those who have persisted in rejection, and no living person has been declared to be among them. The Prophet ﷺ said — and the saying is the tradition's standing reminder that the description is a warning rather than a verdict already delivered:

> *"Verily God extends His Hand during the night so that the sinner of the day may repent, and He extends His Hand during the day so that the sinner of the night may repent — until the sun rises from its west."* (Muslim 2759)

A verse of warning is, in the Qur'an's economy, an act of mercy. The person who reads v. 86 and is frightened has received the intended benefit; the person who reads it and is unmoved has received the warning and declined it. That is why the sūrah, immediately after this scene, states the doctrine of intercession (v. 87) — which is the door that remains open to everyone who is still breathing.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:87

> **none shall possess intercession, save he who has taken a covenant from the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse on Intercession**

The sūrah's two processions lead directly to the question that the doctrine of intercession answers: *lā yamlikūna al-shafāʿata illā man ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "none shall possess intercession, save he who has taken a covenant from the Compassionate." Two claims in one sentence. The first is a denial: no one possesses intercession. The second is a qualification: except the one who has received a covenant.

**The Denial: No One Owns Intercession**

The phrase *lā yamlikūna al-shafāʿata* is emphatic, and the emphasis is carried by the verb rather than by the negation. *Yamlikūna* (root *m-l-k*) means they *possess*, they *own*, they *have authority over*. So the verse is not saying "no one will intercede." It is saying **no one possesses intercession as a right** — and the difference between those two statements is the difference between a denial and a correction. Intercession is real and it will occur; what is denied is that anyone holds it independently, able to deploy it for whom he chooses. That distinction is the whole doctrine, and the Qur'an states it repeatedly:

> *"Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

> *"Say: To God belongs intercession entirely. To Him belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth; then unto Him you will be returned."* (Qur'an 39:44)

> *"On that Day, intercession does not avail, save [that of] him to whom the Compassionate has given permission and whose word He has accepted."* (Qur'an 20:109)

> *"And how many an angel is in the heavens whose intercession avails not at all, save after God gives permission to whom He wills and is pleased with."* (Qur'an 53:26)

> *"And they cannot intercede except for one whom He approves, and they tremble in awe of Him."* (Qur'an 21:28)

Two conditions emerge from those verses, and they are the doctrine in full: **permission** from God to intercede, and **acceptance** of the one for whom intercession is made. Both are God's, and the fact that both are His is what preserves the doctrine from becoming the very thing it denies. An intercession that required only the intercessor's willingness would place a second will alongside God's; an intercession that requires His permission for the act and His pleasure with the subject places nothing beside Him at all.

**The Qualification: "He Who Has Taken a Covenant"**

The exception is *man ittakhadha ʿinda al-Raḥmāni ʿahdan* — "he who has taken a covenant from the Compassionate." The word *ʿahd* — covenant — is the same word the sūrah used at v. 78, where the disbeliever was asked whether he had *"taken a covenant from the Compassionate"* to guarantee that he would be given wealth and children in the Hereafter. The answer there was no. Here, the same phrase is used for a real covenant that some have. The sūrah is thus contrasting a false claim to a covenant with the genuine article:

- **v. 78:** The boaster has no covenant; his claim to future reward is groundless.
- **v. 87:** The intercessors have a covenant; their intercession is authorised.

What is the covenant? The commentators gave two readings, and both are held by the tradition: *The first reading* is that the covenant is **God's permission**, granted to the angels, the prophets, and the martyrs, to intercede for whom He wills. Al-Ṭabarī transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās that the covenant is God's leave to a person to intercede on behalf of another. *The second reading* is that the covenant is **the testimony of faith** — *lā ilāha illā Allāh*. Al-Ṭabarī transmitted a report that the covenant is precisely the declaration of God's oneness, and that the person who has said it sincerely will be interceded for. Both readings are true and are complementary: the intercessor needs permission, and the one interceded for needs the faith. The Prophet ﷺ combined them in a ḥadīth:

> *"My intercession will be for the people of major sins among my community."* (al-Tirmidhī 2435; Abū Dāwūd 4739; graded ḥasan)

That ḥadīth establishes the second condition — the intercession is for believers, even sinful ones — and the scholars cited it as the proof that the intercession described in the Qur'an is not reserved for the flawless. And this ḥadīth establishes the first, and it does so in the form of an event rather than a ruling, which is why it has been narrated so often:

> *"O Abū Hurayrah, who is the happiest of people with your intercession?"* He said: "O Messenger of God, I thought no one would ask me about this ḥadīth before you, because of what I have seen of your eagerness for ḥadīth. The happiest of people with my intercession on the Day of Resurrection is the one who said: *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, sincerely from his heart or from his soul." (al-Bukhārī 99)

**The Types of Intercession in Islam**

The tradition enumerated the forms of intercession recognised on the Day of Resurrection, and it is worth listing them because the doctrine is often presented in a distorted form. *The Greatest Intercession (*al-shafāʿah al-ʿuẓmā*).* This is the *maqām maḥmūd* — "the praiseworthy station" — promised to the Prophet ﷺ, in which he will intercede for all of humanity to be relieved of the terror of the standing and for the reckoning to begin. The Qur'an alludes to it:

> *"And in some part of the night, pray with it as additional [worship] for thee; it may be that thy Lord will raise thee to a praiseworthy station."* (Qur'an 17:79)

And the Prophet ﷺ said — in a ḥadīth that supplies the manner of the first intercession and that the community has recited in its creedal formulas ever since, since it names the station he was promised:

> *"Then I will fall in prostration beneath the Throne, and it will be said to me: 'O Muhammad, raise your head; speak and you will be heard; intercede and your intercession will be granted.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4712; Muslim 193)

*Intercession for entrance into the Garden.* This is the form the tradition has in view when it speaks of the believers entering without reckoning, and the reports describe it as occurring after the believers have already been delivered from the Fire rather than instead of that deliverance. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Every prophet has a supplication that is answered, and every prophet hastened his; but I have stored my supplication as intercession for my community on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6304; Muslim 199)

*Intercession for those deserving punishment not to enter it.* This is the intercession described in the ḥadīth of the major sins. *Intercession for those already in the Fire to be removed.* This is the most frequently transmitted form, and the relevant ḥadīth describes the removal of everyone in whose heart is faith "the weight of a mustard seed," then "the weight of a grain of barley," then "the weight of an atom":

> *"Then the angels will bring them out, recognising them by the marks of prostration..."* (al-Bukhārī 7439; Muslim 193 — the long ḥadīth of intercession)

*Intercession for the elevation of ranks in the Garden.* This is the form most often overlooked, and it is the one that finally settles the question of whether intercession has any place once everyone has been admitted. It does, and its function there is not rescue but increase — which is the strongest possible evidence that intercession in Islam is a gift from God rather than a claim against Him, since it operates even where nothing is at stake but degree.

**What the Verse Excludes**

The verse's denial is as important as its permission, and the tradition has been clear about what it rules out, because a doctrine of intercession that excludes nothing has quietly replaced the Creator with the creatures who intercede. *No intercession for those who died in shirk.* This is explicit, and it is stated in the Qur'an rather than inferred, which is why the commentators treated it as a boundary rather than as a severity:

> *"And they will say: 'Now there are no intercessors for us, nor any intimate friend.'"* (Qur'an 26:100–101)

> *"So there will not benefit them the intercession of intercessors."* (Qur'an 74:48)

*No intercession sought from the dead or from creation.* The intercession described in the Qur'an is performed *by permission*, at the time God permits it — not requested from a creature in the present with the expectation that the creature has independent standing. That is the doctrine's practical edge, and it is worth stating plainly because it is one of the dividing lines in Islamic practice. To ask a living person to pray for one is lawful and is the practice of the Companions, who asked the Prophet ﷺ to pray for them. To invoke a dead person, or an angel, or a saint, with the belief that the invoked party can bring about what is asked independently of God's permission, is what the Qur'an's verses on intercession exclude. The distinction is not about the *form* of words but about the *belief behind them*. The Companions asked the Prophet ﷺ to pray for them because they believed God answers His prophet's prayer — which is to say, they asked God through a means He had established. They did not believe the Prophet ﷺ had independent authority.

**The Name al-Raḥmān, Once More**

The sūrah names the Compassionate for the thirteenth time, and its placement here is exactly right. Intercession is, in its essence, a mercy-mechanism. It is the means by which people who deserve punishment receive less or none, and by which people who deserve a rank receive more. It is therefore entirely fitting that the covenant that authorises it is described as taken *from the Compassionate*. The sūrah is consistent on this: every mechanism of deliverance in this chapter is traced to the Name that opened it. The reminder was "of the Mercy of thy Lord" (v. 2). The Garden is "that which the Compassionate promised" (v. 61). The reverent are gathered "unto the Compassionate" (v. 85). And intercession is authorised by "a covenant from the Compassionate" (v. 87).

**The Practical Conclusion**

For the believer, this verse yields two instructions that are worth carrying. *First: seek intercession by seeking the covenant.* The way to be among those interceded for is to hold fast to the testimony of faith and to die upon it. The Prophet ﷺ's answer to Abū Hurayrah's question is the practical path: *whoever says lā ilāha illā Allāh sincerely from his heart.* *Second: do not rely on intercession as a substitute for obedience.* The tradition is unanimous that intercession is a mercy for the deficient, not a licence for the defiant. The person who sins in the expectation that someone will intercede for him has misunderstood the mechanism entirely — intercession requires God's pleasure with the one interceded for, and the deliberate sinner has no guarantee of that. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Hasten to do good deeds before tribulations come like a portion of the dark night..."* (Muslim 118)

And the Qur'an states the condition in the sūrah's own closing verses, which the next passage will make devastatingly clear. What is being set up is the chapter's final argument: the claim that God has taken a child is precisely the claim that removes a person from the range of intercession, and the sūrah has therefore spent its whole length establishing both the gravity of the claim and the narrowness of the escape from it.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:88

> **And they say, "The Compassionate has taken a child."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim the Sūrah Has Been Moving Toward**

Here, at last, is the claim that the entire narrative of this sūrah has been constructed to address: *wa-qālū ittakhadha al-Raḥmānu waladan* — "And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a child.'" The sūrah has spent eighty-seven verses preparing for this line. It narrated the birth of John to a man whose bones had failed and a woman who was barren — a birth with a father — to establish that God creates outside ordinary means. It narrated the birth of Jesus with no father at all — to establish that God creates outside every means. It had the infant himself declare, in the cradle, *"Truly I am a servant of God"* (v. 30) and *"the day I die"* (v. 33). It stated the doctrine directly at v. 35: *"It is not for God to take a child."* And now the sūrah quotes the counter-claim in the words of those who make it.

**Who Said This?**

The commentators were unanimous that the verse addresses three distinct groups, each of which made the claim in a different form (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī), and the unanimity is significant because the verse's wording is general while the referents are specific. The sūrah names the claim rather than the claimants, which allows a single refutation to cover every version of it. *Some of the Jews* said of ʿUzayr (Ezra) that he was the son of God. The Qur'an names this claim explicitly:

> *"And the Jews say, 'ʿUzayr is the son of God,' and the Christians say, 'The Messiah is the son of God.' That is their statement by their mouths; they imitate the saying of those who disbelieved before. May God destroy them; how are they deluded?"* (Qur'an 9:30)

*The Christians* said it of Jesus, and this is the version the sūrah has been addressing since v. 16 and the version given by far the most attention in the Qur'an — as it must be, since it is the version held by the largest religious community in the world and the one the Muslim community is in continuous conversation with. The Qur'an quotes and answers this claim at length:

> *"O People of the Book! Do not exaggerate in your religion, and do not say about God except the truth. The Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, was only the messenger of God, and His Word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him. So believe in God and His messengers, and do not say 'Three.' Desist — it is better for you. God is only one God; glory be to Him, [far exalted is He] above having a child."* (Qur'an 4:171)

*Some of the pagan Arabs* said it of the angels, whom they described as God's daughters — and the inclusion of this group is what makes the verse's argument reach further than a dispute with Jews and Christians. The Arabs of Makkah were not, in the main, Trinitarians; they were people who had placed created beings between themselves and God and had described those beings as His offspring. The Qur'an's refusal to treat that as a different error is one of its most consequential claims:

> *"And they make the angels — those who are servants of the Compassionate — females. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned."* (Qur'an 43:19)

> *"Or has He taken daughters from what He creates and favoured you with sons? And when one of them is given news of that which he has set up as a likeness to the Compassionate, his face becomes dark and he is filled with grief."* (Qur'an 43:16–17)

That last passage is one of the most rhetorically devastating in the Qur'an, and it is worth pausing on, because the devastation is produced without a single argument being made. The idolaters ascribed daughters to God — while considering the birth of a daughter to themselves a catastrophe. The Qur'an points out the contradiction: you grieve at the very thing you attribute to your Lord. What makes the passage so effective is that it requires no premise the audience did not already hold. It does not argue that daughters are valuable; it simply observes that the people being addressed did not think so, and then asks why they attributed to God what they would not accept for themselves.

**The Form of the Words**

Two elements of the Arabic are worth noting. *The Name.* The claim is phrased with *al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate. The sūrah has used this Name thirteen times before this verse and will use it three more times within six verses (vv. 88, 91, 92, 93). The concentration is deliberate: the sūrah is naming the attribute that the claim *violates*. To say that the Compassionate has taken a child is not merely an error about God's nature; it is an offence against the attribute by which the sūrah has been describing Him from its opening line. *The verb.* *Ittakhadha* — "He has taken" — is the Form VIII of *akh-dh*, "to take." Form VIII implies *taking for oneself, adopting, assuming*. The claim is not that God *generated* a child in any biological sense; it is that He *adopted* one — took someone to Himself and made him a son. That is precisely the formulation the Qur'an rejects. Adoption, in human terms, is a legal act by which a person assumes a relationship that was not natural to him. Applied to God, it is incoherent, because it implies a need — the need for a son, an heir, a companion — and God has no needs.

**The Sūrah's Own Pre-Emption**

It is worth noting that the sūrah has already answered this claim in the most sophisticated possible way, and the sophistication is what distinguishes this chapter's treatment from a polemic: by putting the answer in the mouth of the person the claim is about. The infant Jesus said, in the cradle — and the sūrah's strategy is thereby complete before the refutation begins, since the reader has already heard the denial from the one whose status is in dispute:

> *"Truly I am a servant of God. He has given me the Book and made me a prophet."* (Qur'an 19:30)

> *"Peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive!"* (Qur'an 19:33)

A servant is not a son. One who dies is not divine. And both statements were made by Jesus himself, before any community could interpret him otherwise. The sūrah's strategy here is worth admiring, because it is unusual among religious texts. Rather than answering a rival claim about a figure, it has the figure answer it himself, in the cradle, four centuries before the claim arose.

**The Gravity of the Matter**

Before looking at the sūrah's response, it is worth registering how seriously Islam takes this claim, because modern readers often find the intensity surprising. The sūrah's response in vv. 89–92 is the most violent language in the entire chapter — the heavens nearly rent, the earth split, the mountains collapsed. Nothing else in Sūrah Maryam — not murder, not theft, not even the neglect of the prayer — produces this reaction. Why? The tradition's answer is that the doctrine of God is the foundation of everything else. The Qur'an ranks *shirk* as the one unforgivable sin if unrepented:

> *"Verily God does not forgive that a partner be ascribed to Him; but He forgives what is other than that to whom He wills."* (Qur'an 4:48, 116)

And it identifies the specific gravity of *speaking about God without knowledge*, which is the category the Qur'an places this claim in, and which is therefore the category the sūrah will use when it describes the cosmic reaction two verses later. The claim is not treated primarily as a theological error but as an enormity, and the verse explains why:

> *"Say: 'My Lord has only forbidden indecencies, what is apparent of them and what is concealed, and sin, and wrongful insolence, and that you associate with God that for which He has sent down no authority, and that you say about God that which you do not know.'"* (Qur'an 7:33)

The claim quoted in v. 88 is, in the Qur'an's analysis, exactly that: saying about God what one does not know. The analysis is worth carrying, because it relocates the matter decisively. What is at issue is not devotion to Jesus, which the Qur'an honours, and not the sincerity of those who make the claim, which is not impugned. What is at issue is the making of a statement about the nature of God for which no warrant exists — and the Qur'an's position is that there is no graver category of speech than that, because there is no greater subject than the one being spoken about.

**A Necessary Clarification**

One thing must be stated plainly, because it is the point about which Muslim–Christian misunderstanding is greatest. The Qur'an's rejection of the claim is not a rejection of Jesus. Islam honours Jesus as the Messiah, as a messenger, as God's Word conveyed to Mary, as a spirit from Him, as born of a virgin, as a worker of miracles by God's permission, and as one of the five greatest messengers. It commands belief in him, and denial of his prophethood is denial of Islam. What is rejected is a claim about *God*: that He has a son. And the sūrah's argument, as it will develop over the next five verses, is that the claim *diminishes* God rather than honouring Jesus. That is why the sūrah's response is not directed at Jesus at all. It is directed at the conception of God that the claim implies.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:89

> **You have indeed asserted a terrible thing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Immediate Response**

The sūrah's answer begins with a sentence of eleven Arabic words: *la-qad jiʾtum shayʾan iddan* — "You have indeed asserted a terrible thing." The construction is emphatic in three ways at once. The particle *la-* and the particle *qad* together produce the strongest possible affirmation of a past act: *you have certainly, indeed, done this.* And the verb *jiʾtum* — "you have come with," "you have produced" — treats the claim as a thing brought forth into the world, an object now standing in the open.

**The Word Iddan**

The final word is the striking one: *iddan*. The commentators and lexicographers recorded several glosses, and they converge on a single sense: something terrible, dreadful, monstrous, enormous in its gravity. The root is *ʾ-d-d*, and the classical authorities connected it to words for strength, severity, and calamity. What emerges from the classical glosses is that *idd* denotes a thing of such magnitude that it defies the ordinary vocabulary of wrongdoing. It is not "a mistake" or "an error" or even "a sin" in the ordinary sense. It is a *monstrosity*. That is a measure of the sūrah's assessment, and it is worth being honest about it rather than softening it. In the Qur'an's own scale of values, the claim that the Creator of the heavens and the earth has taken a child is not a minor doctrinal difference. It is, in the sūrah's language, a catastrophe.

**Why the Claim Is Grave**

The sūrah's reasoning, which the following verses will unfold, can be assembled from the Qur'an's statements elsewhere, and it is worth assembling because the sūrah states the conclusion here while distributing the premises across the rest of the Book. There are four grounds, and they are cumulative — which is to say that the claim is not wrong for one reason but for four, each of which would be sufficient on its own. *First: it is said about God without knowledge.* The Qur'an's repeated indictment:

> *"Do you say about God that which you do not know?"* (Qur'an 10:68)

To make a claim about the nature of God is to make the highest-order claim there is, and to make it without warrant is the gravest form of irresponsibility. No human being has access to God's nature except by revelation, and the revelation says what it says — which is why the tradition's theologians placed the question of the divine attributes inside revelation rather than alongside it, and why they regarded reasoning about what God must be like as a category error rather than as a pious exercise. *Second: it attributes need to the Self-Sufficient.*

> *"Say: He is God, the One, God the Eternal Refuge (*al-Ṣamad*). He begets not, nor was He begotten, and there is none comparable to Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

A child is had because something is wanted: an heir, a successor, a companion, an expression. God wants nothing. *Third: it attributes to God the attributes of creatures.* Birth, parentage, and kinship are features of created, temporal, composite beings. To attribute them to God is to assimilate the Creator to the creation — which the Qur'an identifies as the fundamental error:

> *"There is nothing whatsoever like unto Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

*Fourth: it is the claim that historically produced idolatry.* The tradition's account — and the Qur'an's own — is that the worship of creatures begins with the elevation of a creature, and that the elevation is almost never immediate. A righteous person is honoured, then venerated, then described in terms that belong to God alone, and then appealed to in ways that belong to God alone; at no point in the sequence does anyone decide to commit idolatry. The Qur'an describes the mechanism in the case of the angels:

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a son.' Glory be to Him! Nay, they are [but] honoured servants."* (Qur'an 21:26)

**The Plural: "You Have Asserted"**

The verb is plural: *jiʾtum* — "**you** have asserted." The sūrah is addressing all three of the groups named in the previous verse — the Jews who said it of Ezra, the Christians who said it of Jesus, and the Arabs who said it of the angels. That is worth noting, because it establishes that the sūrah is not engaged in a polemic against one community. It is rejecting a *type* of claim wherever it appears, and it rejects all three instances with the same sentence.

**The Tone: Shock Rather Than Argument**

Note that the sūrah's first response is not an argument. It is an exclamation. That is deliberate, and it is worth registering because it tells the reader something about how the Qur'an conceives of the matter. The claim is not treated as a position to be refuted by reasoning alone. It is treated as something at which a rightly-ordered person — or creation itself, as v. 90 will show — recoils. The argument comes afterwards (vv. 90–93). What comes first is the register of shock, and the shock is not rhetorical theatre. It is the sūrah's way of communicating the *magnitude* of what is at stake, which a calm syllogism would fail to convey. There is a parallel in ordinary human experience. When a person says something monstrous, the right first response is not "let me explain why that is incorrect." It is silence, or an exclamation — because the response that would be proportionate to the offence cannot be produced by analysis. The sūrah reproduces that sequence exactly: shock first (v. 89), cosmic convulsion second (vv. 90–91), and then the argument (vv. 92–93).

**A Word on Manners**

It should be noted, for the reader engaged in interreligious conversation, that none of this licenses discourtesy. The Qur'an commands Muslims to dispute with the People of the Book *"in the fairest manner"* (29:46), and it instructs them to say: *"Our God and your God is One, and unto Him do we submit"* (29:46). The Prophet ﷺ dealt with Christian delegations with notable hospitality — he received the delegation of Najrān in his mosque and allowed them to pray there according to their rite, a fact recorded in the sīrah. The distinction is between the gravity of an idea and the treatment of the person who holds it. The sūrah is uncompromising about the first. It is silent about the second, which is to say it leaves the person to God.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:90

> **The heavens are well-nigh rent thereat, and the earth split asunder, and the mountains brought down in ruins,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation's Reaction to the Claim**

The sūrah's response now escalates to a cosmic scale: *takādu al-samāwātu yatafaṭṭarna minhu wa-tanshaqqu al-arḍu wa-takhirru al-jibālu haddan* — "The heavens are well-nigh rent thereat, and the earth split asunder, and the mountains brought down in ruins." Three elements of creation, and three modes of destruction: the heavens are nearly *torn apart*, the earth is nearly *split open*, and the mountains *collapse in ruins*.

**The Verb Takādu — "Well-Nigh"**

The verb *takādu* means "they are about to," "they nearly," "they well-nigh." It is one of the Qur'an's *kāda* constructions, which describe an action that is on the point of occurring but does not. That is theologically precise and important. The heavens do *not* actually rend. The earth does *not* actually split. The claim is so enormous that creation is on the verge of convulsing — and it is only God's forbearance, and the fact that He has ordained a term for everything, that holds it back. The Qur'an uses the same construction for the reaction of the heavens to other enormities:

> *"The heavens well-nigh are rent asunder from above them, and the angels glorify the praise of their Lord and ask forgiveness for those on earth. Unquestionably, it is God who is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 42:5)

And for the near-breaking of the sky at the Hour, in verses that use the same verb and the same construction — which is the textual basis for the commentators' observation that the sūrah is describing the reaction of the Last Day rather than a separate event:

> *"And the sky will be rent asunder, for that Day it will be frail."* (Qur'an 69:16)

The pattern establishes a principle, and it is one the sūrah has been illustrating since the palm tree shook at v. 25: **creation responds to what is done in it.** The universe is not inert; it registers. The Qur'an states this in several registers, and the variety of the registers is itself part of the claim, since the same responsiveness is attributed to the inanimate, the animate, and the angelic rather than being confined to one department of creation:

> *"The seven heavens and the earth and all who are in them glorify Him; and there is not a thing but that it glorifies His praise — but you do not understand their glorification."* (Qur'an 17:44)

> *"Then He turned to the heaven when it was smoke, and said to it and to the earth: 'Come, willingly or unwillingly.' They said: 'We come willingly.'"* (Qur'an 41:11)

**The Mountains: The Image of Stability Collapsing**

The third element deserves particular attention, because it is the one that carries the argument's weight. *wa-takhirru al-jibālu haddan* — "and the mountains brought down in ruins." The verb *takhirru* (root *kh-r-r*) means they fall, collapse, tumble down, and the word *haddan* denotes a crashing fall into fragments rather than a gentle subsidence. The mountains are the Qur'an's standing image for stability, firmness, and permanence — the thing a person looks at when he wants to think about something that cannot be moved:

> *"And He has cast upon the earth firm mountains, lest it shake with you..."* (Qur'an 16:15; 31:10; 21:31)

> *"And the mountains — He set them firmly, as a provision for you and for your cattle."* (Qur'an 79:32–33)

So the image is: even the things that cannot be moved are nearly moved. If the mountains would collapse at this claim, the claim is not a small matter — and the sūrah is thereby measuring the claim by its effect rather than by the intentions of the people making it. The Qur'an uses the same imagery for the Day of Resurrection, when the mountains will be destroyed, and the reuse of the image is what allows the reader to calibrate what is being said:

> *"And the earth and the mountains will be lifted and crushed with a single crushing."* (Qur'an 69:14)

> *"And they ask thee concerning the mountains. Say: 'My Lord will blast them into dust, and leave it a level plain.'"* (Qur'an 20:105–6)

The parallel is exact, and it is the sūrah's point: **this claim is of Resurrection-magnitude.** The convulsion described in vv. 90–91 is the convulsion of the Last Day, and the sūrah is saying that the claim in v. 88 is a matter of that order. The observation completes an argument the chapter has been making since v. 35, where it established that God does not beget, and it supplies the answer to any reader who had supposed the Qur'an's insistence on the point to be a matter of sectarian punctiliousness. It is not. On the sūrah's own measurement, it is the heaviest thing a creature can say.

**The Commentators' Reading**

The commentators elaborated on what is being conveyed. Al-Rāzī noted that the point is to establish the enormity of the claim: the claim is so dreadful that the very structure of creation is on the point of rupturing at it, and were it not for God's restraint, it would. Ibn Kathīr, characteristically, cited the transmitted reports about the angels' reaction — that the heavens are disturbed at the claim and the angels are grieved by it. And al-Zamakhsharī, as always, attended to the rhetoric: the extraordinary image is a *tamthīl* — a parabolic representation — of the magnitude of the offence, and it is not to be read as a literal physical event occurring at the moment someone speaks. That last point is important and is the majority position. The sūrah is describing the *weight* of the claim by describing what creation would do if it reacted proportionately. It is the same device as an English speaker saying "the walls nearly came down" — a statement about magnitude, not about architecture.

**Theological Precision: What This Is Not**

Three clarifications are worth making, because the imagery can be misread. *It is not a statement that God is emotionally perturbed.* The tradition is emphatic that God is not subject to passions, that nothing takes Him by surprise, and that His decree is not altered by what creatures say. The imagery describes the *objective gravity* of the claim, not a divine emotion. *It is not a statement that the universe is conscious in the human sense.* The Qur'an does affirm that creation glorifies God (17:44) and that it responds to His command (41:11), but the manner of that response belongs to God's knowledge, not to ours. The verse describes the response; it does not explain the mechanism. *It is not a licence for believers to imitate the convulsion.* The sūrah's immediate response to the claim is shock; the believer's response is to be *argued* (vv. 92–93) and then to *love* (v. 96). Nothing in the passage licenses violence or rage toward those who hold the claim. The sūrah will close, as noted, with the promise of love for those who believe and do good.

**The Place of This Verse in the Sūrah's Climax**

Verses 88–95 form the sūrah's doctrinal climax, and their structure is worth observing, because the passage is built rather than merely assembled. The sūrah has spent eighty-seven verses arriving at this point and will close within three of leaving it, and everything in between is ordered:

- **v. 88:** The claim is quoted.
- **v. 89:** The claim is declared a monstrosity.
- **vv. 90–91:** Creation nearly convulses at it.
- **v. 92:** The claim is declared incompatible with God.
- **v. 93:** Every creature is a servant; none is a son.
- **vv. 94–95:** God has enumerated them all; each will come alone.

The movement is from the claim, to the reaction, to the refutation, to the universal principle, to the individual reckoning. It is one of the most tightly constructed passages in the entire Qur'an, and its tightness is the reason the sūrah can afford to be brief at the point where other sūrahs are expansive. Each stage is doing work that the next stage depends on, and by the time the individual reckoning arrives at v. 95 the reader has been given everything needed to see why it falls as it does.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:91

> **that they ascribe to the Compassionate a child.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Names the Cause**

This short verse completes the sentence begun in v. 90 and identifies the cause of the cosmic convulsion: *an daʿaw li'l-Raḥmāni waladan* — "that they ascribe to the Compassionate a child." The particle *an* is causal: *because*, *on account of the fact that*. The verb *daʿaw* (root *d-ʿ-w*) means they attributed, they claimed, they asserted — the root of *daʿwah*, "a call or claim." So the sūrah states the cause explicitly and in isolation. The heavens are nearly rent, the earth nearly split, the mountains nearly collapsed **because** of this: the ascription of a child to the Compassionate.

**Why This Particular Claim Produces This Reaction**

It is worth asking why. The Qur'an records many terrible human acts — murder, oppression, the neglect of the prayer, the rejection of messengers — and none of them produces this cosmic imagery. The sūrah's answer, which the commentators drew out, is that every other sin is an offence *within* the created order, whereas this one is an offence *against the Creator*. The distinction is not quantitative but categorical. Murder wrongs a creature. Oppression wrongs a creature. Even the rejection of a messenger is a refusal of a message about how to live. But the ascription of a child to God is a claim about who God *is* — and since everything else in religion depends on who God is, an error at this level corrupts everything downstream. That is why the tradition ranked *shirk* as the unforgivable sin and placed the doctrine of God's oneness at the head of the entire religion:

> *"And your God is One God; there is no god but He, the Compassionate, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 2:163)

> *"Say: He is God, the One, God the Eternal Refuge. He begets not, nor was He begotten, and there is none comparable to Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ — the short chapter quoted above — was described by the Prophet ﷺ as equivalent in reward to a third of the Qur'an, precisely because it is a complete statement of the doctrine of God's oneness. The proportion is worth pausing on, since the Qur'an's teachings are conventionally divided into three: creed, law, and the narration of the earlier communities. A chapter that states the creed in four lines is therefore reckoned as a third of the Book — and the sūrah we are reading has, in its own way, been doing the same work across ninety-eight verses:

> *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, it is equal to a third of the Qur'an."* (al-Bukhārī 5013, 5015; Muslim 811)

**The Name, Once More**

The sūrah names *al-Raḥmān* for the fifteenth time, and it will do so twice more within three verses (vv. 92, 93). The concentration is deliberate and is worth stating as a single observation: **the claim of divine sonship is being answered with the Name of mercy fifteen times in eight verses.** The sūrah is not merely rejecting the claim by argument; it is surrounding it with the attribute the claim denies. For the claim implies that God needs something outside Himself — an heir, a son, a completion. And the sūrah's answer, in the repeated Name, is that God is the One whose mercy encompasses everything and who lacks nothing.

**The Precision of the Legal Language**

There is a subtle and important point in the verb *daʿaw* — "they attributed/claimed" — and the subtlety is worth drawing out because the whole force of the refutation rests on it. The Qur'an typically uses this verb for claims made without warrant. The claim is characterised as an *assertion*, not as a report of something known; the word chosen for it is the word for a call, a summons, a declaration made rather than a fact observed. And the Qur'an's standing question to those who make such claims is — and the question is the same every time, which is what makes it a standard rather than a challenge issued to one group:

> *"Have they any share in [God's] knowledge? Then let them produce it."* (Qur'an 52:38)

That is the challenge: produce the warrant. And none can be produced, because there is none — and the sūrah's confidence on the point is not rhetorical but structural. The claim being made is about the Unseen, and the only admissible evidence for a claim about the Unseen is revelation. The revelation has been consulted, and it says the opposite of what is claimed. There is therefore no further court of appeal, and the sūrah does not pretend that there is.

**The Verse's Function as a Punctuation Mark**

Structurally, v. 91 does something unusual: it isolates the cause in a verse of its own, after the effect has been described in v. 90. The effect is rhetorical and considerable. The reader is told first that the universe nearly convulsed, and only then what convulsed it. The delay creates the weight. It is the difference between saying "the mountains collapsed because of X" and saying "the mountains collapsed — because of X." The sūrah is handing the reader the enormity before the explanation, and the technique is the same one it used at vv. 89–90: magnitude first, grounds second.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:92

> **And it is not fitting for the Compassionate that He should take a child.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refutation, Stated Positively**

Having conveyed the magnitude of the claim, the sūrah now states the refutation: *wa-mā yanbaghī li'l-Raḥmāni an yattakhidha waladan* — "And it is not fitting for the Compassionate that He should take a child." This is the same argument the sūrah made at v. 35 (*"It is not for God to take a child"*), restated here in a slightly different construction and with the mercy-Name. The repetition across the sūrah is deliberate: the doctrine is stated at the opening of the sūrah's argument and again at its climax, bracketing everything in between.

**The Verb Yanbaghī**

The word *yanbaghī* (root *b-gh-y*) means "it is fitting," "it is proper," "it behoves." The construction *mā yanbaghī li-* — "it is not fitting for..." — expresses *incompatibility with the nature of the subject*. That is the same construction, and the same point, discussed at v. 35: this is not a statement about God's power. It is a statement that the thing in question contradicts what God is.

**An Extraordinary Root-Echo**
