There is a root connection in this verse that the sūrah has been building since v. 20, and it is one of the most subtle pieces of wordplay in the entire chapter — subtle enough that a reader who does not track the Arabic will miss the argument entirely. The root *b-gh-y* has appeared three times already, in its sexual-moral sense:

- **v. 20:** Mary said: *"nor have I been unchaste (*baghiyyā*)."*
- **v. 28:** Her people said: *"nor was thy mother unchaste (*baghiyyā*)."*

In both of those verses, *baghiyy* denoted one who *transgresses the rightful boundary in seeking* — the fornicatress, the one whose appetite overruns its limit. Now, at **v. 92**, the same root appears in the verb *yanbaghī* — "it is fitting" — the sense being: this does not *belong within the proper bounds* of what is appropriate for God. The connection is not merely phonetic. The root's core meaning — *to seek, to desire, to overstep* — underlies both senses. The *baghiyy* is one whose seeking oversteps its proper limit. And to say that something *does not befit* God is to say it lies outside His proper bound. So the sūrah has used the same root for the slander against Mary (twice) and for the refutation of the claim about God (once) — and in both cases the underlying idea is *the overstepping of a boundary*. The sūrah's theology and its narrative are bound together at the level of the root.

**The Two Halves of the Refutation**

The verse's argument is complete in its two halves, though the second half is compressed. *The first half — "it is not fitting for the Compassionate"* — establishes incompatibility with the divine nature. God is Self-Sufficient, Eternal, without need, without composition, without peer. Taking a child implies the opposite of all of these, and therefore cannot be attributed to Him. *The second half — "that He should take a child"* — specifies the act. As noted at v. 88, the verb *ittakhadha* means "to adopt," "to take to oneself." And adoption is precisely the claim that the Qur'an rejects, because adoption is an act of acquiring what one did not have. The argument as a whole is therefore: The argument runs in three steps, and it is again a matter of definition rather than of power. First, to take a child is to acquire what one lacked — a son is taken for help, for continuation, for companionship, and every one of those is a want being filled. Second, God lacks nothing. Third, therefore the taking of a child is incompatible with God, not because He is unable but because He is not needy. The sūrah's refutations throughout have taken this form: the incompatibility is located in the concept rather than in the capacity, which is why no demonstration of power could ever settle the question either way.

**The Qur'an's Parallel Statements**

The same refutation appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in forms that add detail, and the recurrence across sūrahs is the reader's indication that he is being given a standing argument rather than a local one. What differs between the occurrences is only what each passage chooses to emphasise:

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a son.' Glory be to Him! Nay, they are [but] honoured servants."* (Qur'an 21:26)

> *"Say: If the Compassionate had a son, then I would be the first of worshippers. Glory be to God, Lord of the heavens and the earth, Lord of the Throne, above what they describe!"* (Qur'an 43:81–82)

> *"God has not taken any son, nor is there any god with Him; [if there were], then each god would have taken away what he created, and some of them would have risen over others. Glory be to God, above what they describe!"* (Qur'an 23:91)

That last verse contains one of the Qur'an's most elegant arguments, and it is worth stating in full because its method is unusual: it argues from the observed state of the world back to the number of its governors. If there were multiple gods, each would assert independence over his own creation, and the result would be conflict — rival wills producing a contested universe. Since the universe is not in conflict but in order, there is one God. The argument is remarkable for proceeding from a fact available to anyone looking up, rather than from a premise that must first be accepted on authority.

**Theological Boundaries, Restated**

As at v. 35, it is important to state what this verse does not mean. *It does not mean God lacks power.* The tradition's formulation is that God's omnipotence extends to everything that is possible, and the claim about a son is not in that category. *It does not mean God cannot love or be relational.* Islam affirms that God loves and is loved, that He is near, that He answers, and that He will grant *wudd* — love — to the believers (v. 96). What it denies is that His relationship to a creature is the relationship of parentage. *It does not demean Jesus.* The sūrah has already established Jesus' honour at length. The refutation is about God, and the sūrah's own next verse will place Jesus among the servants, which is his honour rather than his diminution.

**Why "The Compassionate" and Not Another Name**

The sūrah has used *al-Raḥmān* sixteen times. Here, the choice carries a specific point. A child is, in human experience, the object of the most intense parental love and care. To say that God has a child is therefore, at some level, to say that God's love is *particular* — directed toward one being in a way it is not directed toward others. The sūrah's answer is that God's mercy is not particular but universal: *al-Raḥmān* is the One whose mercy encompasses everything.

> *"My mercy encompasses all things."* (Qur'an 7:156)

And the sūrah will close by stating how that universal mercy becomes particular — and the phrasing matters, because it answers the longing that produces the claim rather than only refuting the claim itself. Not by parentage but by *love granted*:

> *"Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds — the Compassionate will grant them love."* (Qur'an 19:96)

That is the sūrah's answer to the deepest human longing behind the claim, and it is the reason the chapter does not end on refutation. The claim that God has a child is, in part, a claim that God is capable of a special love — that there is someone to whom He is related in a way He is not related to the rest of creation. The sūrah answers: He is — and He grants it to those who believe and do good, without taking anyone as a son. What is being offered is therefore not a subtraction but a relocation. The nearness the claim was reaching for is real, and it is available, and it is available on terms that do not require anyone to be divine.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:93

> **There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the Compassionate as a servant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universal Principle**

The sūrah's refutation now reaches its foundation: *in kullu man fī al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi illā ātī al-Raḥmāni ʿabdan* — "There is none in the heavens and on the earth, but that it comes unto the Compassionate as a servant." The construction *in... illā* is the strongest form of universal negation in Arabic: *there is not anyone... except that...* — which here means: every single one, without exception. The scope is exhaustive: *man fī al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "whoever is in the heavens and on the earth." Angels, jinn, human beings, messengers, prophets, the Messiah himself — all of them. And what is predicated of all of them is *ʿabdan* — "as a servant."

**The Sūrah's Final Word on Jesus**

This verse is, in effect, the sūrah's last word on the figure with whom it has been so concerned, and it is worth stating what it does and does not do. It does not name Jesus. It does not need to. The sūrah has spent twenty verses establishing his story, and it has stated the principle in universal form so that no exception can be carved out for him — or for anyone else. And the word it uses is *ʿabd* — the word Jesus applied to himself in the cradle (v. 30), the word the sūrah used of Zachariah (v. 2), and the word it will use of the creatures it enumerates in v. 95. The logic is airtight and is worth stating: The argument is a syllogism and it runs in three steps. First, everyone in the heavens and the earth is a servant of God. Second, Jesus was on the earth. Third, therefore Jesus is a servant of God. Nothing in the argument mentions his conception, his miracles, or his return; the sūrah does not need any of it, because the conclusion follows from a universal premise that includes him before he is named. That is the sūrah's argument, and its elegance lies in the fact that it does not need to be made. It is entailed by a universal statement that no one would think to dispute — which is precisely why the sūrah states the universal and stops. A reader who accepts the premise has already accepted the conclusion, and a reader who wishes to deny the conclusion must deny a premise that applies to the angels as well, to every human being, and to himself. The sūrah has thereby placed the burden of proof exactly where it belongs.

**The Word ʿAbd — The Highest Station**

As established at v. 2 and v. 30, *ʿabd* is the highest title the Qur'an confers, and the evidence for that claim is the company the word keeps. It is used of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ at the Ascension (17:1) and at the descent of revelation (53:10) — at the two moments of his greatest nearness to God, which is to say that the Qur'an's vocabulary for maximum proximity is the vocabulary of servanthood rather than any other. It is used of the angels:

> *"And they say, 'The Compassionate has taken a son.' Glory be to Him! Nay, they are [but] honoured servants. They do not precede Him in speech, and they act by His Command."* (Qur'an 21:26–27)

And it is used to refute the claim about the Messiah specifically, which is the most pointed of all the occurrences, since the word is deployed in the very passage where his status is being debated:

> *"The Messiah would never disdain to be a servant of God, nor would the angels brought near."* (Qur'an 4:172)

That verse is the direct parallel to ours and makes the same argument: servanthood is not a degradation from which the Messiah must be rescued. It is the station he occupies, and it is the station of the highest angels. The argument is worth carrying in full, because it closes the last gap the claim could occupy. If servanthood were a low station, then the angels — whom no one proposes to promote — would be lower than they are, and the Messiah would need rescuing from a position that the noblest creatures hold without complaint. The sūrah's premise is that no such rescue is needed, and none is available.

**"It Comes Unto the Compassionate"**

The verb *ātī* — "coming" — is a participle denoting one who comes, arrives, and is present. The phrase *ātī al-Raḥmāni* — "coming to the Compassionate" — establishes the direction of the relationship. Every creature *comes to* God. Nobody is God. And the coming is in the mode of servanthood, which means it is a coming of one who belongs to the One he comes to. The sūrah will repeat this verb in v. 95: *wa-kulluhum ātīhi yawma al-qiyāmati fardan* — "and every one of them will come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone."

**The Name, the Sixteenth Time**

The sūrah names *al-Raḥmān* here for the sixteenth time. It will not use the Name again. That is worth noting as a structural fact: the sūrah's final use of its signature Name is in the verse that establishes universal servanthood. The mercy-Name and the servant-word are placed together in the same sentence, which is the sūrah's complete theology in one line: **the Compassionate is the Lord; everyone else is a servant; and the relationship between them is mercy.**

**Anthropological Content: What It Means to Be a Servant**

For the reader, this verse carries the practical weight of the sūrah's entire theology, and it is worth spelling out. To be a *ʿabd* is: *To belong to another.* Not partially, not in some departments of life, but entirely. A servant has no jurisdiction over himself. *To be accountable.* A servant answers for what he was given. *To have no claim.* A servant does not negotiate terms or demand outcomes. He receives. *And yet to be honoured beyond measure.* Because the One to whom he belongs is the Compassionate, and because servanthood is, as the Qur'an uses it, the title God gives to those He most loves. That last point is the one most often missed, and it is the key to the sūrah's whole argument. The claim that God took a child is, at bottom, an attempt to *raise* a human being above servanthood. And the sūrah's answer is that servanthood is not a ceiling to be broken through. It is the highest place there is for a creature, and any attempt to go higher is a fall.

**The Verse's Function at the Sūrah's Climax**

Verse 93 completes the refutation begun at v. 88, and the completion is worth marking because the sūrah has taken six verses to say something it could have said in one. The structure is now complete:

- **v. 88:** The claim.
- **v. 89:** It is a monstrosity.
- **vv. 90–91:** Creation nearly convulses at it.
- **v. 92:** It is incompatible with God.
- **v. 93:** Everyone is a servant; no one is a son.

And the next two verses will draw the consequence, and the sequence from doctrine to accounting is the sūrah's standard movement: since everyone is a servant, everyone is enumerated, and everyone will come alone to answer for himself. The logic is a single chain, and each link is load-bearing. Servanthood establishes that the person is subject to judgement; enumeration establishes that the judgement will be exact; and coming alone establishes that nothing will be available to him at the point of answering except what he himself produced.

---
## Sūrah Maryam 19:94

> **And He has indeed enumerated them, and counted them a number [counting].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Certainty of the Record**

Having established that every creature is a servant (v. 93), the sūrah states what follows from it: *la-qad aḥṣāhum wa-ʿaddahum ʿaddan* — "And He has indeed enumerated them, and counted them a number [counting]." Two verbs, both emphatic, both expressing exact enumeration. *Aḥṣāhum* (root *ḥ-ṣ-y*) means He has enumerated them, encompassed them in a count, kept account of them. The root conveys the sense of *reckoning thoroughly* — not estimating but tallying to the last item. The Qur'an uses it consistently for God's exhaustive knowledge:

> *"And He has enumerated everything in number."* (Qur'an 72:28)

> *"And We have encompassed all things in a Book."* (Qur'an 78:29)

> *"And every thing We have enumerated in a clear register."* (Qur'an 36:12)

*Wa-ʿaddahum ʿaddan* — "and counted them a counting" — is the cognate accusative construction, the standard Arabic idiom for intensifying a verb: He counted them *with a thorough counting*. The construction is worth explaining, because a translation that renders it simply as "He counted them" loses the emphasis the Arabic is carrying. The absolute object strips the verb of any indeterminacy about extent or completeness — the counting is done, done exhaustively, and done in a way that leaves nothing uncounted.

**The Sūrah's Own Echo**

The phrase *ʿaddahum ʿaddan* is a deliberate echo of v. 84: *innamā naʿuddu lahum ʿaddan* — "We are but numbering for them a number." The sūrah has thus used the same root and the same construction twice, ten verses apart, and the two uses are related but distinct — the relation being that the first describes a process still running and the second a process completed:

- **v. 84:** God counts *out the remaining term* of those who reject. (The deadline approaches.)
- **v. 94:** God has *enumerated* all of creation exactly. (The record is complete.)

The first is a warning to the persecutors; the second is a statement about the reckoning. Together they establish that the judgement will be neither late (v. 84) nor inexact (v. 94), and the pairing removes the two comforts that delay reliably supplies. A person who is told that judgement is coming but not yet consoles himself with the interval; a person who is told that it will be thorough but is some way off consoles himself in the same place. The sūrah removes both: the interval is itself being counted, and the counting when it concludes will be complete.

**Why Enumeration Matters**

There is a specific comfort and a specific terror in this verse, and both are intended. *The comfort* is for those whose lives have been small, hidden, or unremarked. Nothing has been missed. Every act of obedience, every patience, every quiet refusal to do what was wrong — all of it has been counted by Someone who was paying attention. The Qur'an states this in terms that have consoled believers in every generation:

> *"So no soul knows what comfort is kept hidden for them as a recompense for that which they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:17)

*The terror* is for those who assumed they were unobserved, and that is the whole of it — the doctrine is not terrifying to a person who has lived as though he were seen. The same counting that preserves the good deed retains the boast, the oppression, and the word spoken in private. The sūrah said as much at v. 79: *"We shall record what he says."* The tradition holds both together and refuses to let either be softened, because softening either destroys the other: a record that retained only the good would not be a record, and one that retained only the bad would not be just. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"O My servants, these are your deeds; I record them for you and then recompense you for them. Whoever finds good, let him praise God; and whoever finds otherwise, let him blame no one but himself."* (Muslim 2577)

**Theological Precision: Knowledge and Reckoning**

As at v. 70, a clarification is necessary. God does not *acquire* the number by counting. His knowledge is eternal and un-mediated; the idiom of counting is used for the hearer's benefit, to convey that the reckoning will be exhaustive and exact rather than approximate. The point was made by the classical authorities in connection with v. 84, and it applies here with greater force: the *counting* language tells the reader what the judgement will be like — itemised, complete, and without omission — not what God's knowledge is like.

**The Verse's Place in the Closing Argument**

Verses 93–95 form a tightly bound unit, and their logic runs in a single direction — each verse supplying the premise the next one needs, and the whole sequence arriving somewhere the reader cannot evade: The three verses state, in order, that everyone is a servant — so no one is a son; that every servant has been enumerated — so none is overlooked; and that every one of them will come alone — so none is accompanied. The sequence is not three descriptions of the same thing but three successive closures of three different exits. The three verses answer, in order, the three evasions available to the person who would prefer not to be judged: *I am not subject to this* (answered: you are a servant); *I will not be noticed* (answered: you have been counted); *I will not be alone* (answered: you will come alone). The thoroughness is what makes the passage so difficult to sit with, and it is worth noticing that the evasions are not malicious. They are the ordinary, quiet assumptions a person makes about himself — that he is an exception, that he is too small to be tracked, and that he will have company when it matters. The sūrah closes all three in three verses.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:95

> **And every one of them will come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Second "Alone"**

The sūrah repeats, in universal form, what it said of the individual boaster at v. 80: *wa-kulluhum ātīhi yawma al-qiyāmati fardan* — "And every one of them will come unto Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone." At v. 80, the word *fardan* was used of the man who claimed he would be given wealth and children: *"and he shall come unto Us alone."* Here it is applied to everyone. The movement from the particular to the universal is significant. The sūrah has taken a feature of one man's catastrophe and revealed it as the general condition. Every human being arrives alone — the boaster, the prophet, the angel, the Messiah, and every person who has lived.

**What "Alone" Means**

The commentators were precise about what the word removes and what it leaves, and the precision matters because the verse is easily read as a general statement about dying when it is in fact a specific statement about what does and does not cross. *What it removes:* wealth, children, tribe, retinue, power, reputation, intercessors who are not authorised, and the gods one took beside God. The list covers everything a person uses to establish who he is, which is the point — what is removed is not merely his possessions but his standing. The Qur'an states the removal in the fullest detail:

> *"And you have certainly come to Us alone, as We created you the first time, and you have left behind you all that We bestowed upon you. And We do not see with you your intercessors, those whom you claimed were partners among you. It has been severed between you, and what you used to claim has forsaken you."* (Qur'an 6:94)

*What it leaves:* the person, his deeds, his faith, and his record. Nothing else crosses. The shortness of the second list against the length of the first is the verse's argument, and the tradition has never attempted to lengthen it. What a person carries is what he made of himself and what God has recorded of him, and everything that appeared to constitute his life — the things by which others identified him and by which he identified himself — remains on the near side of the crossing.

**The Sūrah's Answer to the Argument From Company**

This verse is also the sūrah's final answer to v. 73. The disbelievers asked: *"Which of the two groups is better in station or fairer in company?"* They were arguing from social standing — from *maqām* and *nadiyy*. The sūrah answered at v. 75 that the comparison would be re-run on a Day when the result inverts, and it has spent the intervening verses describing that Day. Now it delivers the final term of the inversion: on that Day, no one has company at all. The *nadiyy* — the gathering of distinguished men, the club whose approval the Quraysh treated as the measure of worth — does not exist. There is no group whose standing can be invoked, because there are no groups. There is one person, one Lord, and one record. That is a devastating and complete answer to the argument from prestige, and it is worth stating in its simplest form: **the thing you were using as your standard will not be present when the reckoning occurs.**

**The Day Is Named**

Note that the sūrah names it here: *yawm al-qiyāmah* — "the Day of Resurrection," literally "the Day of Standing." The word *qiyāmah* (root *q-w-m*) denotes the standing, the rising up. It is the day on which everyone stands before God, and the name itself carries the condition described in this verse: standing, alone, before the One to whom he comes. The sūrah opened with a man standing in the sanctuary calling on his Lord in secret (v. 3). It closes with all of humanity standing before that same Lord, no longer in secret and no longer accompanied.

**The Pastoral Dimension**

There is, as so often in the Qur'an's eschatology, a double use. *For the arrogant,* the verse is a warning: everything you have accumulated as a buffer between yourself and your Lord will be stripped away, and you will be exactly as vulnerable as the poorest person who ever lived. *For the humble,* the verse is a promise: the things that made you feel insignificant will also be stripped away. The person who was poor, unknown, weak, or despised in this world arrives in the same condition as the person who was wealthy, famous, powerful, and honoured — which is to say, alone. And the sūrah's next verse states what then happens. That levelling is one of the most radical teachings in the Qur'an and one of the reasons the message was unwelcome to the Quraysh, whose entire social order rested on the assumption that rank was real. The Prophet ﷺ stated it in terms that left no room for the old hierarchies:

> *"O people! Verily your Lord is One, and your father is one. There is no superiority of an Arab over a non-Arab, nor of a non-Arab over an Arab, nor of a white person over a black person, nor of a black person over a white person — except by reverent awareness of God."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad*; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Sūrah's Final Use of "Servant"**

It is worth noting the pronouns in this verse and the previous one. V. 93 says everyone comes to the Compassionate *as a servant*. V. 95 says everyone comes to *Him* — *ātīhi*, with the pronominal suffix referring to God. So the sūrah's closing anthropology is complete: the creature comes to God, as a servant, alone. That is the human condition, stated without mitigation. And it is precisely because that is the human condition that the next verse is such extraordinary good news.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:96

> **Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds—the Compassionate will grant them love.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Final Promise**

After ninety-five verses of warning, judgement, and cosmic convulsion, the sūrah delivers its closing promise, and it is one of the most beautiful sentences in the Qur'an: *inna alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti sa-yajʿalu lahumu al-Raḥmānu wuddan* — "Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds—the Compassionate will grant them love." The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabrisī, understood this verse to be the sūrah's answer to the isolation of v. 95. Everyone comes alone — and yet the believer is not left alone, because the Compassionate grants him love.

**The Verb: Sa-Yajʿalu**

The verb is *sayajʿalu* — "He will make," "He will appoint," "He will grant" — in the future tense, with the emphatic prefix *sa-*. The choice of *jaʿala* rather than *aʿṭā* ("He will give") is significant. *Jaʿala* means to make something to be the case, to establish it, to bring it about. So the promise is not that God will hand the believer a quantity of love; it is that God will *cause love to exist* for him — will bring it about that he is loved. The commentators drew out the consequence: God will place love for the believer in the hearts of others — in the hearts of the angels, of the people of the heavens and the earth, and of the believers among mankind.

**The Word Wudd**

*Wudd* (root *w-d-d*) means love, affection, and the active desire for another's good. It is a warmer and more intimate word than *ḥubb* in some of its usages, and the lexicographers associated it with the wish to be near the beloved. The root appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in the cognate *mawaddah* — affection, particularly the affection God places between spouses:

> *"And among His signs is that He created for you spouses from among yourselves, that you may find tranquillity in them; and He placed between you affection (*mawaddah*) and mercy."* (Qur'an 30:21)

And in the warning against taking God's enemies as friends, where the word denotes the affection that should be reserved for God and His cause — which is the occurrence that establishes the word's full weight, since it is used there for the strongest form of attachment a person has and is demanded for God alone:

> *"You will not find a people who believe in God and the Last Day having affection (*yuwāddūna*) for those who oppose God and His Messenger..."* (Qur'an 58:22)

So the word carries the sense of a love that *desires nearness* — which is exactly what is being promised, and exactly what the sūrah's whole argument has been moving toward. The believer will be loved, and the love will be a drawing-near. What is being described is not approval and not reward but proximity: the thing the claim of sonship had been reaching for, granted without the claim. The sūrah's closing movement therefore does not simply refute the doctrine of divine parentage; it supplies the longing that produced it and satisfies it on other terms.

**The Ḥadīth That Fulfils the Promise**

The most famous commentary on this verse is a ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim, and it is one of the most-quoted texts in the entire corpus — quoted not because it is unusual but because it states in a sentence what the verse promises and what the promise costs:

> *"When God loves a servant, He calls Gabriel and says: 'Verily I love so-and-so, so love him.' So Gabriel loves him, and then he calls out to the inhabitants of the heavens: 'God loves so-and-so, so love him.' So the inhabitants of the heavens love him, and then acceptance is placed for him on the earth. And when God hates a servant, He calls Gabriel and says: 'Verily I hate so-and-so, so hate him'..."* (al-Bukhārī 3209, 6040; Muslim 2637)

That ḥadīth is the mechanism by which the promise of this verse is fulfilled, and it explains the commentators' reading: the love is first God's, then the angels', and then it is *placed on the earth* — in the hearts of people. The practical consequence is one every believer can verify. A person who is genuinely close to God is, as a rule, loved by the people around him in a way that is disproportionate to his charm or his usefulness. That is not a technique; it is a consequence, and it is what the verse promises.

**The Two Conditions, Once More**

The promise is conditioned on two things, and the pairing is the same one the sūrah has used throughout: *Āmanū* — "they believed." Faith: the heart's assent, the tongue's affirmation, and — in the Sunni formulation — the limbs' action. *Wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — "and did righteous deeds." Works: the outward expression and proof of the faith. As at v. 60 and v. 76, the two are inseparable. The sūrah has never permitted either to stand alone, and it does not do so at its climax.

**The Sūrah's Masterstroke: Mercy Named at the End**

It is worth noting, once more, the Name. *Al-Raḥmān* is used here for the sixteenth and final time in the sūrah. The sūrah opened with *"a reminder of the Mercy of thy Lord"* (v. 2) and closes its argument with *"the Compassionate will grant them love"* (v. 96). Between those two verses, the sūrah has narrated the mercies — a son given to an old man, a virgin delivered, water in the desert, a speaking infant, prophets raised up, a Garden promised — and it has warned of the consequences of refusing them. And the final note is love. That is the sūrah's answer to the claim it has just refuted. The claim that God has a child is, at some level, a claim that God is capable of particular love. The sūrah's answer has been: He is capable of it, and He grants it — not by taking a son but by *granting wudd* to everyone who believes and does good. That is a more generous doctrine than the one it replaces. Sonship would make the love available to one person. The sūrah's promise makes it available to everyone.

**The Practical Use of This Verse**

For the reader, this verse supplies both a test and a hope. *The hope* is direct: the way to be loved is to believe and to do good. Not to be admired, not to cultivate an image, not to be useful or entertaining — but to believe and do good, and to let God place the love. *The test* is subtler and is worth stating. The Qur'an elsewhere warns that being loved by people is not by itself evidence of anything:

> *"And they say: 'We are more abundant in wealth and children, and we are not going to be punished.'"* (Qur'an 34:35)

> *"Say: 'Shall We inform you of the greatest losers in respect of [their] deeds? Those whose effort is lost in the life of this world, while they think that they are doing well in work.'"* (Qur'an 18:103–4)

So the verse is not a promise of popularity, and the tradition has been careful to distinguish the two. The love promised here is the love that follows upon faith and good works — and it is recognisable by being *unearned*, by arriving from sources that had no reason to give it. The Prophet ﷺ spoke of the love that exists between believers for God's sake, and of its reward:

> *"A man set out to visit a brother of his in another town, and God sent an angel to intercept him on the road. The angel said: 'Where are you going?' He said: 'To a brother of mine in this town.' The angel said: 'Have you any favour you are repaying him?' He said: 'No — I love him for the sake of God.' The angel said: 'Then I am a messenger of God to you [to tell you] that God loves you as you loved him for His sake.'"* (Muslim 2567)

**The Sūrah's Structure, Completed**

With this verse, the sūrah's long arc is visible in full, and it is worth taking it in at once, because the chapter's movements are easier to feel than to summarise and a reader who has been following verse by verse will not have seen the shape:

- **v. 2:** A reminder of the Mercy of the Lord.
- **vv. 2–15:** Zachariah's cry, and John given.
- **vv. 16–40:** Mary, the annunciation, the birth, the cradle, and the doctrine established.
- **vv. 41–57:** Abraham, Moses, Ishmael, Idrīs — the gifts given to those who withdraw.
- **vv. 58–63:** The weeping of the prophets, the decline of the later generations, and the Garden promised.
- **vv. 64–87:** The denial of the Resurrection, the Judgement, the intercession.
- **vv. 88–95:** The refutation of the claim of divine sonship.
- **v. 96:** Love.

The sūrah began in mercy and ends in love, and everything in between — the terror, the judgement, the refutation — sits inside that frame. The observation is the key to reading the chapter correctly, because the middle of the sūrah is severe enough that a reader who stops there will draw conclusions the sūrah does not support. The severity is real, and it is enclosed: the first verse names the mercy of the Lord, and the closing movement promises His love, and the purpose of everything between those two is to make the reader understand what he is being offered and what he must not do with it.

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:97

> **So We have made it easy in thy tongue, that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Purpose Statement**

The sūrah now states why the Qur'an was sent in this form: *fa-innamā yassarnāhu bi-lisānika li-tubashshira bihi al-muttaqīna wa-tundhira bihi qawman luddā* — "So We have made it easy in thy tongue, that thou mayest give glad tidings unto the reverent thereby, and that thereby thou mayest warn a contentious people." The verse is the sūrah's statement of purpose, and it is placed at the threshold of its closing line as the explanation of everything that has preceded.

**"We Have Made It Easy"**

The verb *yassarnā* (root *y-s-r*) means We have made easy, facilitated, made accessible. The root's primary sense is of ease and facility — the opposite of *ʿusr*, difficulty — and the same root supplies the word for the left hand and for prosperity, both of which carry the sense of what comes without strain. The Qur'an uses this verb of the Qur'an itself in several places, and the recurrence establishes a doctrine rather than a description: the ease of the revelation is a property God has placed in it rather than a quality it happens to have.

> *"And We have indeed made the Qur'an easy for remembrance, so is there any who will remember?"* (Qur'an 54:17, 22, 32, 40)

> *"So We have made it easy in thy tongue, that they may take heed."* (Qur'an 44:58)

What does the ease consist in? The commentators identified several dimensions, and they are complementary rather than competing — the chapter has been made accessible in more than one sense at once, and the sūrah is inviting the reader to notice all of them. *Ease of language.* It was sent in clear Arabic, in the tongue of the people it first addressed:

> *"Verily We have made it an Arabic Qur'an, that you may understand."* (Qur'an 43:3)

> *"And We have not sent any messenger save with the tongue of his people, that he might make clear to them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

*Ease of memorisation.* The Qur'an's rhythmic and phonetic structure makes it uniquely memorable, and the tradition has treated this as a specific divine facilitation rather than as an accident of style. The phenomenon is observable: a book of some six hundred pages is held verbatim in the memories of millions of people, many of them children, in languages they do not speak — and the sūrah this verse belongs to is among the most frequently memorised of them all:

> *"And We shall make you recite, so you will not forget, except what God wills."* (Qur'an 87:6–7)

*Ease of comprehension.* The Qur'an's guidance is not encoded; its moral and doctrinal demands are stated plainly, and the Prophet ﷺ was sent to explain what needed explaining. The pairing is important, because the claim of clarity is sometimes taken to mean that no interpretive tradition is required — whereas the Qur'an's own account is that the Book is clear and that a messenger was sent alongside it to expound it. Clarity and exposition are not alternatives:

> *"And We have sent down to thee the Reminder, that thou mayest make clear to mankind what has been sent down to them, and that they may reflect."* (Qur'an 16:44)

**"In Thy Tongue"**

The phrase *bi-lisānika* — "in thy tongue," "by means of thy tongue" — has been read two ways, and both are held by the tradition. *The first reading* is that it means the Qur'an was revealed in the Arabic of the Prophet's own people — his tongue in the sense of his language. This is the reading most commentators preferred, and it fits the Qur'an's repeated emphasis on the clarity of its Arabic (12:2; 16:103; 20:113; 26:195; 39:28; 41:3; 42:7; 43:3; 46:12). *The second reading* is that it refers to the Prophet's own recitation — that God made the Qur'an flow easily from his tongue, so that he could recite it readily to his people. This reading is supported by the Qur'an's own instruction to the Prophet ﷺ about the reception of the revelation:

> *"Move not thy tongue with it, to hasten with it. Verily upon Us is its collection and its recitation. So when We have recited it, then follow its recitation. Then verily upon Us is its clarification."* (Qur'an 75:16–19)

Both readings are correct, and they belong together: the Qur'an was made easy in the Prophet's language and easy upon the Prophet's tongue, so that it would reach his people intact and be preserved in recitation rather than only in writing. The consequence of that preservation is without parallel in the history of texts — a scripture held in the memories of its community as well as in its manuscripts, with the two witnesses checking each other across fourteen centuries. The sūrah's claim is that this too is part of what was made easy.

**"That Thou Mayest Give Glad Tidings... And Warn"**

The verse states the two functions of the revelation, and the pairing is invariable in the Qur'an — which is to say that the Book nowhere offers comfort without also announcing something, and nowhere warns without also offering something. The two functions are never separated, and a reader who takes one and leaves the other has taken half of what was sent:

> *"O Prophet! Verily We have sent thee as a witness, and a bringer of glad tidings, and a warner, and a summoner unto God by His leave, and an illuminating lamp."* (Qur'an 33:45–46)

> *"Verily We have sent thee with the truth, as a bringer of glad tidings and a warner; and there is no community but that a warner has passed among it."* (Qur'an 35:24)

The sūrah has practised exactly this pairing throughout. It opened with the glad tidings of John (v. 7) and the promise of the Garden (vv. 60–63) and closed with the promise of love (v. 96). And it warned of the Day of Regret (v. 39), the Fire (vv. 68–72, 86), and the fate of the destroyed generations (vv. 74, 98). Neither half may be dropped. Glad tidings without warning produce complacency; warning without glad tidings produces despair. The tradition's term for the balanced message is *al-targhīb wa'l-tarhīb* — encouragement and intimidation — and the scholars of *daʿwah* have always insisted that the two be held together.

**"The Reverent" and "A Contentious People"**

The verse names the two audiences, and the sūrah has been addressing both. *Al-muttaqīn* — "the reverent" — are the recipients of the glad tidings. The sūrah has used this word as its criterion throughout (vv. 13, 18, 63, 72, 85), and it is fitting that the sūrah's purpose statement names them as the ones the good news is for. *Qawman luddā* — "a contentious people" — are the recipients of the warning. The word *ludd* (root *l-d-d*) is an intensive plural denoting those who are vehement, stubborn, and quarrelsome in opposition. It is the word used elsewhere for those who dispute about God without knowledge:

> *"And of mankind is he who disputes concerning God without knowledge, and follows every rebellious satan."* (Qur'an 22:3)

> *"And they dispute concerning God, and He is Mighty in Power."* (Qur'an 13:13)

The commentators identified the *qawm ludd* here as the Quraysh (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī) — the people who were disputing the Prophet ﷺ, demanding signs, and rejecting what had already been made clear to them. The word *ludd* is intensive, describing a people given to contention rather than one that has raised a difficulty, and the sūrah's use of it is what identifies the obstacle with precision. The problem was never that the message was unclear. It was that clarity was being met with argument, and a person who is arguing will not be helped by further signs:

**A Final Note on the Word "Made Easy"**

There is a pastoral point in this closing statement that deserves to be drawn out, because it is easy to miss. The sūrah has just described the most terrifying things in existence — the rending of the heavens, the collapse of the mountains, the Fire, the reckoning, the arrival of every soul alone. And it closes by saying that the book containing all of this has been *made easy*. Ease, here, does not mean that the content is comfortable. It means that the path is clear. The person reading this sūrah is not being asked to solve a puzzle; he is being told what is true and what to do about it, in language he can understand and remember. The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in the most frequently cited ḥadīth of his ministry:

> *"Make things easy and do not make them difficult; give glad tidings and do not repel."* (al-Bukhārī 69; Muslim 1734)

And the Qur'an states the divine intent in the same terms, which closes the matter: the revelation was made easy for a purpose, and that purpose is named rather than assumed:

> *"God desires ease for you, and He does not desire hardship for you."* (Qur'an 2:185)

---

## Sūrah Maryam 19:98

> **And how many a generation We destroyed before them! Dost thou perceive a single one of them, or hear from them a whisper?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Closing Note**

The sūrah ends where so much of the Qur'an ends — with the silence of the destroyed: *wa-kam ahlaknā qablahum min qarnin hal tuḥissu minhum min aḥadin aw tasmaʿu lahum rikzan* — "And how many a generation We destroyed before them! Dost thou perceive a single one of them, or hear from them a whisper?" The final word of Sūrah Maryam is *rikzan* — the faintest conceivable sound.

**The Word Rikz**

*Rikz* (root *r-k-z*) means a low, faint sound — a whisper, a murmur, the barely-audible trace of a voice. Some of the commentators glossed it as a sound so soft it can hardly be distinguished from silence. The sūrah's closing question is therefore doubly emphatic: *Do you perceive a single one of them? Or even hear the faintest sound from them?* Not a person. Not a trace. Not a whisper. That is the complete silence of those who rejected the messengers, and the sūrah places it at the very end as the last thing the reader hears.

**The Historical Referent**

The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, took the "generations" here to refer to the peoples destroyed for rejecting their messengers — ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Noah, the people of Lot, Midian, and the towns overthrown. The Qur'an's account of them is consistent and detailed, and the consistency is part of the argument: these are not referenced once as illustrations but returned to repeatedly, with the same pattern each time — a messenger sent, a people refusing, a warning given, and then the destruction:

> *"And how many a city We destroyed that exulted in its life [of ease]! There are their dwellings, uninhabited after them, save a little; and it is We Who are the Inheritor."* (Qur'an 28:58)

> *"Have they not travelled through the earth and seen how was the end of those before them? They were greater than them in power..."* (Qur'an 30:9)

> *"And We destroyed what Pharaoh's people were building and what they had erected."* (Qur'an 7:137)

The reference is not mythological, and the sūrah's willingness to be checked is worth dwelling on. The Qur'an repeatedly instructs its hearers to *go and look* at the remains — and the remains are still there. The dwellings of Thamūd at Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ in the north-west of the Arabian Peninsula are visible to this day, and the Qur'an's audience would have passed them on the trade route to Syria. A scripture that relied on fable does not invite its readers to walk through the ruins; and this one does so repeatedly, which means its first hearers were in a position to falsify it and did not.

**The Argument of the Closing Verse**

The sūrah's final argument is simple and unanswerable, and it is worth stating formally, because the chapter closes on an inference rather than on an exhortation. Everything the sūrah has said — the mercy at the opening, the stories of the prophets, the refutation of the claim, the promise of love — arrives at this last piece of reasoning, and the reader is left to complete it: The argument runs in five steps. First, many generations before you rejected their messengers. Second, those generations were destroyed. Third, nothing of them remains — not a person, not a sound. Fourth, you are doing what they did. Fifth, therefore you are in the position they were in. The conclusion is not stated as a threat but as a description, and its force comes from the third premise: the sūrah is not predicting what will happen but pointing at what has already happened, repeatedly, to people who were certain it would not. That is the same argument the sūrah made at v. 74, and it is fitting that the chapter should close with it, since the repetition frames the sūrah's entire second half. The sūrah has warned the Quraysh — and every reader after them — with history, and it leaves them with the evidence of their own eyes. What is being refused is the assumption that the present generation is exempt, and the sūrah's method for refusing it is to point at the ground: the ruins are visible, the trade route runs past them, and the people who built them were as certain of their own security as anyone reading this is.

**The Question Form**

Note that the verse ends in a question, and that the question is unanswerable except in one way. The sūrah asks: *do you perceive any of them? Do you hear a sound from them?* The answer is no, and everyone knows it. The question is not seeking information; it is compelling the reader to *say* what he already knows, which is the sūrah's characteristic method from the opening verses (v. 42: "Why do you worship that which neither hears nor sees?"). And there is a deliberate contrast with the sūrah's own content. The destroyed generations are silent — *no whisper from them*. But the sūrah itself is a recitation, a *dhikr* (v. 2), which is being recited to a living people who can still hear it. The silence of the past is placed directly beside the speech of the present, and the reader is being asked which he intends to join.

**The Sūrah's Complete Arc**

It is worth, finally, seeing the whole chapter in one view, because its architecture is unusually symmetrical, and the symmetry is not decorative — it is the form the sūrah's argument takes. A reader who has worked through it verse by verse will have felt the structure without seeing it, and the sūrah rewards being held entire:

| **Verses** | **Movement** |
|---|---|
| 1–15 | *Kāf Hā Yā ʿAyn Ṣād*; the mercy of the Lord; Zachariah's hidden cry; John given, described, blessed. |
| 16–40 | Mary: withdrawal, annunciation, conception, birth beneath the palm, the cradle's testimony, the doctrine stated. |
| 41–57 | Abraham, Moses, Aaron, Ishmael, Idrīs: the gifts given to those who withdraw. |
| 58–63 | The weeping of the prophets; the decline of the generations after them; the door of repentance; the Garden. |
| 64–65 | The delay of revelation; the Lord of the heavens and the earth. |
| 66–87 | The denial of the Resurrection; the Judgement; the boast of the wealthy; the case of al-ʿĀṣ; the false gods; the two processions; intercession. |
| 88–95 | The claim of divine sonship refuted; every creature a servant; all come alone. |
| 96 | The promise of love. |
| 97 | The purpose of the revelation: glad tidings and warning. |
| 98 | The silence of the destroyed. |

The sūrah opens with the letters *Kāf Hā Yā ʿAyn Ṣād* — whose meaning belongs to God — and closes with a whisper that cannot be heard. Between those two silences, it has told the entire story of what God does for those who turn to Him and what becomes of those who do not. The framing is deliberate, and it is the last thing the sūrah says about itself: the chapter begins where meaning is withheld and ends where sound has ceased, and everything that can be known and said is contained in the interval between them. That interval is the reader's life.

**A Closing Word on the Sūrah's Unity**

The sūrah is named for Mary, and its central section is hers. But its subject is mercy, and its method is the same in every section: a servant is placed beyond the reach of ordinary help, and God reaches him. Zachariah is old and his wife is barren, and the answer comes in secret. Mary is alone and accused, and the answer comes from a cradle. Abraham is banished, and the answer is a lineage. Moses is in the desert, and the answer is a voice from a mountain. The persecuted believers of Makkah have nowhere to go, and the answer is this recitation, made easy in their own tongue. And the sūrah's last word is a promise for anyone standing where they stood:

> *"Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds — the Compassionate will grant them love."* (19:96)

The destroyed generations are silent. The living are still being spoken to. That is the difference, and it is the whole of what is at stake — and it is why the sūrah ends as it does rather than with a summary or a doxology. The last thing the reader is given is not a conclusion but a contrast: those who were addressed and refused can no longer be addressed, and those who are still being addressed are the ones holding this. The sūrah's final sentence is therefore not a threat but an invitation, and it is extended to anyone still able to hear a sound.

---
